<sig>* 3</sig>

# PREFACE
## TO THE
# R E A D E R .

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'I' decorated with floral and vine patterns.</image-desc>

IN what high regard from the times of the Kings of Israel until now the Holy Scripture has been held by all holy and God-loving people, is especially evident among the Jewish People and their ancient Rabbis, alongside so many annotators and interpreters of the same, to this day; indeed, the Prophets testify throughout their writings how useful and necessary the Holy Scripture is, and how much it concerns mankind to know the will of Almighty God, to strengthen their faith thereby, in order to finally obtain eternal happiness, both here in this temporal life and in the hereafter.

Thus, I have judged it not unhelpful to have the principal stories <note>The original Dutch "Historien" refers both to historical accounts and narrative stories.</note> of the Old and New Testament depicted in copper <note>referring to copperplate engravings, a common method for high-quality book illustrations during this period</note>, to lead everyone as if by the hand to the Holy Scripture, and especially to draw away the young youth, who for the most part are inclined toward vanity, and to give them something at hand where

<summary>In this preface, the author highlights the historical and spiritual importance of the Bible and explains his decision to produce copperplate engravings of biblical stories to engage readers, particularly the youth.</summary>
<keywords>preface, Holy Scripture, Bible illustrations, copperplate engravings, religious education, youth, salvation, Rabbis</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, detailing the author's intent for the biblical illustrations and beginning a chronological summary of the Old Testament narratives included in the work.</meta>

# PREFACE

in which they might delight themselves, and be led toward a godly observation and investigation of the unfathomable Wisdom of God, and to allow the lovers of true virtue to broaden their thoughts upon this enjoyable and divine subject matter: For if you look through these figures <note>The Dutch "Figuren" refers to the copperplate engravings/illustrations.</note> with proper attention, you will be able to contemplate the great and wonderful creation of the Universe <note>original: "Heel-Al"</note>; the glorious state of Mankind in Paradise; his Fall, and the destruction of the First World; thereafter, how God established His Covenant of Grace once again with Noah; the building of the Tower of Babel; the history of Lot, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob; how Joseph was sold by his brothers, and how his brothers, afterwards coming to him, bowed down before him, and with what great friendship Joseph receives his father Jacob in Egypt; then, how the Children of Israel, after the death of Joseph, were very miserably tormented by the Egyptians; how God delivered them by the hand of Moses; how Pharaoh with all his host <note>original: "Heir", meaning army or military force</note> drowned in the Red Sea, and how miraculously God led His people through the wilderness to the Land of Canaan; how they crossed through the Jordan, took the entire land, and divided it among the Twelve Tribes; afterwards Israel desired a king, and God gave them Saul the son of Kish, then David and Solomon, who built a Temple for the Lord,

<summary>The author explains that the book's illustrations are intended to inspire godly contemplation and then provides a chronological summary of the Old Testament stories depicted, from Creation to the building of Solomon's Temple.</summary>
<keywords>Old Testament, Creation, Paradise, Noah, Tower of Babel, Joseph, Egypt, Moses, Exodus, Canaan, Twelve Tribes, King Saul, King David, King Solomon, Temple of Jerusalem</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the chronological summary of biblical history, transitioning from the kings of Israel and Judah to the life of Jesus Christ and the mission of the Apostles, concluding the author’s address to the reader.</meta>

-># TO THE READER.<-

as well as Jeroboam, Hezekiah, and so forth; until finally the Scepter of Judah was taken away <note>A reference to the prophecy in Genesis 49:10, historically interpreted as a sign of the Messiah's arrival when Jewish self-rule ended.</note>; from which it appeared, according to the Prophecies, that the coming of the Messiah was now at hand, who also came at the appointed time, under the Reign of Emperor Augustus, when Quirinius <note>original: "Cyrenus"</note> was Governor over Syria; then the Christ was born in Bethlehem, in the City of David, located in Judea, and named Jesus; and growing up, He taught and preached everywhere the Gospel of the Kingdom of God, healing all sicknesses and ailments, proving through signs and wonders that He was powerfully the Son of Almighty God, foretelling to His Disciples the Suffering that would come upon Him, His Resurrection and Ascension, according to the Scriptures of the Prophets, saying: *Ought not the Christ to have suffered these things, and to enter into His Glory?* <note>Luke 24:26</note> All of which the Holy Apostles have seen and experienced, just as they sufficiently testify with their writings in the New Testament, and how they followed their Lord and Master with their Godly Life, to draw us thereby, as far as was possible for them, to the contemplation of the perfect Will of God, and finally to the Salvation of all our souls.

Receive then these **Biblical Figures** <note>The Dutch "Bybelsche Figuren" refers specifically to the copperplate engravings/illustrations contained in this book.</note> with affection, and let the examples of the Patriarchs <note>original: "Oudvaderen"</note> contained in these histories serve as an example for you to follow for your good.

# PREFACE TO THE READER.

Wishing that the readers may find the sweetness of the Heavenly Honey from this **Flower-Garden** <note>original: "Bloem-Hof"; a common 18th-century term for a collection or anthology of stories, literally "flower-garden" or "florilegium."</note> of Sacred Histories, so that this labor of mine might be all the more pleasant, and be received with greater affection; remaining herewith

->Your Humble Servant<- <note>original: "UE: D: W: Dr.", an abbreviation for "Uw Edeles Dienstwillige Dienaar," a standard formal closing of the period meaning "Your Noble [Personage’s] Willing Servant."</note>

Amsterdam
the 5th of February
1736.

### JACOB GRAAL.

<summary>This page concludes the author's preface to his collection of biblical histories, offering the work to the reader with the hope that it provides spiritual sweetness and is received with favor. It is signed and dated by Jacob Graal in Amsterdam, 1736.</summary>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page, serving as the opening thematic declaration for the volume. It illustrates the creation of the world using a polyglot approach—providing the same theological sentiment in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German. This was common in high-quality Dutch publications of the 18th century to appeal to an international scholarly audience.</meta>

->All things were made through him.<-
<note>original Latin: "Omnia per ipsum facta sunt"; a reference to the Gospel of John 1:3, emphasizing Christ's role in creation.</note>

Behold, spectator, the beginnings of the vast World,
And whatever the Father commanded to exist by his word:
Thus the Sea, thus the Heaven, thus the rest were established, as much
As the wise and almighty God is known from this.
<note>original Latin: "Aspice spectator vasti primordia Mundi..."</note>

### *The Creation of the World.*

God drew from nothingness this whole vast Universe,
The Heaven, the Earth, and the Sea, and all pay him homage,
Then, to crown his various works,
He made man, and upon man impressed his image.
<note>original French: "La Creation du Monde."</note>

### The Creation of Heaven and Earth.

When God had cast the world-sphere into its form,
Heaven, Earth, and Sea, and Air sprang forth from nothing,
What rational mind can grasp this wonder-work?
Strong and steadfast Faith makes this reasoning strong.
<note>original Dutch: "De Scheppinge van Hemel en Aarde." The term <term>Wereld-kloot</term> (world-ball) refers to the globe or the physical sphere of the earth.</note>

### *All things are made by Him.*

Matter in motion, figure, rest, and grade,
This is the very sum of All God made,
At first of nought, by his power in six days' space.
Now nature acts its part: hereafter Grace.
<note>This verse appears in English in the original document, demonstrating the book was intended for an English-speaking market as well as Continental Europe.</note>

### God created the Heavens and the Earth.

When God had shaped and cast the mass of the world,
Heaven, Earth, and Air sprouted from nothing,
And everything that one sees, feels, and finds there.
Whoever does not believe this is willfully deaf and blind.
<note>original German: "Gott schuff die Himmel und Erden."</note>

<gloss>Creation, World, Universe, World-sphere, Faith, God, Nature, Grace, Heavens, Earth</gloss>

<summary>This page introduces the biblical narrative of Creation through a series of poems in five languages, emphasizing God's omnipotence in forming the universe from nothing.</summary>
<keywords>Creation, Genesis, polyglot, world-sphere, faith vs. reason, nature and grace, 18th-century theology</keywords>

# GENESIS I.
A 2
<meta>Image plate number: 1; catchword: Deëra</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the biblical creation of the world. In the upper center, the Hebrew Tetragrammaton (יהוה <note>The sacred four-letter name of God, often translated as LORD.</note>) is inscribed within a radiant burst of light surrounded by clouds. To the right, the sun and a crescent moon appear in the sky alongside various flying birds. Below, a lush terrestrial landscape is populated with diverse animals, including a lion, a horse, a stag, cattle, and birds such as a peacock, a heron, and an owl. A cursive caption is written in a box beneath the scene.</image-desc>

->*The Creation of the World.*<-

Before the beginning of the World, there was that Eternal, Almighty, and incomprehensible <term>Being of all beings</term> <note>original Dutch: "Wezen aller wezens"; a philosophical way of describing God as the fundamental source of all existence.</note>, having neither beginning nor end, being the only Blessed and all-sufficient God, without whom nothing is nor can be. Being subject to no change, He remains through all eternity always one and the same, without whom nothing is enduring. This God, through the Word, created Heaven and Earth out of nothing—being the visible and invisible World—to demonstrate His power and glory. In the beginning, it was as a <term>Chaos</term> <note>Referring to the "formless and void" state of the universe before divine order was imposed.</note>, with everything mixed together; and so that it would emerge in a good order, that great God made a separation between the light and the darkness, and He called the light Day, and the darkness Night. And thus God subsequently created in six days the Heaven, the Sun, Moon, and Stars, and the Earth, the Sea, with all the animals; also Man in His likeness, and God rested on the seventh day, and blessed the same.

<summary>This page introduces the narrative of Genesis, describing the nature of God as an eternal being and detailing the creation of the universe from chaos into order over six days.</summary>
<keywords>Genesis, Creation of the World, God, Heaven and Earth, Chaos, Creation of Man, Tetragrammaton, Six Days of Creation</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous description of the initial "Chaos" and the ordering of the world. Having established the heavens, the earth, and the animals, the text now transitions to the crowning achievement of the sixth day: the creation of humanity. The catchword "Deëra" from the previous page matches the opening word of the Latin poem on this page, "Deërat."</meta>

# All things are put under his feet.
<note>original Latin: "Omnia ſubjecti pedibus ejus."</note>

There was still lacking a creature of a more noble mind <note>The phrasing here is heavily influenced by the Roman poet Ovid’s *Metamorphoses*, blending classical literary style with the biblical narrative.</note>, who might rule the created beasts by his will. Man was made, and commanded to lift his face upright toward the stars; in him shines the image of the highest God.

->*The fruit of the Tree is forbidden to Adam.*<-
<note>original French: "Le fruit de l' Arbre eſt defendu à Adam."</note>

How happy Adam was upon receiving the gift of life! God, who made everything for him, subjects the whole world to him; but He intends that Adam, in turn, submit himself, and under penalty of death, obey His command.

->Adam, the first man, is created.<-
<note>original Dutch: "Adam de eerſte Menſch word geſchapen."</note>

Here the clay receives a soul, driven by the Spirit of Heaven, which first gave Adam breath upon the earth: thus a ray descends into him from the <term>uncreated Sun</term> <note>A theological metaphor for God as the eternal source of light and life who exists without being created.</note>, a spark of the eternal fire, a drop from the fountain of life.

->*He laid all things under his feet.*<-

Yet still there is a lack of such a creature as may have rule and sway over these senseless beasts. God then makes man with his face toward the sky; he is the true likeness of the Deity.

->Adam, the first man, is created.<-
<note>original German: "Adam der erſte Menſch wird geſchaffen."</note>

When all things now existed in their being and form, there was still a lack of one who could manage them through wisdom and reason; whereupon the merciful Creator formed the first man after His own image.

<meta>catchword: After</meta>

<summary>This page presents a multilingual celebration of the creation of Adam, highlighting the human being's unique status as a creature made in the image of God with the capacity for reason and dominion over the animal kingdom.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, Creation of Man, Imago Dei, Image of God, Soul, Polyglot, Genesis, Reason, Dominion, Ovid</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Creation from the previous page. Having established the physical world and the animal kingdom, the text now focuses specifically on the crowning achievement of the sixth day: the creation of the first human, Adam. GENESIS 1:2

**An engraving within a rectangular frame depicting the creation of Adam. A nude Adam is seated on a rocky outcrop in a lush, wooded landscape. In the center of the sky, a radiant glory emanates from the Hebrew Tetragrammaton <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, יהוה (YHWH).</note>. Various animals are present, including a deer, a dog, a bird, and a squirrel. At the bottom of the frame, a caption is centered below the scene.**

->The Creation of Adam.<-

After God had created Heaven and Earth, with everything that was therein, out of nothing, He then created Man as the noblest of His creatures. He gave him heavenly and earthly properties to have dominion over the entire earth. Adam did not receive his natural life and body from seed <note>Meaning he was not born through biological reproduction like later humans, but created directly.</note>, but from God Himself through the breathing-in of His Spirit. By this Spirit, he was gifted with divine wisdom, holiness, sincerity, and love for God and his <term>fellow man</term> <note>original Dutch: *Evenmensch*. This reflects the biblical "neighbor," though at this point in the story, Adam is alone.</note>. Knowing the mysteries of all earthly things, he gave all animals their names according to the nature and property of each. In short, he was entirely perfect, with his head facing upwards toward Heaven, so that Man should always contemplate heavenly things more than earthly things. This was so that he might live with an inward and outward joy, to the honor and praise of his Creator, who had formed him so gloriously.

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Jam</meta>

<summary>This page describes the creation of Adam as the "noblest creature," emphasizing his divine origin, his role as ruler of the earth, and his intellectual perfection in naming the animals.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, Creation, Genesis, Tetragrammaton, dominion, naming of animals, divine breath, Image of God</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the Genesis narrative, transitioning from the singular creation of Adam to the creation of his companion, Eve. While the previous page established Adam as the "noblest of creatures" with dominion over the earth, this page explores his initial solitude and God’s provision of a "helpmeet" taken from his own side. The text uses several languages to reinforce the biblical account of the first marriage.</meta>

# Adam was formed first, then Eve. <note>original Latin: "Adam prior formatus est, deinde Eva." This is a citation from the New Testament, 1 Timothy 2:13.</note>

Now the new Adam enters and surveys the ranks <note>original: "agmina," referring to the ranks of animals Adam had just named.</note>,
He sees all created things, but sees not his own heart's desire:
The Almighty snatches a rib from his side, so that soon He
Might return it as a helpmate, a sweet comfort—Eve.

---

### The Creation of Eve.

What would man have done alone, master of so many goods!
God did not create him to be solitary:
He draws Eve from Adam, and by tight bonds
Makes her become for him a necessary help.

---

### Eve, the first woman, is created from a rib of Adam.

God caused a deep sleep to descend upon Adam,
And knew how to take a rib from his body without pain,
He built from it a woman, whom Adam loved upon seeing:
Happy is the soul that binds itself with God.

---

### *Adam was first formed, then Eve.*

Now Adam does begin, as Lord of All,
To rule: yet lacks a fitting help <note>original: "meete hulp." In the 17th century, "meet" meant "suitable" or "proper." This is the origin of the term "helpmeet."</note>, to him equal:
Wherefore God made him sleep: lest he perceive,
How from his side arose our mother Eve.

---

### Eve, the first woman, is created from a rib of Adam.

When Earth and Heaven stood, and man himself stood alongside them,
The mother from whom our life comes was still lacking.
God judged it not good for Adam to be alone:
Therefore He created for him a wife out of his rib-bone.

A
<meta>catchword: As</meta>

GENESIS II. 3
A 4
<meta>handwritten "8" above page number; catchword: Garden <note>original: "Hortus"</note></meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the creation of Eve in the Garden of Eden. Adam lies sleeping on the left, while Eve rises beside him, extending her arms toward a radiant celestial light in the center. Within this glowing oval, the Hebrew Tetragrammaton <note>the four-letter name of God, יהוה</note> is inscribed. The landscape is filled with trees and animals, including a stag in the foreground, a mountain goat on a rocky outcrop, and birds in the sky. Below the scene, a caption is printed.</image-desc>

->The Creation of Eve.<-

When Adam now perceived in what a glorious state God had placed him, and how temperately everything was moistened by a sweet mist; for there was neither cold nor heat, tempests, biting winds, nor anything that could cause harm, so that Man felt all the delights of the Earth and of Heaven in his soul, such as the clarity of the Sun, the beauty of the Trees and Mountains, and the pleasantness of the Herbs; and to increase his joy and gladness, God added to him as his help our mother Eve <note>original Dutch: "Heva"</note>, so that they both should be one flesh. Therefore shall a man leave his Father and Mother and cleave unto his Wife, so that they both should be one flesh. And thus through this first Marriage was symbolized the eternal Union <note>original Dutch: "Egt," a term for marriage or a legal bond</note> between God and the Soul, which according to His covenant shall never leave us as His Bride, if we remain faithful to Him, but lead us through His grace to eternal blessedness.

<note>Vocabulary: Adam, Eve, Creation, Genesis, Marriage, God, Eden, The Tetragrammaton <note>The Hebrew name of God, יהוה</note>, Bride</note>

<summary>This page describes the idyllic climate of the Garden of Eden and the creation of Eve as a companion for Adam. It interprets the first marriage not only as a physical union but as a spiritual symbol of the eternal bond between God and the human soul.</summary>
<keywords>Garden of Eden, Creation of Eve, Marriage, Mysticism, Covenant, Adam and Eve, Paradise</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the early days of humanity in Eden. Having described the creation of Eve on the previous page, the text now shifts focus to the first law given by God and the looming shadow of the Fall. The page presents the same moral lesson in four languages—Latin, French, Dutch, and German—alongside a contemporary English verse, emphasizing the universal nature of this biblical event.</meta>

# The First Divine Commandment.
<note>original Latin: "Primum Præceptum Divinum."</note>

The Garden of Eden stands planted with various trees,
Its inhabitant Adam may taste any of their fruits;
But one is excluded—so stands the sentence—that **DEATH**
He shall meet if, as a transgressor, he eats from it.
<note>original Latin: "Hortus Eden varia ſtat conſitus arbore..."</note>

---

### The Serpent sets a trap for Adam and Eve.
<note>original French: "Le Serpent dreſſe un piege à Adam & à Eve."</note>

*In the happiness of our first parents,*
*The envious Serpent, a treacherous and supple animal,*
*Makes them imagine far greater pleasures,*
*Leads them to revolt, and ruins this happy couple.*

---

### Adam and Eve transgress God's commandment.
<note>original Dutch: "Adam en Heva overtreden 't gebod Gods."</note>

O Eve! do you then taste that Apple, so sweet of flavor?
The aftertaste is too bitter: how dearly this pleasure costs you!
Alas, Adam! do you allow yourself to be led astray and charmed?
Savior, descend; or all the world is lost.
<note>The Dutch text uses "Heva" for Eve, a common older spelling reflecting the phonetic sound of the Hebrew name 'Chavah'.</note>

---

### *The first divine Commandment.*

*In Eden’s Garden full of fruitful trees,*
*Adam is placed, to taste all fruits he sees,*
*Except the fruit of one tree, which if he*
*Do taste, the forfeit is, that he must die.*

---

### Adam and Eve transgress God’s commandment.
<note>original German: "Adam und Heva übertreten Gottes gebot."</note>

God adorns the Garden of Eden with fruitful trees,
Prepared most gloriously, where He lodges the humans;
He allows them every fruit, only one tree He forbade:
Whoever would eat of it, him should Death seize <note>original: "würgen," literally to strangle or slay</note>.

<meta>catchword: Then <note>original: "Doen"</note></meta>

<vocab>Adam, Eve, Eden, Garden, Fall of Man, Serpent, Commandment, Law</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the first divine prohibition given to Adam and Eve in Eden and foreshadows their temptation by the serpent. It presents a multilingual reflection on the transition from innocence to the threat of mortality.</summary>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 8, moving from the initial commandment to the actual moment of the Fall as described in Genesis 3. It expands on the theological consequences of the transgression, transitioning from the multi-language verses of the previous page to a more detailed Dutch prose commentary.</meta>

A Type of the Suffering Church in our first Parents.
<note>original Latin: "Typus Ecclesiæ patientis in primis Parentib." In theology, a "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament seen as a foreshadowing of something in the New Testament. Here, the suffering of Adam and Eve after the Fall is linked to the historical suffering of the Christian Church.</note>

# GENESIS 3.

<image-desc>A copperplate engraving depicting the Fall of Man in the Garden of Eden. Eve stands on the left, reaching up into the branches of the Tree of Knowledge to pluck fruit. Adam is seated on a grassy mound on the right, looking up at her and gesturing. A serpent is coiled around the trunk of the tree between them. The surrounding landscape features a variety of trees and animals, including a lion, a stag, and birds on the ground and in a pond. The number 4 is printed in the upper right corner within the image's border.</image-desc>

->Adam and Eve in Paradise.<-

When it seemed that everything was inclined toward blessedness, and that Man might eat from all the trees in Paradise, including the tree of life, the tempting Devil came in the form of a Serpent and deceived the Woman. She, in turn, led her Husband astray, so that they both transgressed the commandment of God and ate from the forbidden Tree of the knowledge of good and evil, despite God having said: "On the day that you eat of it, you shall surely die." <note>original Dutch: "zult gy den dood ſterven," literally "shall die the death," a common Hebraism found in early Bible translations.</note> And thus, through one man, sin and death entered into the world and passed on to all people. That glorious Creature <note>Referring to humanity as God’s "heerlyk Schepſel" or glorious creation.</note> was completely deformed by the cunning of the Devil and robbed of all joy; and had it been possible for him, he would have plunged us all into the pit of eternal misery.

# A Type of the Suffering Church in the First Martyr.
<note>original Latin: "Typus Ecclesiæ patientis in primo Martyre." In early modern theology, a "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament that foreshadows a future reality. Here, Abel's death is seen as the first instance of the "Suffering Church"—the righteous being persecuted by the wicked.</note>

Two twin brothers offer sacrifice; one brings grain, but the other
Offers little lambs and the firstlings of the flock:
Kind faith commends the younger, while the elder **Hypocrite**
Slays him: hence **Nemesis** <note>Nemesis is the Greek goddess of retribution; here used to signify divine justice or the "sting" of a guilty conscience.</note> pricks him and drives his evil soul.

---

->*Cain kills his brother Abel.*<-

*Envy arms Cain with rage and fury,*
*He finds Abel in the fields, he attacks, he kills him;*
*And of this first murder being the infamous author,*
*To conceal it, he strives in vain.*

---

->Cain strikes his brother Abel dead.<-

O Cain! evil by nature, God hates your **burnt offering**, <term>offerbrand: a sacrifice consumed by fire</term>
Your heart committed that murder and the slaughter by your hand.
Your [brother] Abel was chosen by God through faith;
But the unbelieving heart must smother in its sins.

---

->*A Type of the patient Church in this first Martyr.*<-

Cain and Abel offer Sacrifice,
God accepts Abel's, Cain's he does despise.
Cain enraged his brother Abel slays,
For which God scourges Cain all his days.

---

->Cain sheds his brother's blood.<-

Cain through hypocrisy offers the fruit of the earth;
Through faith, Abel offers the firstlings of the herds.
This sacrifice pleased God, whereupon Cain grew enraged,
Shedding his brother's **blood**; God appoints vengeance for him.

<vocab>Cain, Abel, Martyr, Sacrifice, Envy, Nemesis, <term>Hypocrita</term> (Hypocrite), <term>offerbrand</term> (burnt offering), <term>bluth</term> (blood)</vocab>

It frames Abel's murder as the first instance of a righteous "martyr" suffering at the hands of the wicked, establishing a theological archetype for the persecuted Church.</summary>

# GENESIS 4

->Cain strikes Abel dead.<-

Cain seeks to please God through an outward offering, bringing to Him some of his fruit; but Abel, loving God exceedingly, brings the best of his livestock, adding thereto his faithful heart. This was very pleasing to God, which is why He accepts Abel’s offering and rejects Cain with his offering. For through faith, Abel offered a greater sacrifice to God than Cain, so that God gave testimony over his gifts that he was righteous. <note>This refers to Hebrews 11:4, a common New Testament commentary on this Old Testament story.</note> Because of this, Cain became angry and struck his brother dead in the field, proving that the Serpent had shot its hidden venom into his heart. <note>The "venom" refers to the influence of the Devil, often depicted as the serpent from the Garden of Eden.</note> He found thereafter that sin had wrought great terror and unrest within him instead of peace, with a great gnawing of his **Conscience**. <term>Conscientie: Conscience. In the 17th century, a guilty conscience was often described as a "worm" that physically gnaws at the soul.</term> So it was that he became a fugitive and a wanderer in the land, with the blood of Abel crying out for vengeance from the earth, and the voice of the Lord foretelling his punishment.

<summary>This page describes the first murder in human history, where Cain kills his brother Abel out of jealousy over their sacrifices. It emphasizes that God values the "faithful heart" over outward rituals and details Cain's subsequent psychological torment and exile.</summary>
<keywords>Cain, Abel, sacrifice, faith, murder, conscience, vengeance, Genesis 4</keywords>

# A Type of Baptism and of the Preserved Church.
<meta>catchword: God</meta>

**J**ehovah, about to avenge great wickedness and crimes with waters, <note>original Latin: "Grande nefas ulturus aquis & crimina, JOVA"</note>
Protects you and yours, Noah, from destruction:
He shuts up the seeds of four-footed beasts and birds in the Ark,
Truly **GOD** preserves and loves His own congregations.

---

### God wishes to flood the Earth, and commands Noah to build the Ark.

**G**od, grieved to see man abandoned to crime,
*Explains His plan for their destruction confidentially to Noah;*
*And Noah, following the plan that God Himself gave,*
*Builds a magnificent Ark for his family.*

---

### Noah and his household enter the Ark.

**H**ere I see depicted the <term>counter-image of Baptism</term>, <note>original Dutch: "tegenbeeld der Doop." This refers to the theological concept of "typology," where Old Testament events are seen as symbols or "types" that foreshadow New Testament truths. Here, the water of the flood that saves Noah is a "type" of the water of baptism.</note>
Where such a small number, out of such a great multitude,
Is sheltered with Noah. How little can preaching
Ignite virtue in the hearts of unrepentant people.

---

### *A Type of Baptism, and of a preserved Church.*

**G**od for man's sins intends the world to drown
*With man and beast and all that's in it found,*
*But He in the Ark saves Noah and his seed,*
*With pairs of all kinds which on earth did breed.*

---

### Noah goes with his household into the Ark.

**T**he Lord commands Noah and seven others to enter the chest, <note>original German: "Noah selb acht," meaning Noah himself plus seven others (his wife, three sons, and three daughters-in-law), totaling the "eight souls" mentioned in 1 Peter 3:20.</note>
As He wished to punish the world through the terrible Great Flood;
He took the animals to Himself, so that they would be protected,
For God knows well how to save His Church from destruction.

<summary>This page uses the story of Noah's Ark to illustrate the theological concept of "typology," presenting the Ark as a symbol of the Church and the Flood as a precursor to Christian Baptism. It emphasizes God's dual nature: His righteous judgment of a "wicked world" and His protective love for a "small number" of the faithful.</summary>

<keywords>Noah, Ark, Baptism, Great Flood, Typology, Church, Covenant, Salvation, Divine Judgment</keywords>

# GENESIS 6:6

->Noah Enters the Ark<-

**G**od, seeing that the entire world increased more and more in its sins and lusts, and that people cared for nothing but the flesh—both the descendants of Cain, who for that reason were called the **children of Men**, and the descendants of Seth, who, before they were entangled in such an abyss of sin, were called the **children of God** <note>This distinction between the "Children of Men" (Cain's line) and "Children of God" (Seth's line) was a common historical interpretation used to explain the moral decay leading to the Flood.</note>—He regretted that He had made Mankind, and therefore intended to destroy all flesh by a **Deluge** <note>original Dutch: "Zond-vloed." While often translated as "Flood," the term implies a flood sent as a punishment for sin.</note>, except for Noah with his household, and certain pairs of all kinds of cattle and birds, in order to replenish the earth again after the destruction. For this purpose, He commanded Noah to build an **Ark**; during the construction of the same, he exhorted the people to repentance for <term>one hundred and twenty years</term> <note>This time frame is based on a traditional reading of Genesis 6:3, interpreting the "120 years" not as a lifespan, but as a period of grace given to humanity to repent while the Ark was being built.</note>, but in vain. Therefore Noah, the time being fulfilled, went into the Ark by God’s command with his household and all the ordained animals.

<header>The wrath of God is revealed against all ungodliness <note>original Latin: *Ira Dei manifesta est super omnem impietatem*</note></header>

**C**ontinuously the highest Alps are overcome by rains,
Alas! the human race and the four-legged kind perish,
**A**nd the birds, while the realms of Neptune <note>The Roman god of the sea, used here as a poetic personification of the rising floodwaters.</note> rule the lands:
What <term>repentance</term> <note>original: *Metanœa*, from the Greek *metanoia*, meaning a transformative change of heart or mind.</note> cannot achieve, the wave washes away as sin.

### *The Deluge.*

**T**hen God makes it rain, and from a dark Sky
*He hurls down torrents, and destroys His work:*
*All perish beneath the waters by a frightful deluge,*
*Noah alone, found just, is saved from the shipwreck.*

### The Flood comes upon everything outside the Ark.

**W**here do you flee from the danger? Why do you climb the trees!
The distress is everywhere. Why do you run before the streams?
**T**he Sea now has no shore. Thus the flood purifies
**T**he sins, for which you easily could have atoned with tears.

### *The wrath of God manifested upon all wickedness.*

***T**he highest hills by waters are overspread,*
*Mountains, trees, towers in floods lie buried,*
*Men, women, beasts, and birds are quite destroyed,*
*Waters possess all that the land enjoyed.*

### The Flood comes upon all that is outside the Ark.

**T**he Flood comes with power, the rains wash everything away
Every creature drowns, whatever lives on dry land,
**N**oah’s Chest <note>original German: *Kasten*. A common historical term for the Ark, emphasizing its shape as a great box or container.</note> alone floats high upon the waters,
**W**hat true repentance does not do, the Flood must take away.

GENESIS 7:7

An engraving depicting the Great Flood. In the foreground, numerous people and a few animals struggle in turbulent waters or cling to rocks and a large tree. ->The Deluge over the World.<-

---

Noah having entered the Ark with his household and the ordained animals, the Deluge began with the bursting of the fountains of the great deep from below, and a continuous downpour from above, overwhelming the carefree human race, who were still busy engaging in all worldly pleasures. But these soon changed into a wretched moaning, crying, and wringing of hands. The Ark, carrying the remnant of humans and animals, now became buoyant, while everyone sought to save themselves by all conceivable means, but all in vain. The time of God’s patience was over, and the day of His wrath had come, on which He, as Judge of the entire earth, wished to justly punish the old, wicked world as an example, so that in following centuries, the children of men might be encouraged by it to live with greater godliness in the fear of God hereafter on earth for their own salvation.

It presents the destruction of the "old world" as a moral example intended to inspire piety in future generations.</summary>

# God’s Covenant is confirmed by signs.

**T**he ancient face of the earth returns as the waters recede,
And Noah's Ark came to rest on the Armenian mountains <note>Historically, the mountains of Ararat were often identified with the Armenian highlands</note>.
The Rainbow <note>original Latin: *Iris*</note> shall be the witness, painted in various colors,
That the world shall never again perish by the waters.

### *God places His bow in the clouds as a sign of the alliance He makes with Noah.*

**A**s a sacred sign of an unchangeable peace,
The Monarch of the Heavens <note>A poetic title for God</note> places a bow in the clouds,
And as this beautiful bow shall never fail,
This peace shall be maintained forever.

### God's covenant with His creatures, secured by the Rainbow.

**T**he swollen waters recede. How brilliantly the smooth watercolor of Heaven’s Rainbow shines on high! O Noah, trumpet of repentance <note>original Dutch: *boet-bazuin*; a reference to Noah’s role as a preacher who warned the world to repent before the flood</note>, take comfort in this sign: God shall not break His faithful covenant for all eternity.

### *The covenant of God is confirmed by signs.*

> **T**he heavenly floodgates are shut, and the wind
> Dries up the waters; the Dove finds land,
> The Ark is aground, Noah comes ashore,
> God promises to drown the world no more.

### God makes a covenant with Noah.

**T**he waters recede; the Ark <note>original German: *Kasten*, literally "chest" or "box," following Martin Luther’s translation choice to emphasize it was a vessel for floating rather than a ship for sailing</note> settles down upon Mount Ararat; Noah finds dry ground. A rainbow appears, testifying to God’s covenant that He would not destroy the world through water again.

# GENESIS 9.
<meta>figure number: 8</meta>

->The Covenant of Grace with Noah.<-

**A**FTER the waters had prevailed over the earth for one hundred and fifty days, they began gradually to recede; and when they had entirely subsided, Noah went out of the Ark again with his household, and all that was within it: and having gone out, he and his household kindled a sacrifice to the honor of God for their preservation. So it was that God, through the rainbow that appeared immediately, made it known that this was pleasing to Him <note>original Dutch: "dat hem dit geviel"; meaning the sacrifice was acceptable and well-pleasing to God</note>, and likewise testified that He would no longer destroy the human race in such a manner; furthermore, He blessed Noah and his sons anew with the same blessing with which He had blessed our ancestors in Paradise, and He said: Be fruitful, and replenish the earth, and the fear and dread of you shall be upon every beast of the earth.

# GENESIS 9.
<meta>figure number: 9</meta>

<header>The mocker is condemned to slavery.</header>

<image-desc>A faint copperplate engraving serves as a background for the text, depicting the biblical scene of the drunkenness of Noah. Noah is shown lying naked and asleep on the ground. One of his sons, Ham, stands nearby, pointing and mocking him. The other two sons, Shem and Japheth, are shown walking backward toward their father, carrying a large cloth between them to cover his nakedness without looking at him.</image-desc>

Drunk, Noah is weighed down by sleep; the degenerate Ham
Sees him and laughs, but he departs under a curse:
Japheth and Shem cover their father’s nakedness,
From this, a pious posterity, a blessed race, descends.
<note>original Latin: "Ebrius opprimitur somno Noa..." This verse establishes the moral framework of the page: the contrast between the "degenerate" son and the "pious" ones.</note>

---

->The piety of Shem and Japheth, and the impiety of Ham.<-

Noah plants the vineyard, and from the wine he makes
He first becomes drunk, and reveals his shame:
Ham laughs at it without respect; but both Shem and Japheth
Go to give their father prompt assistance.
<note>original French: "La pieté de Sem & de Japhet..." This version emphasizes the "prompt assistance" of the two sons, highlighting their virtue as an active choice.</note>

---

->Noah being drunk, is mocked by Ham, his youngest son.<-

O Ham! Will you, as a son, still mock your father?
Your offspring inherits your curse, if you choose to conceive.
One who honors his parents receives not only the reward
Of a blessing for himself, but eternally for his son.
<note>original Dutch: "O Cham! zult gy, als Zoon, uw Vader nog begekken?" The Dutch verse focuses heavily on the hereditary nature of the curse and blessing, a common theme in 17th-century morality.</note>

---

->The mocker is destined for slavery.<-

Naked and drunk asleep, Ham saw Noah,
He mocks and jeers him against nature’s law,
Shem and Japheth cover his nakedness,
Ham he does curse, Shem and Japheth he does bless.
<note>original English: "The mocker is destinated to slaverie." This early modern English verse uses the term "nature's law" to describe the inherent duty a child owes a parent.</note>

---

->Noah is mocked by Ham.<-

Noah, the good old man, had overindulged in wine,
And slept naked in the tent; his son Ham laughed at him:
Therefore, as a rightful reward, he brought the curse upon himself;
Upon Shem and Japheth the father’s blessing has come.
<note>original German: "Noah der guth Alt sich mit Wein hadt übernommen..." The German text refers to Noah affectionately as "the good old man," perhaps to soften the depiction of his intoxication.</note>

### GENESIS 9:9

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the biblical scene from Genesis 9. Noah is shown lying asleep and naked under a simple wooden shelter. In the foreground, his son Ham points mockingly at his father's nakedness while looking back at his brothers. The two other sons, Shem and Japheth, are shown walking backward toward their father, holding a large cloth draped over their shoulders to cover him without looking at his nakedness. In the background, a hilly landscape features a vineyard.</image-desc>

->Ham mocks his father Noah.<-

Although God had destroyed and purified most of the sins of the first world through the general Deluge <term>Deluge: the Great Flood of Noah</term>, it became entirely clear afterward that all sins had not been annihilated by it. For when Noah, applying himself to farming <note>original: "tot den bouw", literally "to the building/cultivation," referring here to his new life as a husbandman or farmer</note>, had planted the vineyard, and—unaware of the strength of the wine—was overcome by it and revealed his nakedness, his youngest son Ham mocked him. Out of wantonness <note>original: "dertelheid", implying a disrespectful or frivolous lack of restraint</note>, Ham informed his two brothers of their father’s shame while they were busy outside in the field. However, the two eldest, Shem and Japheth, reverently placed a garment upon both their shoulders and, walking backward, covered him. Through this act, they earned a blessing, while Ham earned a curse.

<sig>B 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Callida (Latin: "Skilful" or "Crafty")</meta>
<meta>translator note: The vocabulary list from the source is incorporated into the narrative translation above.</meta>

<summary>This page recounts the biblical story of Noah's drunkenness and the varying responses of his three sons. It contrasts the mockery of Ham with the filial respect shown by Shem and Japheth, explaining the origin of their respective blessing and curse.</summary>

<header>God humbles the high. <note>original: "Deus alta humiliat"</note></header>

<image-desc>A faint, ghosted illustration, likely from a copperplate print on the reverse or a light impression, depicts the Tower of Babel. The cunning descendants built the city of Babylon and the tall Tower, a work like a mountain <note>original: "Montis opus"</note>, for the love of honor; God rejected them, and because of their daring—unfit for mortals—He confused their tongues; the entire structure collapsed. <note>Translated from the original Latin: "Callida progenies urbem Babylonis..."</note>

### Nimrod builds the Tower of Babel.

Here the pride of the Great ones begins to burst forth,
*And Nimrod wants to carry a Tower up to the clouds:*
*But God breaks this work, and comes to stop it*
*By a sudden confusion of unknown tongues.*
<note>Translated from the original French: "Icy l'orgueil des Grands..."</note>

---

### The Building of the Tower of Babel.

Your building work is disturbed by the disturbance of your speech,
Haughty Babylon, so must your pride descend.
A wise Master-builder <term>Master-builder: a high-ranking architect and project manager</term> observes, before the building work has begun,
When he builds a Tower, whether he has the means to complete it.
<note>Translated from the original Dutch: "UW bouw-werk word gestoort..." This verse alludes to the biblical parable in Luke 14:28 about counting the cost before building.</note>

---

### God humbleth the high.

To build up Babel, Adam's race decreed,
*As high as Heaven, to see what passed there.*
*God then gives diverse tongues to thwart their thoughts,*
*One calls for mortar, and for lime gets moss.*
<note>Original 17th-century English. The final line illustrates the "confusion of tongues" where workers could no longer understand each other, receiving useless moss instead of the binding lime needed for construction.</note>

---

### The Tower at Babel is built.

The people agreed among themselves to build a high Tower
Together with the city of Babylon; they did not want to trust God.
He was angered by this and confused the speech of men;
Soon the building fell to ruin; this was a deserved vengeance.
<note>Translated from the original German: "Die Leuth vergleichen sich..."</note>

<summary>This page uses five languages to interpret the story of the Tower of Babel, focusing on the themes of human arrogance (personified by Nimrod) and the divine punishment of linguistic confusion.</summary>

<header>GENESIS 11:10</header>

<image-desc>A rectangular engraving depicting the construction of the Tower of Babel. At the bottom of the image, a caption reads: "the Building of the Tower at Babel."</image-desc>

THE World, having stood for sixteen hundred and fifty-six years until the time of the Deluge <note>The Great Flood of Noah</note>, was renewed and replenished by Noah's offspring. The first King among them was Nimrod, a proud and haughty Prince; he sought to make the People of God apostate <term>apostate: one who renounces or abandons a religious or political belief</term>; he undertook to build a Tower, which no Deluge should ever overwhelm. The children of men began the work, and started the foundation very wide, in order to create a height that its summit should be in Heaven. But God, whom such an undertaking displeased, confused their Tongues <note>original: "Talen verwerd"</note>, so that they did not understand one another, and they were forced to cease the work. From this it appears that God resists the proud <note>original: "hovaardigen"</note>, and that the counsel of God cannot be brought to naught by Men.

# God humbles the high. <note>original: "Deus alta humiliat"</note>

<image-desc>A light engraving serves as a background for the first three stanzas, depicting the construction of the Tower of Babel. The scene shows a massive circular structure with multiple levels, surrounded by scaffolding and numerous figures engaged in labor.</image-desc>

The cunning offspring <note>original: "Callida progenies"</note> built the city of Babylon and a high tower, a work like a mountain, loving honor; God rejected these things, and because of their deeds that went beyond mortal bounds, He confuses their tongues; the whole structure falls to ruin.

---

->Nimrod builds the Tower of Babel.<-

Here the Pride of the Great begins to break forth,
And Nimrod wants to carry a Tower up to the clouds:
But God breaks this work, and comes to stop it
By a sudden entanglement of unknown languages.

---

->Building of the Tower of Babel.<-

Your construction is disrupted by the disruption of your tongues,
Proud Babylon, so must your haughtiness fall;
A wise Master-Builder <term>Master-Builder: an architect or lead mason</term> sees, before the work is finished,
When he builds a Tower, whether he has the means to complete it.

---

->God humbleth the high.<-

To build up Babel Adam’s race decree,
As high as Heaven, what passed there, to see.
God gives then divers <term>divers: various or several</term> tongues their thoughts <note>original: "thougts"</note> to cross,
One calls for mortar <note>original: "morter"</note>, and for lime gets moss <note>original: "mosse"</note>. <note>This reflects a common folk-legend about the confusion of tongues: when a builder asked for one material, his helper, speaking a different language, brought something useless instead.</note>

---

->The Tower of Babel is built.<-

The people agreed among themselves to build a high tower
Along with the city of Babylon; they would not trust in God,
Who was angered by this and confused the Speech of Men;
Soon the building fell to ruin; this was a well-deserved revenge.

# Wherever the fates call, one must obey. <note>original Latin: "Quo fata vocant, parendum"</note>
<meta>catchword: Abraham</meta>

Faithful Abraham left Haran <term>Haran: referred to here by the classical name 'Charras'; the city where Abraham's family settled after leaving Ur</term> and his ancient household gods <term>Penates: originally Roman spirits of the pantry and home, here used poetically to signify his ancestral home and family traditions</term>, as well as his fields, and soon seeks the land of Canaan. ->*Abram Journeys with Lot to Canaan.*<-

Abram lived in Ur of the Chaldeans <term>Ur of the Chaldeans: an ancient city in Mesopotamia, located in modern-day Iraq</term> when God chose him to carry the truth of His name throughout the world and to carry out through him what He had decided in His wisdom; and especially, to set him as an example of Faith. God then commanded Abraham to depart from his Fatherland, to leave his kindred and lineage, and to go to a Land that He would show him; taking with him his wife Sarai and Lot, his brother’s son. From Haran <note>original: "Haran," where Abraham stayed after leaving Ur before moving into Canaan</note> he set out at God’s command into the Land of Canaan, where he was a stranger, always following God’s commands and comforting himself with God’s promises—not only that God would be his God, and the God of his seed <term>seed: a traditional biblical term for descendants or offspring</term> after him, but that in his seed all the families of the Earth would be blessed.

->*Three Angels come to Abraham.*<-

After Abram had waited a long time for the promises of God, God renewed His covenant once more; He changed the name of Abram to Abraham, and the name of Sarai to Sarah; and He established <term>Circumcision</term> <note>original Dutch: "Besnydinge"</note> as a sign of the covenant. Sarah laughed at this and could not believe it, saying: "Shall I have pleasure, now that I have grown old, and my lord <note>A traditional term of respect for her husband, Abraham.</note> is old?" But the Lord, as if rebuking her, said to Abraham: "Why did Sarah laugh? Could anything be too wonderful for the Lord?"

### GENESIS 19:14

->The Sodomites Struck with Blindness.<-

THE two Angels, who had been to eat with Abraham, going by God's command to Sodom to make known to Lot the city's downfall and to drag him and his family out of that gruesome destruction, arrived there in the evening. They found Lot in the Gate <note>In biblical times, the city gate was the center of public life and legal proceedings.</note>, who, seeing these Men, received and entertained them well, praying that they would spend the night with him. When the Sodomites learned of this, their arrogance was so great that they committed violence before the pious man’s house in order to abuse those two guests who had entered, according to their cursed lust; but the Angels struck them with <term>blindness</term> <note>original Dutch: "blindheid"; this refers to a divine dazzlement or confusion of sight.</note>, so that they, groping around, could not find the doors. They protected the repentant Lot, who had welcomed them in, exhorting him to leave this godless place, as they had come to destroy it.

# GENESIS 19:14

->*The Sodomites Struck with Blindness.*<-

THE two Angels, who had been guests at Abraham’s table, went at God’s command to Sodom to make known to Lot the coming destruction of the city, and to pull him and his household away from that gruesome ruin. They arrived there in the evening and found Lot at the Gate; upon seeing these men, he welcomed and entertained them, pleading with them to stay the night in his home. When the Sodomites <note>The inhabitants of the city of Sodom, described in the Bible as exceptionally wicked.</note> learned of this, their arrogance was so great that they committed violence before the door of the righteous man’s house, intending to abuse the two guests who had entered, according to their own cursed desires. However, the Angels struck them with <term>blindness</term> <note>original Dutch: blindheid; here implying a supernatural state of confusion or loss of sight that prevented the mob from finding the entrance.</note>, so that they groped about and could not find the doors. Thus, they protected the repentant Lot, who had welcomed them, urging him to flee this godless place, for they had come to destroy it.

<meta>catchword: Turbine (Whirlwind)</meta>

**Vocabulary:** Angels, Abraham, Sodom, Lot, Sodomites, blindness, destroy.

<header>A sad example of human weakness.</header>

While the wicked kingdoms of Gomorrah
And Sodom fall in a smoking rain of pitch and sulfurous whirlwind:
Lot flees destruction, yet burned by no better fires,
Alas! he lies with his own daughters. 
<note>original Latin: "Turbine sulphureo dum regna nefanda Gomorrhæ / Et piceo fumans imbre Sodoma ruit: / Loth fugit exitium, sed non melioribus ustus / Ignibus, heu! natas comprimit ipse suas."</note>

---

->*The two daughters of Lot sleep one after the other with*<-
->*their father, to preserve his lineage.*<-

Believing all other men to have perished in that great fire,
The two daughters of Lot, seeking to preserve their race,
Make their father drunk; his senses being suspended,
His memory fails him, and he embraces them.
<note>Translated from the French verse: "Croyant dans ce grand feu tous les hommes perdus..."</note>

---

### Sodom and Gomorrah consumed by fire from Heaven.

O Lot, do you then flee with your whole household
From Gomorrah’s sulfurous blaze, but not from the fire of lust?
Can that forbidden fire of passion still ignite the heart
When you have drunk deep of the wine of desire?
<note>Translated from the Dutch verse: "O Loth, ontvlied gy dan..."</note>

---

### *A sad example of human weakness.*

God burns Sodom and Gomorrah entirely,
Lot and his wife save themselves by flight.
Yet Lot burns with a flame far more vile,
For he gets both his own daughters with child.
<note>This section was originally written in early modern English.</note>

---

### Sodom and Gomorrah are burned.

God consumed Sodom and three more cities with fire
Because of their atrocious sin; the entire land was overturned.
Lot alone escaped, only to fall into a grave transgression:
Committing <term>incest</term> <note>original German: "Blutschand"; literally "blood-shame."</note> through drunkenness with his daughters.

# GENESIS XIX: 15
### Magnus

->Lot with his two Daughters.<-

The angels, having warned Lot of the destruction of Sodom, led him out with his wife and two daughters, commanding them not to look back; yet Lot’s wife transgressed God’s command and was changed into a pillar of salt. The daughters of Lot, having left their betrothed <note>original: "Bruidegoms," referring to the men pledged to marry them who remained in the city.</note> behind and seeing all the land around them devastated, imagined that no people were left remaining; through this misunderstanding, they lay with their father, who was overcome by wine—a sad example of human weakness. So great an evil is the sin of drunkenness that it strives against the commandments of God and is a womb <note>original: "baar-moeder," used here metaphorically to mean a breeding ground or source.</note> of many abominations and foolish desires.

# ABIMELECH RETURNS SARAH TO ABRAHAM <note>original Latin: "Abimelech Saram reddit Abrahamo"</note>

Great Abimelech, seized by love for Sarah,
Rises from his bed at the warning voice of God;
Then he says: O Abraham! Take your sister for yourself,
Lest such great crimes touch my scepter <note>original: "sceptrum," a metaphor for his royal authority and the purity of his kingdom.</note>.

->Abimelech returns Sarah to Abraham, without having done her any injury.<-

The beauty of Sarah, her air full of sweetness,
Charmed Abimelech; he took her for his wife.
He believed Abraham, who called her his sister,
But he returned her to him, and bore no blame <note>original French: "n'en eût point de blâme." This emphasizes that because Abimelech acted in ignorance, he was not held guilty of adultery.</note>.

->Abimelech gives Abraham his wife Sarah back.<-

Did you have to suffer for Sarah, Abimelech, though you were misled?
When Abraham calls Sarah his sister, who could avoid the mistake?
Yet it was your innocence for which you were pardoned;
Sarah had to be Abraham's, just as it was shown to you <note>This refers to the dream in which God revealed Sarah's true identity to the King.</note>.

->Abimelech restores Sarah to Abraham.<-

Abimelech falls in love with Sarah,
But in a dream is warned by God above,
That she is Abraham's wife; he implores
God's mercy, and restores Sarah again.

->Abimelech gives Abraham his wife back.<-

What sorrow presses upon your heart, O Abraham?
Even though Abimelech causes you great pain
By taking your Sarah, he gives her back to you soon,
Because God commands it to him in a dream from heaven above.

### GENESIS 20:16

->*Abimelech restores Sarah to Abraham.*<-

When Abraham had traveled toward the South to Gerar, and had said that Sarah his wife <note>original Dutch: "Huisvrouw," a term historically meaning a female spouse or lady of the house.</note> was his sister (out of fear that those people might kill him on her account), King Abimelech of Gerar sent for and took Sarah away. But God made it known to Abimelech in a dream that she was married to a man, and commanded him to restore her to Abraham unharmed, which he fulfilled, asking Abraham why he had done such a thing. To this Abraham answered that he had done so to protect his own life, and that he had not lied, since Sarah was his father's daughter, but not his mother’s <note>Abraham justifies his claim by explaining that Sarah is his half-sister; in this era, such a distinction was a significant legal and genealogical detail.</note>. Then Abimelech took sheep, cattle, and a thousand pieces of silver <term>silverlingen</term> <note>These "silverlings" or shekels served as a public token of Sarah's vindication and the restoration of Abraham's honor.</note>, and gave them to Abraham; and he restored Sarah his wife to him, and Abraham prayed to God, and God healed Abimelech and his wife, whom He had afflicted <note>The text refers to the closing of the wombs in Abimelech’s household as a divine judgment.</note> on account of Sarah.

<header>He was steadfast in faith regarding the promises of God.</header>

To test his obedience, the Lord commands that Abraham himself sacrifice his son, from which he should soon offer a burnt sacrifice <term>holocauſta</term> <note>original Latin: "holocauſta," referring to a religious animal sacrifice completely consumed by fire.</note> to Him. Thereupon the elder seizes the knife, ready to slaughter the boy at once; for nourishing Faith makes him certain.

### *The admirable faith of Abraham.*

To obey God, one consults nothing;
See the prompt obedience of the great Abraham,
Ready to sacrifice his son, his only support,
God, content with his faith, becomes his reward.

### Abraham’s obedience tested regarding Isaac.

Who does not think in his heart, how Abraham’s heart sighs?
Love against duty, faith against reason <term>vernuft</term> <note>original Dutch: "vernuft," meaning human intellect or ingenuity as opposed to divine revelation.</note>
Here struggle against one another, where the sword is raised.
Faith hopes, even when all hope has been surrendered.

### *He was steadfast in the belief of God’s Promises.*

God commands Abraham to slay his Son
(To try his heart) for an offering <term>oblation</term>.
He was ready to strike, but God’s Angel spoke,
"Hold your hand Abraham, spare your son Isaac."

### Abraham sacrifices his son Isaac.

God tests Abraham, bids him kill Isaac,
He who held the Promise; the old man was in distress;
Yet he doubts not at all, quickly grasps the knife,
Intending to slaughter the youth. This is the power of faith.

<header>GENESIS 22</header>

->Abraham sacrifices his son Isaac.<-

Among all the trials <term>beproevingen</term> <note>original Dutch: "beproevingen." In this context, these are not merely hardships but divine tests designed to reveal the quality of a person's faith.</note> that God employs to allow Abraham’s faith to shine so much more gloriously and to stand out above the entire world, and to provide an example to always trust firmly in God’s promises, the greatest trial is when God commanded him to sacrifice his dear and only son Isaac (on whom he had fixed and grounded all his hope from the beginning) on Mount Moriah; for here nature <term>natuur</term> <note>original Dutch: "natuur," referring to natural paternal instinct and human reason which struggled against the seemingly contradictory command of God.</note> struggled against it—indeed, the Divine promises seemed to be in vain. Nevertheless, Abraham, overcoming everything through a strong faith, is obedient and prepared to the utmost to slaughter Isaac. God, seeing this faithfulness, delivered Isaac and renewed the promises more strongly than before.

-># A virtuous wife is from the Lord. <note>original Latin: "Virtuosa uxor est à Domino."</note><-
<sig>A</sig>

The fair Rebecca draws water from the cool spring for strangers, a maiden unaware of her own destiny. In Isaac's name, she receives precious gems, for she is sought as a wife who will be pleasing to her husband. <note>original Latin: "Pulchra peregrinis gelido de fonte Rebecca / Haurit aquam, sortis nescia virgo suae. / Accipit Isaci pretiosas nomine gemmas, / Cui petitur conjux grata futura viro."</note>

### *Isaac receives his Wife.* <note>original French: "Isâc reçoit son Epouse."</note>

*One evening, while meditating upon the works of God,*
*Isaac walked along a green grove,*
*He saw several camels approaching this place,*
*And it was his dear Wife with all her belongings.*

### Rebecca waters Abraham's servant and his camels. <note>original Dutch: "Rebecca drenkt Abrahams knegt en zyn Kamelen."</note>

When the Supreme Ruler <term>opperste bestierd</term> <note>original Dutch: "opperste bestierd," referring to Divine Providence or God's guidance.</note> directs the chaste bonds of marriage, Rebecca prepares herself and sets out through foreign lands until she is joined in wedlock to Isaac, Abraham's son; thus the Bride gains her beloved, and he a worthy Wife.

### *A virtuous wife is from the Lord.*

The fair and chaste Rebecca comes to draw,
Water out of a well where him she saw,
Who gifts to her in Isaac's name presents,
Which she accepts, and to wed him consents.

### Rebecca draws water for Abraham's servant and his camels. <note>original German: "Rebecca schöpft wasser voor Abrams knecht und sein Kamelen."</note>

To honor the strangers, Rebecca draws water from the well of Nahor and gives Abraham's servant to drink. For such service, he presents her with many beautiful jewels; she is thereafter recruited as a bride for Isaac, his master.

# GENESIS 24:18

->The Marriage of Isaac to Rebecca.<-

When Abraham had grown old, he took care for his son, to arrange a good Marriage <term>Huwelyk</term> for him, knowing how much depended upon it; therefore he sent his servant (who had promised him by oath not to take any other Wife for his son than from among his own kindred <term>Maagschap</term> <note>This refers to one's extended family or clan; Abraham was adamant that Isaac not marry a Canaanite.</note>) to Mesopotamia, to fetch a wife from there. Arriving there at the City of Nahor, he said: the woman who gives me and my camels water to drink, she shall be the one. Before he had finished speaking, pouring out prayers to God, Rebecca came to him through divine providence <term>Goddelyke schikking</term>, doing exactly what he had said beforehand; whereupon the servant, boldly putting his mission into action, persuaded the maiden to travel with him, thus bringing home a wife for his master Isaac through God’s guidance <term>bestieringe</term>.

->The way of all flesh.<-
<note>original Latin: "Carnis universæ via."</note>

Abraham, sated with life and weary from his labors, waits; and he eases his limbs in long-lasting rest. His sons entomb the bones of their venerable father here, where a double cave stands beneath an oak tree.
<note>original: "Abramus vitæque fatur feſſuſque laborum..." The "double cave" refers to the Cave of Machpelah.</note>

### *The Death of Abraham.*

Down here on earth, joy and sorrow are shared by all; Isaac, amidst the sweetness of a happy marriage, sees his father—full of years and losing his vital warmth—pass away, leaving him a magnificent inheritance.

### *Abraham is buried in the cave of Machpelah.*

Truly blessed is the person who dies in the Lord, and who, along with earthly pleasures, also leaves behind eternal sorrow. So it was with Abraham; and whoever wishes to live through faith as he did, lifts their soul with him from the dust to the heavens above.

### *The way of all flesh.*

When Nature's heat in Abraham was spent, Death did seize <term>disstraine</term> <note>A legal term meaning to seize property to compel the payment of a debt.</note> his life as payment for Adam’s debt <note>“Adams rent” is a poetic way of describing mortality as the "rent" humans must pay due to the original sin of Adam.</note>. His sons leave their father's body in the grave, beneath an oak where a double cave stands.

### *Abraham dies and is buried.*

The pious Abraham, weary of age and years, dies and is gathered to his beloved ancestors. He is laid to rest by his son beside his wife <note>Referring to Sarah, who was already buried there.</note>, in the double cave there by the city of Hebron.

->Abraham’s Deathbed.<-

When Isaac had taken Rebecca as his wife, and Sarah, Abraham’s wife, had died, Abraham also died shortly thereafter, giving all that he possessed to Isaac, after he had lived one hundred and seventy-five years. And Isaac and Ishmael, his sons, buried him in the Cave of Machpelah, in the field of Ephron, which Abraham had purchased from the sons of Heth; it was there that Sarah his wife was also buried. Thus, this Father of the Faithful <note>original: "Vader der gelovigen," a title emphasizing Abraham's role as the spiritual ancestor in Judaism, Christianity, and Islam.</note>, satisfied with life <note>original: "zat van leven," an idiom meaning he had lived a full and complete life.</note>, was gathered to his people. His life served as an example for all Christians of how one should fully trust and believe in God; and though such faith is painful for human nature, it shall nonetheless ultimately crown us, along with Abraham, with eternal glory through grace.

# A representation of those who reject the grace of God. <note>original Latin: "Typus rejicientium gratiam Dei." In this context, a "Type" refers to a biblical figure or event that serves as a symbol for a spiritual or moral condition—here, the rejection of eternal rewards for temporary ones.</note>

A

<image-desc>A very faint, nearly faded engraving in the center of the upper half of the page depicts the biblical scene of Esau and Jacob. One figure is dressed as a hunter with a bow, while the other is near a pot or bowl, illustrating the exchange of the birthright for a meal.</image-desc>

Behold the twin brothers following very different paths,
For the younger is skilled with the flocks, the elder with weapons.
Returning from the hunt, fierce Esau resigns the rights
Of the firstborn to Jacob for a red pottage. <note>original Latin: "rufo jure." The "red pottage" refers to the lentil stew Jacob was cooking.</note>

---

->Esau sells his birthright.<- <note>original French: "droit d'aisnesse."</note>

This young man, as famished as a hunter can be,
Gives up his birthright for a wretched stew;
Jacob, much craftier, knows its true value,
Triumphs over his brother, and laughs at his weakness.

---

->Esau sells his birthright to his brother Jacob.<-

O Esau! you sell the birthright promised to you,
And do not consider how you now overlook your own salvation. <note>original Dutch: "uw' heil ziet over 't hooft," an idiom suggesting he is blind to his own spiritual well-being.</note>
But Jacob, remain pious, even if he [Esau] wishes to ensnare you.
It is the comfort of the pious to endure the wicked.

---

->A representation of those who cast off Grace.<-

The twin-born brothers are of different minds:
Jacob loves cattle, Esau finds pleasure
In hunting, whence returning once he does
Sell his birth-right to Jacob for red broth.

---

->Esau sells his birthright for a dish of lentils.<-

Two twin brothers are of very unequal minds;
Jacob cooked a grain-mash, <note>original German: "Korn-muſs."</note> which Esau, to obtain—
Being hungry and weary, so that he disregards his birthright—
Sells it to his brother for a dish of lentils.

<summary>This page uses the biblical story of Esau selling his birthright to Jacob for a bowl of pottage as a moral allegory (a "Type") for those who foolishly trade spiritual grace for worldly desires.</summary>

# GENESIS 25:20

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a domestic interior. On the left, Jacob stands by a hearth where a pot is heating, holding a bowl. In the center, Esau has just returned from the hunt, carrying a long spear and accompanied by a dog; he gestures toward the bowl Jacob is holding. To the right, a table is set with a loaf of bread and a tall pitcher. Two windows in the background reveal a landscape with hills and trees. The entire scene is enclosed in a rectangular border.</image-desc>

->Esau sells his birthright.<-

Isaac was childless for a long time, but two sons were born to him by Rebecca, namely Esau and Jacob. Esau, the firstborn, being a wild and rough man, sold his inheritance and birthright to Jacob, the younger, upon returning from the hunt. He traded it for a bowl of pottage <term>pottage: a thick soup or stew, usually made of lentils or vegetables</term>, an act of recklessness that showed contempt for God and His Promise. Nevertheless, this did not happen without Divine permission <term>Divine permission: the theological idea that God allows human actions, even flawed ones, to fulfill His greater plan</term>, so that He might demonstrate His wonderful works, as He had intended to bring the blessing upon Jacob, the younger. This story serves as a mirror <note>In 17th-century moral literature, a "mirror" (spiegel) is a cautionary example or a lesson intended for the reader's self-reflection.</note> to all unholy people and lovers of this world, who value the fleeting enjoyment of sin more than the eternal right to be children of God.

# Abimelech, King of the Edomites, enters into a covenant with Isaac.
<note>original Latin: "Abimelec, Rex Idumæorum, cum Isaaco init foedus." In the biblical Book of Genesis, Abimelech is described as the King of the Philistines in Gerar; however, the Latin text here identifies him as "Rex Idumæorum" (King of the Edomites).</note>

> The King of Edom, mindful of Isaac’s holy integrity,
> and believing that a sacred Deity resided within the man,
> visits him in his exile. Having been civilly received 
> as an enemy at a feast, he prepares to enter into a firm covenant.

## *Abimelech, King of the Philistines, makes an alliance with Isaac.*

> Isaac is sought out by those same Philistines;
> Abimelech, their King, values his wisdom.
> He comes to visit him, and amidst the festivities,
> they contract a close alliance together.

## Abimelech and Isaac make a covenant.

> See here Abimelech, who first drove Isaac away,
> now, to great wonder, offers him his favor.
> Thus must even unfaithfulness love the one who is pious,
> if he wishes, through a loyal covenant, to finally win back trust.
<term>covenant: a formal, solemn, and binding agreement; in the Bible, these are often witnessed by God.</term>

## *Abimelech and Isaac make a Covenant together.*

> See how Abimelech, who once despised Isaac,
> find his spirits revived, much to his own astonishment.
> So must unworthiness embrace honesty with love,
> if he is to be established by truth, as innocent as a dove.
<note>This section translates the early modern English verse provided in the original text, which uses the phrase "by troth be plant" to mean "be established or rooted through faithfulness."</note>

## Abimelech and Isaac make a covenant.

> The Prince Abimelech, who had sent Isaac away,
> afterward gazes upon him with great joy, to his own wonder.
> Thus must even unfaithfulness love the one who is pious,
> if he is to eventually win loyalty through a faithful bond.

# GENESIS: XXVI.

<image-desc>A copperplate etching depicting a biblical scene from Genesis 26. A distant landscape features hills, a fortified castle, and birds flying in the sky.</image-desc>

->The Covenant Between Isaac and Abimelech.<-

When Isaac came to Abimelech, the King of the Philistines, because of the distress of a famine <note>original Dutch: "hongers noot," referring to a period of extreme food scarcity that forced migrations</note>, God commanded him to live in that land and blessed him abundantly, so that he became very great in that land. This caused the Philistines to become envious, and they stopped up all the water wells with earth. The King also issued a command, saying: "Depart from us, for you have become more powerful than we." Consequently, Isaac moved away and camped in the valley of Gerar. From there, he traveled to Beersheba <note>original: "Berseba," a site famous for its wells and often associated with oaths and treaties</note> and pitched his tents there. Then King Abimelech came to Isaac to make a covenant <term>covenant: a formal, sacred agreement or treaty between two parties</term> with him, just as it happened. And after they had eaten and drunk together in friendship at a great feast, Abimelech returned to his own land, and they maintained friendship with each other from that time forward.

<meta>catchword: Deci-</meta>

## Abimelech, King of the Idumaeans, enters into a treaty with Isaac.

<note>original Latin: "Abimelec, Rex Idumæorum, cum Isaaco init fœdus." Note that while the biblical text identifies Abimelech as King of the Philistines, this Latin heading uses the term "Idumaeans," referring to the Edomites.</note>

The King of Idumea, mindful of Isaac’s holy integrity,
And believing that a sacred Deity <note>original Latin: "sacrum Numen," referring to the divine presence or power of God</note> dwells within the man,
Visits him in his exile: though formerly an enemy, he is civilly
Received with a feast, and prepares to enter into a firm treaty.

->Abimelech, King of the Philistines, makes an alliance with Isaac.<-

*Isaac is sought out by those same Philistines;*
*Abimelech their King values his wisdom,*
*He comes to visit him, and amidst the banquets*
*They contract a close alliance together.*

->Abimelech and Isaac make a covenant.<-

See here Abimelech, who first rejected Isaac,
To great wonder, now offers him his favor;
Thus must even the unfaithful love the one who is pious,
If he wishes at last to win back trust through a faithful covenant <term>covenant: a formal, binding agreement, often used in a religious context to describe the relationship between God and humanity or a sacred treaty between people</term>.

->Abimelech and Isaac make a Covenant together.<-

*See how Abimelech, who once despised Isaac,*
*To his own astonishment, finds his spirit now revived;*
*In this way, even the unworthy must embrace honesty with love,*
*If he wishes to be established in truth, as innocent as a dove.*
<note>This section translates the Early Modern English verse found in the original document, which used archaic spellings like "troth" for truth or loyalty.</note>

->Abimelech and Isaac make a bond.<-

The Prince Abimelech, who sent Isaac away from him,
Later looks upon him with great joy, to the wonder of all.
Thus must unfaithfulness itself entirely love the one who is devout,
If he is to finally gain loyalty through a faithful bond.

# GENESIS: XXVI. 21

->The Covenant Between Isaac and Abimelech.<-

When Isaac, on account of the hardship of famine, came to Abimelech the King of the Philistines, God commanded him to dwell in that land and blessed him abundantly, so that he became very great in that land. This caused the Philistines to feel envy, and they stopped up all the water wells with earth; the King also commanded him, saying: "Depart from us, for you have become much more powerful than we." And after they had eaten and drunk with one another in friendship at a great feast, Abimelech returned again to his own land, and they maintained their friendship with each other from that time forward.
