# The elder shall serve the younger.
<note>original Latin: "Major serviet minori." This is a reference to the divine prophecy given to Rebekah during her pregnancy that the elder son would serve the younger.</note>

Jacob deceives the son of Abraham <note>original Latin: "Abramidem," referring to Isaac</note> whose eyes are seized by darkness,
    While he feigns to be his brother at his mother’s command:
He anticipates his father’s blessing. The other returns from the field,
    Weeping at the fraud, but the spoken words remain fixed.

->*Jacob, to obtain the blessing of Isaac his father,
deceives him in the clothes of Esau.*<-

Jacob, moved by his mother and by the divine Spirit
    To displace his brother, has put everything to use;
His father blesses him, and his happy destiny
At this moment gives him the best inheritance.

---

->Jacob obtains the blessing of
his father Isaac through deceit.<-

O Esau! Jacob’s trickery and pretense have succeeded;
    Through his mother’s quick deception, he carries away the blessing from you.
When one has sold his birthright for the sake of his own pleasure,
    Then repentance is sought too late, through later regret.

---

->*The elder shall serve the younger.*<-

Isaac, dim-sighted, takes Jacob to be [Esau],
    Esau is deceived through his wife’s <note>The 17th-century English text uses "wives policie," referring to the strategy or "policy" of Isaac's wife, Rebekah, who orchestrated the deception.</note> strategy:
Jacob gets the blessing, Esau returns,
    And having noticed the cheat, he grieves and mourns.

---

->Jacob obtains the blessing from his father Isaac.<-

Through lies and deceit Jacob obtains the blessing,
    While Esau wanted to strike down a game animal on the hunt.
He finds himself overtaken, weeps, and becomes very indignant;
    But it was too late, caused by clever cunning.

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the biblical scene from Genesis 27. Isaac, an elderly man, lies in a large canopied bed. Jacob is kneeling by the side of the bed, leaning toward his father to receive a blessing. Rebecca stands behind Jacob, watching the scene. ->Isaac blesses his son Jacob.<-

Isaac, having become old and desiring to give Esau the blessing, commanded him to go out hunting and to prepare a savory dish <term>savory dish<gloss>The original Dutch "smakelyke spyze" refers to the specific meal Isaac requested to enjoy before performing the solemn rite of blessing.</gloss></term> for him, just as he liked it, so that he might bless him amidst that pleasure before he died. After Esau, in obedience, had gone out into the field, the mother Rebekah meanwhile presented her son Jacob—whom she loved more than Esau—to the father under the guise of Esau, thus receiving his father’s blessing. Esau, coming in afterward, wept in vain over this loss, for Isaac had already given the blessing away to Jacob; this serves as a warning <note>original Dutch: "verschrikkinge," literally "terror" or "horror," used here as a sobering moral example.</note> to all worldly people, who, for the sake of the pleasure found in sin, throw themselves into extreme danger of both soul and body.

### A Prefiguration of the Lord’s Incarnation <note>original Latin: "Dominicæ Incarnationis typus"</note>

Jacob flees far from the traps and threats of his brother, and as a traveler, he experiences a sweet vision: he sees celestial spirits descending from Heaven <note>original Latin: "Olympo," a classical reference to the dwelling of the gods, used here by the author to mean the Christian Heaven</note>; then he solemnly makes a vow and names the place Bethel.

---

->*Vision of the Ladder.*<-

Jacob sees in his sleep a standing ladder,
*Which makes a path for the angels from the earth to the Heavens;*
One descends, another ascends, and God stands at the top,
*Who promises him multitudes of brave descendants.* <note>original French: "Neveux," which historically could refer to grandchildren or descendants in general.</note>

---

->Jacob in his dream sees the vision of a ladder.<-

Awake, my soul, and sing the Lord's praise with Jacob,
Where the angels, on the ladder, open up the heavenly court
Through Christ’s merit on the cross. One can attain that place
Now that the Cross is like a stairway, to ascend to God’s throne.

---

->*A Prefiguration of our Lord's Incarnation.*<-

Jacob flies from his brother's hate away,
*And sees a vision as he sleeping lay;*
It is a ladder on which angels walk
*From Heaven to earth, whence God to him does talk.*

---

->A ladder appears to Jacob in a dream.<-

Jacob, fearing Esau, wishes to escape his wrath,
A ladder appears to him, set up toward Heaven,
He sees the angels climbing up and down upon it;
This spiritual sign signifies Christ’s coming in the flesh <term>coming in the flesh<gloss>Also known as the Incarnation, the theological belief that God became man in the person of Jesus Christ.</gloss></term>.

# GENESIS: XXVIII. 23 <note>Genesis 28:10-22. Verse 23 does not exist in the standard biblical canon, which ends the chapter at verse 22; this likely refers to a specific theological commentary or a variation in early modern editions.</note>

<meta>catchword: Isacides <note>Latin: "Son of Isaac," a classical patronymic for Jacob.</note></meta>

An engraving depicting Jacob's Dream from Genesis 28. Jacob is shown sleeping on the ground in a rocky landscape. A ladder extends from the earth beside him up into a cloud-filled sky. ->Jacob’s Vision in his Dream.<-

**W**hen Esau was overtaken by an evil intent against Jacob because he had carried away the Blessing, his Mother <note>Rebecca</note> sent her dear Son away to her brother Laban, so that he might marry there among her own kindred and lineage. As Jacob was traveling toward Mesopotamia, the Lord appeared to him in a vision: a Ladder was set up reaching toward Heaven, along which the Angels of God were climbing up and down. Here, God renewed the covenant of his Forefathers with Jacob, promising to always be with him; the Ladder gave him no small measure of assurance, while the ascending and descending Angels foretold a successful journey and return under God’s guidance.

# God is the Author of marriages among the godly. <note>original Latin: "Deus Author matrimoniorum inter pios."</note>

Jacob, the son of Isaac <note>original: "Isacides," a classical patronymic for the son of Isaac.</note>, arrived at Haran <note>original: "Charras," the Latin name for Haran.</note> and rolled the stone away
From the well, so that the parched Rachel could lead the cattle to drink.
Upon hearing of this, her uncle <note>Laban</note> tearfully welcomed him
As a son-in-law, and joined both daughters to the man in marriage.

---

->*Jacob meets Rachel, and goes to find Laban.*<-

Beside a well filled with clear waters, Jacob has
A pleasant meeting with the beautiful Rachel:
He kisses her, and first waters her flocks,
And receives a favorable welcome at Laban's house.

---

->Rachel arrives with the sheep, and Jacob helps her water them.<-

See Rachel, Laban’s child, in the prime of her youth,
Like a sea of white, curly hair <note>A poetic description of the thick wool of the sheep surrounding her.</note>,
Crowded in by lambs in the middle of the throng.
Who would not toil for such virtue for seven long years? <note>A reference to Genesis 29:18, where Jacob agrees to serve Laban for seven years to marry Rachel.</note>

->*God is the author of marriage between the godly.*<-

Jacob to Haran comes, a stone there rolls
From off a well to water Rachel’s folds,
Laban receives him kindly, whom he serves,
And for his pains his daughters both deserves.

---

->Jacob gives water to Rachel's sheep.<-

Jacob travels toward Haran, where he discovers the well;
The fair Rachel arrives, which awakens joy in him.
She waters her sheep and informs her father of this,
Who shows Jacob much kindness and makes him his son-in-law.

# GENESIS 29:24

->Jacob waters Rachel’s sheep.<-

Jacob, having arrived in Mesopotamia <note>The ancient region between the Tigris and Euphrates rivers, corresponding to modern-day Iraq and parts of Syria.</note>, near the place where Laban lived, and lingering by a fountain <term>well: In older Dutch, 'Fonteine' was often used to describe a natural spring or a deep well.</term>, there first made the acquaintance of his cousin Rachel, Laban’s daughter. She arrived with her father's little sheep <note>original: 'Schaapkens'. The use of the diminutive suggests the gentle nature of the flock or the tender care Rachel provided.</note> to water them. Jacob, who immediately fell in love with her because she was very beautiful, rolled the stone from the mouth of the well to assist her in the work. By this single act, he demonstrated his willingness to be of further service. He also made it known to her that he was her father’s nephew <note>The Dutch word 'neef' can mean either cousin or nephew; here Jacob identifies himself as the son of Laban’s sister, Rebecca.</note> and the son of Rebecca. When Rachel reported this to her father Laban, Jacob was very well received by him. Shortly thereafter, Laban made a contract with Jacob, stating that he should serve him for seven years in exchange for his daughter Rachel. This was willingly accepted by Jacob and joyfully completed, for he loved her.

# The heart of man is in the hand of God.
<note>original Latin: "Cor hominis est in manu Dei." This refers to the belief that divine intervention can change a person's temperament or intentions.</note>

**T**errified by the encounter, Jacob trembled at Esau,
Whom he strives to win over with a sent gift:
There is no cause for fear; brother receives brother in a tight
Embrace, and gave many kisses to the fearful one.
<note>original Latin: "Territus occurfu Jacob trepidabat Efavi..." This verse emphasizes the physical manifestation of Jacob's fear—trembling—contrasted with the physical warmth of the eventual embrace.</note>

->*Jacob humbles himself before his brother Esau, and gives him presents.*<-

**J**acob, fearing his brother, wishes to flatter him,
And believes that gifts are a powerful lure,
But his elder brother steps forward and comes to embrace him,
And one sees here that blood has more power.
<note>The French verse highlights the "power of blood" (le sang), suggesting that natural family bonds are stronger than long-held grudges.</note>

->Jacob meets his brother Esau.<-

**O** Jacob! Will the fear in these parched, foreign lands
Through which you travel before Esau, travel anxiously with you?
Is flight your only weapon? Why are you now afraid?
Brothers are either of one nature, or they become of one mind.
<note>original Dutch: "Is 't vlugten uw geweer?" Jacob’s primary defense throughout his life had been fleeing (from Esau to Haran, then from Laban back to Canaan); the poet asks if he has any other "weapons" left.</note>

->*The heart of man is in God's hand.*<-

**A**t Esau's coming Jacob is dismayed,
And to get favor, gifts before him laid;
Instead of blows he Jacob kisseth oft,
Instead of wrestling gives embraces soft.
<note>This English verse contrasts the "wrestling" Jacob did with the Angel in the previous chapter (Genesis 32) with the "soft" embrace he receives from his brother here.</note>

->Jacob meets his brother Esau.<-

**E**sau meets him with four hundred soldiers,
At which the pious Jacob fell into a great terror;
Yet there was no need for fear, for brotherly love
Drove Esau to a kiss and bitter tears.
<note>original German: "vier hundert Soldaten." The Bible mentions Esau coming with 400 men, which Jacob interpreted as a private army coming for revenge.</note>

**A**fter Jacob had wrestled with the Angel, he lifted up his eyes and saw Esau approaching with four hundred armed men; he therefore divided the children among Leah and Rachel and the two maidservants. He himself went on ahead of them and bowed himself to the ground seven times, until he came near to his Brother. Then Esau ran to meet him, and they embraced one another and wept. From this we can observe how God assists His own in their greatest need and can incline the hearts of men as He wills. What do the wicked bluster, and whom do they threaten? Why do the <term>Righteous</term><note>The "Righteous" (Regtvaardigen) refers to those who live in accordance with God's will, in this case, Jacob.</note> worry, and whom do they fear? God looks down from Heaven; He fashions and pays attention to all their works. It is a <term>Royal word</term><note>original Dutch: "Koninglyk woord." This refers to a proverb written by King Solomon: "When a man’s ways please the Lord, he maketh even his enemies to be at peace with him" (Proverbs 16:7).</note> that we see proven true here: when a person's ways please the Lord, He shall make even his enemies be at peace with him.

<header>Force by force, and injury is punished with injury. <note>original Latin: "Vis vi, Injuria injuriâ punitur."</note></header>

Dinah suffers violation; the violent Shechem takes her
By force, then asks for her as his wife.
Eager for revenge, the brothers with cruel weapons
Raging, kill many with the sword.
<note>original Latin: "Dina stuprum patitur, Sichem violentior illam / Vi rapit, uxorem postulat inde sibi. / Vindictæ cupidi fratres crudelibus armis / Bacchantes, gladio corpora multa necant."</note>

->*Hamor asks for Dinah as a wife for his son Shechem after he had raped her.*<-

Shechem robs young Dinah of her honor,
And the sons of Jacob feel this outrage:
Hamor, to calm everything, asks that their sister
Be given to his son Shechem in marriage.
<note>original French: "De la jeune Dina Sichem ravit l’honneur, / Et les fils de Jacob ressentent cet outrage: / Hemor pour tout calmer demande que leur sœur / Soit à Sichem son fils donnée en mariage."</note>

->Simeon and Levi attack Shechem and rescue Dinah.<-
<note>original Dutch: "Simon en Levi overvallen Sichem, en verloffen Dina."</note>

O Shechem, when your lust was so highly raised,
Why did you shut your eyes by opening them? <note>This poetic line suggests that by looking upon Dinah with lust, Shechem became "blind" to the consequences of his actions.</note>
Ah Dinah, when nature poured her gifts upon you,
What a troublesome jewel your beauty was!
<note>original Dutch: "O Sichem toen uw lust zo hoog was opgetogen, / Wat floot gy ogen toe, door ’t openen van uw ogen, / Ach Dina, als natuur haar gaven op u goot, / Wat was uw Schonigheid een kommerlyk kleinoot."</note>

->*Force by force, and injury is punished with injury.*<-

Shechem does ravish Dinah, whom to wed,
He and his are to <term>circumcision</term><note>A ritual surgical procedure required by Jacob's sons as a condition for the marriage; however, the brothers used the recovery period to attack while the men of Shechem were incapacitated.</note> led.
Her brothers are for her rape so enraged,

# GENESIS XXXIV.

<meta>catchword: Joseph</meta>

<image-desc>A black and white engraving illustrating a scene from Genesis 34. On the left, a man (Shechem) is depicted forcefully grabbing a woman (Dinah) in the street. ->Shechem violates Dinah.<-

When Jacob had arrived at Shechem, and Dinah went to view the city out of curiosity; she was seen, desired, and raped by Shechem, the son of Hamor. When this deed reached the ears of Dinah's brothers, it caused them great pain. Meanwhile, Shechem, overcome with love for Dinah, requested her in marriage through his father, which the sons of Jacob deceitfully agreed to, provided that he and the entire city allowed themselves to be <term>circumcised|The surgical removal of the foreskin, which served as the physical sign of the covenant between God and the descendants of Abraham.</term>, as they did. 

Now when Simeon and Levi, full brothers <note>Simeon and Levi were Dinah’s full brothers, as all three were children of Jacob and Leah, making them particularly protective of her honor.</note>, knew that the pain was at its peak on the third day after the circumcision, they ambushed the city with their attending servants, killing all males with the sword, including Shechem himself and his father Hamor, thus taking their sister Dinah back with them. In this way, foreign curiosity, carnal wantonness, and one sin are frequently punished by God with and through another. <note>The author interprets the tragedy as a chain of sins: Dinah's "curiosity" led to Shechem's "lust," which led to the brothers' "deceit" and "slaughter," suggesting that one transgression often breeds another.</note>

   He had begun to be dearer to the elderly man:

->Joseph tells his Dream.<-

**ISrael** <note>Israel is another name for the patriarch Jacob.</note> loved Joseph above all his sons, because he was to him a son of his old age; but his brothers envied and hated him exceedingly, partly because of his dream, and partly because his father had made him a many-colored <term>coat</term> <note>The "veelverwiggen bonten rok" is the famous "coat of many colors," a symbol of Jacob's favoritism that distinguished Joseph from his brothers who wore standard working clothes.</note>, so that they could not speak peaceably to him. It then happened that Joseph told his brothers the dream which he had dreamed, which was as follows: Joseph was with his brothers in the field, and they were each binding their sheaves, and behold, Joseph’s sheaf stood upright, and his brothers' sheaves all bowed down before Joseph's sheaf. And the other dream was as follows, which he also told to his father and brothers together, and he said: "Behold, I saw the Sun and the Moon with eleven stars, which bowed down before me." For this his father rebuked him, yet he kept all these things in his heart; and Joseph’s brothers were all bursting with resentment because of those dreams.

<meta>This page continues from the description of Joseph’s dreams on page 55. While the previous page focused on the content of the dreams and the favoritism of Jacob, this page moves to the climax of the brothers' resentment: the conspiracy to kill Joseph, his imprisonment in a pit, and his eventual sale to passing merchants. This transition is framed through the lens of "typology," a traditional method of biblical interpretation that views Old Testament events as prophetic "types" or shadows of the life of Jesus Christ.</meta>

# The selling of Joseph as a foreshadowing of the selling of Christ.
<note>original Latin: "Josephi venditio typus Christi venditi." In historical theology, a "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament believed to prefigure something in the New Testament—in this case, Joseph being sold by his brothers prefigures Jesus being betrayed and sold by Judas.</note>

Joseph had seen a memorable vision in his dreams,
Which was a sign of his coming prosperity.
Because of this, the band of brothers hated him; they cast
The youth into a deep pit, and a sun-burnt Arab bought him.

### Joseph is sold to the Ishmaelites.

*At the suggestion of Reuben, Judah first gives way;* <note>In the biblical account, Reuben suggests the pit to save Joseph's life, and Judah later suggests selling him rather than leaving him to die.</note>
*Merchants pass by, and through a lesser offense*
*They agree to sell Joseph to them,*
*And his death is finally changed into slavery.*

### Joseph is thrown into a pit by his brothers and sold.

The bright and clear day of the dark night-vision,
Though Joseph is sold, shall yet come to light;
It is painful for a callous soul to endure
The inflicted sorrow, of which one may not complain.

### The selling of Joseph as a <term>type</term> <note>A symbolic foreshadowing or precursor.</note> of the selling of Christ.

*Joseph sees good fortune in a dream,*
*And signs that he shall hold supreme power,*
*Through hate his brothers lay him in a cistern,*
*A merchant buys him and leads him away as a slave.*

### Joseph is thrown into a pit.

God shows Joseph his future happiness and joy in a dream—
His honor and high station, for which his brothers envy him;
They sink him into a pit, but Judah looks after him;
For ready money, merchants from Midian buy him. <note>The Bible uses the terms "Ishmaelites" and "Midianites" almost interchangeably in this passage to describe the caravan of traders.</note>

<meta>This page concludes the narrative sequence regarding Joseph’s betrayal by his brothers. GENESIS 37:28

A rectangular copperplate engraving depicting the biblical story of Joseph being sold by his brothers. ->*Joseph was Sold.*<-

---

Among all the sons of Jacob, Joseph was the most beloved, both by God and by his father, for which reason he was envied and despised by his brothers. This was mostly due to his dreams of the bowing sheaves <note>In Joseph's first dream, his brothers' bundles of grain (sheaves) bowed down to his own, symbolizing their future submission to him.</note> and the heavenly lights, which he related to them and to his parents; for the brothers sensed deep down what these dreams portended. 

When Joseph was once sent out by his father to see what his brothers were doing, they saw him and conspired to kill him. However, on the advice of Reuben—who intended the best for Joseph <note>Reuben hoped to secretively rescue Joseph from the pit later and return him to his father.</note>—they threw him into a pit instead. Meanwhile, some Ishmaelite merchants passed by, to whom they sold the youth at Judah's suggestion. They believed that by doing this, everything was now sufficiently destroyed and the dreams were proven to be lies; on the contrary, this was the very means by which the predicted outcome was fulfilled. In the same way, the Jews, by crucifying Christ, served the Counsel of God <note>The "Counsel of God" refers to the theological idea that God uses even the evil actions of men to bring about His divine plan for salvation.</note> unknowingly and against their own will.

->**He who deceives, is in turn deceived.**<-

For the love of offspring, Tamar takes up the harlot's robes; 
    Judah sees her and seeks to lie with her, 
And he possesses her, but the crafty daughter-in-law deceives him well, 
    He who had so often before mocked his daughter-in-law with falsehood.

### Judah and his Daughter-in-law Tamar.

O Judah! Oh, how is it that you do not keep your word?
    You are not as you seem, you seem not as you are.
Has Tamar moved you to wanton love on the road?
    One who deceives by appearances is in turn deceived by appearances.

### *He that deceives, will be likewise deceived.*

Tamar, in the guise of a harlot, deceives Judah,
    *He gives her pledges; she immediately conceives twins,*
He hears she is pregnant <note>The phrase "she's great" is an archaic English term for being late in pregnancy.</note> and decrees she be burned,
    She shows his pledges; he frees her from harm.

### Tamar deceives Judah.

Because, against her hope, her state of widowhood was prolonged,
    Tamar deceives Judah, who unknowingly makes her pregnant.
When this was discovered, she was to be burned;
    Judah accepts the guilt and recognizes her as righteous.
<note>Upon seeing the signet and staff, Judah famously declared, "She is more righteous than I," acknowledging his failure to provide for her legally and the legitimacy of her claim to a child.</note>

# GENESIS 38.

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a biblical scene in a landscape. On the left, a woman (Tamar), wearing a veil and robes, is seated on a rock beneath a large, leafy tree. A man (Judah) stands on the right, leaning on a staff and gesturing towards her. In the distance, a small house and additional trees are visible. ->Judah and Tamar.<-

The Patriarch Judah, having lost his first son Er, gave his second son Onan to the widow Tamar <note>According to the custom of levirate marriage, a brother was required to marry his deceased brother's widow to provide an heir for the dead man's name.</note>; and he also having died, Judah—fearing to give his third son Shelah to the same widow, lest he also die and his name perish entirely—comforted Tamar with a vain hope, yet did not fulfill it. To prevent this outcome, she devised another means to deceitfully ensnare Judah. She goes and sits by the roadside in the guise of a harlot, where Judah, coming to her and inflamed by a foul passion, strikes a bargain with her to quench it, giving his staff and ring <note>original: "Ring," referring to his signet ring, a symbol of personal identity and authority.</note> as a pledge for the agreed-upon wages of iniquity. Thus, unknowingly, he impregnates his own daughter-in-law, who had already gone her way by the time Hirah <note>Judah's friend, the Adullamite, who was sent to deliver a young goat in exchange for the return of the pledges.</note> arrived to redeem the pledge. Afterwards, when it became known that Tamar was pregnant, Judah condemned her to be burned; but she, showing him the given pledge, forced him to confess that she had acted more righteously than he <note>Judah admitted his guilt because he had withheld his son Shelah from her, leaving her with no other way to continue the family line.</note>.

<page-num>60</page-num>

## Mortify your earthly members: fornication, uncleanness.
<note>original Latin: "Enecate membra vestra terrestria, scortationem, Impuritatem." This is a citation from the New Testament, Colossians 3:5, used here to provide a moral framework for the Old Testament story of Joseph.</note>

Potiphar, the guardian and master of the court of the King of the Nile <note>original: "Niligenæ Regis," referring to the Egyptian Pharaoh</note>, buys the youth for himself with coin. He denies the sexual advances <note>original: "Veneris commercia," literally the commerce of Venus, the Roman goddess of love</note> of his mistress, and though innocent, enters the depths of the prison through feminine guile.

->*Potiphar's wife unsuccessfully entices Joseph to*<-
->*sleep with her.*<-

Observe the admirable virtue of the chaste Joseph, who cannot be seduced by false charms; perhaps another would have struggled longer, but for fear of yielding, he immediately takes flight.

->*Joseph is solicited to unchastity by Potiphar's*<-
->*wife.*<-

O chaste youth! Oh, do not stray from virtue for the flattery of your mistress or the charms of your youth. Remain faithful to Potiphar, even if she has forgotten her own faithfulness. What unease forbidden lust breeds in the conscience!

## *Mortify your earthly members: Fornication, Uncleanness.*

Potiphar, one of great King Pharaoh's train,
Does Joseph from the merchant buy again.
His mistress solicits him to base lust,
And for refusing, he is into prison thrust.

->*Potiphar's wife seeks Joseph for adultery.*<-

Potiphar buys Joseph, whose wife desires him for adultery and evil deeds; however, he resists her and flees. He leaves behind his cloak, at which the woman is enraged, and makes him pay for his innocence in the deep dungeon.

->Joseph Solicited to Unchastity.<-

---

WHEN Joseph was sold to the Ishmaelite merchants and they led him to Egypt, Potiphar, Pharaoh’s courtier, bought him again from the hand of the Ishmaelites; and because God blessed all that Joseph took in hand, he placed him over his entire house, in which he conducted himself so well <note>original Dutch: "zo queet," from 'kwijten,' meaning to acquit oneself of one's duties with distinction</note> that his Lord and Master loved him above all others. It happened meanwhile that his Master’s wife cast her eye upon his beauty, and after she saw that she could win nothing from his mind <note>original: "met gemoede," suggesting she could not change his moral disposition or will</note>, she seized his cloak to force the adultery by power. Joseph, who feared God, left his outer garment in her hands and fled away in spite of this unchaste woman, whose lustful love—as most often happens—turned into hate, so she plotted a godless betrayal against Joseph. She accused him of the very thing of which she herself was guilty, and thus brought it about that the young man was cast into a grim prison.

->Joseph in Prison.<-

**N**ow when Joseph, through the false accusation of his Master's Wife, was brought into the prison, he found favor in the eyes of the Warden <note>original Dutch: "Overſte," meaning a superior or chief official</note> of the prison, who delivered all the prisoners into his hand, and took no notice of anything that was in his hand, because the Lord was with him, and made everything he did to prosper. Some time thereafter it happened that the chief of the Cupbearers and the chief of the Bakers sinned against their Lord the King of Egypt, who delivered them both to the Captain of the Guards <term>Trawanten: historically refers to bodyguards or halberdiers serving a high official</term> in the prison house, in the place where Joseph was. After they had been there for some days, both of them dreamed a dream in a single night, which they recounted to Joseph when he came to them in the morning. Then Joseph said to the Cupbearer, "After three days you shall be restored to your previous office again; remember me when it goes well with you." And to the Baker Joseph said, "After three days King Pharaoh shall have you hung from a tree <note>The Dutch "aan een hout" refers to the gallows or a wooden beam used for execution</note>;" just as it came to pass.

GENESIS XXXIX. 31 <note>While the header references Chapter 39, the text describes the narrative found in Chapter 40.</note>

**An engraving depicting Joseph in a dungeon. Joseph stands on the left in classical garments, gesturing toward two other prisoners. These men, identified as the chief butler and the chief baker, are seated on a wooden bench on the right, their hands and feet shackled with chains secured to heavy rings in the stone wall. ->Joseph in the Prison.<-

When Joseph, by the false accusation of his Lord’s Wife, had been brought into the prison, he found grace in the eyes of the Warden of the prison, who committed all the prisoners into Joseph's hand. The Warden did not look at anything that was in Joseph’s hand, because the Lord was with him, and made everything he did to prosper. Some time after, it happened that the chief of the Cupbearers <term>Schenkeren: the high official responsible for the King's wine</term> and the chief of the Bakers <term>Bakkeren: the official in charge of the royal kitchens</term> sinned against their Lord the King of Egypt. He delivered them both to the Captain of the Guards <term>Overste der Trawanten: literally "Chief of the Followers/Guards," often identified as Potiphar himself</term> in the prison house, in the place where Joseph was held. After they had been there for some days, they both dreamed in a single night, each having a Dream, which they related to Joseph when he came to them in the morning. Then Joseph said to the Cupbearer, "In three days you shall be restored again to your previous office; remember me when it shall go well with you." And to the Baker Joseph said, "In three days King Pharaoh shall have you hanged from a tree"; and so it came to pass.

# The wise man is raised on high. <note>original Latin: *Tollitur in altum sapiens.*</note>

The Ruler of Egypt <term>Canopus: an ancient Egyptian city, used here by the poet as a name for the whole kingdom</term> has a somber vision in his dreams; he calls the youth from his chains and prison. This young man explains what the harsh dreams signify, and then, by the King's will, he takes command of Egypt. <note>original Latin: *Visum habet in somnis Regnator triste Canopi...*</note>

---

->*Pharaoh brings Joseph out of prison to explain a dream to him.*<-

*Surprised by a recurring dream, Pharaoh*
*discovers the ignorance of his circle of diviners;*
*But Joseph, reassuring the King's mind,*
*suddenly displays his wisdom before the King’s eyes.* <note>original French: *D'un songe redoublé Pharao tout surpris...*</note>

---

### Joseph explains the dream of Pharaoh.

The wise Joseph foretells, through withered and fruitful ears of corn,
Seven fat years and as many lean years for Pharaoh here.
Though deceit might strike down virtue for a while,
Virtue still gains the advantage to rise even higher.

---

->*The wise man is exalted on high.*<-

*Strange dreams appear to Pharaoh in his sleep,*
*No one can be found who could make their meaning clear;*
*Joseph declares their meaning immediately,*
*After which he is made the ruler of Egypt.* <note>The original English text uses "meaningh" and "Ear," which are archaic spellings or errors for "meaning" and "E'er/Before."</note>

---

### Joseph explains the Dream.

Pharaoh had a vision of the years to come,
But he could not rightly learn what the meaning was;
Joseph explains the dream and is greatly rewarded for it,
Being appointed as Regent over the entire land.

# The wise man is raised on high. <note>original Latin: *Tollitur in altum sapiens.*</note>

The Ruler of Egypt <term>Canopus: an ancient Egyptian city, used here by the poet as a name for the whole kingdom</term> has a somber vision in his dreams; he calls the youth from his chains and prison. This young man explains what the harsh dreams signify, and then, by the King's will, he takes command of Egypt. <note>original Latin: *Visum habet in somnis Regnator triste Canopi...*</note>

*discovers the ignorance of his circle of diviners;*
*But Joseph, reassuring the King's mind,*
*suddenly displays his wisdom before the King’s eyes.* <note>original French: *D'un songe redoublé Pharao tout surpris...*</note>

---

### Joseph explains the dream of Pharaoh.

While Joseph was in prison, Pharaoh dreamed a dream that none of all the wise men in Egypt could explain. Therefore, he had Joseph summoned from the prison, because he had heard from the Cupbearer <note>The "Schenker," often translated as the Chief Butler.</note> that Joseph possessed the Spirit of Prophecy, as had also been proven to him. Once Joseph heard Pharaoh’s dream, he explained it clearly, saying that the seven fat ears of corn, as well as the seven fat cows, must be understood as seven fruitful years; and the seven withered ears and seven lean cows represented seven barren years. Pharaoh, being gladdened by this, made him a Lord over all Egypt, bestowing upon him the highest honor and majesty. In this way, the Christ also had to suffer much and thus enter into his glory; indeed, the fundamental rule of Christianity still stands firm: that we must enter into the Kingdom of God through many tribulations <note>A reference to the biblical principle found in Acts 14:22.</note>.

---

->*The wise man is exalted on high.*<-

*Strange dreams appear to Pharaoh in his sleep,*
*No one can be found who could make their meaning clear;*
*Joseph declares their meaning immediately,*
*After which he is made the ruler of Egypt.* <note>The original English text uses "meaningh" and "Ear," which are archaic spellings or errors for "meaning" and "E'er/Before."</note>

---

### Joseph explains the Dream.

Pharaoh had a vision of the years to come,
But he could not rightly learn what the meaning was;
Joseph explains the dream and is greatly rewarded for it,
Being appointed ruler over all the land.

# Honor shown to Joseph <note>original Latin: Honor Josepho exhibitus.</note>

**From** that place he is carried on an Egyptian <term>Pelusian: referring to the ancient city of Pelusium, often used in poetry to represent all of Egypt</term> chariot, exalted in honor, and he was second only to the supreme King. A decree is issued, by which the common folk, a crowd struck with awe, are ordered to bend their knee to the prophet. <note>original Latin: "UNde Pelusiaco curru, sublimis honore..."</note>

---

->*Honor shown to Joseph.*<-

**Upon** a triumphant chariot Joseph is paraded; he shows himself everywhere, from province to province. Everywhere the people prostrate themselves at his knees; they praise and adore him, for such is the Prince’s command. <note>original French: "SUr un char triomphant Joseph est promené..."</note>

---

->Joseph is proclaimed a Father of the Fatherland.<-

**How** marvelous is this fortune! Here one sees the triumph of that Joseph, whom but a moment ago we saw being brought out of the dungeon; now he is crowned with glory, so that the land of Egypt and the house of Jacob <note>Joseph's father and his extended family</note> might together be fed by his hand. <note>original Dutch: "HOe wonder is 't geluk..."</note>

---

->*Joseph comes to great honor.*<-

**Joseph** has now become, next to the King, the greatest man in Egypt. Everything is done by his command; the people must bow down before him, who has been placed in such a position of trust.

---

->Joseph is proclaimed a Father of the Fatherland.<-

**How** wonderfully it happens! Here one sees the enslaved boy Joseph rise quite high, before whom everyone must now bow. He is honored as a prince, though previously he was led away by envy! Thus it goes well for the pious when God guides their affairs. <note>original German: "WIe wunder geht es zu, man sieh't hier gaar hoch steigen..."</note>

# GENESIS 41:33

->Joseph Elevated in Glory.<-

**When** Joseph had finished interpreting King Pharaoh’s dream, it pleased the King of Egypt greatly. He appointed Joseph over the entire land of Egypt and placed his own golden ring upon Joseph’s hand. He had him dressed in garments of fine white linen and placed a gold chain around his neck. He then had him ride in the second-best chariot that he possessed, and the people ran before him, shouting for joy, so that he made Joseph very great. And the King said to him, "I am Pharaoh, the King of Egypt, but without your word, no one shall lift up hand or foot in all the land of Egypt." The King also gave him Asenath, the daughter of Potiphera <note>The Dutch text uses "Potiphar," though biblical accounts distinguish between Potiphar the officer and Potiphera the priest.</note>, the high priest <term>Overste: a title meaning leader or chief; in this context, the Priest of On</term> of On <note>The ancient city of Heliopolis, a center of sun worship.</note>, to be his wife. Thus Joseph went out across the entire land of Egypt.

# JOSEPH PROVIDES FOR THE FUTURE FAMINE BY GATHERING GRAIN

### <note>original Latin: Josephus futuræ fami congestis frugibus occurrit.</note>

While Egypt was fertile for seven years,
Rich in land, and productive with much harvest and wine:
The foresighted Joseph gathered immense crops into granaries,
So that he might drive away the dire famine from that place.

---

->Joseph prevents the famine, and gathers a great store of grain.<-

As heaven spread its favor along the Nile,
Joseph gathered an incredible store of grain;
Thus he became the Savior of a whole people,
Triumphing through his care over a merciless monster <note>The "monster" is a poetic personification of the famine.</note>.

---

->Joseph gathers during the seven years of plenty. <note>original Dutch: vette Jaren (fat years)</note><-

God’s blessing, poured out over seven fruitful years
Throughout all the land of Egypt, causes Joseph to gather here,
So that there is a supply in time of famine,
And through his clever management <term>kloek bestier: shrewd or brave administration</term>, he saves all from death.

---

->Joseph provideth for the future famine.<-

While Egypt’s seven years of plenty last,
Joseph takes care to fill the great and vast
Granaries <note>original: "Corn-Garners"</note>, which might serve him well;
When all the people cry for want of bread.

---

->Joseph gathers during the seven years of plenty.<-

God’s salvation now poured out in seven fat years
Throughout all the land of Egypt; Joseph guards it well,
So that there is no lack in the great time of hunger,
And all that has life is delivered from death.

# JOSEPH PROVIDES FOR THE FUTURE FAMINE BY GATHERING GRAIN

### <note>original Latin: Josephus futuræ fami congestis frugibus occurrit.</note>

While Egypt was fertile for seven years,
Rich in land, and productive with much harvest and wine:
The foresighted Joseph gathered immense crops into granaries,
So that he might drive away the dire famine from that place.

---

->Joseph prevents the famine, and gathers a great store of grain.<-

As heaven spread its favor along the Nile,
Joseph gathered an incredible store of grain;
Thus he became the Savior of a whole people,
Triumphing through his care over a merciless monster <note>The "monster" is a poetic personification of the famine.</note>.

---

->Joseph gathers during the seven years of plenty <note>original Dutch: vette Jaren (fat years)</note>.<-

God’s blessing, poured out over seven fruitful years
Throughout all the land of Egypt, causes Joseph to gather here,
So that there is a supply in time of famine,
And through his clever management <term>kloek bestier: shrewd or brave administration</term>, he saves all from death.

---

->Joseph provideth for the future famine.<-

While Egypt’s seven years of plenty last,
Joseph takes care to fill the great and vast
Granaries <note>original: "Corn-Garners"</note>, which might serve him well;
When all the people cry for want of bread.

---

->Joseph gathers during the seven years of plenty.<-

God’s salvation now poured out in seven fat years
Throughout all the land of Egypt; Joseph guards it well,
So that there is no lack in the great time of hunger,
And all that has life is delivered from death.

---

When Joseph was now appointed by King Pharaoh as a Prince and Lord over the whole land of Egypt, he traveled through all the cities of Egypt, making provision that throughout the land, suitable arrangements were made everywhere to gather during the seven years of abundance <note>original Dutch: zeven Jaren des overvloeds</note>. This was done so that when the seven lean years arrived, the land might be spared by this good foresight from the great famine that would come upon it, just as God had made known to King Pharaoh in his dream. Thus, Joseph gathered a vast amount of grain <note>original Dutch: Koorn; in this era, "corn" referred to any cereal grain like wheat or barley</note> and stored it in all the cities, in barns and in attics, so that it might be preserved from spoilage. 

When the seven infertile years arrived, Joseph sold this harvest and, in time, amassed great treasures of money, and furthermore everything the Egyptians possessed, even down to their livestock, which all became the King's own property. Indeed, at last they even sold themselves into slavery, and so the entire land of Egypt, with everything found within it, became the King's, due to that severe hunger which was in the land at that time.

# An example of divine providence toward His own
### <note>original Latin: Providentiæ divinæ erga ſuos exemplum.</note>

Famine oppresses the Canaanite kingdoms,
For the earth—alas, ungrateful—denies the grain 
Entrusted to the farmer. 
The father sends his sons into Egypt to bring back 
Purchased grain for him: they carry out the wise orders of the Old Man.

---

*The land of Canaan being pressed by famine, Jacob sends his sons to Egypt to buy grain.*

As grain was being sold in the cities of the Nile,
The good old man Jacob, pressed by famine,
Fortifies ten of his sons with manly courage,
Sends them to Egypt, and prevents their ruin.

->Jacob sends his sons to Egypt for grain.<-

O Jacob! Never has more salvation come to your house,
Than when you so sorrowfully mourned Joseph's death;
The one whom Dothan <note>The valley where Joseph's brothers originally conspired against him and sold him into slavery.</note> sold, Memphis <note>An ancient capital of Egypt, here representing the place of Joseph's exaltation.</note> has chosen.
Joy is born, in the end, from the appearance of disaster.

---

*An example of the divine providence towards his own.*

Canaan wastes with famine and with dearth,
The grain that’s sown scarcely grows from the earth,
Israel sends his sons to the Egyptian land,
To fetch home grain; they yield to his command.

---

->Jacob sends his sons for grain.<-

When hunger heavily burdened the land of Canaan,
The elderly Jacob took care for their nourishment;
He sent his ten sons far into Egypt,
They brought back grain and money. God helps at the right time.

->*Joseph’s brothers come for grain.*<-

---

When the expensive and barren years, according to the prophecy of Joseph, had arrived, and grain was for sale only in Egypt, Jacob—who was weary of travel and hardship due to his old age—sent his ten sons from Canaan (where hunger reigned supreme due to the lack of grain) to Egypt to buy grain for himself and his household. This was done through a wonderful ordinance of God. He kept only Benjamin at home, Joseph’s full brother, who was also born to him from Rachel; so that, he said, harm might not perhaps befall him. For he loved him above the others and cherished him dearly, just as he had loved Joseph, who, in his opinion and according to the words of his brothers, had been torn apart by a wild beast. The ten sons then, obeying their father for their own sake and for the sake of their children, set out on the road to Egypt, thinking little of the wonders that would meet them upon their arrival there.

# The stone rejected by the builders becomes the cornerstone. <note>original Latin: "Lapis ab Architectis reprobatus, fit angularis." This is a biblical reference to Psalm 118:22, often used in Christian tradition to compare Joseph—who was rejected by his brothers but became a ruler—to Jesus.</note>

Joseph receives them with a face unknown to them, and says
   That they have come as spies against his King.
They excuse themselves and deny the act with a humble prayer: but all
   Is in vain, for their brother shuts them in prison.
<note>Translated from the original Latin: "Excipit hos facie ignota Joſephus..."</note>

### *The Israelites offer gifts to Joseph.*

Here is the fulfillment of the dream for which Joseph was once blamed:
   His brothers bow down deeply before him.
They offer him gifts, by which he feels moved,
And all this profound respect pierces his heart.
<note>Translated from the original French: "Les Israëlites font des presens à Joſeph..." The French text uses the word "assassinat" metaphorically to describe Joseph's intense emotional reaction to seeing his brothers' submission.</note>

### Joseph’s brothers bow down before him.

The brothers, paralyzed by fear, to Joseph’s eternal honor,
   Here descend to the earth like the eleven sheaves of grain:
   <note>A reference to Joseph’s dream in Genesis 37, where his brothers' sheaves of wheat bowed to his own.</note>
Though he, gentle by nature, has not taken revenge upon them,
The tribunal of their own conscience has not set them free.
<note>Translated from the original Dutch: "Joſephs Broeders buigen haar voor hem neder..."</note>

### *The stone which is cast away by the builders becomes the corner-stone.*

Joseph, quite unknown to his brothers, says
   That they have come as spies to betray Egypt;
This they deny, and explain the reason they have come,
Yet Joseph immediately sentences them to prison.
<note>Updated from the original 17th-century English: "Joſeph of's Brothers quite unknown, doth ſay..."</note>

### Joseph’s brothers bow down before him.

Joseph adopts a stern demeanor and calls his brothers traitors
And spies of the land, accusing them to their faces;
They deny it stiffly and firmly, but it does not help them,
For he takes them into custody like common evildoers.
<note>Translated from the original German: "Joſephs Brüdern biegen ſich für ihm nieder..."</note>

# Jacob sets out for Egypt.

BUT the father left his ancient home and his accustomed hearth, that he might dwell in the ancient lands of Egypt; he brought all his wealth with him in a long procession, so that he might see the Governor of Egypt before his death.
<note>original Latin: "AT pater antiquas ſedes ſolitoſque penates / Liquit..." The poet uses the Roman term "penates," meaning household gods, to signify Jacob's deep roots in the land of Canaan.</note>

->Jacob goes to Egypt.<-

*Jacob joyfully takes the road to Memphis,* <note>"Memphis" was an ancient capital of Egypt; here it represents the entire kingdom.</note>
*The hope of seeing Joseph gives him courage,*
*He departs with a great caravan: bringing all his sons,*
*And in his great joy, he does not feel the weight of his age.*

->Jacob comes to Joseph in Egypt.<-

How the loving heart of Jacob yearns for his son; though stiff and worn with age, he still comes to dwell with Joseph, that dearest child born to him by Rachel. To the one whom he once gave life, Joseph now wishes to give life in return. <note>This refers to Joseph providing for his elderly father during the famine, sustaining the one who sired him.</note>

->Jacob is going to Egypt.<-

*Old Jacob goes to Egypt, and he leaves*
*His dwelling place, because now he believes,*
*That Joseph is alive; which made him cry,*
*I'll see my son again before I die.*

->Jacob comes to Joseph in Egypt.<-

Jacob, full of love, comes here to Joseph; he travels from Canaan with his whole household, so that he might be fed by his pious son, the child given to him by Rachel. To the one whom he gave life, Joseph also wishes to give life.

->Jacob comes to Joseph in Egypt.<-

When the Patriarch Jacob had heard from his children that his son Joseph was still alive, and that he had been appointed by King Pharaoh as a ruler over the entire land of Egypt, he could not easily believe it. But seeing the wagons, the horsemen, and the gifts that the King had sent there through Joseph, Jacob was overcome with emotion <note>The Dutch "bezweek" literally means "succumbed" or "fainted," reflecting the biblical description of his heart failing him from shock and joy.</note> and said: "Is my son Joseph still alive? I shall go to him in Egypt." And so Israel <note>Jacob is frequently referred to as Israel after his encounter with the angel; here the names are used interchangeably to denote the father of the nation.</note> set out from Canaan with sixty-six souls (excluding the wives of his sons), along with all his possessions, and came to Beersheba. There he offered sacrifices to the God of his father Isaac, who appeared to him with this promise, saying:

> Do not fear, I will travel down to Egypt with you and make you into a great nation there, and afterwards I will bring you up again; and Joseph shall lay his hand upon your eyes. <note>This is a cultural reference to Joseph being present at the moment of Jacob's death to close his eyes.</note>

Then Jacob set out from Beersheba for Egypt with his entire household and everything he possessed. He also sent Reuben <note>The eldest son of Jacob.</note> ahead to Joseph to announce his father’s arrival. Then Joseph hitched his chariots and went to meet his father Israel, and he received him with great joy, falling upon his neck.

# Jacob, being infirm, blesses all his sons.

Now the old man, about to die, calls forth his twelve children <note>original Latin: "duodena ... pignora" — literally "twelve pledges," a poetic term for children</note> at last,
And commanded the whole household to be present:
So that he might wish happiness to all his sons alike,
And sing of many things to come with a prophetic voice <note>original Latin: "fatidicâ voce"</note>.

->Jacob gives his blessing to his twelve children.<-

Jacob brings all his sons to his bedside,
And describes the character of each one individually;
His blessings are all of great value,
And each one enfolds an important mystery.

->Israel blesses his Sons.<-

Jacob's blessing comes and rests well upon his children,
If they, being faithful to God, do not hinder the blessing;
For unfaithfulness <note>original Dutch: "ontrouw"</note> works no good, but he who honors his Father
Shall live after Death, just as God's word teaches him.

->Jacob being sick blesseth his Sons.<-

When Jacob was grown sick, and like to die
He called his Sons, and spoke to them orderly,
Bestowing upon them blessings manifold,
Which he in zeal prophetically foretold.

->Israel speaks the blessing over his Children.<-

Jacob's blessing comes and rests well upon the children,
When unfaithfulness and sin <note>original German: "zünd," a variant of "Sünde"</note> do not hinder the blessing.
He who loves his Mother from the heart and honors his Father
Shall live after Death, as God's word teaches him.

<note>This page features a polyglot presentation of Jacob’s deathbed scene from Genesis 49. The verses in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German all emphasize the prophetic nature of Jacob's last words and the moral obligation of children to remain faithful to God and their parents to receive the full weight of the patriarchal blessing.</note>

# GENESIS XLIX.

->Jacob Blesses his Sons.<-

---

As the time of Israel <note>Jacob is frequently referred to by his God-given name, Israel, in this context</note> drew near, he said to Joseph: "I am dying; but God will be with you all, and He will bring you back to the Land of your Fathers." Then Jacob called his children and blessed each of them individually, including Manasseh and Ephraim, concerning what would happen to them in the future. When Jacob had finished blessing his sons, he commanded that he should be buried in the Cave of Machpelah in the Land of Canaan, opposite Mamre, with his forefathers Abraham and Isaac. Thus Israel died. Joseph, seeing now that his father had died, fell upon his neck and kissed him because of the great love he had for him, and he commanded the physicians <term>Medicyn-meesters</term> <note>literally "medicine-masters"</note> to embalm <term>balzemen</term> him. They did as Joseph had commanded them; they spent forty days on this, as was the custom for those who were embalmed, and the Egyptians mourned Israel for seventy days.

<summary>Jacob delivers his final prophetic blessings to his sons and requests burial in the family tomb at Machpelah before passing away. The page concludes with the Egyptian process of embalming and a seventy-day period of mourning for the patriarch.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob, Joseph, Israel, Manasseh, Ephraim, Machpelah, Canaan, Mamre, Abraham, Isaac, embalming, physicians, Egyptians, mourning, deathbed blessing</keywords>

Jacob, drawing his final breath, commands that he be carried to Hebron. <note>original: "Jacob animam agens, jubet ut in Hebrum efferatur"</note>

But the sick father, breathing his last, requested the final gifts, even for the funeral rites and a place for burial. Once dead, he was carried to the tomb in leafy Hebron, and the promise made to him while living was fulfilled. <note>original Latin: "At pater æger agens animam..."</note>

*The holy man Jacob, ready to surrender his spirit, gives orders to his sons regarding his burial: they carry him to Hebron, as he had prescribed, and throughout the journey their mourning is beyond measure.* <note>original French: "Jacob prest à rendre l'ame..."</note>

### Joseph Buries his Father Jacob.

How lovely is Death, nourished by hope and virtue, to one whose soul hastens to God and whose body to the dust. The command of Israel <note>Jacob's honorific name</note> ensures that his sons carry him to the grave, traveling until they arrive in Canaan.

*Jacob at his departure charges his sons that they should bury him in Ephron's ground.* <note>Ephron the Hittite was the original owner of the field containing the Cave of Machpelah, which Abraham purchased</note>

When Jacob was dying in his bed,
He said where he wished to be buried:
His sons therefore carried him to Ephron's ground,
For they were bound by his order to do so.

### Joseph Buries his Father Jacob.

How sweet is Jacob's death, refreshed by true virtue, when the soul goes into eternity and the body commits itself to the dust. The command of Israel ensures that his sons lead him to the grave, until they come into Canaan.

->Joseph Buries his Father Jacob.<-

Now when Jacob had died, and the days of weeping were over, Joseph went to the house of King Pharaoh and said: "Let it please be requested of the King that I may bury my father, just as he made me swear." Then King Pharaoh said, "Go up and bury your father, as you have promised him." So Joseph went up to bury his father, and with him traveled all of Pharaoh’s servants, the elders of his house, and the elders of all the land of Egypt, as well as Joseph’s entire household and those of his brothers; but they left the little children and the livestock in the land of Goshen <note>A region in ancient Egypt assigned to the Hebrews</note>. When they came to the threshing floor of the thornbush <note>Refers to the 'Threshing Floor of Atad'; the word 'Atad' in Hebrew means 'thorn' or 'bramble'</note>, they held a very great lamentation there for seven days, and they buried their father there in the Cave of Machpelah, opposite Mamre, in the land of Canaan, just as Israel <note>The honorific name given to Jacob by God</note> had commanded them; and they all returned to Egypt afterward, each to his own occupation.

# The Divine Calling is Unchangeable.
<note>original: "Divina vocatio immutabilis"</note>

Then, a small basket smeared with pitch and woven 
of rushes, takes up the boy Moses, who floats amid the waters;
The King's daughter draws him out as he clings 
to the weedy shore, and, unaware of his great destiny, she nourishes him.
<note>Translated from the original Latin: "Illita dein puerum fiscella bitumine..."</note>

---

->*The divine calling is unchangeable.*<-

Moses is here exposed, and enclosed within a chest;
He is left to the mercy of the Lord and the waves.
But the future Law-giver <note>Refers to Moses' later role in receiving the Ten Commandments</note> is saved from the flood,
Falling into the hands of the King's daughter.
<note>Translated from the original French: "Moïse ici exposé..."</note>

### Moses is found and raised by the daughter of Pharaoh.

How in vain do men rage when God protects His people?
Now Moses is saved from the flood, just as later Jesus is saved from the sword.
<note>This compares Pharaoh’s decree to kill Hebrew infants with King Herod’s "Massacre of the Innocents" in the New Testament</note>
What are you doing, tyrant? Do you wish to break God’s words?
Wickedness shows its true nature, even if it does not know how to achieve its revenge.
<note>Translated from the original Dutch: "Wat woed men te vergeefs..."</note>

---

->*The divine calling is unchangeable.*<-

In a small basket stopped with pitch and slime,
On the Nile floats a babe of Levi's line.
Whom Pharaoh's daughter saves and puts to nurse,
And names him Moses. It was well it was no worse.
<note>This stanza was originally written in early modern English. "Levie's line" refers to the Tribe of Levi, one of the twelve tribes of Israel.</note>

### Pharaoh’s daughter draws Moses out of the water.

Amram and Jochebed <note>The parents of Moses</note>, with great sorrow,
Placed their child, whom they had hidden, in a small chest
Upon the water; but God played a wondrous part with him:
The King's daughter drew him out and supported him.
<note>Translated from the original German: "Amram und Jochebeth setzen mit grossen sorgen..."</note>

<image-desc>A rectangular engraving depicting the discovery of baby Moses in the Nile. In the foreground, the infant lies in a woven basket among river reeds. ->*Moses Rescued by Pharaoh’s Daughter.*<-

---

During the raging of the furious Pharaoh against the innocent Hebrew boys, Jochebed, the wife and aunt <note>The original Dutch 'Moeye' means aunt; this refers to the biblical account in Exodus 6:20 where Amram married his father's sister.</note> of Amram, bore a son; whom she hid for three months because he was beautiful. But when she could hide him no longer, she placed him in a chest of rushes in the river. And when he was thus cast away, it happened that Pharaoh’s daughter went down to wash herself in the river, and seeing the chest, she had it fished out by her handmaidens. When she opened it, a living child was found therein, weeping and calling for human compassion as if for help. The Princess, seeing such a sorrowful sight, took pity on him and had him raised by a Hebrew wet-nurse—who was actually his mother—naming him Moses, because he was drawn out of the water <note>The name Moses is traditionally linked to the Hebrew verb 'masha', meaning 'to draw out'.</note>, with the intention of keeping him as her own son and raising him in honor.

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>The divine calling is unchangeable. <note>original Latin: "Divina vocatio immutabilis."</note></header>

Then, in a basket smeared with pitch and woven from rushes, she places the boy Moses, who floats upon the waters. From the seaweed-covered shore, the King’s daughter draws him out and—unaware of his great destiny <note>original Latin: "magnae nescia fortis"</note>—she nourishes him.

#### *The divine calling is unchangeable.*

Moses is exposed here, enclosed in a chest, and left to the mercy of the Lord and the waves. But the future Law-giver <note>A reference to Moses's eventual role as the recipient of the Ten Commandments.</note> is saved from the flood, falling into the hands of the King's daughter.

### Moses is found and raised by Pharaoh’s daughter.

How futile is human rage when God protects His people? Now Moses is saved from the flood, just as later Jesus is saved from the sword <note>The author draws a parallel between Pharaoh’s decree against Hebrew infants and King Herod’s "Massacre of the Innocents" in the New Testament.</note>. What are you doing, tyrant? Do you hope to break God’s word? Wickedness reveals its true nature, even when it fails to achieve its revenge.

#### *The divine calling is unchangeable.*

In a small basket sealed with pitch and slime, a baby of the line of Levi floats upon the Nile. Pharaoh’s daughter saves him and provides a nurse, naming him Moses. It was a mercy that things were no worse.

### Pharaoh’s daughter draws Moses out of the water.

Amram and Jochebed, filled with great worry, placed their hidden child in a tiny chest upon the water. But God worked wondrously with him; the King’s daughter rescued him and provided for his upbringing.

->*Moses rescued from the water by Pharaoh's daughter.*<-

DUring the raging of the furious Pharaoh against the innocent Hebrew boys <note>The Dutch "knegtkens" literally means "little servants," but here refers to the male infants targeted by Pharaoh's decree.</note>, Jochebed—the wife and aunt <note>The text uses "Moeye" (aunt), reflecting the biblical genealogy in Exodus 6:20 where Amram marries his father's sister, a practice later forbidden by Mosaic Law.</note> of Amram—gave birth to a son. When she opened it, a living child was found inside, weeping and seemingly calling out to human compassion for help. The Princess, seeing such a sorrowful sight, took pity on him and had him raised by a Hebrew nurse—who was, in fact, his own mother. She named him Moses, because he had been drawn out of the water, intending to keep him as her own son and to raise him in honor.

### Moses is found and raised by Pharaoh’s daughter.

How vainly do men rage when God protects His people?
Now Moses from the flood, then Jesus from the sword. <note>A parallel is drawn between Pharaoh's decree to kill Hebrew infants and King Herod's "Massacre of the Innocents" in the New Testament.</note>
What are you doing, tyrant? Do you wish to break God’s word?
Wickedness shows its nature, even when it fails to achieve its vengeance.

# Moses defends the daughters of Jethro.
<note>Original Latin: *Moses defendit filias Jethronis.*</note>

Zipporah, who had led the tender lambs to the rivers,
Is driven from her father's spring, along with her flock, by the local youths.
Moses, a fugitive and an exile, flies to the sisters' aid,
And by force he repels the youths and their sheep.
<note>This Latin verse recounts the event in Exodus 2, where Moses, having fled Egypt after killing a taskmaster, arrives in Midian and protects the daughters of the priest Jethro from aggressive shepherds.</note>

### *Moses defends seven lovely sisters against the insults of some shepherds.*

Near the waters, Moses undertakes the defense
Of seven lovely sisters against several shepherds;
He drives them away and provides water for the sisters' flocks,
And one of them shall soon be his reward.
<note>The "reward" mentioned is Zipporah, Jethro's daughter, who becomes Moses's wife.</note>

---

### Moses delivers Jethro's daughters from the violence of the shepherds.

God’s Wisdom ordains all things and rules in the lives of men;
The daughters of Jethro find Moses by the well.
Their own countrymen are their affliction, but the stranger is their support;
Thus the exile is given a place, a livelihood, and a wife.
<note>The Dutch text highlights the theme of divine providence, showing how Moses’s misfortune (exile) leads to his new life and family.</note>

---

### *Moses delivers Jethro's daughters out of the hands of the shepherds.*

Like a knight, Moses defends the seven virgins
From the country fellows who would have driven their flocks from the troughs.
This kindness so impressed their father,
That he gave Zipporah to him as his wife.
<note>The original English text uses "Knight like," comparing the biblical Moses to a chivalrous figure of medieval romance.</note>

---

### Moses rescues Jethro's daughters.

God makes all things turn out well and rules in our lives.
Moses faithfully looks after the daughters of Jethro:
Their own countrymen are their threat, but a foreign man is their protection;
Thus the fugitive is given a home, food, and a wife.

->**Moses rescues Jethro's daughters.**<-

Because of the killing committed against the Egyptian, Moses took flight, left Egypt, and traveled to the Land of Midian. Here he had sat down by a water well, from which <margin>the</margin> shepherds of that region usually watered their livestock; <margin>so</margin> then the daughters of Jethro, who was the <margin>priest</margin> in that land, arrived first to draw water, and they filled the drinking <margin>troughs</margin> <note>Original: *drinkbaksteen*, referring to stone basins used for livestock</note> with water, so that the flock of their father, of which <margin>they</margin> were shepherdesses, might drink with ease. But the other shepherds, respecting neither them nor the priestly digni-<margin>ty</margin> of their father, drove them away from there, in order to serve themselves and their own flock first, and <margin>without</margin> effort, with <margin>the</margin> water that had been drawn. Moses, to whom this injustice was <margin>revolting</margin> <note>Original: *tegens de borst*, literally "against the chest," a Dutch idiom for finding something offensive or unacceptable</note>, stood up, delivered the maidens from the violence, <margin>and</margin> further watered their flock himself; by which incident the <margin>Prov-</margin>idence of God led him to the place where he would find <margin>a</margin> wife <note>Original: *Huis-vrouw*, literally "house-wife"</note> and a safe sanctuary in his ex-<margin>ile</margin>.

->Moses in the Burning Bush <note>original: *Braambosch*, literally "bramble-bush"</note>.<-

After Moses had lived for a considerable time in Midian in the house of his father-in-law Jethro, tending his flock, it happened that on a certain day he came to the Mountain of God at Horeb. Looking up, there appeared to him the great Angel of the Covenant in a flame of fire from the midst of a bush; by this fire, the bush appeared to be in bright flames, yet it was not consumed. The Man of God, wondering at this, stepped closer, but as he approached, he was called by name and warned by divine speech not to come closer, and to take the shoes off his feet, for the place where he stood was holy ground due to God's visible presence. And here, for the first time, he receives the command to go to Pharaoh and to demand the freedom of the Children of Israel from their slavery, as well as the assurance that the time had now come in which God would demonstrate to His people that He truly was the Lord, the <term>Jehovah</term> <note>The personal name of God in the Hebrew Bible, often interpreted in this period to signify His eternal and self-existent nature</note>.

# God hardens whom He wills. <note>original: *Deus quem vult indurat.*</note>

When King Pharaoh tramples the descendants of Abraham <note>original Latin: *ABramidas*</note> with unjust servitude, Moses brings the supreme commands: and so that he may prove his authority, he changes his staff into a snake; the Magician deceives Pharaoh with a similar art.

->*The rod of Moses, changed into a serpent, devours the serpents of the Magicians.*<-

When Aaron’s rod is seen as a serpent, the Black Art does the same; but God confounds the Mages, and shows to the eyes of all His supreme power, through the fateful staff which destroys their works.

->Moses and Aaron perform wonder-signs before Pharaoh.<-

How the Sun makes its power felt so unevenly upon snow and mire, upon hoofprints and filthy mud-puddles! God’s goodness is a Sun: it is no fault of His if He hardens Pharaoh’s heart, while Pharaoh abuses His patience. <note>The author uses a common theological metaphor: the same sun that melts wax also hardens clay, suggesting the "hardening" of Pharaoh's heart was a result of his own nature reacting to God's presence.</note>

->*God hardneth whom he pleaseth.*<-

Pharaoh the Hebrews with sore work doth load,
Moses desires to have them forth abroad,
(As sent from God) he makes a rod a snake,
The like for Pharaoh his Magicians make.

->Moses performs a miracle before Pharaoh.<-

Pharaoh oppresses the people and will not hear Moses’s word; he despises God’s command and pays no heed to it. Soon, before him, Moses’s staff became a snake; yet through sorcery, his heart becomes stubborn and hardened.

EXODUS VII.

F 4

<meta>Plate number: 43</meta>
<meta>Catchword: Yet</meta>

->Moses comes before Pharaoh.<-

Moses, after God had commanded him to speak to Pharaoh on behalf of the Children of Israel, and after offering some excuses <note>Moses initially hesitated to lead, citing his "slow speech and tongue."</note>, obeys God's command. He is strengthened by various signs and wonders, taking with him Aaron, whom God had sent to meet him on the way. They both reveal their mission to the Israelites and are believed by the people. After this, they went to Pharaoh, presenting their message and performing signs with the Staff, which changed into a Serpent; but Pharaoh, seeing that his sorcerers <note>original: *tovenaars*, referring to the court magicians like Jannes and Jambres</note> did the same, behaved defiantly. He stated that he did not know the God of the Hebrews, nor did he wish to let the people of Israel go, since they were currently subject to his slavery. Pharaoh, thus hardening his heart just as God had foretold, tormented the children of Israel with even heavier burdens <note>original: *plagen*, which can mean both "plagues" and "torments"; here it refers to the increased labor of making bricks without straw.</note>.

<header>Christ is our Passover, sacrificed for us.</header> <note>original: *Pascha pro nobis immolatum Christus est.*</note>

Night destroyed the firstborn of both beasts and men;
Every crossroad lies filled with corpses.
But the Lamb saves the Hebrews by the shedding of blood:
From this, the followers of Christ also celebrate their Passover.

### *The Lamb is eaten in haste.*

God, wishing at last to deliver the Hebrews,
Desires also that this people keep a memory of it;
That on this very night, by a fortunate sign,
They eat a lamb as a mark of their victory.

### The Children of Israel eat the Lord's Passover.

God's Israel celebrates that day when the Passover lamb is to be slaughtered;
As he smears his door, he renews his thoughts.
The Lamb of the Cross is our feast. I kiss in my soul,
With a reverent heart, that doorpost <note>original: *Kruispaal*, literally "cross-pole." The Dutch poet uses this word to explicitly link the wooden doorposts of the Israelites to the wooden Cross of the Crucifixion.</note>, red with blood.

### *Christ is the Passover which is offered for us.*

God in one night the firstborn slew,
Both man and beast which in Egypt grew.
One lamb each Hebrew family does serve;
Whence Christians learn their Passover to observe.

### The Children of Israel eat the Passover Lamb.

Soon all the firstborn in Egypt were struck down,
Even the King's own son who was to wear the crown.
To the people of Israel, the little Passover lamb <note>original: *Osterlämlein*. In German, the word for Passover and Easter is the same (*Ostern*), reinforcing the connection between the Exodus and the Resurrection.</note> brought protection;
Christ is God's Lamb, slain for our sins.

It emphasizes the "typology"—the idea that the sacrificed lamb protecting the Hebrews from the Angel of Death was a symbolic preview of Jesus Christ's sacrifice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Exodus 12. Following the previous page's poetic reflections, this page provides a prose commentary on the specific rituals of the Passover and their symbolic fulfillment in the life of Jesus Christ, a practice known as typology.</meta>

# EXODUS XII.

->*The children of Israel keep the Passover.*<-

In the month in which the Children of Israel had departed from Egypt, God commanded them—both for that journey and for every year thereafter—to choose a lamb without blemish <term>unblemished: a sacrificial animal without physical defects, symbolizing purity</term> on the tenth day to be a Passover lamb. They were to sprinkle the doorposts with the blood of the lamb so that He <note>Referring to God or the "Destroyer"</note> would pass over them during the slaying of the firstborn in Egypt. Regarding the preparation and other circumstances of this Passover lamb, all of which pointed toward our Lord Jesus Christ, the men of God <note>Likely referring to the Church Fathers or biblical scholars</note> have derived very sweet teachings. When Moses recounts that the Passover lamb is taken from the sheep, they interpret this as Christ’s innocence, gentleness, and patience in his death. Where it is written that the lamb had to be kept for a period of time <note>Exodus 12:6 specifies the lamb be kept from the 10th to the 14th day of the month</note>, they teach that this represents the preparation and proper contemplation of our redemption wrought by our Lord.

<sig>F 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: Abra-</meta>

<summary>This page describes the institution of the Passover ritual in Egypt, specifically the selection of an unblemished lamb and the marking of doorposts with blood. The text provides a Christological interpretation, linking the qualities of the sacrificial lamb to the character and sacrifice of Jesus.</summary>
<keywords>Passover, Exodus, Jesus Christ, typology, Lamb of God, Moses, Firstborn of Egypt, redemption</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Exodus from the previous page's discussion of the Passover. While the previous page focused on the sacrificial lamb as a symbol of Christ, this page moves to the Departure from Egypt and the Crossing of the Red Sea, interpreting these events through multilingual poetry as symbols of salvation and baptism.</meta>

# Our fathers were baptized under Moses in the sea.
<note>original Latin: "Patres noſtri ſub Moſe in mari baptizati ſunt." This is a reference to 1 Corinthians 10:1-2, where the Apostle Paul interprets the crossing of the Red Sea as a "type" or foreshadowing of Christian baptism.</note>

The descendants of Abraham <note>original: "ABramidæ"</note> leave the hostile lands under the leadership of Moses, who opens a path for them through the Red Sea <note>original: "Erythræum mare," a classical name for the Red Sea</note>. The tyrant <note>Pharaoh</note>, raging and mindless, sees them and follows, but the returning waves soon overwhelm him and his companions.

## *God walks before the Israelites in a pillar of cloud.*

Do not fear to march through the terrible deserts, people beloved of God, for God himself is your guide. He secures your steps; he has broken your chains; by night, he presides in the cloud to lead you.

## Pharaoh drowns with all his people in the Red Sea.

Relentless tyrant, do you despise God's command? The Sea is not for you as it is for Israel. The water knows its duty; you wished to destroy everything; now it is a double death to die according to another's desire <note>The Dutch "lust" suggests Pharaoh's own bloodlust or obsession led to his ruin</note>.

## *Our Fathers under Moses were baptized in the sea.*

The Red Sea Moses strikes and makes it land,
Through which he leads the newly-freed Hebrew band.
Pharaoh pursues them, Moses strikes again,
And the late land drowns Pharaoh and his train.

## Pharaoh drowns in the Red Sea.

So that God's people might once wander out of servitude, the Red Sea gives way and parts itself. Pharaoh follows afterward, fearing they would escape; with all his army, the tyrant was forced to drown.

<summary>This page presents a series of poems in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German depicting the Israelites' escape from Egypt and the destruction of Pharaoh's army. It emphasizes the theological connection between the crossing of the Red Sea and the sacrament of baptism.</summary>
<keywords>Moses, Pharaoh, Red Sea, Exodus, Baptism, Israelites, Tyranny, Divine Providence, Typology</keywords>

The text moves from the symbolic sacrifice of the lamb to the physical liberation of the Israelites and the miraculous crossing of the sea, which the previous page identified as a "type" or foreshadowing of Christian baptism.</meta>

<header>EXODUS 14, PLATE 45 <note>The Roman numeral XIV refers to Exodus chapter 14; the number 45 likely refers to this specific illustration in a series.</note></header>

<image-desc>A framed copperplate engraving depicting the biblical crossing of the Red Sea. In the center, a dry path is cleared between two towering walls of churning water. ->Moses crosses the Red Sea.<-

Pharaoh, having let the children of Israel depart, again feels regret for doing so; he therefore assembles his power, weapons, and horsemen, and pursues them in all haste. Because of this, a mournful cry went up to Heaven among the Israelites, with great displeasure directed against Moses <note>The Israelites were terrified and complained to Moses that it would have been better to serve the Egyptians than to die in the wilderness.</note>; but Moses comforts them, and the Angel of the Lord shows them the way with the pillar of cloud <term>pillar of cloud: a miraculous guiding presence that represented God's protection during the day</term>. As they now approached the Sea, Moses lifted up his Staff, and the Sea divided itself, so that the water stood by them like walls. Israel, seeing this deliverance, went through it with joy; but as Pharaoh followed them into the middle of the Sea, God sent a great storm, whereupon Moses stretched his hand over the Sea, so that he with all his people drowned in the middle of the Sea.
