<header>The People of Israel are fed with Manna. <note>original Latin: "Populus Israëlis pascitur Manna."</note></header>
<meta>catchword Na</meta>

While the people beloved by God lead their lives in the woods among the desert's desolate paths and dwellings, behold—when they grumble with murmurs spurred on by hunger—dew falls from heaven and is turned into food. <note>original Latin: "Dum vitam in filvis inter deserta viarum / Lustra domosque trahit Gens adamata Deo, / Exstimulante fame cum murmure murmurat, ecce / Ros cadit è cælo, vertitur inque cibum."</note>

---

### *The Manna falls from heaven.* <note>original French: "La Manne tombe du ciel."</note>

The chosen nation, at the rising of the sun, suddenly sees a celestial bread fall upon its camp. Everyone finds something to eat upon waking, and the glory of God is manifested through this.

---

### The Children of Israel receive the Manna from Heaven. <note>original Dutch: "De Kinderen Israëls verkrygen het Manna uit den Hemel."</note>

Ungrateful Israel! Turn your murmuring <note>The "murmuring" refers to the Israelites' repeated complaints and lack of faith despite witnessing prior miracles.</note> into gratitude toward God, and honor His goodness; He who frees you from bondage <term>bondage: the "dienst" or forced labor the Israelites endured in Egypt</term> and gives you heavenly bread. Oh! If only ungratefulness had been put to death in the wilderness.

---

### *The People of Israel are fed with Manna.*

No time or place excludes God’s favor from us. In the midst of famine, He can shower upon us bread out of heaven: Moses prays, and God’s love rains down a store of Manna <term>Manna: a miraculous edible substance provided by God; the name is said to derive from the Hebrew "Man hu," meaning "What is it?"</term> on Israel from above.

---

### God gives the Children of Israel the Manna. <note>original German: "Gott gibt den Kinderen Israëls das Manna."</note>

Where is there a place or city where God does not show His help? Even in the wilderness, He feeds Israel here: Heaven opens itself and gives them heavenly bread; God’s fatherly care soon rescues them from their distress.

<summary>This page commemorates the miracle of the Manna, the "bread from heaven" provided to the Israelites during their journey through the desert. Through poetry in five languages, it contrasts the people's lack of faith with God's reliable providence.</summary>

->*The Manna in the Wilderness.*<-

AFTER the deliverance of the Children of Israel out of the hand of Pharaoh, and after songs of praise to God in gratitude for this, a lack of food began to arise among such a great multitude, so that with sighs and murmurings <note>The term "murmuring" is a recurring biblical motif describing the Israelites' lack of faith and vocal complaints against God’s leadership.</note> they complained against Moses and Aaron about their miserable state. But God, hearing their murmuring, promised to give them bread from Heaven, and that on every day, except the Sabbath <term>Sabbath: referred to in the Dutch text as the "Rust-dag" or Day of Rest</term>, just as He had commanded Moses to make known to them; adding to this, that on the sixth <note>The OCR reads "zeften" (seventh), but contextually and biblically, this refers to the sixth day, when a double portion was gathered to prepare for the seventh-day rest.</note> day before the Sabbath, He would cause it to rain double, and they should then also gather a double portion; in order thus to keep the Sabbath unviolated. This serves as an example <note>In Christian tradition, this is viewed as a "type" or foreshadowing of future spiritual truths.</note> that Christ is the true Bread of Heaven, and a spiritual food for our souls.

<header>The prayer of a righteous man avails much. <note>original Latin: "Precatio justi multum valet," a reference to James 5:16.</note></header>

From this place, the Israelites <note>original: "Isacides," literally "sons of Isaac."</note> gain the upper hand over their enemies the Amalekites beyond all expectation, and prepare their weapons to pass through by force. War rages, and victory flits on uncertain wings, but it was made certain for the descendants of Abraham <note>original: "Abramidis."</note> through the prayer of Moses.

When Israel is at blows with Amalek,

### Amalek battles against Israel in Rephidim.

O Moses! Let your hands be supported,
So you shall protect all your people from the dangers of war.
Devout prayer is, for the Jewish army
Prepared for battle, the most powerful weapon.

With Amalek the Israelites do fight,
To make their way through, despite their foes;
Moses holds up his arms, the Hebrews gain
The day in battle, and the Amalekites are slain.

### Amalek leads a war against Israel.

Amalek wished to deny passage to Israel,
Wherefore Joshua <note>The leader of the Israelite army and eventual successor to Moses.</note> had to fight for the pass.
It was a heated march, a doubtful war,
But through the prayer of Moses, Israel achieved the victory.

# EXODUS XVII. 47

<header>EXODUS XVII. 47</header>

<image-desc>A detailed engraving depicting the biblical Battle of Rephidim. In the foreground, Joshua leads the Israelite forces in a chaotic melee against the Amalekites, with soldiers on horseback and on foot clashing with swords and spears. Several bodies lie on the ground. In the distant background, on a rocky prominence, the figure of Moses is visible, seated with his arms held up by two companions, Aaron and Hur, while the battle rages below.</image-desc>

->Joshua Defeats the Amalekites.<-

---

<margin>To blot out Amalek.</margin>
The Children of Israel being at Rephidim, and having drunk there to quench their thirst from the rock in Horeb <note>Referring to the previous miracle where Moses struck the rock to provide water.</note>, Amalek marches against them in battle out of ancient hatred. He ambushes the Children of Israel from the rear <note>original: "in den staert," literally "in the tail," referring to the vulnerable back of the marching column</note>, where they were weakest and most exhausted. Against them, Joshua—having been chosen by Moses as Commander—takes to the field and conquers, so long as Moses prayed with uplifted hands. But when his hands grew weak, Amalek prevailed; because of this, Aaron on one side and Hur on the other supported Moses' hands when they became heavy. Thus, Joshua destroyed his enemies by the edge of the sword. This indicates to us how fervently one must pour out their prayers before God if one wishes to be helped. Furthermore, God commands Moses to write this same event in a book as a memorial, and orders him to blot out <note>original: "uit te roeyen," meaning to root out or exterminate</note> Amalek.

### EXODUS XXXII.
<meta>pencil note: 99</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a group of Israelites dancing in a circle around a high, ornate pedestal topped with a statue of a golden calf. Smoke rises from a rectangular altar in the foreground. In the background, encampment tents are visible at the base of a mountain under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

->The Golden Calf.<-

WHEN Moses had received the Ten Commandments, written by the finger of God <note>The phrase "finger of God" is a biblical idiom signifying direct divine action or authorship without human intervention.</note> upon two stone Tablets, in order to present them to the people, and had remained upon the Mountain for forty days, the People made Aaron create a golden Calf. They did not think of the promise they had made to God, nor of the return of Moses from the Mountain, although he had stayed there a long time. God made this known to Moses, threatening to destroy them; but Moses, praying for them, obtained mercy. Now as Moses climbed down from the Mountain, hearing and seeing such apostasy <term>apostasy: the abandonment or renunciation of a religious or political belief</term>, he threw the Tablets into pieces and burned the Calf. After that, he climbed the Mountain again and brought back other Tablets, in which the Ten Commandments were written anew. When he displayed these, his face shone so brightly that he cast out rays of light from himself, for which reason he had to hang a veil <note>original Dutch: "dekzel"; here referring to the traditional veil used to shield the people from the divine radiance reflecting off Moses' skin.</note> over it whenever he would speak to the people.

# The Cloud shines around the Tabernacle.
<meta>page number: 100</meta>

<image-desc>A faint, ghost-like engraving serves as the background for the text. It depicts the biblical scene of the Israelites' camp in the desert. In the center is the Tabernacle (the tent of meeting), surrounded by a courtyard. A massive pillar of cloud or smoke rises vertically from the Tabernacle toward the heavens. Around the central area, numerous smaller tents belonging to the twelve tribes are arranged in a systematic layout.</image-desc>

Scarcely had the final touch been added to the labor,
When God’s glory shone around the mystic shrine.
He took the form of a cloud, within which
He was known to clear the path for those in doubt.
<note>original Latin: "Ultima vix fuerat superaddita lima labori, / Cum circumfulsit mystica fana Deus. / In nubis speciem se transformaverat, in quâ / Consuevit dubias dissoluisse vias."</note>

->God covers the Tabernacle with a cloud full of light.<-

Once the Sanctuary was worthily consecrated,
The glory of the King of Heaven immediately surrounded it;
He showed thereby that everything was to His liking,
As a cloud covered it like a crown.

->The Glory of God in a cloud above the Tabernacle.<-

The Tabernacle <term>Tabernacle: a portable dwelling place for the divine presence, used by the Israelites from the time of the Exodus until the building of Solomon's Temple</term>, with its equipment and finery
Built in all its parts to exact measure, according to God's command,
One sees His holy cloud rising there,
Which shows Israel the certain paths by night and day.

When Moses had the Tabernacle made,

Then the Glory <note>The "Glory" or "Herligkeit" refers to the *Shekhinah*, a Hebrew term for the dwelling or settling of the divine presence.</note> came to light up the Temple,
So that all Israel might set out on their journey with joy.

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>EXODUS XL. 34-38</header>
<note>The header in the original reflects a different numbering or typo as "50", but the text corresponds to the conclusion of Exodus 40.</note>

<image-desc>A rectangular engraving illustrating a scene from the Book of Exodus. In the center-left, the Tabernacle is shown with a dense column of cloud and fire descending upon it. ->the Tabernacle ..<-

When God had now commanded Moses to set up the Tabernacle on the first day of the month, he did just so, according to all the words as the Lord had spoken to him, and placed there in the Tent of Meeting the golden Altar, the golden Lampstand, the table of the <term>Showbread: twelve loaves of bread set out in the sanctuary as an offering, representing the twelve tribes of Israel</term>, the <term>Basin: also known as the laver, a large vessel used by priests for ritual washing</term>; and furthermore, he arranged everything belonging to it alongside them. He had Aaron put on the holy garments, as well as his sons, who washed their hands and feet, and he anointed everything that was in the Tabernacle, just as God had commanded. They also placed incense upon the Altar and made offerings. Then the Glory of the Lord appeared in a cloud and filled the Tabernacle, so that Moses could not enter the Tent of Meeting because of the radiance of the Lord. When the cloud was lifted from above the Tabernacle, then the children of Israel journeyed onward, but if the cloud was not lifted, they did not journey; for the cloud of the Lord was upon the Tabernacle by day, and the fire was there by night throughout all their travels, before the eyes of all Israel.

->*The Spies from the Land of Canaan.*<-

When the Children of Israel approached the Promised Land, Moses, by God’s command, sent twelve spies—one from each tribe—to inspect the Land of Canaan <note>A region roughly corresponding to modern-day Israel, Palestine, and Lebanon</note>, to see what it was like and what sort of people lived there. Once these men had traveled through and scouted the land, they eventually came to the Valley of Eshcol <note>Dutch: "Efcol"; the name means "cluster," referring to the grapes found there</note>, where they cut a branch with a single cluster of grapes. They carried it on a carrying-pole <note>The weight of the fruit was so great that it required two men to carry it</note>, along with some pomegranates and figs. Upon returning with these fruits, the messengers gave an account of what they had encountered on their journey. All but two of them considered the conquest of the land to be impossible, due to the fortified cities and the terrifying giants <note>Referring to the Anakim, a race of formidable stature mentioned in the biblical text</note> who lived there. However, Joshua and Caleb sought to calm the congregation, comforting them with the certain help of the Most High, as well as with a report on the magnificence of the Land of Canaan.

# NUMBERS XIII.

<image-desc>An ornate rectangular frame contains an engraving of two men in biblical attire carrying an enormous cluster of grapes suspended from a wooden pole resting on their shoulders. They are walking through a landscape featuring leafy trees and distant hills. In the background, other figures and a town are visible. The number 51 is printed in the upper right corner inside the frame.</image-desc>

->The scouts from the Land of Canaan.<-

When the Children of Israel approached the Promised Land, Moses, by God’s command, sent twelve scouts—one from each tribe—to inspect the Land of Canaan: to see what the land was like and the character of the people who lived there. When these men had traveled through and scouted the land, they finally came to the Valley of Eshcol <note>original Dutch: "dal Escol"</note> and cut down a branch with a single cluster of grapes, which they carried on a carrying-pole <note>The "Draag-stok" was a wooden pole resting on the shoulders of two men to support the immense weight of the fruit</note>, as well as some pomegranates and figs. When they returned with these fruits, the messengers gave an account of what they had encountered on their journey. All of them, except for two, maintained that the conquest of the land was impossible, owing to the fortified cities therein and the terrifying giants <note>referring to the Anakim or Nephilim mentioned in the biblical text</note> who inhabited it. However, Joshua and Caleb sought to calm the congregation, comforting them with the certain help of the Almighty, as well as with a report on the magnificence of the Land of Canaan.

# Korah, Dathan, and Abiram are swallowed as the earth gapes open. <note>original Latin: "Cora, Dathan & Abiram terra dehiſcente deglutiuntur"</note>

While Moses—the symbolic figure of the true Prophet <note>The Latin "Typicus Moſes" refers to the theological concept of "typology," where Moses is seen as a "type" or a foreshadowing of Jesus Christ</note>—rebukes their agitated souls, they persist despite being warned: behold! the gaping earth consumes the bodies of Korah, Dathan, and Abiram in the violent wrath of the Divine.

### *The sedition of Korah, Dathan, and Abiram punished.*

*The seditious Dathan had just stirred up revolt,*
*Leading Israel away from their duty;*
*But the Almighty God in His righteous wrath*
*Consumes the instigators, and afterwards pardons the rest.*

### Korah, Dathan, and Abiram go down alive into Hell. <note>In this context, "Hell" (Dutch: "Helle") refers to Sheol or "the pit," describing the rebels falling into a chasm in the earth</note>

They dispute the priesthood of Aaron, and they reach
For Moses' authority; yet God’s vengeance goes forth
To destroy Korah’s conspirators in flame and fire,
And hundreds with them—oh, horror!—hell swallows them up.

### *Korah, Dathan, and Abiram are swallowed up by the Earth.*

*While Moses reproves the wicked crew*
*And shows them their lawlessness,*
*God causes the Earth suddenly to devour*
*Korah, Dathan, Abiram, and all they owned.*

### Korah, Dathan, and Abiram go down alive to Hell.

They quarrel over the priesthood of Aaron, and they challenge
Moses' great authority; but God’s calamity brings
Korah and his gang to ruin in smoke and flame,
And hundreds die with them in terror in hell.

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>NUMBERS XVI. 52 <note>This number likely refers to the plate or illustration number in the series, as the biblical chapter of Numbers 16 contains only 50 verses.</note></header>

Korah took Dathan and Abiram, along with two hundred and fifty men from the Children of Israel, leaders of the assembly and chosen ones of the gathering, men of renown, who stirred up rebellion against Moses and Aaron, saying: "Why do you exalt yourselves over the community of the Lord, since they are all holy, and the Lord is in their midst?" When Moses heard this, he fell on his face and said to Korah: "Tomorrow the Lord will make known whom He has chosen; come with your censers <term>Wierook-vaten: metal vessels used for burning incense during religious rituals</term> and put fire in them, and lay incense <term>Reuk-werk: a mixture of aromatic spices burned as an offering</term> upon them, and thus appear before the face of the Lord; and the man whom the Lord shall choose, he shall be holy." Then the glory of the Lord appeared, and caused the whole assembly to separate themselves from the tents of Korah, Dathan, and Abiram; for God let the earth split apart, so that they, with their entire households, sank alive into hell <note>original Dutch: "helle." In this historical biblical context, this refers to *Sheol* or "the pit"—a literal opening in the ground—rather than the modern concept of a place of afterlife punishment</note>, and the earth covered them over again. Also, a fire went out from the Lord and consumed the two hundred and fifty men who had risen up with Korah against Moses.

### 106 Moses raised the Serpent in the Wilderness.

Straightaway the thirsty serpents <term>Dipsades: A legendary type of venomous snake whose bite was said to cause an unquenchable, fatal thirst; here used to describe the "fiery serpents" mentioned in the Bible</term> tore at the ungrateful company, killing many bodies with their death-bearing bite. Moses suspended a serpent in the air upon hollow bronze; the sight of it relieves those dire wounds. 
<note>original Latin: "Protinus ingratum laniantes Dipsades agmen / Lethifero morsu corpora multa necant. / Ære cavo colubrum suspendit in aëre Moses, / Illius aspectus vulnera dira levat."</note>

->Moses raises the Brazen Serpent.<-

*The Hebrews, murmuring with signal audacity, are bitten by serpents and die on the spot: but whoever turns his eyes toward the serpent of brass is healed by Christ, the sovereign Master.*
<note>original French: "Les Hebreux murmurans par une insigne audace, / Sont mords par les serpens & meurent sur la place: / Mais que dresse ses yeux vers le serpent d'airain, / Est regueri par Christ, le maître souverain."</note>

->Moses raises a copper Serpent in the Wilderness.<-

When my soul rightly beholds the serpent of copper, it then looks upon Christ in the bitter sorrow of the cross, so shamefully exalted: what has it to fear? He who crushes the serpent <note>A reference to the "Protevangelium" in Genesis 3:15, where the offspring of the woman is prophesied to crush the head of the serpent</note> shall also heal its wound.
<note>original Dutch: "Wanneer myn ziel de Slang van koper regt beziet, / Dan zietze Christum aan, in 't bitter kruis-verdriet, / Zo smadelyk verhoogt: waar heeftze voor te vrezen; / Hy die de slang verplet zal ook zyn wond genezen."</note>

->God did erect a Serpent in the Wilderness.<-

At God the Hebrews murmur and complain,
For which by serpents they are stung and slain,
Moses erects a brazen serpent which
Heals every man that looks upon it from the serpents' touch.

->Moses sets up a copper Serpent.<-

The people murmur against God; He sends fiery serpents among the evil crowd, by which many were killed. Moses, at God’s command, sets up a copper serpent; whoever looks upon it shall obtain health.
<note>original German: "Das Volk wider Gott murrt, der schickt feurige Schlangen / Unter die böse Rott, dadurch viel hingericht, / Moses auff Gotts befehl ein kupffren Schlang aufricht, / Wer solche schauet an, gesundheit solt erlangen."</note>

NUMERI XXI. 53

->The Serpent raised in the Wilderness.<-

AFTER Moses had sent spies into Canaan, and they had made the people’s hearts discontented <note>Referring to the report of the twelve spies that caused the Israelites to fear the inhabitants of the Promised Land</note>, they remained for forty years in the Wilderness, where many wonders and strange histories occurred. Among others, they were plagued with fiery serpents, which were sent to them by God because they murmured <note>To "murmur" in this biblical context means to complain or rebel against divine authority</note> and, despising Moses, felt a loathing for the Bread <note>Manna, the miraculous food provided by God during the Exodus</note> that the Lord had provided for them. But the people, feeling repentance, entreated Moses to pray to the Lord that He would take the serpents away from them; which Moses does, and by God's command, he raises up a copper serpent <note>Also known as the Nehushtan</note>, placing it upon a pole. And whoever looked upon it was healed after he had been bitten; serving as an example for us, through which we are cleansed from the bites of the evil Serpent, the Devil, if we but gaze fixedly through the eyes of faith.

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

# They shall curse, but I shall bless. <note>original Latin: "Ipsi maledicent, ego autem benedicam." A reference to Psalm 109:28, contrasting the ill intentions of men with the favor of God.</note>

I <note>Signature mark "I," used by early printers to help binders assemble the book's pages in the correct order.</note>

Behold, the Moabite Balak calls you, Prophet Balaam,
To curse the Hebrew people. 
The Divine Spirit does not permit this: why do you beat the wretched donkey, 
Who rebukes you, speaking with a miraculous mouth?
<note>original Latin: "EN maledicturum Vatem Moabita Balacus / Hebraeo populo te Bileame vocat. / Non sinit hoc Genius: miseram quid caedis asellam, / Quae te mirifico corripit ore loquens?"</note>

->Balaam’s Donkey speaks.<-

Balak, son of Zippor, sent for Balaam to be brought 
To him, so that he might curse the Hebrew people; 
But the Angel forbids him, and the mute animal—
God made her speak—commands her master to be silent.
<note>original French: "L'Asne de Balaam parle. / BAlak fils de Tsippor envoya pour conduire / A soi Balaâm, pour le peuple Hebreu maudire ; / Mais l'Ange l'en defend, & l'animal muët, / Dieu le faisoit parler, à son maître fait têt."</note>

->Balaam is rebuked by his speaking she-donkey<-

Who does not stand as if stunned, wondering in his spirit, 
When he reads of Balaam and of his donkey? 
Heaven shall never lack for wonders;

<note>original Dutch: "Bileam word door zyn Ezelinne sprekende berispt / WIe staat niet als verbaast, verwondert in zyn geest, / Als hy van Bileam en van zyn Ezel leest? / Den Hemel zal het noit aan wonderen ontbreken, / Als Godt zyn magt betoont, dan zal een Ezel spreken."</note>

Balak, to curse the Israelites, does call

The Angel stays the donkey, who speaks when Balaam beats;

<note>original English: "BAlak to curse the Isra'lites doth call / Balam, when Gods bids not to curse at all: / The Angel stays the asse who speakes when Bal'am beates / In stead of curses, blesings he repeats."</note>

->Balaam’s she-donkey who speaks.<-

Balak calls to Balaam to seek counsel from him, 
Because he was in anguish; he wanted him to curse Israel. 
The Angel hinders him; the donkey speaks;

<note>original German: "Bileams Eselin die spricht. / BAlack rufft Bileam bey ihm um rath zu suchen, / Weil er in anghsten war, er solt Israël fluchen. / Der Engel wehret ihn, die Eselin die spricht, / So sag het der Prophet und fluchet dem volk nicht."</note>

NUMBERS 22.

<meta>image plate number: 54</meta>

<image-desc>A rectangular copperplate engraving framed by a double-line border. The scene illustrates a story from Numbers 22. In the center, Balaam (Bileam) is seated on a donkey that has collapsed to its knees. Balaam raises a staff above his head to strike the animal. To the left, an angel with large feathered wings and a plumed helmet stands in the way, pointing forward with one hand and holding a sword in the other. In the background to the right, two men are following on foot. On the far left, a ruined stone archway stands against a hilly landscape. At the bottom of the frame is a centered caption.</image-desc>

->Balaam rebuked by the She-Donkey.<-

Balak, the King of the Moabites <note>Moab was a kingdom located east of the Dead Sea; in the biblical narrative, Balak feared the migrating Israelites.</note>, wishing to curse Israel, summoned Balaam for this purpose, who consulted the Lord on the matter; however, God forbade him from going to Balak. When Balak sent for him a second time, the Lord allowed him to go with the Princes of Moab who had been dispatched for that task. Once Balaam had saddled his she-donkey and departed with the Princes, God's anger was kindled because of his journey; therefore, the Angel of the Lord stood in the path to oppose him. The she-donkey saw the Angel and thus turned aside every time, eventually lying down beneath him. Balaam, incensed with rage (for he did not see the Angel), beat the she-donkey a third time most dreadfully. Then God opened the mouth of the she-donkey so that she spoke, accusing her Master of injustice; immediately, Balaam's eyes were opened so that he saw the Angel, who rebuked him and, upon his confession of guilt, allowed him to proceed.

<term>Moabites</term> — An ancient Levantine people often in conflict with the Israelites.
<term>Balaam</term> — A non-Israelite prophet or diviner featured in the Book of Numbers.
<term>She-donkey</term> — The animal's miraculous speech is one of the few instances of an animal speaking in the Bible, signifying divine intervention.

<meta>catchword: Vene-</meta>

<keywords>Balaam, Balak, Moabites, Israel, Angel of the Lord, talking donkey, Numbers 22, divine intervention, prophecy</keywords>

# Here in hope, one day in reality.
<note>original Latin: "Hic in spe, olim in re." This refers to the theological idea of seeing a promise now through faith, but experiencing it fully after death.</note>

Moses had come to the river, where he climbed the mountain peaks and from on high saw the future kingdoms. Here you die, O man who was a friend to God, thrice-holy Prophet, whom God buried in unknown ground.
<note>original: "Venerat ad fluvium Moses..." This Latin verse recounts the events of Deuteronomy 34, where Moses views Canaan from Mount Nebo before his death.</note>

### *God shows Moses the promised land.*

*From Mount Nebo the Lord lets Moses see*
*The regions and beauties of the promised land:*
*There this faithful servant passed away,*
*And was buried by God his Creator.*

### God lets Moses see the Promised Land on Mount Nebo.

The leader of God's people sees the harbor from afar, where God on high shall bury his body on the mountain. How peacefully the body dies when the weary soul is inwardly nourished with the hope of salvation!

### *Here in hope, afterwards in deed.*

*God from a mountain's top lets Moses see*
*The land of promise, where he may not be.*
*He dies in Moab, and is buried there*
*By God, but to this day none knoweth where.*
<note>This section was originally written in seventeenth-century English; the spelling has been slightly modernized for clarity while preserving the rhyme.</note>

### God shows Moses the land of Canaan.

Finally, the Prophet Moses sees the land from afar, into which Joshua was to lead the people of Israel. On Mount Nebo the dear man of God dies; God leads him through death into the Heavenly Canaan.
<note>The "Heavenly Canaan" is a common historical metaphor for Heaven.</note>

# DEUTERONOMY 34.
<meta>additional page number at top right: 111</meta>

*An engraving depicting the death of Moses on Mount Nebo. Moses, portrayed as an old man with a long beard, lies in eternal rest against a rocky outcrop. In the upper left corner, a radiant celestial light in the shape of an oval shines through clouds, containing the Hebrew Tetragrammaton <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, יהוה, often transliterated as YHWH</note>. The scene is set in a mountainous landscape under a heavy sky.*

->Moses dies on Mount Nebo.<-

Moses, having reached the age of one hundred and twenty years, declared before the people that he would soon die and would not enter the Land of Canaan <note>The Dutch text uses "aanschouwen" (to behold), but in this context, it refers to his inability to physically enter the land he was permitted only to see from afar</note>; he promised that Joshua would lead them into it and blessed the twelve tribes of Israel before his death, offering further blessings should they conduct themselves well before the Lord. When Moses had completed these things, he climbed from the plains of Moab toward Mount Nebo, ascending to the heights of Pisgah, from where God allowed him to see the Promised Land. After he had gazed upon it, he died there, and the Lord buried him in a valley in the land of Moab, opposite Beth-Peor, so that no one has ever known the location of his grave. Some believe this occurred through a special providence <term>providence: the protective care or spiritual guidance of God</term> of God, so that the Israelites would not commit idolatry <term>idolatry: the worship of a physical object or person as a god</term> with his dead body. Thus Moses died, and was mourned by Israel for thirty days, as Joshua took his place.

<meta>catchword: To the ungrateful [original Latin: Ingrato]</meta>

### As I was present with Moses, so I shall be present with you.
<note>original Latin: "Sicut adfui Moſi , ita tibi adero." This is a reference to God’s promise to Joshua in the Book of Joshua 1:5.</note>

After Moses had bid farewell to the ungrateful world,
Immediately Joshua takes up the scepters of the sons of Isaac <note>original: "Iſacidum" — a poetic way of referring to the Israelites as descendants of the patriarch Isaac</note>,
Here the Ruler of Olympus <note>original: "Regnator Olympi" — a classical, Latinized way of referring to God as the ruler of heaven</note> divides the waters of the Jordan,
The Canaanite fields are sought by the people on dry feet.

->The Crossing of the Jordan.<-

Finally all Israel will taste beautiful days:
God, through a miracle second to none,
Splits the waters of the Jordan, interrupts its course,
And the people pass through the waves on dry feet.

->Joshua leads the Children of Israel dry-shod through the Jordan.<-

The valiant Joshua fears no deep streams,
He shall, through Heaven’s help, cross over happily:
There the water of the Jordan piles itself up like a wall,
The Creator of the Universe is stronger than nature.

->As I have been present with Moses, so will I be present with thee.<-

God chooses Joshua in dead Moses' place,
To sway the scepter of the Hebrew race.
He leads the people where deep waters ran
Through Jordan's dry ground unto Canaan.

When Moses had taken his leave from the world,
And then the command <note>original German: "Regiment"</note> had come to Joshua,
God divided the water in the Jordan quite wonderfully,
Israel went through it into the land of Canaan.

# JOSHUA 4

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the crossing of the Jordan River. In the foreground, three priests in ornate robes and head coverings carry the Ark of the Covenant on poles. Joshua stands to the left, gesturing with his hand. To the right and in the background, a large procession of Israelites follows across the dry riverbed. On the left side, the river water is shown heaped up into a massive, turbulent wall. Rocky hills and a clear sky are visible in the background. A small plate number "56" is in the upper right corner of the image frame.</image-desc>

->*Joshua passes through the Jordan.*<-

Joshua, the son of Nun, (ordained by God to be a leader of the Children of Israel in the place of Moses, who had died), was commanded by God to cross the Jordan, and so enter the Promised Land. For this purpose, Joshua sent out spies to observe the entire land, as well as the city of Jericho. Having returned with good news, Joshua departed with the Children of Israel from Shittim <note>original: "Sittim" — an encampment of the Israelites on the east bank of the Jordan</note> to the Jordan. According to God's promise, the water rose up in a heap very far away, near the city of Adam <note>The Bible mentions the water piling up at the town of Adam (Joshua 3:16) while the flow to the sea was cut off</note>. As soon as the feet of the Priests who carried the Ark were dipped into the edge of the water, the lower water flowed away toward the Salt Sea <note>The Dead Sea</note>, and the Jordan became completely dry. While the Priests stood with the Ark in the middle of the Jordan until the entire multitude had crossed over on dry feet, they then crossed over themselves with the Ark of the Lord.

*Joshua crosses the Jordan.*

Joshua, the Son of Nun, (ordained by God as a leader of the Children of Israel, in the place of Moses, who had died,) is commanded by God to cross the Jordan, and thus toward the Promised Land; for which purpose Joshua sends out spies to inspect the entire land, as well as Jericho; and having returned with good tidings, Joshua departed with the Children of Israel from Shittim <note>a location east of the Jordan River</note> to the Jordan, which, according to God's promise, rose up in a heap, very far from the city of Adam, and as soon as the feet of the priests, who carried the Ark, were dipped into the edge of the water, the downstream water flowed away toward the Salt Sea <note>The Dead Sea</note>, and the Jordan became completely dry. While the priests now stood with the Ark in the middle of the Jordan, until the whole multitude had crossed through on dry feet, they also crossed over with the Ark of the Lord.

# BY FAITH THE WALLS OF JERICHO FELL.
<note>original: "Fide corruerunt mœnia Jerichuntis"</note>

She had closed her gates against the Hebrews; three times <note>The Latin text says "ter" (three times), though the biblical account in Joshua 6 describes a seven-day procession.</note> around her
    The crowd of Priests was ordered to blast the trumpets.

# JOSHUA VI.

<image-desc>An etching illustrating a biblical scene from Joshua 6. A procession of Israelites, including priests carrying the Ark of the Covenant and seven priests blowing trumpets, marches around the city of Jericho. In the background, the stone walls and towers of the city are shown crumbling and collapsing. Armed soldiers are visible on the left side of the procession. The entire scene is enclosed in a rectangular border, which includes the number 57 in the upper right corner and a caption at the bottom.</image-desc>

->*Through faith the walls of Jericho fall.*<-

---

All of Israel, having crossed through the Jordan dry-shod, were circumcised at Gilgal by the command of the Lord—namely, all the males who had been born during the journey <note>The generation born in the wilderness had not been circumcised; this ritual at Gilgal marked their formal entry into the covenant before the conquest began.</note>—where they also celebrated the Passover <term>Passover</term> <note>Original: "Pascha." The Jewish festival commemorating the Exodus from Egypt.</note>. Afterward, they surrounded the city of Jericho by the command of Joshua, who—following God's instructions—ordered the soldiers, as well as the Ark <term>Ark</term> <note>The Ark of the Covenant, the sacred chest containing the tablets of the Law, signifying God's presence among the army.</note> and seven priests, to process around the city for seven days. But on the seventh day, the city was captured, with the walls falling down through Faith (for such is the power of Faith when it remains steadfast and perseveres to the very end). When the city was thus taken, Joshua ordered everything to be burned, except for the vessels of gold, silver, copper, and iron, and he also placed a curse upon anyone who might attempt to rebuild the city in the future.

# THE SUN AND MOON STAND STILL AT THE COMMAND OF FAITHFUL JOSHUA <note>original: "Sol ſtat & Luna ad juſſa Joſuæ fidelis"</note>

While Joshua as victor takes vengeance on his fierce enemies,
The Sun stood still at its height and the Moon at the full Pole,
And the stars, changed, rested from their courses;
There was no longer day in all the world. <note>original Latin: "Longior in toto non fuit orbe dies." This refers to the biblical account in Joshua 10, where God extended the daylight to allow the Israelites to complete their victory.</note>

---

->*The Kings of Canaan are defeated by Joshua.*<-

At the name of Joshua all the Kings are alarmed
And form a strong league against all of Israel;
But God first abandoned them all to him,
And is always lavish with favors to His people.

---

->The Sun stands still while Joshua destroys his enemies.<-

God, who favors His war-hero in his battle,
Makes a full day out of twenty-four hours through the slow Sun:
Creation follows His will: if He wishes to perform a miracle,
Then the Moon shall light the day, and the Sun the night. <note>The Dutch poem emphasizes the total subjection of nature to God's will, suggesting He could even swap the celestial roles of the sun and moon if He so desired.</note>

---

->*The Sun and Moon stand by the command of faithful Joshua.*<-

Whilst Joshua conquers his fierce enemies,
The Sun and Moon then full stand in the skies,
And the changed heavens their wonted course do stay:
This world yet never [saw] a longer day.

---

->The Sun stands still as Joshua strikes his enemy.<-

Joshua in the battle slays the Amorites <term>Amorites</term> <note>The Amorites were one of the powerful nations inhabiting Canaan whom the Israelites fought under Joshua's leadership.</note>,
Pursues them in flight, and strikes many thousands down.
The dear Sun stood still, which gave a double day;
The Moon also stayed back; see what Faith can accomplish!

# JOSHUA X.

An engraving of a biblical battle scene. Joshua is the central figure, mounted on a rearing horse and wielding a sword. Behind him, a soldier blows a trumpet. The sky features a prominent sun, and the landscape includes a valley filled with soldiers and fallen bodies, with steep mountains in the background. The engraving is enclosed in a rectangular border containing the number 58 in the top right corner.

->*Joshua destroys his Enemies.*<-

After Joshua was encouraged by God to besiege Ai, with the promise and assurance that he would capture it; the Kings of Canaan were greatly troubled by this, as well as by the fact that the Gibeonites <term>Gibeonites</term> <note>A Canaanite people who secured a peace treaty with Israel through deception, leading the other Canaanite kings to view them as traitors.</note> had made peace with Joshua. For this reason, five of these kings allied themselves to go and besiege Gibeon; but Joshua, strengthened by the Lord, marched up quickly from Gilgal with his army to meet them. He struck them so thoroughly that not a single one among them remained: God Himself also fought for Israel by means of large stones <note>The biblical text in Joshua 10:11 identifies these "stones from heaven" as massive hailstones.</note> which He threw from Heaven upon their enemies. Joshua pursued them until the evening, and seeing that the Sun was setting, he prayed to the Lord that the Sun might stand still at Gibeon until the people had avenged themselves on their enemies, which came to pass. Joshua, further understanding that the five fleeing kings had hidden themselves in a cave, had them brought out. He commanded the leaders of the fighting men of Israel to set their feet upon the necks of the kings, then he struck them, killed them, and hanged them. Continuing his victory, he captured their cities, destroying everything that was in them.

# THE BOOK OF JUDGES

Just retribution <note>original Latin: "Talio justa"</note> seizes unjust men.
<meta>catchword: Na</meta>

The descendants of Abraham occupy the city and Kingdom of Bezek,
The King flees, Judah pursues, and captures the man.
They mutilate the thumbs and great toes of the Tyrant:
Thus retribution, having taken its revenge, weighs upon cruel men.

->A Worthy Recompense.<-

Judah takes Jerusalem and Gaza by storm,
And razes Bezek from its foundations to the heights;
He cuts off his sacrilegious fingers,
Just as he [the King] had done to seventy powerful Kings.

### Adoni-Bezek has the Thumbs of his hands and feet cut off.

You let many Kings, with severed Thumbs
And stump-truncated toes, pick up crumbs from under the table;
And, Bezek, this is your reward. Thus is vengeance satisfied,
And the rightful punishment is visited upon the punisher. <note>In the biblical account (Judges 1:7), Adoni-Bezek acknowledges that God has repaid him exactly for his cruelty toward seventy other kings.</note>

->The law of retribution <note>original: "Talio"</note> destroys unjust men.<-

King Bezek’s Town and country Judah takes,
Him flying he pursues, and prisoner makes,
Of toes and fingers the Tyrant rid
By the law of retaliation; the like before he did.

### Adoni-Bezek has the Thumbs on hands and feet cut off.

Judah along with Simeon captured the City of Bezek,
The King took flight, yet they caught him regardless,
They lopped off his thumbs on both hands and feet,
What he had done to others, this bloodhound must pay for.

<term>Talio</term> <note>Short for "Lex Talionis," the law of retaliation, often summarized as "an eye for an eye."</note>, <term>Adoni-Bezek</term> <note>A title meaning "Lord of Bezek." He was a powerful Canaanite ruler who was defeated shortly after the death of Joshua.</note>, Bezek, Judah, Simeon, retribution, mutilation.

<summary>This page depicts the capture and punishment of the Canaanite king Adoni-Bezek by the tribes of Judah and Simeon. It serves as a moral lesson on "lex talionis" (the law of retribution), showing how the king suffered the same mutilation he had inflicted on seventy other rulers.</summary>
<keywords>Adoni-Bezek, Bezek, Judah, Simeon, retribution, Lex Talionis, justice, biblical history, Judges 1</keywords>

JUDGES I.

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a biblical scene from the Book of Judges. In the foreground, soldiers in classical-style armor and helmets stand near a large stone column. In the middle ground, on a raised stone platform outside a city wall, an executioner or soldier is shown cutting the thumbs and toes off a man (Adoni-Bezek) who sits bound in a chair. In the background, a fortified city with a tower flying a flag is visible under a clear sky. The plate is numbered "59" in the upper right corner of the border.</image-desc>

->The Punishment of Adoni-Bezek.<-

---

After the death of Joshua, Judah, by the command of God, set out against the Canaanites, taking with him his brother Simeon; and the Lord delivered the Canaanites and Perizzites into their hands, so that they struck down ten thousand men at Bezek, where they found Adoni-Bezek <note>His name literally means "Lord of Bezek."</note>. They took the field against him and overcame him, cutting off the thumbs of his hands and the toes of his feet. Then Adoni-Bezek said: "Seventy kings, with the thumbs of their hands and the toes of their feet cut off, gathered crumbs under my table; thus has God repaid me." Thus he spoke of his well-deserved punishment, which always follows at the heels of the guilty; for evil never remains unpunished. Moreover, the Judge of the whole Earth <note>A reference to God as the ultimate arbiter of justice.</note> often maintains this order: that He repays like with like, and punishes in such a way that the guilty party is forced by the punishment itself to acknowledge His omniscience and justice.

# JUDGES I.

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a biblical scene from the Book of Judges. In the foreground, several soldiers in classical armor and a leader stand beside a massive fluted column. In the middle ground, on a raised stone platform or city battlement, two figures are engaged in an act of punishment; one man is being held while another uses an axe or blade to cut his digits. A crowd of soldiers with spears and shields observes from below. A building with a flag is visible in the background. The image is framed with a thin border and includes the number 59 in the upper right corner.</image-desc>

->The Punishment of Adoni-Bezek.<-

AFTER the death of Joshua, Judah set out, by the command of God, against the Canaanites, taking with him Simeon his brother; and the Lord delivered the Canaanites and Perizzites into their hand, so that they struck down ten thousand men at Bezek, where they found Adoni-Bezek. They took the field against him and defeated him, cutting off the thumbs of his hands and the toes of his feet. Then Adoni-Bezek said: "Seventy kings, with the thumbs of their hands and the toes of their feet cut off, gathered crumbs under my table; thus has God repaid me." In saying this, he spoke of his well-deserved punishment, which always follows at the heels of the guilty; for evil never goes unpunished. Furthermore, the Judge of the whole Earth <note>A biblical title for God, emphasizing His role as the ultimate arbiter of justice.</note> often maintains this order: that He repays like with like, and punishes in such a way that the guilty party is forced by the punishment itself to acknowledge His omniscience and justice.

<sig>H 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: Ad</meta>

<summary>Following Joshua's death, the tribes of Judah and Simeon defeat the Canaanites and capture King Adoni-Bezek. The king confesses that his mutilation is a divine retribution for his own cruelty toward seventy other kings.</summary>
<keywords>Adoni-Bezek, Judges, Judah, Simeon, divine justice, Bezek, Canaanites, law of retaliation, retribution</keywords>

### Judges 4:60

->Jael defeats Sisera.<-

When the Judge <term>Regter|A 'Judge' in this era was not a legal official in a court, but a charismatic military and spiritual leader of the Israelite tribes</term> Ehud had died, and Israel—on account of their sins—was plagued by God through Sisera (the General of King Jabin), the children of Israel humbled themselves before the Lord. He raised up for them the Prophetess Deborah, who went out with Barak to meet Sisera and defeated the entire army; so that the General Sisera fled on foot to the tent of Jael. She received him under the guise of friendship and covered him with a blanket in her tent. When he was overcome by a deep sleep—whether from the drink she had given him or from exhaustion—she took a tent nail <note>original: *Nagel*; a large wooden or metal tent peg</note> and a hammer in her hand, and drove the nail into the temple of his head, so that it was fastened into the ground. Thus she showed the slain Sisera to Barak, whom she went out to meet. God often has the habit of performing the greatest and most significant things through weak and minor means, so that it may be shown that His power is made perfect in our weakness. <note>This is a reference to the biblical principle found in 2 Corinthians 12:9: "My power is made perfect in weakness."</note>

<meta>catchword: While [original Latin: Dum]</meta>

The text emphasizes the theological theme that God achieves great victories through those considered weak or humble.</summary>

# God's admirable callings.
<note>original Latin: *Vocationes Dei admirabiles*</note>

While you strip Ceres <note>original: *Cererem*; the Roman goddess of agriculture, used here as a poetic metonym for grain</note> of her husks, while you beat the ears of corn,
   The Angel calls you, Gideon, to the sword.
You receive the command; soon the fire flashing from the
   rock devours the wet meat, and makes the spoken words certain.

That he should be a redeemer for the poor people;
The fire from the stone consumes his food offering.

# JUDGES VI. 61

<image-desc>An engraved illustration depicting a scene from the Book of Judges. On the left, Gideon stands in a dynamic, surprised pose near a tree. In the center, a fire blazes atop a rock which serves as an altar. From the right, a winged angel descends through the air, touching the fire with the tip of a long staff. The background features a rolling landscape with mountains and two small figures in the distance. Below the illustration, within the frame, is the caption "Gideons Offer."</image-desc>

->Gideon's Sacrifice.<-

The Israelites, having been delivered from the hand of Jabin by the prophetess Deborah, sin again against the Lord, and are therefore plagued by the Midianites; but the children of Israel, repenting of their sins through the remembrance of God’s benefits and their own unfaithfulness, lead God to send an angel to Gideon, the son of Joash, who was standing in his barn <note>The text uses the word *Schure* (barn/granary), though the biblical account in Judges 6:11 specifies he was threshing wheat in a winepress to hide it from the Midianites.</note> threshing wheat. This messenger set apart by the Lord <note>original Dutch: *Afgezonderde des Heeren*; this refers to the Angel as a consecrated messenger</note> admonished him to deliver Israel from the hand of the Midianites; yet he considered himself far too humble to begin so great a work; for which reason the angel performed a sign, lighting with his staff the sacrifice that lay there upon a stone. Through this, Gideon gained so much courage that he tore down the altar of Baal and built an altar to the Lord. To God it is all the same whether He helps through little power or great, for there is no one in the world powerful—much less capable of achieving something great—unless they are empowered by being called by God for that purpose.

# JUDGES VII
### A Representation of the Gracious Calling of God <note>original Latin: *Typus gratuitæ vocationis Dei*. In biblical study, a "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament seen as a "foreshadowing" of something in the New Testament—in this case, God's choosing of the few or the unlikely.</note>

When the chosen company of people was too numerous, they were led to the river and ordered to quench their thirst: Gideon, the leader, chose for fierce battle those whom he saw lapping the water with their mouths and tongues like puppies. <note>original Latin: *Cum populi delecta cohors...*</note>

->*Gideon Chose his Men-at-Arms.*<-

God gave Gideon an express command to choose from the entire Hebrew host only those who lapped the water as dogs do at the springs, in order to conquer Midian and their vain schemes. <note>original French: *Gedeon Choisoit ses Gendarmes...*</note>

->Gideon reduces his warriors to three hundred men.<-

He who laps up the water with his tongue like a dog binds himself anew to the covenant of war. The commander must prune back the size of the army; one always wins when God chooses to intervene in the struggle. <note>original Dutch: *Wie 't water met zyn tong opflabbert...*</note>

->*A type of God’s gracious call of the Gentiles.*<-

Lest Gideon's men, upon overcoming, should say
That it was their number, not God, who won the day:
Such went to fight as lapped water like dogs,
While those who knelt to drink stayed behind, being greater in number.
<note>This verse interprets the event as a "calling of the Gentiles," suggesting that just as God chose the "unlikely" soldiers who lapped water, He would later call the non-Jewish nations (Gentiles) into His covenant.</note>

->Gideon chooses only three hundred men for the battle.<-

From the people, Gideon chose three hundred men who stretched themselves upon the earth and lapped the water like dogs with their tongues (because there were too many of the others); with these he marched against the enemy and obtained a glorious victory. <note>original German: *Vom Volk drey hundert Man...*</note>

<meta>catchword: Gideon,</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the biblical account from Judges 7 where Gideon reduces his army to 300 men based on how they drink water, serving as a theological "type" for God's sovereign choice and the calling of the Gentiles.</summary>
<keywords>Gideon, Midianites, Judges 7, water test, three hundred men, divine providence, typology, Gentiles, vocation</keywords>

->Gideon tests his People.<-

Gideon, strengthened by two miraculous signs <note>This refers to the "signs of the fleece" from Judges 6, where Gideon asked God to make wool wet while the ground stayed dry, and then vice-versa, to confirm his calling.</note>, takes to the field against the Midianites and camps at the Spring of Harod: then God, wishing to prove that He alone would help them, without human strength in which human ingenuity <note>The original Dutch *vernuft* suggests human cleverness or self-reliance.</note> might take comfort, commanded Gideon first to send away all those who, out of fear, preferred to return to their houses; this number amounted to twenty-two thousand. After that, he was to lead the other ten thousand—being the remainder of his people—to the water, in order to observe and select there, by their manner of drinking, those persons (and as many as God had chosen) to carry out this great work of deliverance: those who scooped with their hand and lapped from it were set apart by Gideon, at God’s command; their number was three hundred. The rest, having drunk at their ease upon their knees, were let go.

### Abimelech dies by the throw of a millstone.

->Abimelech intends to set the Tower on fire.<-
<note>The original Dutch text uses "Toorn" (Wrath), which is a common archaic spelling or printer's error for "Toren" (Tower), especially given the context of "setting it on fire."</note>

**A**fter the death of Gideon, Abimelech, the son of Gideon, persuaded his friends to choose him as King; but having been established as King, he behaved godlessly in his office, for he murdered his seventy brothers upon a stone, except for Jotham the youngest, who had hidden himself. After he had ruled over Israel for three years, a great conflict arose between him and the **Shechemites**, whom he defeated, as well as **Thebez** <note>The text refers to the city as "Thebes," known in modern English Bibles as Thebez.</note>, as far as the city was concerned; but while there stood a strong **Tower** or Castle in the middle of the city, most of the people had fled there to halt the enemy's pride. Yet as Abimelech intended to set the Tower of the people ablaze in fire and flame, a woman from above threw a piece of a **millstone**, smashing his **skull** <note>original Dutch: "Herssen-panne," literally brain-pan.</note> to pieces, upon which he was then stabbed by his armor-bearer, at his own request. In this way, God returned the evil that he had done to his father's house by killing his seventy brothers.

<header>A Rash Vow. <note>original Latin: "Votum temerarium."</note></header>

Why, alas, Jephthah, do you act as a cruel tyrant instead of a father,
While you vow to sacrifice your innocent daughter?
A fitting victim should be slain at the Lord's altars:
Those who are wise take care to avoid fulfilling impious vows.
<note>This introductory Latin poem criticizes Jephthah’s rigidity, suggesting that a wise man knows when a promise is too sinful to keep.</note>

### *A Rash Vow.*

O Jephthah! how unwise, was it truly necessary,
To swear such a vow upon defeating your adversary?
That you would offer up whatever first crossed the threshold of your house?
No: a vow made without reason is a false one.

### Jephthah, returning from battle, is met by his only daughter.

Alas! Alas! Innocent maiden, what is it you wish to welcome?
Your father, returning from the battle with joyful songs of victory?
You run to meet your death. O daughter! Stay within the court:
A rash promise stains the glory of the laurel crown.
<note>The "laurel-lot" refers to the "laurier-krans" or laurel wreath, a symbol of victory that is here tainted by the tragic consequence of the vow.</note>

### *The rash vow.*

Jephthah doth vow to God if he o'ercome
To sacrifice the first he meets at home:
His only Daughter comes to welcome him
With mirth and joy, whom he makes a victim.

### Jephthah’s daughter goes out to meet him.

Jephthah vowed to slaughter as a burnt offering
Whatever first came to meet him: his daughter came full of joy,
With kettledrums and song; that brought him heartache.
Before one makes a vow, one should first consider it.

<meta>catchword: Jephthah</meta>

<summary>This page recounts the biblical tragedy of Jephthah from the Book of Judges, specifically his "rash vow" to sacrifice the first thing he saw upon returning home, which turned out to be his only daughter. The text provides poetic reflections on the event in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German, all emphasizing the moral lesson that a vow made without foresight can lead to ruin.</summary>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>JUDGES XI. 64 <note>original Latin: "Judicum"</note></header>
<sig>I</sig>
<meta>catchword: Farra</meta>

<image-desc>A copperplate engraving depicting the biblical scene of Jephthah's return. On the left, a group of women emerges from a classical building with large pillars; the foremost woman, Jephthah's daughter, reaches out to greet him. Behind her, women play tambourines and other instruments. On the right, Jephthah, dressed in ornate military armor and a feathered helmet, recoils in grief, clutching his garments. ->*Jephthah is met by his Daughter.*<-

Jephthah, having been chosen as Military Leader against the Ammonites, sends Messengers to the King of Ammon, who indicated what borders had been taken from him by the Israelites; which Jephthah justifies and answers, but in vain: therefore he takes the field against the Ammonites. But before he begins, he makes an unthinking promise <note>The "rash vow" mentioned on the previous page.</note> to God, that in case of victory, he would sacrifice as a Burnt-offering <term>Burnt-offering: a religious sacrifice where the offering is completely consumed by fire.</term> whatever should first meet him from his House. When he had now conquered the Ammonites, he is met by his only Daughter, who welcomes her Father with drums and dances <note>original: "Trommelen en Reyen." This refers to the traditional celebratory processions of women in the Old Testament.</note>, and is the first to receive him; Jephthah, becoming aware of her and mindful of his promise, tears his clothes out of dejection, bound to an oath that would exchange all his triumph for mourning. Which teaches us that we must be more thoughtful in our promises, so that the same do not turn our happiness into distress.

# Divine Victory <note>original Latin: "Victoria divina"</note>

**The grain** <note>original Latin: "Farra," referring to emmer or spelt</note> of the Philistines burns from the foxes sent in,
  Then, the raging Samson kills a thousand bodies;
Not with a sword, but with the jawbone of a slow donkey,
  From a split tooth a spring leaps; he slakes his thirst.
<note>Translated from the original Latin verse.</note>

*Samson kills a thousand Philistines with the jawbone of an ass, and*
  *brings water out of it to quench his thirst.*
<note>Translated from the original French summary.</note>

**FROM** the weakest instrument God produces a great effect:
  *A thousand men struck down with the jawbone of an ass,*
*And water coming from one of its teeth as desired*
*Show that God often uses the smallest means.*
<note>Translated from the original French verse.</note>

->Samson defeats a thousand Philistines with an Ass’s<-
->Jawbone.<-

**When** Samson, hot for revenge, is inflamed with rage,
  Then he drives the flaming fire and Foxes through the Grain.
A Jawbone, with which he overcame his Enemy,
  Serves him now as a Sword and then as a cool Spring.
<note>Translated from the original Dutch verse.</note>

->*The victory of the living over the dead.*<-

**Samson** burns with fire tied to Fox-tails
  *The Philistines' corn-fields on every side,*
With an Ass-Jawbone he kills a thousand,
  *And drinks the water which distills from it.*
<note>This section was originally in Early Modern English; spelling has been modernized for accessibility.</note>

->Samson slays a thousand Philistines.<-

**INTO** the Philistines' grain Samson lets fire-foxes run,
  Afterwards he slays the enemy, a thousand men in a heap,
An Ass’s jawbone was his sword, a spring that gushes
  From a split tooth, the hero stills his thirst there.
<note>Translated from the original German verse.</note>

# Divine Victory <note>original Latin: "Victoria divina"</note>

**The grain** <note>original Latin: "Farra," referring to emmer or spelt</note> of the Philistines burns from the foxes sent in,
  Then, the raging Samson kills a thousand bodies;
Not with a sword, but with the jawbone of a slow donkey,
  From a split tooth a spring leaps; he slakes his thirst.
<note>Translated from the original Latin verse.</note>

*Samson kills a thousand Philistines with the jawbone of an ass, and*
  *brings water out of it to quench his thirst.*
<note>Translated from the original French summary.</note>

**FROM** the weakest instrument God produces a great effect:
  *A thousand men struck down with the jawbone of an ass,*
*And water coming from one of its teeth as desired*
*Show that God often uses the smallest means.*
<note>Translated from the original French verse.</note>

->Samson defeats a thousand Philistines with an Ass’s<-
->Jawbone.<-

**When** Samson, hot for revenge, is inflamed with rage,
  Then he drives the flaming fire and Foxes through the Grain.
A Jawbone, with which he overcame his Enemy,
  Serves him now as a Sword and then as a cool Spring.
<note>Translated from the original Dutch verse.</note>

->*The victory of the living over the dead.*<-

**Samson** burns with fire tied to Fox-tails
  *The Philistines' corn-fields on every side,*
With an Ass-Jawbone he kills a thousand,
  *And drinks the water which distills from it.*
<note>This section was originally in Early Modern English; spelling has been modernized for accessibility.</note>

->Samson slays a thousand Philistines.<-

**SAMSON**, having killed a young lion and found a swarm of bees within its remains, created a riddle from this at the wedding of his wife, whom he had married from among the Philistines against his parents' wishes. This marriage seemed to be a beginning of friendship with those peoples, but it quickly turned into open enmity; for when the Philistines took his wife from him, he, to avenge this, sent three hundred foxes with burning tails into their grain. Because of this, the Philistines burned Samson’s wife and father-in-law alive, and eventually took Samson bound into their hands. But breaking his bonds, he took up an ass’s <term>jawbone</term> <note>often called a "kinnebakken" in older Dutch, referring to the mandible</note> and slew a thousand Philistine men. Being exhausted and thirsty after this, he obtained a spring from God through prayer, drank from it, and was restored.

**INTO** the Philistines' grain Samson lets fire-foxes run,
  Afterwards he slays the enemy with the bone of an ass.

# JUDGES 19 <note>original Latin: "JUDICUM XIX."</note>
<meta>66</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a dramatic scene from the Book of Judges. In the foreground, a man wearing a turban and a billowing cloak stands in a doorway, raising his hands in a gesture of grief or shock as he looks down at a woman lying motionless on the stone steps. ->The deed committed against the Levite’s concubine.<-

A certain Levite <term>Levite: a member of the Hebrew tribe of Levi, who performed religious duties</term>, living as a foreigner, took for himself a concubine <note>original Dutch: "By-wyf," referring to a secondary wife or woman of lower social status than a primary wife</note> from Bethlehem in Judah. However, when she did not behave well toward her husband, she returned to her father's house. Seeing this, her husband went to her home and was well received there; after staying for several days, he set out with his wife to return home. When he came to Gibeah, which belongs to the tribe of Benjamin, he stayed there for the night, but was assaulted by the men of that city. In the end, he surrendered his concubine to them, and they mistreated her so shamefully that he found her lying dead before the door in the morning. Binding her to his donkey and returning home, he cut her into twelve pieces and sent them to the twelve tribes of Israel. They were so displeased by this that they eventually marched against Benjamin to avenge the deed. After the third battle, the Benjaminites were completely defeated, their city of Gibeah was burned, and every living thing was terribly put to death, except for six hundred men who narrowly escaped.

### A Type of the Calling of the Gentiles in Ruth <note>original Latin: "Typus vocationis Gentium in Rutha." In theology, a "type" is a historical figure or event believed to foreshadow a future reality, such as the inclusion of non-Jews in the Christian faith.</note>

# RUTH II.

I 4

<page-num>135</page-num>
<page-num>67</page-num>

<image-desc>An engraving within a decorative border illustrating a scene from the Book of Ruth. In the foreground, Ruth is kneeling on the ground, gathering stalks of grain. To the left stands Boaz, dressed in a turban and robes, leaning on a staff and looking toward her. Behind him, a male laborer carries a large bundle of wheat on his shoulders. ->Ruth gathers grain in Boaz’s field.<-

Ruth, the daughter-in-law of Naomi (being a widow left behind by death), goes out with the permission of her mother-in-law to the field to glean ears of grain <note>Gleaning was the practice of allowing the poor to gather the stalks of grain left behind by the primary harvesters.</note>, specifically in the field of Boaz, who was a close kinsman <term>kinsman: original Dutch "Bloed-vriend" (blood-friend), referring to a relative with legal and protective obligations to the family</term> of Naomi. Boaz of Bethlehem, arriving at his field and seeing this woman, asks the young man who was set over the reapers who she was; and having understood that she was the young Moabite woman who had come down with Naomi from the fields of Moab, he speaks to her kindly and comfortingly. He gives the servants a generous command on her behalf, so that she could gather as much as she desired; he also had her sit down with the reapers, giving her food until she was satisfied, and commanded his young men that they should not reproach or shame her while she was gleaning. In this we see God’s Providence and Faithfulness in rewarding those who, for His sake, leave the world and all that is in it; and His Mercy in receiving into His grace those who were formerly outside the Covenant <term>Covenant: the sacred agreement and relationship between God and His chosen people</term>.

> Saul is anointed as the first King of the Hebrews, though he was of humble strength <note>original Latin: "impar Viribus"</note>; Samuel teaches him the royal laws, and everything that the Prophet had predicted comes to pass for the man; thus the establishment of the kingdom was made certain. <note>Translated from the original Latin verse.</note>

### Saul, the first King of the Hebrews.

> The poorly-taught people cry out loudly, "Let us have, let us have a King; let his command be a holy Edict for us." To this end, Samuel anoints Saul, the son of Kish <note>original: "Kis"</note>, by a divine destiny. <note>Translated from the original French verse.</note>

### Samuel anoints Saul as King over Israel.

> The brave Son of Kish, why does he search for the donkey? <note>Referring to the biblical narrative where Saul was searching for his father’s lost donkeys when he encountered Samuel.</note> Why does he worry over the cares of his father's household? Let the care of the Realm be his burden; may God give His blessing to this. The Mountain upon which he has climbed is high, but the peak is narrow. <note>Translated from the original Dutch verse. > To prove that his election's from above.

# 137
### I, SAMUEL X. 68

<image-desc>A detailed copperplate engraving illustrating a scene from the Old Testament. ->Saul Anointed as King.<-

Samuel having grown old, he appointed his two sons as Judges over Israel, who did not conduct themselves well in their office <note>The biblical text in 1 Samuel 8 notes that Samuel's sons, Joel and Abijah, turned aside after lucre and took bribes.</note>, whereupon the People cried out for a King. This displeased Samuel; however, because they persisted constantly, God commanded Samuel to grant the People their desire, which was then done, and in this manner: Saul, sent out by his father Kish <note>original: "Kis"</note> to search for the lost donkeys <note>The original Dutch "ezelinne" refers specifically to female donkeys.</note>, and not finding them, traveled through the whole land. By the advice of his servant, he went to Samuel to seek the counsel of such a great Prophet in this difficulty. At the feast (to which he was invited), Samuel told him that the donkeys had already been found and that he would become King; and after the feast, rising early in the morning, Samuel anointed Saul as King over Israel and foretold what he would encounter on the way. It is from God alone, then, that all power and capability for this must descend.

# Disobedience, the Greatest of Sins.

Saul, commanded to destroy the Amalekites, spared
The fat herds and the wool-bearing flock.
Samuel rebukes him; then, filled with holy zeal, 
The prophet seizes Agag and cuts him down with a sword.
<note>original Latin: "Saul Amalekitas jussus delere, pepercit / Pinguibus armentis, lanigeroque gregi..." This verse references the biblical account in 1 Samuel 15, where Saul fails to follow the "ban" or total destruction of the enemy.</note>

---

->*Disobedience, the greatest sin.*<-

Saul, who was supposed to destroy the Amalekites,
Weakly fulfilled the duty he was commanded:
For which he is rejected: and Agag, by a blade,
Is cut into pieces, losing both his life and his soul.

->tes, into pieces.<-

An obedient heart is worth more to God than sacrificial flames,
Even if one spares Amalek and his fattened rams

But praises the willing heart and the unstained soul.

# I, SAMUEL XV.
## 69

->King Agag put to death.<-

After the Philistines were defeated, Samuel (by the command of God) ordered Saul to root out the Amalekites, both men and women; which Saul, having mustered his people, carried out, slaying all that lived from Havila to Shur, except for Agag along with the best of the sheep and cattle, which he did not kill. This was evil in the eyes of the Lord. Because of this, God sent Samuel to him, who rebukes him quite harshly for his disobedience, and proclaims that he shall be cast out from the kingdom for his ungratefulness and disobedience. A little while later, Samuel hews Agag into pieces before the face of the Lord at Gilgal: so that the curse of God over Amalek, spoken by Moses, would be fulfilled; showing that obedience is better than sacrifice, and heedfulness <note>The original Dutch *Opmerking* implies "paying attention" or "hearkening" to God's word.</note> than the fat of rams, just as, on the contrary, rebelliousness is a sin like witchcraft, idolatry, and the worship of images. <note>This passage paraphrases the famous biblical rebuke in 1 Samuel 15:22-23, establishing the moral lesson that ritual sacrifice cannot compensate for a lack of genuine obedience.</note>

<term>Amalekites</term> — A nomadic tribe identified in the Bible as a persistent enemy of the Israelites, against whom God declared a perpetual war.
<term>Gilgal</term> — An important ancient Israelite sanctuary and assembly point where Saul was originally confirmed as king.
<term>Beelden-dienst</term> — Literally "image-service," referring to the worship of physical idols, which was strictly forbidden in Hebrew law.

<summary>This page narrates the downfall of King Saul following his failure to fully execute the divine command against the Amalekites. It concludes with the prophet Samuel executing King Agag and declaring that obedience to God is superior to ritual sacrifice.</summary>
<keywords>Saul, Samuel, King Agag, Amalekites, Gilgal, disobedience, sacrifice, idolatry, Havila, Shur</keywords>

# I. SAMUEL XVII.
## 140

->The Victory of David over Goliath, a type of Christ. <note>original Latin: "Victoria Davidis de Goliatho, typus Christi."</note><-

Goliath, of giant mass, speaking dire things,
David sends a stone from his sling into his face.
Then the Hero, son of Jesse, <note>original: "Jessëius Heros"</note> cuts off the massive head,
While the enemies flee, the Hebrew troop slaughters them.

->The Victory of David over Goliath. <note>original French: "La Victoire de David sur Goliath."</note><-

*The frightful Cyclops <note>The French text uses the term "Cyclope," likely as a poetic synonym for a monstrous giant, comparing the Philistine to the one-eyed giants of Greek myth.</note> feeling the sling*
*At his forehead, falls dead by a round missile:* <note>original: "fleche ronde," literally "round arrow."</note>
*Then the holy Warrior approaching, with the giant’s own iron* <note>meaning Goliath's own sword.</note>
*Sends his head to the ground & his soul into hell.*

->David defeats the Giant Goliath of Gath. <note>original Dutch: "David verslaat den Reuse Goliath van Gath."</note><-

The earth thunders with terror at the dreadful crashing down
Of the clumsy Goliath, struck by David’s stone:
Here I see in spirit a mightier power,
And a stronger tyrant <note>A reference to Satan or Sin, whom Christ defeats just as David defeated the giant.</note> struck down by Jesus.

->David's victory over Goliath, a type of Christ.<-

Goliath challengeth the Hebrew band,
And askes who dares his single force withstand:
David a shepherd-stripling entertains
The fight, and with a stone flings out his brains.

->David slays the Giant Goliath. <note>original German: "David erschlägt den Riesz Goliath."</note><-

When Goliath the giant spoke scorn to Israel,
David stepped onto the field to avenge such things upon him;
He threw a stone at his head so that he fell down dead,
The people slew many enemies, and obtained a beautiful victory.

<term>Typology</term> — A traditional way of interpreting the Bible where people and events in the Old Testament (the "type") are seen as foreshadowing Jesus Christ or the Church in the New Testament (the "antitype").
<term>Son of Jesse</term> — A common biblical title for David, emphasizing his lineage from the tribe of Judah.
<term>Gath</term> — One of the five major cities of the Philistines and the hometown of Goliath.

<summary>This page presents five poetic interpretations of David’s victory over Goliath in various languages, framing the battle as a prophetic symbol of Christ’s victory over the devil. The imagery emphasizes the contrast between Goliath’s physical might and David’s spiritual strength.</summary>
<keywords>David, Goliath, Christ, Jesus, Typology, Philistines, Sling, Gath, Victory, Giant</keywords>

# I. SAMUEL XVII.
## 141

<image-desc>A copperplate engraving depicting the biblical battle of David and Goliath. ->David defeats the Giant Goliath.<-

AFTER Samuel had hewn King Agag into pieces, and had chosen David, the youngest son of Jesse <note>original: "Isaï"</note> as King, anointing him in the midst of his brothers, Saul—having heard that David was an accomplished player of the harp—summons him to his side. David drives away Saul's evil spirit through his playing, for which Saul loves him greatly and makes him his armor-bearer. Meanwhile, the armies of the Philistines and the Israelites advance against one another, and before they begin the assault, the giant Goliath steps forward from the Philistines and terrifies Israel with his stature, weapons, and proud challenges. David, seeing this, becomes vexed by the pride of the uncircumcised <note>A biblical term used to distinguish the Philistines from the Israelites, often implying they were outside of God's covenant.</note> Goliath and requests to fight against him, which Saul permits. Therefore, David goes unarmed with his staff and sling against Goliath, who curses him; yet David, trusting in God alone, drives a stone with the sling through his head and defeats him, whereupon the Philistines fled.

<vocab>Samuel, King Agag, David, Jesse, Saul, Harp, Philistines, Israelites, Goliath, Sling, God</vocab>

# David’s Victory over Goliath, a Type of Christ.
<note>original Latin: "Victoria Davidis de Goliatho, typus Christi." In biblical interpretation, a "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament that prefigures or foreshadows something in the New Testament, specifically concerning the life of Jesus.</note>

While Goliath, with his massive frame, spoke terrible things,
David sent a flint stone from his sling into the giant’s face.
From there, the Heroic Son of Jesse <note>original: "Jesseïus Heros," referring to David’s father, Jesse.</note> cut off the immense head,
And the Hebrew host slaughtered the enemies as they fled.

### *The Victory of David over Goliath.*
<note>Translated from the French: "La Victoire de David sur Goliath."</note>

The frightful Cyclops <note>original: "Le Cyclope." The author uses this classical Greek mythological term for a one-eyed giant to describe the biblical Goliath.</note> feeling the sling
At his brow, falls dead by a round projectile:
Then the holy Warrior approaching, with the giant’s own blade <note>original: "de son propre fer," literally "with his own iron."</note>
Sends his head to the earth and his soul to hell.

### **David defeats the Giant Goliath of Gath.**
<note>Translated from the Dutch: "David verslaat den Reuse Goliath van Gath."</note>

The earth trembles with terror from the dreadful thud
Of the clumsy Goliath, struck by David’s stone:
Here I see in the spirit <note>original: "in den geest," implying a prophetic or spiritual insight.</note> a mightier power,
And a stronger tyrant struck down by Jesus.

### *David’s victory over Goliath, a type of Christ.*
<note>This section was originally written in 17th-century English.</note>

Goliath challengeth the Hebrew band,
And asks who dares his single force withstand:
David a shepherd-stripling <note>A term for a youth or young man.</note> entertains
The fight, and with a stone flings out his brains.

### **David Slays the Giant Goliath.**
<note>Translated from the German: "David erschlägt den Riesz Goliath."</note>

When Goliath the giant spoke with mockery to Israel,
David stepped onto the field to avenge such scorn;
He threw a stone at his head so that he fell down dead;
The people slew many enemies and obtained a beautiful victory.

Thus, David went unarmed with only his staff and sling against Goliath, who cursed him.
