# The Triumph of Saul and David.

> Having driven out the enemies and secured peace, Saul triumphs,
> David goes as his companion and carries high the trophies:
> The massive head of Goliath <note>original Latin: "Antæi." The author uses a classical allusion, comparing Goliath to the Greek mythological giant Antaeus.</note> and the terrible sword.
> "Victory! O, Victory!" <note>original Latin: "Pæan iô pæan." An ancient Greek and Latin shout of triumph or joy, often associated with the god Apollo.</note> sings the crowd meeting them.

### *The Triumph of Saul and David.*

> All the people are joyful, seeing David as the victor
> Over the fallen monster, and they praise the Lord:
> In the triumph, Goliath’s sword is seen,
> As well as the severed head of this Giant.

### The Women praise David in their song above Saul.

> Defended Israel, in its singing, calls David
> The fist of the Fatherland, the fear of the foreigners:
> Thus his praise rises high on the shouts of joy.
> Thus virtue receives its reward, in the bloom of its youth.

### *The triumph of Saul and David.*

> The Philistines <note>original: "Philistians."</note> being vanquished, Saul returns
> In a grand triumph, and so his camp breaks up:
> David leads the procession before him
> Honored with these spoils; Goliath's sword and head.

### The people cheer for David's victory.

> Saul triumphs magnificently, for he has struck the enemy.
> David carries the Giant’s head and sword before him
> As a sign of his victory; the people celebrate greatly,
> With congratulations and stringed instruments <note>original German: "Seytenſpiel." Literally 'string-play,' referring to harps, lyres, or lutes used in celebration.</note>, much joy is felt.

David, having felled the Great Goliath to the ground, and
having cut off his head with his own sword,
is recognized by Saul, and made a **Commander** <note>original: "Overste." A high-ranking military officer or chief.</note> over his **army** <note>original: "Krygs-volk." Literally "war-people," referring to the gathered Israelite forces.</note>; also Jonathan makes a covenant with him, because

David his ten thousands! by which it was sufficiently evident,
that they gave more honor to David than the King himself. Then
Saul became very incensed, and that word was evil in his eyes, and
he said; they have given David ten thousands, but
to me they have only given thousands, and surely the king-
dom will be for him. And from that time Saul had an **evil eye** <note>A biblical idiom indicating deep-seated jealousy and malicious intent.</note> on David.

# A prudent wife saves her husband's life. <note>original Latin: "Uxor prudens ſervat Marito vitam."</note>

> Nabal, by denying meager food and sustenance to David,
> was to pay the price for his folly with death.

->David meets Abigail.<-

David, being a fugitive from Saul, fled with many distressed men from his father’s house who had gathered to him, and traveled to the wilderness of Paran. There lived a man of great substance, provided with much wealth and an abundance of sheep. David and his people served as guards for this man’s household. In consideration of this, when Nabal was shearing his sheep at Carmel, David very politely requested some assistance for his army from him. However, Nabal very haughtily refused to give food to David’s messengers—provisions which he was nevertheless squandering at that time in excessive revelry <note>original Dutch: "brasseryen," referring to gluttonous feasting or carousing</note>—calling David a servant who had run away from his master. David, incensed by this and considering the favors he had shown to Nabal’s flock, threatened to destroy Nabal along with his entire household. But Abigail, Nabal’s wife, having heard of this, went quickly to meet David and calmed his anger with moving arguments and gifts. When this wise woman returned home, she told her husband what had happened; he was so terrified by the news that he died suddenly, after which David married her.

**D**avid, being a fugitive from Saul, fled with many troubled men from his father's house who had gathered to him, and moved to the wilderness of Paran <note>original: "Pharan"</note>. There lived a prosperous man, provided with great riches and an abundance of sheep. David and his people served as protectors <term>Huiswagters: literally "house-watchers," referring to the security David's armed men provided to Nabal's shepherds against desert raiders</term> to this man. In light of this service, when Nabal was shearing his sheep at Carmel, David very politely requested some assistance for his army. However, Nabal very arrogantly refused to give food to David's messengers—food which he was nevertheless at that very time wasting in abundant revelries <note>original: "brasseryen," referring to gluttonous feasting or carousing</note>—and called David a runaway servant of his master. David, provoked by this and considering the kindnesses he had shown to Nabal's flock, threatened to destroy Nabal along with his entire household. But Abigail, Nabal's wife, having heard of this, went hastily to meet David and stilled his wrath with moving words and gifts. This wise woman, upon returning home, told her husband what had happened; he was so terrified by the news that he died suddenly <note>original: "schielyk," meaning quickly or abruptly</note>, after which David married her.

->David meets Abigail.<-

David, fleeing from Saul, escaped with many distressed men from his father’s house who had gathered to him, and moved to the wilderness of Paran. In that place lived a wealthy man <note>original Dutch: "begaafd Man" — literally "gifted," but used here to mean "well-endowed" with worldly goods</note> provided with many riches and an abundance of sheep. David and his people served as protectors for this man’s household; in consideration of this, when Nabal was shearing his sheep at Carmel, David very politely requested some assistance for his army. <page-num>146</page-num>
<header>The tragic death of tyrants.</header>

While the barbarian enemy engages in battles with the people,
The descendants of Abraham <note>original Latin: "Abramidæ," referring to the Israelites</note>, defeated, turn their backs in flight.
Seeing all is lost, Saul pierces his own open breast,
The armor-bearer, following the example of his Leader, kills himself.

->Saul, seeing himself defeated, kills himself in despair.<-

The unfortunate Saul sees his three sons slain,
His soldiers terrified, the battle lost,
Himself wounded, without help, on the point of being taken:
Then, out of despair, the miserable man kills himself.

->Saul falls on his own sword, as does his armor-bearer.<-

The degenerate <note>original Dutch: "Ontaarde" – implying one who has fallen from his noble nature or lineage</note> son of Kish, after his army fled,
Grudged the Philistines his royal death.
What use was the rich balsam <note>original Dutch: "vette Balzem," referring to the holy anointing oil used to consecrate a king</note> to that kingly crown?
A prince who abandons God is, in turn, abandoned by God.

->The tragical end of Tyrants.<-

The Philistines do charge the Hebrew bands,
Which fly and make more use of heels than hands.
Saul seeing all lost, on his sword's point doth fall:
His squire succeeds in his death tragical.

->Saul falls on his own sword.<-

Israel is divided and beaten by the enemy,
The whole army takes flight with much terror and trembling;
Saul, out of great despair, takes his own life,
His armor-bearer perishes by his own sword.

# I, SAMUEL XXXI.
## K 2
<meta>plate-num: 73, catchword: Fœde-</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving within a rectangular border depicting the death of King Saul. In the foreground, Saul is seen falling onto his sword, which is planted point-upward in the ground. ->Saul falls upon his sword.<-

After David had recovered the plunder <note>original: "den buit," referring to the goods David reclaimed from the Amalekites</note>, he sent gifts from it to his friends. Meanwhile, the Philistines marched against the Israelites and oppressed them greatly, pressing hard upon Saul. Therefore Saul, fearing that he would fall into the hands of the Philistines, spoke to his armor-bearer <term>armor-bearer: a young soldier who carried the king's shield and weapons</term> that he should run him through. But when he refused, Saul drew his own sword and fell upon it, so that he died. Seeing this, his armor-bearer also fell upon his own sword. Saul’s three sons, among whom was Jonathan, were also slain and died in the war. Thus Saul died with his sons; and after that battle, his head was cut off by the Philistines, and his weapons were placed in the temple <note>original: "huis," meaning house or house of worship</note> of Ashtaroth <note>Ashtaroth: a goddess of the Canaanites and Philistines; placing a defeated king's weapons in her temple was a sign of victory</note>. Truly then had David, when he was urged <note>original: "opgerooit," meaning incited or stirred up by his men to take Saul's life in the cave</note> to murder Saul, foretold: that the Lord would strike him, and that he would either perish by a natural death or fall in battle <note>original: "aldaar zou blyven," an idiom meaning to stay there or perish in that place</note>.

# The tragic death of tyrants.
<note>original Latin: "Tyrannorum tragicus interitus"</note>

While the barbarian enemy joins in battle with the people,
The defeated sons of Abraham <note>original: "Abramidæ," a poetic term for the Israelites</note> turn their backs in flight.
Seeing all is lost, Saul pierces his own breast,
And following his leader’s example, the armor-bearer kills himself.

->*Saul, seeing himself defeated, kills himself out of despair.*<-

*The unfortunate Saul sees his three sons murdered,*
*His soldiers terrified, the battle lost,*
*Himself wounded, without help, and on the verge of being captured:*
*Then, in despair, the miserable man kills himself.*

->Saul falls upon his own sword, as does his armor-bearer.<-

The degenerate son of Kish <note>original: "Zoon van Kis"; Saul was the son of Kish from the tribe of Benjamin</note>, after his army fled,
Envies the Philistines a royal death.
How could the anointing oil <note>original: "vette Balzem," referring to the sacred oil used to crown him king</note> benefit that king’s crown?
A prince who forsakes God is, in turn, forsaken by God.

->*The tragic end of tyrants.*<-

*The Philistines charge the Hebrew bands,*
*Who fly and make more use of heels than hands.*
*Saul, seeing all lost, on his sword’s point does fall:*
*His squire succeeds in his death tragical.*

->Saul falls upon his own sword.<-

Israel is divided and beaten by the enemy,
The entire army takes flight with great fear and trembling;
Saul, out of deep despair, takes his own life,
His armor-bearer perishes by his own sword.

### 1 SAMUEL 31

<image-desc>An etched illustration set within a rectangular border. In the foreground of a rocky landscape, King Saul is depicted in the act of committing suicide by falling onto his sword, which is upright on the ground. ->Saul falls upon his sword.<-

---

After David had recovered the plunder <note>referring to the spoils recovered from the Amalekites in the previous chapter</note>, he sent gifts from it to his friends; meanwhile, the Philistines march against the Israelites and oppress them greatly, pressing hard upon Saul. Therefore Saul, fearing that he would fall into the hands of the Philistines, spoke to his **armor-bearer** <term>armor-bearer|A servant or soldier responsible for carrying a commander's heavy equipment and acting as a personal guard.</term> so that he might run him through; but when he refused, he drew his own sword and fell upon it, so that he died. Seeing this, his armor-bearer likewise fell upon his own sword. Saul’s three sons, among whom was Jonathan, were also slain and died in the war. Thus Saul died with his sons; and after that battle, his head was cut off by the Philistines, and his weapons were placed in the temple <note>original: "huis" (house)</note> of **Ashtaroth** <term>Ashtaroth|A Canaanite goddess; her "house" refers to her pagan temple in the city of Beth-shan.</term>. Truly then David had foreseen the truth, when he was incited to murder Saul: that the Lord would strike him, and that he would either perish by a natural death, or that, entering into battle, he would perish there <note>David’s prophecy is found in 1 Samuel 26:10, where he refuses to kill the sleeping Saul, trusting God to determine the king's end.</note>.

# Uza the meddler <note>original Latin/Greek: "Uza πολυπραγμων" (polypragmōn), meaning one who meddles in affairs that are not his own</note> is struck by God.

The Ark of God's Covenant is brought forth; while fearing it might fall,
Uza supports it with an extended hand,
He whom God himself kills, being jealous of His own honor.
The King of those above hates meddling <note>original Greek: "τὴν πολυπραγμοσύνην" (tēn polypragmosynēn), the quality of being a busybody or interfering</note>.

->*Uza is killed.*<-

David in triumph, all joyfully rejoices,
Leading the holy, sacred Ark to its proper place;
But Uza, touching it because he feared it might fall,
Found his mortuary tomb right beside the Ark.

### Uza, taking hold of the Ark of the Covenant, is killed by God.

O Uza! Did you think that God would take no care,
To guard his **Covenant-chest** <term>Covenant-chest|The Ark of the Covenant, the sacred wooden chest containing the stone tablets of the Ten Commandments.</term> on the wagon with His own hand?
Thus punishment is his reward; he who, acting outside of his office and duty,
Instructs the unhallowed world in matters of religion <note>This suggests that Uza’s sin was not just physical contact, but the presumption that he needed to "save" God's sacred object without being authorized as a priest.</note>.

### Uza meddling with another’s office is struck by God.

David in triumph leads the Ark away;
Uza, lest it should fall, hand on it doth lay:
Whom God doth punish for his insolence
With death; to touch it was such an offense.

### Uza is punished with death.

When the Ark of the Covenant tilted as if it would fall,
Uza stretched out his hand, wanting to lift and hold it;
For this transgression <note>original German: "Frevel" (outrage or sacrilege)</note> he is robbed of his life,
Let everyone do only what is commanded and permitted to them.

# II SAMUEL VI.

**David**, hearing of the death of **Saul**, mourns for him, and shortly thereafter is anointed **King** over **Judah**, as well as over **Israel**, and twice defeats the **Philistines**. Having defeated the **Philistines**, he brings the **Ark** of the **Covenant** again from the house of **Abinadab** with many **people** and great joy, playing before it with **harps**, **lutes**, and all manner of other **stringed instruments**. When they came to **Nacon's Threshing Floor** <note>The Dutch source uses "Nachors Dorsvloer." Standard English Bibles usually identify this location as the threshing floor of Nacon or Chidon.</note>, the **oxen** pulling the **Ark** stumbled. **Uzzah**, fearing for it, reached out his hand to steady it; then the anger of the **Lord** was kindled against **Uzzah**, and **God** struck him for his **rashness**, so that he fell down dead beside the **Ark**. By this, He makes it known that in His sacred things, He has no need of human help (so long as it is not commanded), especially not from those whose duty it is not. However, **David**, being grieved by this, leaves the **Ark** to be placed in the house of **Obed-Edom**.

# The great errors of great men <note>original Latin: "Magnorum virorum magni errores"</note>

While her husband is away, Bathsheba enters the flowing waters,
Washing her limbs, which are whiter than Scythian snow.
The King sees her; having seen her, he desires her, and he possesses what he desired;
David defiles his previous deeds with adultery.
<note>original Latin: "Bathſeba dum vir abeſt, latices ingreſſa liquentes..." This verse highlights the contrast between David’s former righteousness and this sudden fall from grace.</note>

->*David commits adultery with Bathsheba.*<-

*Beautiful Bathsheba! You take no care,*
*That while washing your body your Prince watches you,*
*He covets you, wants you, and aspires, all pensive,*
*Only to the act and the deed of his lustful love.*

->David falls in love with Bathsheba, and sends messengers to<-
->fetch her.<-

O man after Heaven’s heart! Does that fair skin entice you
Through mad lust, toward murder and shameful adultery?
To what depths does virtue fall, alas! What does this loving breed—
A senseless worry within careless minds?
<note>The Dutch "zinnelooze zorg, in zorgelooze zinnen" is a poetic play on words, contrasting the irrationality of David's obsession with his previously untroubled or reckless state of mind.</note>

*And from her clothing her fair limbs she unwraps.*
*David sees her, and scorched with unjust flames,*
*Defiles her marriage-bed with his base lust.*

->David commits adultery with Bathsheba.<-

David sees her body, and thereafter falls in love with her;
He summons her to him, and commits adultery with her,

## 2 SAMUEL 11

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a biblical scene. On the left, King David stands on a palace balcony looking down into a garden. In the foreground, Bathsheba is seated at a fountain while being attended by a maidservant. The background features a large classical building with a dome and manicured trees behind a stone balustrade.</image-desc>

->David falls in love with Bathsheba.<-

---

While Joab and all Israel, by David’s command, marched against the children of Ammon to besiege the city of Rabbah <note>The capital city of the Ammonites, located in present-day Amman, Jordan.</note>, David rose from his couch toward evening and walked upon the roof of the King’s House. From the roof, he saw a beautiful woman washing herself, whereupon he inquired through his servants who she was. He then had her brought to him; and when she—namely Bathsheba, for that was her name—had come to him, he lay with her. Understanding that she had become pregnant, he summoned her husband Uriah from the army to cover his shame. When this did not succeed <note>Uriah refused to go home to his wife while his comrades were still in the field.</note>, he sent Uriah back to the army with a letter to Joab, whom he commanded to arrange that Uriah perish by the hands of the enemy. To what depths one can fall! The King of Israel, such a holy prophet, while the army is in the field, becomes an <term>adulterer</term> <note>original: "Overspeelder"</note> and a <term>murderer</term> <note>original: "Doodslager"</note>. Go now, whoever you are, and judge from this whether it is not extremely necessary to pray: *Lord, lead us not into temptation, but deliver us from evil.*

<meta>catchword: As (translated from "Ut")</meta>

<meta>This page continues from the previous account of King David’s fall into adultery and murder. Having established David’s guilt on page 151, the narrative now shifts to his confrontation by the prophet Nathan and his subsequent repentance, as recorded in 2 Samuel 12.</meta>

# Nathan denounces the punishment to David.
<note>original: "Nathan Davidi poenam denunciat."</note>

Just as Nathan points out his crimes to the son of Jesse <note>The "son of Jesse" is a traditional title for King David.</note>, David is now ashamed and raises both hands toward the stars. But he wishes in vain to avoid the penalty; punishment pursues the guilty. Yet, happy is he who grieves for his deeds.

### *Nathan reveals his sin to David.*

Nathan the Prophet, full of zeal and faith, boldly reveals the sin committed by King David and the just punishment for it; but by confessing it, he receives full pardon.

### Nathan places David’s sins before his eyes by God’s command.

Although David laments his grave sins with repentance, having been exposed by a parable <note>Nathan told David a story about a rich man stealing a poor man’s only lamb to illustrate David’s theft of Uriah's wife.</note> from Nathan; nonetheless, God still punishes them. Thus, punishment follows the sin: yet blessed is he who was wounded in his heart by it.

### *Nathan denounces David’s sin and his punishment.*
<note>This section was originally written in 17th-century English.</note>

Nathan, by use of a parable, here discovers the heinous fault of fair Bathsheba’s lover; David, with bended knees, prays, sighs, and groans, bemoaning his sin and the pain of Israel with all his heart.

### Nathan sets David’s sin before his eyes.

Nathan approaches David to point out the sin which he committed so secretly with Bathsheba. David pronounces his own death-sentence <note>In the biblical account, before David realizes the story is about him, he declares that the man in Nathan's parable deserves to die.</note> and, with anguish and lamentation before God, has carried the burden of true penance.

<meta>catchword: Na</meta>

II, SAMUEL XII. 76

->Nathan rebukes David.<-

AFTER David had committed those two grave sins of Murder and Adultery, God sends Nathan to David. Nathan skillfully places David’s crime before his eyes through a parable <note>Nathan used a story about a rich man stealing a poor man’s pet lamb to trick David into judging his own behavior without realizing he was the culprit.</note>, namely of the Rich man who had taken the Poor man’s only little Sheep; at which David, inflamed with very great anger, said: "As the Lord lives, the man who has done this is a child of death" <note>This phrase, "Kind des doods," is a Hebraism meaning someone who deserves to die.</note>. Then Nathan said to David: "You are that man; you have caused Uriah the Hittite <note>Uriah was a loyal soldier in David’s army; David orchestrated his death in battle to cover up Bathsheba's pregnancy.</note> to be killed, and have taken his wife to be your wife; therefore the sword shall not depart from your house, because you have despised me," says the Lord. David, deeply moved by this, begs for forgiveness, which is promised to him in God’s Name by the Prophet Nathan, as a proof of God’s infinite mercy, which can take away and blot out the sins of repentant, believing sinners, even if their sins were as red as blood <note>A reference to Isaiah 1:18: "though your sins be as scarlet, they shall be as white as snow."</note>.

### Divine Vengeance <note>original: "Vindicta divina."</note>

The wicked Absalom <note>Absalom was King David's third son; he led a famous rebellion against his father, attempting to seize the throne of Israel.</note> wrests the royal scepter from his father,
And drives the old man from the holy city.
Fleeing, he hangs by his hair from an ancient oak,
While Joab <note>Joab was the commander of King David's army. Despite David's orders to spare Absalom, Joab killed him to end the rebellion.</note> hurls three spears at the ungrateful man.
<note>original Latin: "Impius extorquet Patri regalia sceptra / Absalon, & sancta pellit ab urbe senem. / Crinibus annosa fugiens ex ilice pendet, / Torquet in ingratum spicula trina Joab."</note>

->*Absalom, finding himself hanging by his hair from a tree, is killed by Joab.*<-
<note>original French: "Absalon se trouvant pendu par les cheveux à un arbre est tué par Joab."</note>

To the rebel Absalom the crime is repaid,
And God finally allows, through His just vengeance,
That while remaining in the air, hanging by his hair,
Joab, who pursues him, pierces him with his lance.
<note>original French: "Au rebelle Absalon le forfait est rendu, / Et Dieu permet enfin par sa just' vengeance, / Que demeurant en l'air par les cheveux pendu, / Joab qui le poursuit, le perce de sa lance."</note>

Wretched Absalom! Your hair becomes a noose:
Through the flowing of your wound, Israel learns to stop
The source of the Kingdom's danger. How little the blood takes
The color of honor and virtue, in a dishonorable soul!
<note>original Dutch: "Rampzalige Absalon! uw hayren worden stroppen: / Door 't vloeien van uw wond' weet Israël te stoppen / De bron van 't Ryks-gevaar. Hoe luttel vat het bloed / De verf van eer en deugd, in 't eereloos gemoed!"</note>

Absalom on David's scepter hands doth lay,
And his aged father chaseth quite away,
Flying he hangs by his hair on a tree,
Whom Joab finding kills with lances three.

# II. SAMUEL XVIII.

*An engraving illustrates the biblical story: in the center, Absalom is suspended in the air, his long hair entangled in the branches of a gnarled oak tree. His mule gallops away to the left. In the background, soldiers on horseback approach through the woods.*

->*Absalom hangs by his hair.*<-

Absalom, after he had committed the murder of his brother <note>Amnon, who was killed by Absalom’s servants in revenge for the rape of their sister Tamar.</note>, fled to Geshur, but through the counsel of Joab, he was eventually restored to his father’s favor. However, abusing his father’s kindness, he stirred up the people against his father and made himself King with the help of Ahithophel <note>David’s former counselor who joined the rebellion; he was known for his great wisdom, which David feared.</note>. David, hearing of this, fled from Jerusalem so that Absalom entered the city without any effort. Shortly thereafter, Absalom crossed over the Jordan. When David heard this, he mustered his men and organized for battle, though he did not join the fight himself, giving orders to spare Absalom. As the armies advanced upon each other, Absalom’s army was finally defeated and scattered. While fleeing on a mule, Absalom became caught in a thick oak by his long hair; he remained hanging there and was pierced through by Joab <note>Joab defied David’s explicit command to protect the young man, viewing Absalom’s death as necessary for the stability of the kingdom.</note>, and was subsequently thrown into a pit.

<meta>catchword: What- <note>original: "Quæ-", likely the start of a Latin phrase like "Quærite" or "Quæstio" on the following page.</note></meta>

# Solomon’s Prudent Judgment.

Let the judges Minos, Rhadamanthus, and Aeacus <note>In Greek mythology, these three were the judges of the Underworld, famed for their absolute impartiality.</note> <insert>here fall deeply silent,</insert> for behold, a more righteous judge sits here. Piety and the maternal heart are recognized by their natural affection; thus Solomon cuts through the dispute between the women. <note>original: "Quæſitor Minos, Rhadamantus & Æacus altum / Hic ſileant, Judex juſtior ecce ſedet. / Noſcitur affectu pietas maternaque fibra, / Litigium Salomon ſic muliebre ſecat."</note>

---

### The Prudent Judgment of Solomon.

*King Solomon, with a most fortunate judgment,*
*Decides the famous debate of two harlots:* <note>The term "harlot" is used here as in the King James Bible to refer to the two women who lived in the same house and both claimed the living child.</note>
*"Here," he says, "bring the sharpened knife:*
*And let the living child be divided immediately."*

---

### Solomon’s first verdict over two women, who quarreled over a child.

O Wise Solomon! How your name is adorned
With the laurels of praise and honor! You who have passed the judgment
By which the mother’s heart was moved throughout your kingdom.
Here Nature has carried out the sentence with power.

---

### Solomon's wise Judgment.

*Each of the harlots claims the child that lives,*
*To part it Solomon his sentence gives,*
*One calls for half, "take all" the other cries:*
*The last to be the Mother he decrees.*

---

### Solomon's first judgment over two women.

The wisdom of Solomon clearly reveals itself here,
When one of these women smothered her little child;
Maternal affection could not be hidden,
This commendable wise judgment brings praise to the King.

# I KINGS III.

![An engraving depicting the Judgment of Solomon. King Solomon is seated on a raised throne under a canopy within a classical pillared hall. Two women are positioned at the base of the throne; one kneels with her arms outstretched in an emotional plea, while the other looks on. A soldier stands over them, holding a small child by the leg and preparing to draw a sword. Several onlookers are gathered on either side of the scene. The engraving is framed by a simple black line, with a title beneath it.]

->Solomon's First Judgment.<-

After the passing of his father, Solomon, having been chosen as King, prays to God (who appears to him in a dream) for wisdom, which he receives from God, as well as wealth and honor. He gave a clear demonstration <note>original: "geen duiſter blyk," literally "no obscure proof," meaning a vivid or clear sign</note> of his wisdom in the judgment over two women who were quarreling over a child. They had lived together until then. It happened afterward that one of their sons was smothered <note>original: "dood gelegen," literally "lain dead," referring to the accidental death of an infant when a mother rolls over them in her sleep</note> by the mother, who was then accused by the other that she had swapped her dead son during the night. From this arose a dispute <term>geding: a legal case or lawsuit</term> as to whom this living son belonged. Solomon, commanding that the living child should be divided in two, judged by the stirrings of the heart <note>original: "beweging des gemoeds," referring to the internal emotional compass or maternal instinct</note> which one was the true mother, and gave the child to that woman who, seeing the peril of her son, had pleaded with him: "Oh my Lord! Give her the living child, and do not kill it at all."

<meta>catchword: Inno-</meta>

**Vocabulary:** Solomon, King, wisdom, dispute, mother, Child, I Kings III, Solomon's judgment

# Naboth is Stoned at the Instigation of Jezebel.

> Innocent Naboth loses his estate along with the light <note>original: "cum lumine," a poetic way of saying "along with his life"</note> of life,
> He is crushed by stones, but vengeance follows.
> Will your heaped-up gold profit you, Jezebel,
> If you, foolish woman, forsake the eternal riches of heaven?
<note>Translated from the original Latin: "Innocuus Naboth perdit cum lumine villam..."</note>

->Naboth is stoned by the command of Jezebel.<-

> Are you not yet satisfied, Jezebel, in your great greed?
> Instead, you seize the poor property of Naboth like a barbarian;
> For having him stoned through a false trial,
> The dogs shall devour you and your descendants.
<note>Translated from the original French: "N'es tu encor contente Jesabel trop avare?..."</note>

->Naboth is stoned for his vineyard.<-

> O wicked Jezebel, filled with sin,
> How can you accuse Naboth so, despite his innocence?
> And make him die like a martyr for his vineyard?
> Yes, stoned! How will vengeance yet claim its due for your guilt?
<note>Translated from the original Dutch: "Osnode Jezabel van zonden opgevult..."</note>

->Naboth is stoned to death by Jezebel's command.<-

> Behold how Jezebel poor Naboth doth deprive,
> Both of his Vineyard and his innocent life:
> He's falsely stoned to death: but vengeance fails
> Not upon her that at the Prophet <note>A reference to the prophet Elijah, who confronted Jezebel and King Ahab for this crime.</note> rails.

->Naboth is stoned for his vineyard.<-

> O wicked Jezebel, filled with malice,
> How can you bear witness against Naboth so, despite his innocence?
> And make him die like a martyr for his vineyard?
> Yes, stoned! How shall vengeance yet inherit your debt?
<note>Translated from the original German: "Osnode Jezabel von boosheid auffgefüllt..." The German text mirrors the Dutch version almost exactly.</note>

### 1 KINGS 21.
159 79
<meta>catchword: Fer-</meta>

<image-desc>An etching illustrating the biblical scene of the stoning of Naboth. In the foreground, Naboth is on the ground while several men lift heavy stones to cast at him. A crowd of observers stands behind them, and in the far background, a large fortified palace and city skyline are visible under a bright sky.</image-desc>

->Naboth Stoned.<-

It happened that Naboth in Jezreel had a vineyard, which was situated next to the palace of King Ahab. The King desired this vineyard from Naboth to use as a herb garden <note>original: "Kruit-hof," a kitchen or vegetable garden</note>, promising to give him another in its place, or as much money as it was worth. Naboth refused the King because it was an ancestral inheritance <note>In ancient Israelite law, land was meant to stay within the family tribe</note> from his forefathers. Because of this, the King went to his palace very sullen and angry, and out of spite went to lie in his bed. 

Now when Jezebel the Queen found her husband so mournful, she asked him the cause. Upon understanding that it was because of Naboth's vineyard, she spoke to him, saying that he should be of good cheer, for she would see to it that he possessed Naboth’s vineyard as an inheritance. Thereupon she wrote letters to the city where Naboth lived, and commanded that they should have him falsely accused of having "blessed" <note>original: "gezegend." In the biblical context, "blessing" was often used as a euphemism for cursing or blasphemy to avoid writing the actual word for a curse against God.</note> God and the King, and that they should then have him stoned until he died. This now being accomplished, they made it known to Jezebel, and thus Ahab took possession of Naboth’s vineyard as an inheritance.

<vocab>Naboth, Ahab, Jezebel, Jezreel, Vineyard, King, 1 Kings 21</vocab>

->Elijah’s Ascent to Heaven.<-

**E**lijah, having taken Elisha as his companion, led a sequestered life with him for a long time; but when God the Lord had intended to take Elijah up to Heaven, Elijah sought to be alone in solitude in some place; yet as Elisha knew all this through the Spirit of the Lord, he did not wish to separate himself from Elijah, even though he traveled from one place to another, hither and thither. At last, coming by Jericho to the Jordan, Elijah divided the waters with his <term>Mantle</term><note>A cloak or wrap that symbolized the prophet's authority.</note>, so that they passed through on dry feet; there, a fiery chariot with fiery horses separated these two; for Elijah, sitting upon it, ascended to Heaven. Elisha took up the fallen mantle and again divided the Jordan in the presence of the <term>Sons of the Prophets</term><note>A biblical term referring to a school or guild of prophets who studied under a master like Elijah.</note>, because of which he was recognized by them as a prophet.

# 162
<header>A Pattern of the Bath of Rebirth.</header>
<note>original Latin: "Typus Lavacri Regenerationis." In Christian theology, a "Type" is an Old Testament event or person thought to foreshadow a New Testament reality. Here, Naaman’s cleansing in the river is presented as a "type" of Christian baptism.</note>

<image-desc>A faint, light engraving in the background depicts a biblical scene. A man (Naaman) stands in the water of a river (the Jordan), washing himself. In the background, other figures are standing on the riverbank, and a landscape with distant trees is visible.</image-desc>

Elisha commands Naaman, his limbs burning with <term>leprosy</term><note>original Latin: "Mentagra scabieque." These terms refer to skin diseases or scabs. In the Bible, "leprosy" was a general term for many infectious skin conditions.</note> and sores,
To submerge himself seven times.
He obeys, and having wiped away the leprosy, he emerges renewed from the waves:
He who does what the Prophets command is wise.
<note>Translated from the original Latin verse.</note>

# 164

<header>The Punishment of Idolatry and Cruelty. <note>original Latin: *Pœna Idololatriæ & crudelitatis.*</note></header>

At the command of the new King Jehu, the insolent Jezebel
    Falls, hurled from the window to the ground.
Dogs chew her limbs and her condemned entrails:
    Thus she, stained by the murder of the Prophets, perishes.
<note>Translated from the original Latin verse.</note>

---

->Jezebel is thrown down from a window and devoured by dogs.<-

The infamous Jezebel could not avoid
    The just punishment that crimes attract;
From a window, Jehu has her thrown down,
And starved dogs immediately tear her apart.
<note>Translated from the original French verse.</note>

---

->Jezebel is thrown out the window, and her blood is licked by the dogs.<-

Could Zimri’s <note>Zimri was a previous usurper who died in a palace fire; Jezebel mockingly addressed Jehu by this name just before her death.</note> death not serve as a lesson to you?
    O wanton Jezebel! You plunge down from above:
You sought through your finery to prevent your fate;
    But oh! cruelty grins all the more through the makeup. <term>blanketten</term> <note>The Dutch *blanketten* refers to the white lead-based makeup used in the 17th century; the text suggests her cosmetic "mask" only made her cruel nature more visible.</note>

---

    And cast from of her castle window down.
Her body dogs devour; so God repays
    His Prophets' murder, and her Idol-ways.

<header>Ahaz sacrifices his sons to idols. <note>original Latin: *Achaz filios idolis immolat.*</note></header>

God clouds the heart of the unjust with dark mists,
And headlong he falls into his own destruction.
Why, Ahaz, do you offer boys to vain altars?
Impious worshipper, fie! Learn to fear God.
<note>original Latin: *Cor Deus injusti nebulis obnubilat atris, / Inque suum præceps labitur exitium, / Quid pueros, Achaz, vanis altaribus affers? / Impie, phy! cultor, disce timere Deum.*</note>

->*King Ahaz sacrifices his children to idols.*<-

Serve the mighty God, Ahaz, remove the idol,
Spare your children; alas! most foolish impiety.
The true worship of God is found in humbling oneself
In tears & sighs; He forbids the opposite.

He who distrusts God, and steps away from humanity,

Who, hardened in punishment, drags the Kingdom into a bloodbath.

King Ahaz yields to great Idolatry
Not fearing God, but walking quite awry;
Therefore he makes his Sons pass through the fire

He who leaves God and runs alongside humanity <note>This implies stepping outside the bounds of natural human compassion and natural law.</note>,
And thus prepares his son for the fire, for the idols,
Is Ahaz, who was punished by God and hardened in sin,
And destroys <unclear>his</unclear> cruel Kingdom until it became a bloodbath.

### Second Kings 16 <note>original Latin: *II, REGUM XVI.*</note>

<image-desc>An engraving illustrating a biblical scene. In front of a grand building with classical columns and statues in niches, a large crowd gathers. In the center, a priest-like figure holds a small child over a flaming altar. Smoke billows upwards from the fire. Several figures in the foreground are kneeling in prayer or observation. Below the image is a centered caption.</image-desc>

->Ahaz makes his Sons pass through the fire.<-

**In** the seventeenth year of Pekah, Ahaz became King over Judah, in the twentieth year of his age, and reigned in Jerusalem for sixteen years; however, he became a very idolatrous King, and made cast images for the Baalim <note>The plural of Baal, referring to various local manifestations of the Canaanite deity.</note>, and burned his sons in the fire, just as the idolatrous heathens had done, whom God had driven out before the children of Israel. He also sacrificed and burned incense on the high places <term>high places</term> <note>These were elevated sites used for worship and sacrifice, often associated with pagan rituals rather than the centralized worship at the Temple in Jerusalem.</note>, as well as under every green tree, and thus provoked the Lord. He took the silver and the gold from the Temple and sent it to the King of Assyria as a gift, at the same time requesting his help; the King of Assyria did come to his aid, though it benefited him little. For God, because of his idolatry, delivered him into the hands of his enemies, and also into the hands of the King of Israel, so that in one day in Judah, one hundred and twenty thousand people were slain—all valiant men—and furthermore, they led captive to Samaria two hundred thousand women and children, along with much plunder. Yet, the more God punished him, the more godless he became.

->Our Life & Death are in the Lord's hand. <note>original Latin: *Vita & Mors nostra in manu Domini.*</note><-

The Prophet commands Hezekiah, who is weakening from a deadly boil <note>original Latin: *Anthrace mortifero*; likely a carbuncle or infected swelling</note> and mourning his fate, to put his house in order. ## *The Life of Hezekiah prolonged.* <note>original French: *La Vie de Hizkia prolongè.*</note>

"Take heart, my King; take courage," says the Seer;

Causing your death to retreat by fifteen years; just so,
As the shadow turns backward on this clock here."

Prayer knows how to buy off death for a while longer.

## *Our Life and Death is in God's hand.*

At which when grieved, God spares him fifteen years;
As He steers the shadow backward ten hours <note>The biblical text refers to "steps" or "degrees" on the sundial of Ahaz, rather than modern hours.</note>.

Hezekiah lies deathly ill; the Prophet tells him to set
His kingdom in order by testament, for death was at the threshold.
He weeps, turns to God, and obtains another fifteen years.
The shadow moves backward on the dial miraculously.

AFTER the Assyrian Army, which lay before the walls of Jerusalem, was defeated by the Angel, and Sennacherib <note>original: *Sanherib*</note>—having arrived in Nineveh—was murdered by his sons, Hezekiah <note>original: *Hiskia*</note> became ill and was threatened with death by God through Isaiah <note>original: *Jesaia*</note>. But having prayed to the Lord, he obtained fifteen more years of life; and as assurance of this, God caused a great sign to occur, for He made the shadow turn back ten degrees, which it had already descended on the degrees of Ahaz’s sundial. Thus Hezekiah became healthy again and lived for another fifteen years; and after his death, his son Manasseh <note>original: *Manaffes*</note> reigned—a gruesome idol-worshipper, who in no way resembled his father. The unchangeable God often brings His people to the gates of death and tests our innermost thoughts, so that He may bind us most fervently to Himself.

# Pride is purged by prayers and humility. #
<note>original Latin: "Precibus & humilitate Fastus expiatur."</note>

The King numbers the people, while Jehovah is greatly indignant,
Hence Libitina <note>The Roman goddess of funerals and burial, here used as a poetic personification of death.</note> snatches away many thousands of men:
Apollo <note>The poet uses the name of the Greek god associated with plagues to represent the destroying angel of the biblical account.</note> brandished a death-dealing sword against the people of Jerusalem,
But David restrains him with prayers and supplications.

---

->*Pride Punished.*<-

David, by numbering the people, shows his pride,
And in three days sends the pestilence to the grave,
A great many of Abraham’s sons; but David, confessing his sin,
Makes God cease the plague and the righteous punishment.

---

->David, seeing the destroying Angel over Israel, prays for his People.<-

When David counts his people, to boast of his power,
He is struck to the very heart by remorse.
Moses succeeded before to Heaven’s praise and honor;
But see, high-minded pride is immediately cast down. <note>This refers to the contrast between the censuses commanded by God in the time of Moses and David’s unauthorized census, which was seen as an act of vanity.</note>

---

->*Pride is purged with prayers and humility.*<-

David the people numbers, God's displeas'd;
Thousands by plagues and sickness die diseased,
God's Angel with drawn sword destroys apace,
But David's humble cries with God find Grace.

---

->David counts the people.<-

David counts the people, whereby God is greatly angered,
The pestilence takes away seventy thousand men,
Over Jerusalem the Destroyer stretches his sword,
David prays for mercy, and the punishment is turned away.

<page-num>173</page-num>
# 1 CHRONICLES 21 #
<meta>secondary-page-num: 86</meta>
<meta>catchword: Walls (original Latin: "Moenia")</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a biblical scene from 1 Chronicles 21. In the foreground, King David is kneeling in prayer, wearing a crown and royal robes. Above him, an angel with a sword emerges from heavy clouds, representing the divine punishment. To the left, several people are shown lying on the ground, suffering or dead from the plague. In the top left corner, a radiant oval contains the Hebrew Tetragrammaton <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, יהוה, often translated as 'LORD'.</note>. In the background, other figures and the threshing floor of Araunah are visible.</image-desc>

->David chooses the Pestilence.<-

---

After David had subdued his enemies, Satan rose up against Israel and incited David to count the people of Israel. He commanded Joab to count all Israel from Beersheba to Dan <note>A common biblical expression meaning "from the south to the north," covering the entire extent of the kingdom.</note>. The number reached one million one hundred thousand men; however, in Judah, the number of men who could draw the sword was four hundred and seventy thousand. God, angered by this pride <note>The census was considered a sin of pride because it suggested David relied on the size of his army rather than on God's protection.</note>, presented David—through a Seer <term>Seer: A prophet who receives divine revelations through visions; in this account, the prophet is Gad.</term>—with a choice of three plagues: three years of famine, three months of war, or three days of pestilence. David chose the pestilence. Therefore, God sent a plague so that seventy thousand men of Israel fell. God, looking upon this number, commanded the striking Angel to stop. David, seeing the Angel, prayed humbly and was heard by God. He was commanded by the Angel on God's behalf to erect an altar and offer sacrifices on the threshing floor of Araunah, so that the plague might cease; just as it happened following David’s prayer.

# A Figure of the Architecture of the Spiritual Temple. #

While the Hebrew people restore the walls of Jerusalem <note>original Latin: "Solymorum," a poetic name for Jerusalem</note>,
Their envious neighbors hinder those who would build.
Therefore, to be safe from ambushes and open force,
One hand lays stones, the other holds a weapon.

->Jerusalem rebuilt.<-

To rebuild Jerusalem <note>original French: "Salem"</note>, the children of Isaac <note>original: "Isâc"; here used to represent the Hebrew people</note> hurry;
But the faithless people hinder the holy work;
Israel, however, does its work with one hand,
And with the other, fights with lively courage.

---

### Nehemiah has the Walls of Jerusalem rebuilt. ###

When Israel was allowed to regain her land,
The enemy people boasted they would destroy the building-work.
But while one hand works, the other hand holds the sword.
A zealous soul is afraid of no task.

->*A type <term>Type: In biblical interpretation, a "type" is a person, event, or institution in the Old Testament that is seen as a prophetic shadow of something in the New Testament—in this case, the struggle to build the "spiritual temple" of the soul or the Church.</term> of the building of the spiritual Temple.*<-

*To build the Temple of God the Hebrews strive;*

*Whom to prevent they all wear swords to slay*
*In one hand, while the other stones does lay.*

The Jews are permitted to build their city again,
Envious neighbors hinder this out of mistrust;
So that they might now secure peace and rest for themselves,
One hand works, the other holds the weapons.

# NEHEMIAH III. #

<meta>handwritten page number 175; catchword: Conjugis</meta>

->*Nehemiah rebuilds Jerusalem.*<-

When Nehemiah had received word in Susa <note>original: "Susan," the capital of the Elamite empire and winter residence of the Persian kings</note> regarding the mournful condition of the People, as well as the state of the Walls and Gates of Jerusalem, he fasted and prayed to God for mercy. He prayed especially that God would bless him in the rebuilding. For this task, he chose certain persons from among his People, showing them in what order he wanted the Walls rebuilt. When their enemies heard of this, they mocked him and his people; nevertheless, Nehemiah did not cease to promote the work. 

When the Enemies then learned that they were moving forward briskly, they conspired together to hinder the work by force of Arms. But Nehemiah, having been informed of this, set a good watch against them, thereby thwarting the Enemies' plot. He then returned to the labor and established an order for the men to work with one hand and keep a weapon ready in the other <note>This famous image of the "sword and the trowel" symbolizes the necessity of being both a builder and a defender of the faith</note> against any and all who would seek to halt the work.

Conjugis <note>original Latin: "spouse" or "consort." This catchword likely refers to the mention of King Artaxerxes' queen in the subsequent text.</note>

<summary>This page recounts Nehemiah’s leadership in rebuilding the walls of Jerusalem, describing how he organized the workers to labor and defend the city simultaneously against mocking and hostile neighbors.</summary>
<keywords>Nehemiah, Jerusalem, Susa, reconstruction, city walls, defense, Jewish history, Persian Empire</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the rebuilding of Jerusalem under Nehemiah to the story of Esther, which took place in the Persian capital of Susa. # Through the weak, God works salvation. #
<note>original Latin: *Per imbecillos Deus operatur salutem*</note>

At the prayers of his wife Esther, the Persian King spared 
The captive people who were about to perish. 
The treacherous schemer Haman hangs from a high beam; 
Thus the gallows and the noose kill the one who fashioned the trap.
<note>original Latin verse: *Conjugis Hesteræ precibus...* The Latin "crucis" here refers to the high wooden structure or gallows Haman built.</note>

*Esther prostrates herself before King Ahasuerus <note>Historical King Xerxes I</note>, whom she begs for the salvation of her people, and Haman is hanged on the gallows he had prepared for Mordecai.*

Esther is moved by the need to save her people,
And fearing Haman’s burning anger,
She runs to the Prince and dares to approach him;
She speaks, and it is enough that Esther is loved by him.

### Queen Esther finds grace before the King and delivers her People.

Lady Esther finds grace, kneeling upon the earth:
Thus the Jews are not destroyed according to Haman's desire.
One who seeks to trap another through clever deceit,
Often ends up hanging in his own net and snares.

### *God works safety through the weak.*

At Esther's request, Ahasuerus forgives
The captive Jews, and grants that they shall live.
False Haman hangs upon a high gallows:
On the very one he made for the Jews, he himself must die.

### Haman is hanged on a gallows.

The Persian Monarch is persuaded by Esther,
Not to kill the Jews as he had intended.
Haman the slanderer <note>original German: *Lastermaul*, a colorful term for a malicious gossip or slanderer</note> must die on the gallows
Which he built for others; that was a rightful punishment.

# ESTHER VII. #

<sig>M</sig>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting an interior scene from the Book of Esther. Queen Esther is reclining on a daybed underneath an ornate canopy. Haman is on his knees on the tiled floor before her, pleading for his life with outstretched arms. To the left, King Ahasuerus stands holding a scepter, accompanied by another man. On the right, two women stand near a large, decorative fireplace. The room is luxuriously furnished with wall hangings and a patterned floor.</image-desc>

->*Haman begs Esther for mercy.*<-

After Ahasuerus, King of Persia, had taken Esther as his wife because of her beauty—having cast off Vashti, his first wife, for her disobedience—Haman was exalted very highly by the King. He was raised to such a position that all the courtiers bowed down before him, except for Mordecai. This angered Haman, and he intended to exterminate Mordecai along with the entire race of the Jews, thinking little of the fact that Esther was also a Jewess. To achieve this, he obtained edicts <note>original: *Placcaten*, official public decrees or placards</note>, the reading of which caused great sorrow among the Jews. When the Queen was in great distress over this, she dared, at the urging of her uncle Mordecai, to go to the King unsummoned. He graciously extended his golden scepter toward her <note>In Persian law, approaching the king without being called was punishable by death unless he extended his scepter</note> and promised to come to a feast at her request with Haman—an invitation which later turned out poorly for Haman.

# You have heard of the patience of Job. #
<note>original: *Patientiam Hiobi audivistis*, a reference to James 5:11 in the New Testament.</note>

Lost livestock, herds consumed by fire, offspring
Crushed by ruin; dreadful ulcers of the skin,
And the mockery of his wife—such is your patience, Job—
They did not conquer your faith combined with Piety.
<note>original Latin: *AMissæ pecudes, exustæ armenta, ruinâ / Oppressæ soboles; ulcera dira cutis, / Et sannæ Uxoris (tanta est patientia) Hiobe / Non vicere tuam cum Pietate Fidem.*</note>

### *Job, variously afflicted, overcomes the Devil through his patience.*

You see the illustrious Job struck by various evils,
And never was there a man more miserable;
Because he suffers much, he is human, and he complains,
But through his patience he overcomes the Devil.

### Job is tested through Satan's instigation and God's permission.
<note>The Dutch word *verzogt* here carries the dual meaning of being "tried" or "tempted."</note>

Whoever looks well upon the earthly world is never surprised by calamity.
The palm tree of virtue grows against hardship and burden.
<note>In Renaissance symbolism, the palm tree was a sign of resilience, as it was believed to grow stronger and straighter when weighed down by heavy loads.</note>
The more Job suffers here, the more he is able to bear:
Tough patience does not yield before the plagues.

### *You have heard of Job’s patience.*

Job loses all his oxen, asses, sheep,
His children smothered are; and sores most deep
Rankle <note>original: *Rancle*, meaning to fester or cause persistent pain</note> his flesh, his wife tempts him to blaspheme;
Yet he thanks God, and gives him praise supreme.

### Job is tormented by the Devil.

The patient Job is struck by the Devil,
His wife and wretched friends still mock and plague him;
Yet he holds fast to God; his faith and great patience
Finally win the victory, keeping him in God’s favor.
<note>original German: *Huldt*, meaning grace, favor, or divine affection.</note>

# JOB I. #

<image-desc>An engraving set within a rectangular frame. On the left, the figure of Job sits nearly naked upon a heap of straw and debris (the ash heap) amidst the ruins of large stone arches and crumbling walls. He appears to be in distress, gesturing with his hands. On the right, two men dressed in flowing robes approach him; one man points toward Job while talking to the other. In the background, rolling hills and sparse vegetation are visible under a clear sky.</image-desc>

->Job on the Ash Heap.<-
<note>The "ash heap" (Dutch: *mishoop*, literally a manure or refuse pile) represents the absolute lowest point of Job's social and physical standing, where he sat to scrape his sores away from the community.</note>

Just as God’s children here on Earth have God as a Father, so too they likewise have Satan as their enemy; of which the excellent and pious Job provides and shows a prominent example in his own life. For he was an upright, pious man, blessed by God with children, riches, and honor; yet through the instigation of Satan and the permission of God, he is robbed of all his goods and children, and is also struck by Satan with painful sores upon his body. He is mocked by his wife, whom he rebukes for this, saying: “Should we receive the good from God, and not receive the evil?” <note>A paraphrase of Job 2:10.</note> Thus, he bore himself steadfastly and patiently in his ailments. But afterward, becoming weary <note>The Dutch *verdrietig* here implies a deep sorrow or becoming heavy-hearted under the weight of his trials.</note>, his friends arrive, urging him toward endurance. In this dialogue, Job errs here and there and is rebuked by God Himself; but coming to repentance because of his faults, he is doubly blessed before the eyes of all men.

<header>We passed through water and fire.</header> <note>original Latin: "Per aquam & ignem transivimus," a reference to Psalm 66:12.</note>

While the devoted trio of youths refused to worship the idols, they were cast into a furnace burning far too hot; yet the divine Spirit <note>original: *Genius*, here referring to the protective angel or divine presence.</note> overcame the fire so they might escape: thus God preserves his innocent friends.

->*Three young Hebrews, refusing to commit idolatry, are thrown into a burning furnace; the flame does not touch them at all, but instead consumes those who were putting them to death.*<-

These three young Hebrews, faithful to their God,
Fearing to offend him, are thrown into the flames;
To protect them, an Angel stands in their midst,

## Three Youths in the Fiery Oven Wonderfully Preserved by God.

The hot flame grows cool for three men in the furnace,
Who, unharmed in the fire, praise their Creator there.
The faithful heart rejects the King’s command
Whenever he dares to arrogantly oppose God.

### *We have passed through fire and water.*

While the three children refuse to bow
Unto that statue which the King set up,
Into a fiery furnace they are cast,
Yet the Angel saves them from all harm and waste.

## Three Youths in the Fiery Oven.

When the three youths would not honor the statue,
The furnace's glowing heat was meant to consume them;
An Angel extinguished the flame so that no one was scorched,
Neither in clothing nor body; God holds his pious servants dear.

<page-num>181</page-num>
<header>DANIEL III.</header>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the biblical scene of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego in the fiery furnace. On the left, a large, rounded furnace emits thick smoke and bright flames. Inside, the three figures are seen standing unharmed, accompanied by an angelic being. In the background, a cityscape with a prominent tower is visible. To the right, King Nebuchadnezzar and his court officials watch from a stone balcony adorned with columns. ->Three Youths in the Fiery Oven:<-

After Daniel had revealed his dream to Nebuchadnezzar, and he and his companions had thereby attained great prestige, it happened that the King had a golden statue erected in the Province of Babylon <note>original: *Landschap van Babel*, referring to the administrative district.</note>, the height of which was sixty cubits <note>original: *ellen*, a traditional unit of measurement based on the length of the forearm.</note>, and the width six cubits. He desired it to be worshipped by everyone; otherwise, those who refused would be thrown alive into the fiery oven. Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego refused this, for which reason they were thrown into the fiery oven, yet were miraculously delivered from it by God, so that not even the hair of their heads was scorched. The King, being astonished by this, praises God on account of His faithfulness and power, and thereafter issues a decree that every people, nation, and tongue that should speak blasphemy against the God of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego should be cut into pieces, and their house turned into a dung-heap.

# DANIEL III.

### DANIEL III. 181 90

**Three Youths in the Glowing Oven:**

AFTER Daniel had revealed his dream to Nebuchadnezzar, and he along with his companions had thereby attained great prestige, it happened that the King had an image of gold erected in the province <note>original: *Landschap*. Here referring to the administrative district or province of Babylon.</note> of Babylon. Its height was sixty cubits <note>original: *ellen*. While the Dutch "el" varied in length, it is used here to translate the biblical "cubit."</note> and its width was six cubits. He commanded that this image be worshiped by everyone, or else those who refused would be thrown alive into the glowing furnace. Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego refused to do so; for this reason, they were cast into the glowing furnace, yet they were miraculously delivered by God, so that not even a hair of their heads was singed. The King, being astonished by this, praised God for His faithfulness and power, and subsequently issued a decree that any people, nation, or tongue that speaks blasphemy <note>original: *lasteringe*. Slander or speaking ill of a deity.</note> against the God of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego shall be cut into pieces and their house turned into a dunghill.

# DANIEL VI.

->*Daniel in the Lions' Den.*<-

WHEN Darius the Mede had received the kingdom, being about seventy-two years old, he intended to exalt Daniel above his one hundred and twenty Land-Princes <note>original: *Land-Vorsten*. These are the satraps or provincial governors appointed to oversee the kingdom.</note>. These men, being inflamed with envy over this, and knowing that nothing could be found against Daniel except regarding his religion, persuaded the King to make an idolatrous decree: namely, that for thirty days no one might make any request to any God or man, except to the King, on penalty of being thrown into the Lions' Den. Daniel, then, violating the command <note>Daniel continued his practice of praying toward Jerusalem three times a day despite the royal edict.</note>, was thrown into the lions' den, though against the King’s will; but he was miraculously preserved by God and not touched. Seeing this—after he had risen early in the morning and come sorrowfully to the pit—the King had him taken out and his accusers thrown in, who were immediately torn apart by the lions. Because of this, God was praised and glorified.

<vocab>Darius the Mede, Daniel, Lions' Den, Land-Princes, Idolatrous decree, Religion <note>or "Divine Service."</note></vocab>

<summary>A narrative summary of Daniel 6, describing how Daniel's rivals tricked King Darius into signing a decree that led to Daniel being cast into a den of lions, only to be saved by divine intervention.</summary>
<keywords>Daniel, Lions' Den, King Darius, envy, idolatry, miracle, satraps, Medes and Persians</keywords>

# A prefiguration of the Lord’s resurrection in Jonah.

AI

<meta>catchword: Jonah</meta>

Jonah, fleeing the holy commands of the high-thundering God <note>original: *Altitoni*, a Latin epithet meaning "thundering on high," used here for Jehovah.</note>, 
Cleaves the glassy waters in a sail-winged ship <note>original: *velivola abjete*, literally "sail-flying fir-wood."</note>. 
The sailors cast him out as a mighty storm prevails, 
Whom a whale devours; and vomits forth from its wide jaws.

*After three days and three nights, the fish casts Jonah*
*upon the sand, and God commands him a second*
*time to go to Nineveh.*

Jonah was in the sea for three days and three nights,
A figure <note>A "figure" or "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament believed to predict something in the New Testament.</note> of the Savior, who was as long in the earth;
Then, placed upon the shore for the Ninevites,
He went to speak on behalf of the Master of Thunder.

->**Jonah, swallowed by a fish, was spat out again after three days.**<-

A sea-fish with a great mouth vomits Jonah from its jaws,
Just as death had to release the Lord from its prison.
Everything was prefigured that happened thereafter.
Weak faith becomes strong when it beholds wonders.

->*A Type of our Lord's resurrection in Jonah.*<-

Jonah is sent to preach to Nineveh
Storms overtake him as he shuns by sea,
By lot found guilty he’s thrown overboard,
A whale cast him ashore which him devoured.

->**Jonah is swallowed by a fish.**<-

Jonah flees upon the sea, wishing to run away from his God,
A great storm arrives, they were soon to drown,
He is thrown into the sea, the weather is changed,
A whale swallows him, and spits him back onto the land.

Jonah, in the time of King Jeroboam, the second of that

let such a storm arise that the sailors,
(though they were idolaters) could nonetheless clearly perceive
that it came upon them through a singular divine providence;
wherefore, out of great distress, all their labor being
in vain, they sought out by lot who the guilty man was: this
fell upon Jonah; who, by his own counsel, was at last thrown
into the Sea, and the storm was thereby stilled: Meanwhile,

# TOBIT VI.

<image-desc>An engraving within a framed border illustrating a biblical scene. On the right, a young man (Tobias) is kneeling by a riverbank, wrestling a large fish out of the water. To his left stands the Archangel Raphael, depicted with large feathered wings and holding a long staff. ->Tobias accompanied by the Angel.<-

**A**fter Tobias, having become blind, had suffered several bitter words from his wife over a little goat <note>In the Book of Tobit, a domestic dispute arises when Tobit's wife, Anna, receives a young goat as a bonus for her work; the blind Tobit wrongly accuses her of stealing it, leading to a moment of deep despair.</note>, he desired of God that he might soon die, and taught his son (who was also named Tobias) that after his death he should be obedient to his mother; he also revealed to him that he had left money in the care of Gabael at Rages in Media <note>Media was an ancient region in present-day Iran; the journey from Nineveh to Rages was significant and dangerous.</note>. The young Tobias, hearing this, immediately sought a man who could lead him to Media, and found the Angel Raphael, who told him that he was Azarias (one of his kin) <note>The Archangel Raphael conceals his divine identity, using the name Azarias to act as a guide and protector without causing fear.</note>; having taken leave with him, he undertook the journey, and by evening they arrived at the Tigris river; while washing himself there, he was jumped upon by a fish, which he (at the Angel's command) grabbed, cut into pieces, and took from it the heart, the liver, and the gall, which he laid aside to keep; the uses of these things the Angel showed him <note>The Angel explains that the smoke from the heart and liver drives away demons, and the gall can cure blindness.</note>, and they traveled onward.

# The Battle of Sobriety and Drunkenness.

# JUDITH XIII.
N 3

<image-desc>An etching depicting the biblical scene of Judith and her maidservant in the tent of Holofernes. Judith stands on the right, holding a sword in one hand and grasping the severed head of Holofernes by the hair with the other. Her maidservant, on the left, holds open a sack or cloth to receive the head. In the background, Holofernes' body is visible on a bed inside the draped tent. A candle or lamp burns on a pedestal to the left, and a large chest sits on the floor.</image-desc>

->Judith kills Holofernes.<-

Nebuchadnezzar <note>original: "Nabuchodonofor". While historically the King of Babylon, the Book of Judith identifies him as the King of the Assyrians.</note>, after he had destroyed Ecbatana <note>An ancient capital city in Media.</note> and defeated Arphaxad, returned to Nineveh and resolved to take revenge on the lands that had refused him assistance in that war. For this task, he chose Holofernes as his commander-in-chief, who marched toward the Land of the Jews at his command. However, the inhabitants of Bethulia <note>A Jewish city whose location is debated, often seen as a symbolic or strategic gateway to Jerusalem.</note> blocked his entry into Judea, whereupon he besieged them with a great force. While they were thus trapped and distressed, Judith (being a wealthy and pious widow) promised to deliver them. She prayed to God that He would bless her intended work and, having adorned herself <note>This refers to Judith setting aside her mourning clothes to dress in her finest attire to deceive Holofernes.</note>, went to Holofernes in his camp. He received her kindly and, after three days, invited her to a feast. When she came to him, she was left alone in the tent with Holofernes, who had fallen into a deep sleep as he was very drunk. Seeing this, Judith prayed to God for strength and struck off his head. She brought it back to Bethulia; upon hearing of this, the Assyrians fled.

**Vocabulary:** Nebuchadnezzar, Ecbatana, Arphaxad, Nineveh, Holofernes, Judith, Bethulia, Judea, Assyrians.

### Truth is pressed, but not suppressed.
<note>original: "Veritas premitur, non opprimitur." This Latin proverb suggests that while truth can be temporarily hidden or burdened by lies, it can never be fully destroyed.</note>

DANIEL III. 95

N 4

->*Susanna Falsely Accused.*<-

Susanna, the wife of Joakim, a beautiful, honorable, and godly woman, went one day into her garden to cleanse or wash herself, believing that no stranger was in the garden. She was solicited for dishonor by two judges <note>original: "Rechters." In most English Bibles, these figures are referred to as the "Elders."</note> who were enamored with her and had hidden themselves in the garden. They threatened that if she would not comply, they would accuse her of adultery. She nonetheless refused them; for this, she was accused of adultery by the judges, condemned, and led to her death. Finally, by Daniel (through God’s providence), she was judged innocent. Conversely, the judges were convicted of false accusations out of their own mouths and were therefore punished with death. Whatever the case may be, it is certain that God is a champion of innocence <note>original: "onnozelheid." In 17th-century Dutch, this term meant "harmlessness" or "purity," specifically the state of being without sin or guilt.</note> and the strong protector of those who are oppressed despite their innocence.

---

**Vocabulary:** Susanna, Joakim, Daniel, adultery, Judges, innocence, godly

<keywords>Susanna, Daniel, Joakim, Elders, false accusation, adultery, divine providence, innocence, justice</keywords>

->There is no power or strength against the Lord.<-
<note>original: "Non est vis aut robur adversus Dominum." This Latin heading is a paraphrase of Proverbs 21:30.</note>

The elephant, that most powerful beast, prepares the hostile lines for battle <note>original: "Barrus." An archaic Latin term for an elephant, used here to emphasize the exotic and terrifying nature of the enemy's cavalry.</note>, while the Greek soldier attacks the Hebrews. Undaunted, Judas Maccabeus charges them both; he strikes down the men with his sword and scatters the monstrous beasts.

### Judas battles Antiochus Eupator.

King Eupator, with a terrifying force,
Attempts to uproot the beloved race of the Jews;
But the valiant Judas, in the heat of the fight,
Cuts down many regiments of these pagan soldiers.

### Judas Maccabeus fights against King Antiochus Eupator.

Let the Greek boldly set himself against Israel in the field,
With such a great force of people, and so many elephants;
Still, he is overpowered. What does great violence avail him,
When God has placed His own stronger power against it?

### *There is <unclear>no</unclear> force or strength against the Lord.*

Numbers of men and Elephants do go
Against the Jews, assured to overthrow;
Whom Judas meets with such undaunted force,
As he destroys their beasts, their foot, and horse.

### Judas Maccabeus fights against King Antiochus Eupator.

The heathens lead the elephants to the battle
Against the Jewish army, to destroy them completely;
The people were struck with fear and were in no small danger,
Yet they wounded the enemy on every side in the end.

<header>I MACCABEES VI. 96</header>

<image-desc>An engraving of a battle scene. In the center, a large war elephant is collapsing under the attack of several soldiers. On the elephant's back is a wooden tower with arched openings, from which armed men are fighting. One soldier is seen falling from the tower, while others on the ground thrust spears into the elephant. In the foreground, a soldier lies on the ground, seemingly trampled. ->Maccabeus overcomes Antiochus.<-

Judas Maccabeus, having achieved many victories one after another, marches with an army to Jerusalem, captures it, and restores the religious worship <note>original: "Godsdienst." This refers to the cleansing of the Temple and the restoration of Jewish rites after they had been suppressed by the Greeks.</note>; furthermore, he continues his victory, destroying the heathens who conspired against him; and he takes many cities across the Jordan, and thereafter returns again to the Land of Judah. Antiochus, hearing of this, dies of grief, leaving his son Antiochus Eupator in his place, who comes with a strong army before Beth-zur <term>Bethsura: A strategic hilltop fortress that guarded the road to Jerusalem from the south.</term>, against which Judas sets himself; but he returns again to Jerusalem and makes peace with Lysias, which the King does not keep. A little while later, Demetrius the King of Syria (who had caused Antiochus Eupator and Lysias to be killed) sends Nicanor to Jerusalem, who seeks to deceive Judas and threatens to burn the Temple. Judas, understanding this, marches against him and defeats him and his whole army; however, in the following battle, he remains dead <note>original: "blyft hy dood." An idiomatic way of saying he fell in battle or was killed on the field.</note>.

<header>The discord of the wicked is the safety of the godly. <note>original: "Discordia improborum salus piorum." This refers to the political strategy where the Jewish leaders benefited from the internal infighting of their Syrian-Greek oppressors.</note></header>

Everything rushes to arms, blood and slaughter everywhere;
Demetrius, the enemy, brings war against Alexander,
Jonathan aids Alexander’s side in the struggle,
And in the battle-line, he strikes down and puts Demetrius’s men to flight.

The field is entirely scattered with soldiers.
The combat against one is led by two Princes;
But Alexander receives triumph in his favor,
Through Jonathan, who strikes down Demetrius and his fury.

The land, sown with people, bears a harvest of soldiers,
Two Princes are unleashed in battle against one;

He who fights alongside God’s friends always triumphs.

Arms, blood, and slaughter wander over all,
Demetrius is overcome by Alexander:
Jonathan, in league with Alexander, goes

The land is full of war; King Alexander
And Demetrius strike each other bravely,
The noble hero Jonathan also enters the fray <note>original: "kommt auch mit in das Spiel," literally "comes also into the game."</note>,
He strikes Apollonius; there are many battles.

### 1 MACCABEES 10.

*An engraving depicting a large-scale battle between two armies. In the foreground, fallen soldiers and horses are strewn across the ground among discarded shields and arrows. In the center, cavalry units clash with swords raised. The background shows infantry with spears and distant mountains. A bordered text box at the bottom of the illustration contains the caption "Alexander defeats Demetrius."*

AFTER the death of **Judas Maccabeus**, **Alexander** <note>Alexander Balas, a pretender to the Seleucid throne who claimed to be the son of Antiochus IV Epiphanes.</note>, the son of **Antiochus Epiphanes**, captures **Ptolemais**;
hearing this, **Demetrius** <note>Demetrius I Soter, the reigning king who had long been an enemy of the Maccabean rebels.</note> marches against him with a mighty army,
and seeks to bring **Jonathan**, the brother of **Maccabeus**, to his side.
**Alexander** does the same and, to that end, writes letters to **Jonathan**, promising him the **High Priesthood** <term>High Priesthood: The supreme religious and political office in Jerusalem. By accepting this appointment from Alexander, Jonathan established the Hasmonean line of high priests.</term>, which **Jonathan** accepts.
**Demetrius**, on the other hand, also writes to the **Jews**, promising them many freedoms and gifts if they would remain loyal to him:
but the **Jews** find it better to side with **Alexander**, whereupon the two Kings engage in battle, in which **Alexander** achieves the victory and **Demetrius** is killed:
when the enemies of **God’s Church** <note>original: "Godts Kerk." The author uses this term to describe the ancient Jewish community, viewing them as the biblical predecessors of the Christian Church.</note> quarrel among themselves, the faithful often find peace.

<meta>This page moves forward to the end of the life of Simon Thassi, the last of the sons of Mattathias to lead the Jewish people. Following the events of 1 Maccabees 10–15, where the Hasmonean dynasty was established, this page focuses on the betrayal and murder of Simon as recorded in 1 Maccabees chapter 16.</meta>

# Ptolemy slaughters Simon together with his sons,

Why, O host, do you slaughter your father-in-law amid the drinking cups? <note>original: "Quid, convivator, socerum inter pocula mactas?"</note>
If relatives do such things, who can ever be safe?
But behold, Ptolemy, what has long been known:
What you do to a father, a son shall do to you. <note>A warning that Simon’s surviving son, John Hyrcanus, would succeed him and seek justice for this betrayal.</note>

### *Ptolemy kills Simon & his sons.*

Here is Ptolemy completing his treacherous murder, <note>The original French uses "parricide," which in this era could refer generally to the murder of a close relative or a social superior.</note>
Inviting his father-in-law, and ensuring that many
Glasses full of wine are emptied; then, taking his moment,
He kills him by his servants' hands, along with his two children.

### *Simon killed with both his sons.*

How can Ptolemy go so heavy with deceit
To the banquet, and hide his betrayal? And dare
To shed the Priest-blood, <term>Priest-blood: Simon held the office of High Priest. In this context, the term emphasizes that the murder was not just a political assassination but a sacrilege against God’s anointed leader.</term> as well as that of his sons?
How can a son-in-law, so unfaithful, reward such kindness in this way?

### *Ptolemy killeth Simon and his Sons.*

Ungrateful Ptolemy, why do you kill
Your father-in-law, and make his blood to spill,
While at a feast you seem to entertain him,
Who by your hand is most barbarously slain?

### *Simon killed along with his two sons.*

Can Ptolemy here still walk with such treachery
To the meal, and cover his betrayal? And dare
To spill the Priest-blood, and that of his two sons?
How can the son-in-law here unfaithfully reward such a good deed?
