# 1 MACCABEES 16.

<meta>catchword: Aufus</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a scene of assassination inside a grand hall with classical columns. In the foreground, bodies lie on the floor next to overturned bowls and plates, suggesting a banquet has been violently interrupted. In the background, soldiers with swords and shields are engaged in combat or pursuing survivors. A caption at the bottom of the image frame reads: "Simon and his sons killed."</image-desc>

When John had now related to his father Simon how the commander <term>Commander: The Dutch "Veldoverste" refers to a high-ranking military leader or general.</term> Cendebeus had invaded Judea and had caused much damage to the Jewish people, Simon related to his two sons how he and his ancestors had always warred against the enemies of the Jews with great success. He exhorted them—as he had now grown old—that they should follow his example with courage, wishing them the blessing of Heaven.

Thus Judas and John chose twenty thousand fighting men, with some cavalry, and they marched against Cendebeus and put him to flight; yet Judas was wounded in this encounter, but his brother John pursued the enemies until they were conquered. Simon, traveling through the land, came to his son-in-law Ptolemy, who received his father-in-law and both sons kindly, yet with great deceit; for while they were at the banquet, he had Simon and both his sons murdered, and thus repaid evil for good. <note>This event took place in the fortress of Dok, near Jericho, around 134 BCE. Ptolemy was Simon's own son-in-law, making the betrayal particularly shocking to contemporary readers.</note>

# The Just Punishment of Sacrilege

<meta>catchword: When</meta>

You dared to bring profane feet into the inner rooms, and to apply greedy hands to the Sanctuary of the Temple: You are beaten by three Spirits, Heliodorus, and warned that the impious crowd should stay away from holy places. <note>original Latin: "Aufus es interiora pedes inferre profanos...". The "inner rooms" refers to the *Adytum*, the most sacred part of the Temple where only priests were permitted.</note>

->*Heliodorus, sent by Antiochus to carry off the Treasury,*<-
->*is diverted from this plan by a sudden punishment.*<-

You are overwhelmed with blows, and an angry horse crushes you under its feet, impious Heliodorus; you shall learn that no one has ever attacked the God whom these holy people adore and escaped without punishment. <note>original French: "On t'accable de coups...". This refers to the vision in 2 Maccabees of a horse with a terrible rider and two young men of great strength who scourged Heliodorus.</note>

### Heliodorus was punished in the Temple.

Heliodorus treads with unholy soles into the Holy of Holies, which was entrusted to God’s Priests: but he is turned out from there, and blocked by God’s power; the Holy would otherwise be defiled by the unholy.

### The just punishment of sacrilege.

To rob the Temple, Heliodorus was sent;
He enters as one who would seize property for unpaid rent:
But three Angels strike and beat him sorely,
Which should warn all not to attempt such a thing wickedly. <note>The original English uses the term "distreyne," an old legal word meaning to seize someone's property to obtain payment for rent or debts.</note>

### Heliodorus is punished in the Temple.

The proud Heliodorus defiantly ventured to enter the Sanctuary and lay his hands upon the treasure, where he was thrashed by angels to such an extent that he no longer desired what did not belong to him.

<summary>This page recounts the biblical story of Heliodorus, a Syrian official who attempted to loot the Temple of Jerusalem but was miraculously intercepted and beaten by divine messengers. It serves as a moral warning against sacrilege, presented through verses in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German.</summary>
<keywords>Heliodorus, sacrilege, Temple of Jerusalem, divine intervention, 2 Maccabees, treasury, Antiochus IV Epiphanes, punishment</keywords>

# 2 MACCABEES III

<meta>secondary number 99 within the illustration border</meta>

->The Temple Robber Heliodorus Punished.<-

WHen everything in Jerusalem was once again in good peace, Simon (an enemy of Onias the High Priest) went to Apollonius, the Governor of Coele-Syria and Phoenicia, and told him how the Temple treasury <term>Gods-kist|Literally "God’s chest," referring here to the sacred treasury where funds for widows and orphans were kept</term> in Jerusalem was exceedingly well-stocked with money, indeed more than was necessary for the sacrifices. Apollonius, believing this, told it to King Seleucus, who immediately sent Heliodorus, his Chamberlain <term>Kamerling|A high-ranking court official or steward</term>, to take the money from the Temple treasury. Heliodorus accepted the task, traveled there, arrived in Jerusalem, and desired to carry out his command, which Onias opposed. Therefore, at a certain time, Heliodorus entered the Temple with his soldiers to rob the treasury; upon this, the people began to weep and prayed to God to prevent it. This God did, causing a rider on horseback to appear; the horse leapt upon him with its two front feet, and two young men also appeared who struck him with all their might. Heliodorus, having become faint <term>aam-agtig|An archaic term meaning breathless, gasping, or physically overcome</term> because of this, was nonetheless (at the request of his friends) restored to health through a sacrifice offered by Onias.

<sig>Hoof</sig> <note>original: "Ungula"; referring to the horse's hoof mentioned in the text and depicted in the engraving.</note>

# 200

<header>Be constant and I will give you a crown. <note>original Latin: "Esto constans & dabo tibi Coronam," a reference to Revelation 2:10.</note></header>
<meta>catchword: Antiochus</meta>

The cruel claw, wheels, and cauldrons boiling with fire,
   Slay the seven sons in order, together with their mother.
Yet Antiochus could not compel any of them, despite such great tortures,
To violate the commands of the divine law.
<note>original Latin: "Ungula sæva, rotæ, ferventes igne lebetes..." This verse describes the specific instruments of torture—the iron hooks (ungula), the breaking wheel, and the boiling pans—used by the King to try and force the family to eat forbidden swine's flesh.</note>

->*Seven brothers with their mother constantly suffer martyrdom for the Law of God.*<-

*The admirable virtue of the seven martyr brothers*
   *Has astonished every age with its great brilliance;*
*Each one, clothed in the spirit of their mother,*
*Far surpassed even the noblest courage.*
<note>original French: "Des sept freres martyrs l'admirable vertu..." This section emphasizes the maternal influence, noting how the mother encouraged her sons to die for their faith rather than live as apostates.</note>

->Antiochus’s cruelty committed against seven brothers and their mother.<-

Let Antiochus freely show his cruel heart,
   The Mother remains faithful with all her seven sons
To God and to His Law. When the body is tormented,
The soul is gladdened, if it so pleases God.
<note>original Dutch: "Antiochi wreedheid begaan aan zeven Broeders..." The text reflects a common Baroque theme: the triumph of the eternal soul over the temporary suffering of the flesh.</note>

->*Be constant and I will give you a Crown.*<-

*Seven Hebrew brothers would rather endure*
   *Such tortures as have no cure, than break the law:*
*Through fire and sword they pass unto their deaths*
*With their aged mother, and gain martyrs' wreaths.*

->Antiochus has seven sons burned together with their mother.<-

Terrible martyrdom and torment did seven Hebrew boys
And their pious mother endure with steadfastness;
Maimed and burned, rather than acting against God's commandment,
They suffered a bitter death.
<note>original German: "Schröklich Marter und Qual sieben Hebræische Knaben..."</note>

<meta>This page follows the poetic descriptions of the martyrdoms on page 200, transitioning into a prose summary of the biblical account from 2 Maccabees 7. It maintains the theme of the "Seed of the Church" established in the previous page's reflections on constancy and divine reward.</meta>

# 201
<header>II, MACCABEES: VII. 100</header>

->Seven Brothers killed with their Mother.<-

Antiochus having barbarically captured the city of Jerusalem, forces the Jews to abandon the Law of God, which many refuse, though they had to pay for it with their lives: among others, seven brothers with their mother. Because they would not eat <term>pork<note>original Dutch: "Verkens-vleeſch." The consumption of swine is a violation of the dietary laws (Kashrut) established in the Torah.</note></term>, which was forbidden to them by the Law, they had to endure his cruelty, and in a particularly gruesome manner. For he commanded that the first—in the sight of the others and the mother—have his tongue cut out, the skin flayed from his body, his <term>extremities<note>original: "uiterſte leden," referring to the hands and feet.</note></term> hacked off, and then be roasted in a pan. This was likewise done to the second, third, fourth, fifth, sixth, and most cruelly of all to the seventh; and finally, after her sons, the mother was killed. See here, as if in a mirror, how religion has been persecuted even unto blood since ancient times, and there has never been a lack of those who have allowed the most inhuman pains to be inflicted upon themselves for the sake of the truth. The blood of the martyrs has truly been the seed of the Church <note>A historical reference to the famous statement by the early Christian author Tertullian: "The blood of martyrs is the seed of the Church," suggesting that persecution only serves to grow the faith.</note>.

**Antiochus, Jerusalem, Jews, Law of God, seven Brothers, Mother, Pork, Martyrs, Seed of the Church**

<summary>This page recounts the graphic martyrdom of seven Jewish brothers and their mother under the Seleucid King Antiochus IV, emphasizing their refusal to violate divine law by eating pork. It concludes with a reflection on how such suffering for the "truth" has historically served to strengthen the foundation of the religious community.</summary>
<keywords>Antiochus IV Epiphanes, Jerusalem, Maccabean Martyrs, 2 Maccabees 7, religious persecution, dietary laws, Torah, martyrdom, Seed of the Church</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the title page of the 1742 collection of New Testament illustrations. ### The Holy Spirit shall come upon you. <note>original Latin: "Spiritus Sanctus superperveniet tibi." This is a quote from the Gospel of Luke 1:35, where the Angel Gabriel explains the miraculous conception to Mary.</note>

Hail, Virgin, blessed mother of your Parent,
*Virgin soon to bear your Creator.*
To nurse with your breast the Nourisher as a child,
You shall give life to him, who himself existed before you. <note>original Latin: "Salve Virgo tui genitrix Benedicta Parentis..." These verses play on the theological paradox of the Incarnation: Mary giving birth to the God who created her.</note>

->*The Angel is sent to Mary.*<-

Virgin, Mother of God, by this divine title,
*Your soul is suddenly ravished by a holy transport,*
What glory for you to nourish in your breast,
*The Christ, the King of Kings, & the Author of life!*

->The Angel Gabriel announces to Mary that she shall bear the Son of God.<-

The holy Heavenly Dove, descended from the Starry-roof, <note>original Dutch: "Starre-dak" — a poetic term for the firmament or the heavens.</note>
Shadows his Mary with wings without spot. <note>original: "vlak" — meaning a blemish or stain, referring to the purity of the conception.</note>
Thus a Virgin becomes pregnant. Who can sharpen their senses,
To comprehend a Wonder beyond Nature?

->*The holy Ghost shall come upon thee.*<-

Hail blessed Virgin Mother to thy Lord,
*Virgin which shalt bring forth thy God the Word.*
Him that gave life to thee, thy self shalt bear,
*And with thy breast shalt feed thy Nourisher.*

->The Message from the Angel to Mary.<-

God greet you, tender Virgin, Mary full of grace,
For you shall bear Him, who shall unburden
His People of the load of Sin, with which they are heavily weighed,
You are chosen by God, that you should become the Mother
Of Him, who created you yourself, Heaven and Earth,
Should nourish at the breast Him who nourishes all things.

### LUKE 1:1

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the Annunciation. The Angel Gabriel appears on a cloud in the upper left, pointing towards Mary. Mary is seated in a chair on the right, her hands raised in a gesture of surprise or acceptance. The room contains a bed with curtains in the background, a small table with a basin, and a bookshelf.</image-desc>

->The Greeting of Mary.<- <note>This refers to the "Ave Maria" or the Annunciation, the moment the Angel Gabriel greets the Virgin Mary.</note>

---

After the Angel **Gabriel**, sent by **God**, had fulfilled his message to **Zacharias** regarding the **Birth** of a Son from **Elizabeth** in the old age of both, whose name would be **John**, the forerunner of **Christ**, the same Angel comes by **God’s Command** to the **Virgin Mary**, greets and blesses her, and announces to her that she shall conceive, without the intervention of a man, and bear a **Son** named **Jesus**, the **Son** of the **Most High**, of whom all **Prophets** have testified; and that he would come and dwell among men, and that he would save his people from their sins. To which **Mary** answers: "Let it be done to me according to your word." And after the departure of the Angel, she rises up and goes to her **Cousin Elizabeth**, who receives her with joy and praises her as blessed. <note>original Dutch: "zalig pryst" — to praise as blessed or happy, referring to the "Beatification" of Mary in the Magnificat.</note>

<meta>This page continues the multilingual devotional sequence. Having previously described the Annunciation (the announcement of Jesus's birth to Mary), the text now moves to the Nativity itself. The structure remains consistent: a Latin quatrain followed by poetic reflections in French, Dutch, English, and German, all focusing on the humility of Christ's birth in Bethlehem.</meta>

->A child is born to us.<- <note>original Latin: "Puer natus est nobis." This is a reference to Isaiah 9:6, a central text for the Christmas liturgy.</note>

Look upon the wondrous plan of salvation achieved, <note>original Latin: "Adspice patratæ mirabile schema salutis"</note>
Behold God clothed in the body of a child.
A Virgin gives birth, a manger shelters him, an Angel sings a Hymn,
The night shines bright, and the shepherd is the first in the World to see.

---

### *Christ is born in Bethlehem.*

To finally rescue you from captivity,
*Admire, O Mortals! how God willed to be born;*
*See the humility to which he brought himself,* <note>original French: "l’abbaissement" — literally a "lowering" or "abasement," referring to the theological concept of Kenosis, where God empties himself of divine glory to become human.</note>
*Hiding his divine Being beneath our frail flesh.*

---

### Jesus born in Bethlehem.

The Angels are amazed that without pomp or splendor
The Creator of the Universe is brought into the World.
Thus wanders the poor Man who seeks pleasure in life,
While He did not seek it, He who has given all. <note>The Dutch verse provides a moral lesson, contrasting human pursuit of luxury with the poverty chosen by Christ.</note>

---

### *A Son is born to us.*

Behold our Pledge, the shield of our hoped-for Salvation,
See God clad in the body of a child.
A Virgin in a manger bears Heaven’s King,
Poor Shepherds see him first, and Angels sing.

---

### The little child Jesus is born.

When the time was fulfilled for God to become man,
God’s Son revealed himself in the form of a servant, <note>original German: "knechtlichen Geberden" — the gestures or appearance of a servant, a reference to the Epistle to the Philippians.</note>
A child naked and small is born to the world,
From a tender Virgin, placed in a manger.
He who sustains and carries all things through his mighty Word,
The first who saw such a thing was a poor shepherd.

->The Birth of Christ.<-

Now when Jesus was born in Bethlehem, located in Judea, there was immediately a very great joy in Heaven over this birth of the Son of the almighty God, which the Angel of the Lord first of all announced to the shepherds in the field, who were keeping the night watch <note>original: "nagtwake"</note> over their flock, and said: I proclaim to you great joy, which shall happen to all people, namely that today is born the Savior <note>original: "Zaligmaker" — literally "Bliss-maker," a traditional Dutch term for the Redeemer.</note> Christ Jesus in the city of David, whom you shall find lying in a manger and wrapped in swaddling clothes. And when the Angel of the Lord had said this to the shepherds, there appeared with him the multitude of the Heavenly Host <note>original: "Hemelsche Heirlegers" — literally "Heavenly Armies."</note>, praising and glorifying God in the highest Heavens; whereupon the shepherds took counsel among one another to go hence to Bethlehem and to see what had happened in this matter which had been made known to them. They then went hence with haste, and coming to the appointed place, they found Mary, and Joseph, and the Infant, lying in the Manger, just as it had been told to them before by the Angel.

<meta>This page continues the sequence of reflections on the birth of Christ. While the previous page focused on the announcement to the shepherds (the Jewish audience), this page presents the arrival of the Magi, representing the "firstfruits" of the non-Jewish nations (Gentiles). The text follows the same multilingual structure as the earlier sections of the manuscript, providing the same core theological message in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German.</meta>

# The Firstfruits of the Gentiles. <note>original Latin: "Primitiae Gentium." In Christian tradition, the Magi represent the first non-Jewish people to recognize Jesus as the Messiah.</note>

From the East, with a star leading to the Child, the Wise Men <note>original Latin: "Magi"</note>
Bring three gifts: gold, frankincense, and myrrh.
Yellow metals signify a King, while myrrh signifies the tomb,
And Sabaean <note>Sabaea refers to the Kingdom of Sheba (modern-day Yemen), famous in antiquity for its high-quality frankincense.</note> incense reveals the true God.

### *The Wise Men come from the East to adore Jesus Christ*

From the regions of the East toward the newborn Child,
One also sees three venerable Wise Men coming;
A star leads them, and each prostrates himself,
Offering him homage with gold, myrrh, and incense.

### The Wise Men from the East adore Christ and bring Him gifts.

Heaven is on Earth; the Wise Men from the East
Give gifts to the World’s Salvation <note>original Dutch: "’s Werelds Heil"</note>, who comforts sorrowful souls,
With incense, gold, and myrrh: a star knew how to lead them.
It is still night among the Jews, while it is day for the Gentiles <note>original Dutch: "Heiden" — literally "Heathens," but used here in the biblical sense to denote non-Jewish nations.</note>.

### *The first-fruits of the Gentiles.*

Three wise men from the east come Christ to adore,
Bringing Gold, Frankincense, & Myrrh, before
Whom a star leads: Him Gold a King proclaims,
Myrrh speaks his grave, his God-head incense names.

### The Wise Men from the Orient bring gifts to Christ.

Far from the Orient, the firstfruits of the Gentiles <note>original German: "Heyden"</note>
Come to Bethlehem; a star accompanied them.
Incense, myrrh, and gold are honored there for the Child;
A hidden meaning lies in these three gifts:
Incense testifies to his divinity, that he will be buried
Is signified by myrrh, and gold preserves his kingdom.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page features five poetic versions (in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German) of the Adoration of the Magi. Each poem explains the symbolic meaning of the three gifts: gold for kingship, incense for divinity, and myrrh for his eventual death and burial.</summary>

# MATTHEW 2. <note>original Latin: MATHÆI. 2.</note>
O 4
<meta>catchword: Arise; illustration-number: 3</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving of the Adoration of the Magi. Mary is seated on the right, holding the Christ child on her lap. Joseph stands behind her. Three Magi approach from the left; one is kneeling in front of the infant, and others stand behind him bearing gifts. The background features classical architecture and onlookers. A label at the top of the frame reads "MATTHEW 2." and the number "3". A caption at the bottom of the frame reads "The wise men from the East offer gifts to Christ."</image-desc>

->The wise men from the East offer gifts to Christ.<-

**The Wise Men**, (according to common opinion) <note>While the Bible doesn't specify their origin beyond "the East," early modern scholars often identified them as Persian Zoroastrian priests known for their study of the stars.</note> had their dwelling place in **Persia**, in order to interpret certain future things there through specific signs. These men then, understanding by an unusual **Star** that something more than ordinary was born—namely, the **King of the Jews**—traveled immediately toward the Jewish Land (where the Star led them), taking with them **Gold, Frankincense, and Myrrh**. After they had now arrived in **Jerusalem**, they inquired precisely after the place where the **Messiah**, according to the content of the prophecies, was to be born; in doing so, they filled Jerusalem with wonder and **Herod** <note>Herod the Great, the Roman-appointed King of Judea, who feared any rival to his throne.</note> with terror. Herod, learning that the **Christ** was to be born in **Bethlehem**, cunningly directed the Wise Men there. Following the path of the Star, which stood still over the place where the Child was, they found Christ; and by God’s command, without bringing word back to Herod, they returned to their own country by another way.

<vocab>Magi <note>Latin term for the Wise Men or astrologers.</note>, Persia, King of the Jews, Jerusalem, Bethlehem, Herod, Gold, Frankincense, Myrrh, Messiah <note>The "Anointed One" promised in Jewish scripture.</note></vocab>

<summary>This page recounts the journey of the Magi from Persia to Bethlehem, emphasizing their interpretation of the star and their avoidance of King Herod's treachery. It provides a narrative context for the gifts of gold, frankincense, and myrrh.</summary>
<keywords>Magi, Star of Bethlehem, King Herod, Persia, Epiphany, Three Kings, Gold, Frankincense, Myrrh, Messiah</keywords>

# The Flight into Egypt <note>original Latin: Fuga in Ægyptum.</note>
N

<image-desc>A faint, faded copperplate engraving is visible in the background, partially obscured by the overprinted text. It depicts the Flight into Egypt: a bearded Joseph, holding a staff, leads a donkey upon which Mary sits, cradling the infant Jesus. They are traveling through a landscape with sparse trees and hills.</image-desc>

## Arise, Joseph! You are permitted to leave your native lands, <note>original: "Surge Joseph! patrias tibi fas est linquere terras."</note>
## With Mary and the Child, inhabit the Egyptian fields; <note>original: "Coptia arva," literally "Coptic fields," a poetic reference to Egypt.</note>
## The Child's destruction is sought, so the Angel says
## In sleep: and behold, he suddenly obeys the commands.

### *Joseph and Mary flee to Egypt with the Child Jesus.*

Warned by an Angel in a deep sleep,
Joseph prepares for departure without delay;
He takes the mother and the child immediately upon waking,
And flees far from the danger to which the Tyrant <note>Referring to King Herod, who ordered the "Massacre of the Innocents" to eliminate the newborn "King of the Jews."</note> exposes him.

### *Joseph and Mary flee with the Little Child into Egypt.*

Here the Salvation of the People <note>A title for Jesus.</note> flees, and must wander as an exile
In a far-off Land, so as to escape death;
Among strangers he finds refuge, while his own People hate him;
How little does Man notice the true state of his affairs.

### *Joseph and Mary flee to Egypt.*

Herod seeks to kill Christ; without delay
Joseph, warned by the Angel, must flee away
To Egypt; Christ and Mary flee with him,
Thus Christ was pursued from his infancy.

### *Joseph and Mary flee with the child to Egypt.*

What does raging help, when God only wishes to preserve
His well-beloved Child, the salvation of young and old?
Although Herod's wrath wanted to bring him to death,
Joseph flees from this distress according to God's command.
He (along with Mother and Child) moved away by night;
Thus the tyrant is here deceived through God's power.

<vocab>Flight into Egypt, Holy Family, Joseph, Mary, Jesus, Herod, Egyptian fields</vocab>

<header>MATTHEW 2:4 <note>While the header cites verse 4, the text describes the events of Matthew 2:13–15.</note></header>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the Flight into Egypt. Mary is seated on a donkey, holding the Christ child. Joseph walks beside them, leading the donkey with a rope and carrying a staff. They are traveling through a hilly, rocky landscape under a sky with several clouds.</image-desc>

->Joseph’s Flight to Egypt.<-

AFTER the Wise Men had departed by another way (through a special arrangement of God), the Angel of the Lord appeared to Joseph at night in a dream, commanding him to flee and go into exile, so as to preserve the life of the Little Child from those who sought to kill him. The place to which the Angel told him to travel is Egypt, a region in Africa, named after Aegyptus, the brother of Danaus; <note>The author includes a reference to Greek mythology here; in classical myth, Aegyptus and Danaus were twin brothers who feuded over the kingdom of Egypt.</note> where he would remain until the Angel should tell him otherwise. Joseph, obeying this vision, immediately traveled with Mary and the Little Child during the night to Egypt, staying there until the death of Herod, so that what was spoken by the Lord through Hosea might be fulfilled, saying: *Out of Egypt I have called my Son,* <note>Hosea 11:1</note> and in Jeremiah: *They shall come again from the land of the enemy.* <note>Jeremiah 31:16</note>

<meta>catchword: Ad</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>It is necessary for us to fulfill all righteousness. <note>original Latin: "Oportet nos omnem juſtitiam implere." (Matthew 3:15)</note></header>

At the waters of the Jordan, the Lord seeks the nourishing washing of Baptism from his servant, John. He did this so that he might become the author of all perfection for everyone, and to teach us the same through this life-giving law. <note>Translated from the original Latin verse.</note>

### John baptizes Christ at the Jordan.

The Savior, in the waters of the famous Jordan,
Wishes to receive baptism from his Precursor; <note>The "Precursor" refers to John the Baptist, who preceded Jesus to prepare his way.</note>
This example shows the whole human race
That for salvation, one must be washed in the same way.

### Christ is baptized by John the Baptist.

He who washes us with his blood, let himself be washed with water,
And taught us by his actions to follow his commandments:
God speaks: "This is my Son, my delight; listen to him."
Whoever follows the words of Heaven follows an infallible voice.

### It behooveth us to fulfill all Justice.

Our Lord in Jordan is baptized by John,
Himself requiring that it should be done.
That us by that example he might frame,
As by a wholesome law, to do the same. <note>This section preserves the original 17th-century English translation provided in the source text, with minor spelling adjustments for modern readers.</note>

### Christ is baptized by John at the Jordan.

At the Jordan, Christ desired of John
That he should baptize him, and his request was granted,
Where God the Father declared him his most beloved Son;
Our Lord wished to fulfill all righteousness.
Showing that he was perfect in doing his Father’s will,
He teaches us the meaning of baptism through his own example.

<meta>catchword: While (original Dutch: "Terwyl")</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates and describes the Baptism of Jesus by John the Baptist in the Jordan River. It emphasizes Christ's humility in submitting to the rite as an example for humanity and records the divine recognition of Jesus as the Son of God.</summary>

# MATTHEW 3.

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the baptism of Jesus Christ in the Jordan River. John the Baptist, wearing a simple garment, stands on a rock on the left bank, pouring water from a small vessel over the head of Jesus, who is kneeling in the shallow water. In the sky above, the heavens open with radiant light and clouds, from which a dove representing the Holy Spirit descends. Other figures are visible in the background, along with palm trees and a mountainous landscape.</image-desc>

->Christ is Baptized by John.<-

---

WHILE John was baptizing in the Wilderness of Judea, converting many in those parts and pointing out the dignity of the Person and the Baptism of Jesus Christ, so Jesus came from Galilee to the Jordan to him, to be baptized by him: but John (on account of the humble nature <note>original Dutch: "slegtigheid" – in this period, the word implied simplicity, lowliness, or unworthiness rather than "badness."</note> of his own person) refusing this, is better instructed by Christ, who shows him how it was necessary thus to fulfill all righteousness, or the keeping of the Law <term>Law: the religious and moral laws given to the Israelites in the Old Testament</term>; upon this John lets himself be persuaded and baptizes Jesus; who, having been baptized, climbs up out of the water: But notice in what glory; the Heavens are opened, and the Holy Spirit in the form of a Dove descends and comes upon Jesus; therewith a voice is heard from Heaven, saying: This is my Son, my beloved, in whom I am well pleased.

# Christ, the Comfort of Pious Spouses.

Cana wonders at liquids changed into wine <note>original Latin: "falerno" — a reference to Falernian wine, the most famous and highest-quality wine in the Roman world.</note>; 
The water jar <term>Hydria: a large Greek-style ceramic jar used for carrying water</term> which used to hold water now pours out wine. 
These are comforts for pious spouses, whose 
sad fates Christ lightens with joy.

---

->*The Wedding at Cana.*<-

Christ shows a first sign of his divine power; 
These spouses, honored by his holy presence, 
Feel the effect, and the water changed to wine 
Shows the elements are submitted to his power.

### Christ at the Wedding at Cana in Galilee changes the Water into Wine.

All Cana stands amazed that Christ at her feast 
Makes vineyard-juice that never grew on a vine, 
And can ignite the faint spirit with new desire; 
Where the true Vine-stock <note>A reference to John 15:1, where Jesus says, "I am the true vine."</note> blooms, wine can never be lacking.

---

->*Christ the comfort of pious spouses.*<-

Cana of Galilee admires the change 
Of water into wine; a thing most strange! 
But cease to wonder, Christ is He who has done it, 
Who is accustomed to assist holy wedlock.

### Christ changes the Water into Wine.

The well-water is turned into good wine, 
The noble grape-juice increases in the water jar, 
Through which Christ the Lord clearly proves His divinity: 
That He will not leave pious spouses in poverty, 
But will likewise change their sadness into joy, 
Just as He still grants His help and assistance today.

# Christ, the Comfort of Pious Spouses.

Cana wonders at liquids changed into wine <note>original Latin: "falerno" — a reference to Falernian wine, the most famous and highest-quality wine in the Roman world.</note>; 
The water jar <term>Hydria: a large Greek-style ceramic jar used for carrying water</term> which used to hold water now pours out wine.

sad fates Christ lightens with joy.

->*The Wedding at Cana.*<-

Christ shows a first sign of his divine power; 
These spouses, honored by his holy presence, 
Feel the effect, and the water changed to wine 
Shows the elements are submitted to his power.

### Christ at the Wedding at Cana in Galilee changes the Water into Wine.

All Cana stands amazed that Christ at her feast 
Makes vineyard-juice that never grew on a vine, 
And can ignite the faint spirit with new desire; 
Where the true Vine-stock <note>A reference to John 15:1, where Jesus says, "I am the true vine."</note> blooms, wine can never be lacking.

---

->*Christ the comfort of pious spouses.*<-

Cana of Galilee admires the change 
Of water into wine; a thing most strange!

Who is accustomed to assist holy wedlock.

### Christ changes the Water into Wine.

The well-water is turned into good wine, 
The noble grape-juice increases in the water jar, 
Through which Christ the Lord clearly proves His divinity: 
That He will not leave pious spouses in poverty, 
But will likewise change their sadness into joy, 
Just as He still grants His help and assistance today.

---

Among all the wonders that our Savior <note>original: "Zaligmaker"</note> has performed to powerfully prove and confirm his teaching—that it was not his own, but rather from the one who sent him, namely the Father—the first significant miracle occurred at the wedding in Cana in Galilee on the third day, where he turned water into wine. In this way, he expressed the improvement he brought through the teaching of the Gospel, and showed that he is the one who satisfies weary souls <note>original: "dronken maakt" — literally "makes drunk," here used in the spiritual sense of being filled to overflowing with divine joy</note> and causes the new wine of the Gospel to overflow richly for the refreshment of all who are weary and burdened. Through this miraculous deed, Christ taught that marriage, when begun in his name, may indeed be tempered and softened with the wine of joy, and that a child of God may use the things of this world and be merry, provided they are not abused.

<header>Christ, the fountain of living water.</header>

**C**hrist asks for a drink from the fountain's water from a Samaritan woman, 
To whom he tells the hidden sins of her life. 
The **Fountain** of leaping water <note>original Latin: "Fons salientis aquæ" — a reference to John 4:14, describing water "welling up" or "springing up" to eternal life.</note> (is it not a wondrous thing?) was thirsty, 
He from whose body the living water flows.

### *Conversation of Jesus Christ with the Samaritan Woman.*

> **T**he source of living water, which never runs dry,

### Christ rests by Jacob’s Well and desires a drink from a Samaritan Woman.

### The Samaritan Woman gives Christ to drink.

> To the Lord, who was aware of her secret deeds and life, 
> Who teaches and instructs her rightly in prayer; 
> Is it not a wondrous thing? He who desires the water 
> Is the one who refreshes, moistens, quickens, and nourishes all things. 
> • The eternal Source, from which the Water of Life flows.

<meta>This page transitions from the poetic verses on page 14 to a prose narrative and commentary on the same event. # JOHN 4.

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>JOHN 4.</header>

<image-desc>A black and white engraving depicting the biblical scene of Jesus at the well. Jesus is seated on the right, gesturing toward a woman standing on the left who holds a large water jar. They are positioned by a stone well with a pulley system. In the background, visible through a large stone arch, is a city with towers and several distant figures. A caption at the bottom of the illustration reads "Christ and the Woman of Samaria."</image-desc>

->Christ and the Woman of Samaria.<-

AFTER the instruction that Christ had given to Nicodemus <note>Nicodemus was a Pharisee and member of the Sanhedrin who visited Jesus by night, as recorded in John 3.</note>, he returned again from Judea to Galilee through Samaria, where he (being weary) rested by the **Well of Jacob**, located not far from the City of Sychar in Samaria; while Jesus is sitting here, a Samaritan Woman comes to draw water, of which Christ asks a drink, thirsting not so much for the outward water, as for the conversion of many souls: on this occasion, he then kindly instructs the woman how one must worship **God** (since he is a Spirit) in the **Spirit and Truth**, teaching her to move away from the preference for specific places to serve God <note>This refers to the dispute between Jews and Samaritans over whether God should be worshipped in Jerusalem or on Mount Gerizim.</note>, to which the Jews (by the command of Moses until the time of the Messiah) and the Samaritans (through superstition and apostasy) were bound, and primarily teaching her of the **living water**, which he gives to all Believers, after which no one (if they have once tasted it) thirsts anymore, whereas on the contrary the natural water, which he desired from her, quenches no thirst but for a time.

<summary>This page provides a prose commentary on John 4, explaining Jesus' journey through Samaria and his spiritual teaching to the woman at Jacob's Well regarding "living water" and true worship.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus, Samaritan Woman, Jacob's Well, Sychar, Living Water, Spirit and Truth, worship, salvation, Nicodemus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of events in John 4. Following his encounter with the Samaritan woman, Jesus returns to Cana in Galilee, where he performs his second miracle—healing the son of a royal official. This page provides poetic summaries of the miracle in multiple languages, all emphasizing the connection between physical healing and the growth of spiritual faith.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>Who is the true Physician of soul and body.</header>
<meta>catchword: Christ,</meta>

An intermittent fever <note>original: "querquetra febris," likely referring to a recurring or quartan fever</note> afflicts the official's boy;
The terrified father prays for help from Christ:
The Lord restores both life and health to the son,
And kindles the father's small faith with fire. <note>original: Latin verse</note>

---

-> *The Faith of the Official.* <- <note>The original French text uses "Centenier" (Centurion), though the Gospel of John describes the man as a "basilikos" or royal official.</note>

The official's son receives his healing;
Christ says to him, "Your son lives"; his soul is overjoyed;
He believes without hesitation, as does all his household,
And returning home, he finds his son alive.

### A Royal Courtier prays to Christ, who makes his Son healthy.

The official <note>original: "Hooft-man"</note> seems to fear the death of his son out of anxiety;
And begs Him who can heal with words alone;
A heathen heart does not trust God without signs;
But Christian faith believes, even before it sees wonders.

---

-> *Who is the true Physician of soul and body.* <-

A certain Ruler's son is near death;
To Christ the father does apply himself
To cure him; who He straightway restores to health,
And through belief the father gains the soul's wealth.

### Christ makes the Nobleman's son healthy.

The son of the nobleman <note>original: "kleinen Königs," literally "little king," a term for a royal official or petty ruler</note> is plagued by fever;
The father, full of anxiety, laments his distress to the Lord;
Christ, without any burden, helps both of them freely,
For He quickly cures the son from the danger of death,
Strengthens and confirms the father's weak faith,
And shows that He is the Physician of both body and soul.

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>JOHN 4.</header>
<meta>Illustration number: 8; catchword: Since <note>original: "Cum"</note></meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a biblical scene from the Gospel of John. On the left, a Roman officer (Hoveling/Hooftman) in classical armor with a plumed helmet kneels before Jesus Christ, leaning on a long spear. Behind the officer are several soldiers and a glimpse of a city in the distance. Jesus stands in the center-right, gesturing toward the officer. He is followed by a group of disciples. The setting is a landscape with water in the background and a grove of trees on the right. The entire scene is enclosed in a rectangular frame with the caption below.</image-desc>

->The Centurion. <note>original: "de Hooftman over Hondert," literally "the captain over a hundred." While the text calls him a courtier, the artist depicts him as a Roman soldier, likely conflating this story with the centurion in Matthew 8.</note><-

Christ, having come into Galilee—where he had previously turned water into wine—was met by a Royal Official <note>original: "Hoveling," meaning a courtier or member of the royal court</note> whose son lay at the point of death. This man begged Christ that he would please come down to heal his son, and to do so with all haste, as the child was dying. However, Christ (the true Physician <note>original: "Genees-meester"</note> of body and soul), having first rebuked this man and all the Jews with him for their lack of faith—as those who would not believe unless they saw signs and wonders—spoke but a single word, saying to him: "Go your way; your son lives." The Official, believing the word of Christ, returned home and found (just as Christ had told him) his son healthy and recovered at the very same hour Christ said to him, "Your son lives." Through this miracle, this man and his entire household were converted.

## Christ heals the lame man at the Pool of Bethesda
<note>original Latin: "Christus claudum ad Piscinam Bethesdae sanat"</note>

With this cast-down lame man, place your salvation in Christ,
And the healing stream <note>original: "vena," literally "vein" or "conduit."</note> of Siloam <note>The author refers to the Pool of Siloam, another famous pool in Jerusalem, perhaps associating it with the Pool of Bethesda mentioned in the heading.</note> is present for you.
Truly happy are those following the commands of Christ!
To us, nothing ought to be dearer than this.

### *Jesus Christ heals the Lame Man of Bethesda.*
<note>original French: "Jesus Christ guerit le Boiteux de Bethesda."</note>

The lame man, who often sees the Angel descend,
Remains there for lack of help, without being cured:
Good Jesus sees him, and moved by mercy,
Causes him to be healed, and he rises at His command.

## Christ heals a man who was ill for thirty-eight years.
<note>original Dutch: "Christus geneest een agt-endertigjarige Kranke."</note>

An invalid, exhausted and completely broken by illness,
Stands up and carries his bed at Christ’s spoken word.
The wicked Pharisees, blinded to the Divine light,
Reject Zion's comfort, God's most beloved Child.

### *Christ cures the powerless man at the Pool of Bethesda.*
<note>original English: "Christ cureth the impotent man at the Pool of Bethesda." This section appears in early modern English in the original manuscript.</note>

Christ full of mercy makes the poor man whole <note>original: "whool"</note>,
That lay a-waiting at Bethesda's Pool;
For all diseases he can readily cure,
His medicine <note>original: "Phyſick"</note> being infallible and most sure.

## Christ makes a sick man healthy.
<note>original German: "Christus macht einen Krancken gesundt."</note>

He who for thirty-eight years was broken by sickness
Stands up and carries his bed, through Christ’s spoken word.
The wicked Pharisees, entirely blind to the Divine light,
Reject their Comfort and God’s dearest Child.

**Vocabulary used in this text:**
Bethesda <note>A pool in Jerusalem believed to have healing powers when its waters were stirred by an angel.</note>, Siloam <note>A rock-cut pool in Jerusalem, often associated with healing in the Gospels.</note>, Lame Man <note>original: "Boiteux"</note>, Pharisees <note>A Jewish sect known for strict adherence to traditional laws, often depicted as Christ's critics.</note>, Medicine <note>original: "Phyſick"</note>, Sick/Invalid <note>original: "Krancken"</note>, Entirely <note>original: "gantſch"</note>.

# JOHN 5:9

A detailed engraving depicting the healing at the Pool of Bethesda. On the left, Jesus stands gesturing toward a man who is kneeling on the ground and lifting his mat. In the background and around them, other infirm individuals are resting near the water under large classical stone columns and arches. Below the illustration is a text caption within the same frame.

->Christ Heals an Illness of 38 Years.<-

---

So it was that there in Jerusalem at the Sheep Gate <note>The "Sheep Gate" was an entrance to the city near the Temple, where sheep intended for sacrifice were brought in.</note> was a certain healing pool, which in Hebrew was called Bethesda <note>Bethesda (Hebrew: *Beit Hesda*) translates to "House of Mercy."</note>, in which lay a great multitude of sick, blind, and crippled people. Among these was one who had lain there for thirty-eight years. When the Lord Christ came there at a certain time, He found this sick man, and being moved with inner compassion for him, He asked the sick man whether he truly wished to be healthy. The man answered Him immediately, making it sufficiently known that he did indeed wish to become healthy, but that there was no one to cast him into the pool when the water was stirred by the Angel. Whereupon the Lord Christ then said to him:
<margin>n,</margin> "Rise up, take your little bed, and carry it to your house." The sick man, believing, did just as the Lord had commanded him,
<margin>en.</margin> and was immediately healed of his illness, becoming strong and in good health <note>original: "wel te pas," an idiomatic expression meaning to be in good spirits or physical condition.</note>. However, Christ finding him later, exhorted him that he should take great care not to sin again, so that he might not thereby fall into a worse affliction than that which he had been in.

# JOHN 5:9

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the biblical scene of Jesus healing the paralytic at the Pool of Bethesda. Jesus stands on the left, gesturing towards a man who is stooping to pick up his mat. ->Christ Heals an Illness of 38 Years.<-

<margin>and,</margin>
<margin>and.</margin>
Since there in **Jerusalem** at the **Sheep Gate** was a certain **Pool** <note>original: "Bad-water"</note>, which in **Hebrew** was called **Bethesda**, in which lay a great multitude of **sick**, **blind**, and **lame** people, there was among these one who had lain there for thirty-eight years; and at a certain time the Lord **Christ** coming there, finds this **Sick Man**, and being moved with inner compassion for him, asks the **Sick One** if he would like to be made well. He immediately answered Him, making it sufficiently known that he indeed wished to become well, but that there was no one to throw him into the Pool when it was stirred by the **Angel**: whereupon the Lord **Christ** then said to him: Arise, take up your **Small Bed** <term>Beddeken: A diminutive form of bed, referring to a simple sleeping mat or pallet.</term>, and carry it to your house. But **Christ** finding him later, warned him that he should be careful not to sin again, so that he might not fall into a worse affliction than that which he had been in.

# Christ, King of Life and Death.
<note>original Latin: "Christus Rex vitæ & mortis."</note>

> **P**ale death had stolen the light from the widow’s son:
> The Lord [found her] weeping; but He forbade her to cry.
> "Arise," He says, and touches the coffin; death, being driven away, recedes,
> Life returns: these are the deeds of the true God.
> <note>original Latin: "Abstulerat viduæ Nato mors pallida lucem..."</note>

### *Christ the King of life & death.*
<note>original French: "Christ le Roi de vie & mort."</note>

> **T**his widow, losing her dear and only son,
> Whom barbarous and iniquitous death snatched from her:
> Christ, arriving, said to her, "Woman, do not weep":
> Then, touching the casket, His life-giving word
> Makes this dead man rise, whereby the overjoyed mother
> Recognizes that Jesus is Lord of the passing.
> <note>original French: "Cette vêve perdant son cher fils & unique..."</note>

### Christ raises a Widow's Son from death at Nain.
<note>original Dutch: "Christus verwekt van de dood eener Weduwen Zoon tot Nain."</note>

> **C**ease now, you widowed woman, you have moved your Lord,
> Through the sighs from your heart and the tears from your eyes,
> Your son is healthy again, He helps you in your need;
> For Jesus is a Lord of Life and Death.
> <note>original Dutch: "Hou op gy Weduwvrouw, gy hebt uw Heer bewogen..."</note>

### *Christ King of Life and Death.*

> **D**eath did despoil the widow's son of life;
> Christ found her weeping, and overcharged with grief
> He bid her weep not, and the bier <note>A stand on which a corpse or coffin is placed.</note> doth touch,
> The man revives: He is God that doth thus much.

### Christ brings the youth at Nain to life.
<note>original German: "Christus macht den Jüngling zu Nain lebendig."</note>

> **T**he widow’s only son is carried to the churchyard
> Outside the city of Nain; the mother makes great lamentation,
> The Lord comforts her, and speaks, saying she has no need of weeping,
> Thereupon He touches the coffin: "Stand up, young man, without delay!"
> He sits up; there death must make room for life.
> Such a deed proves that Christ is the true God.
> <note>original German: "Der Witwen ein'ger Sohn zum Kirchhoff wird getragen..."</note>

<meta>catchword: Christus</meta>

<note>Vocabulary from the original: Jesus, Nain, resurrection, widow's son, death, life.</note>

LUKE 7:10
<note>The heading cites verse 10, though the primary story of the Widow of Nain begins at Luke 7:11.</note>

->*Christ raises a widow’s son.*<-

---

Having healed the man with the palsy <note>original Dutch: "Gigt-zieke," which literally translates to "gout-sick," but historically referred to paralysis or the "sick of the palsy" mentioned in the Gospels.</note>, Christ went on to heal many other sick and possessed people among a great multitude, as well as a Centurion’s servant <note>original: "Hooft-mans Knegt"</note> in Capernaum. After performing these and other miracles, he traveled to the city of Nain. As he reached the city gate, he saw a dead man being carried out; he was the only son of his mother, and she followed behind him weeping. At this sight, Christ was moved with inward compassion and, touching the bier <note>A wooden frame used to carry a coffin or a body to the grave.</note>, he said: "Young man, I say to you, arise." And behold, the dead man became alive and began to speak. The onlookers, seeing this with great wonder, praised God, saying: "A great prophet has risen among us," and "God has visited his people." From this, one can see with what great justice and truth David testified of Christ, saying that with Him are the escapes from death <note>A reference to Psalm 68:20: "to God the Lord belong the issues from death."</note>.

<meta>catchword: Pecca-</meta>

### The one who comes to me I will not cast out.
<note>original Latin: "Venientem ad me non ejiciam foras," a quotation of John 6:37.</note>

The sinful woman, while the Lord dines in Simon's house,
    Wets His legs and feet with a flood of tears;
She wipes them dry with her hair—which once attracted lovers—
    And because she loved much, she is released from her crimes.
<note>original Latin: "Peccatrix Domino... crimina nulla luit." In the Catholic tradition of the time, the "sinner" in this story was almost always identified as Mary Magdalene.</note>

---

->*A woman pouring a precious ointment upon the feet of the Lord.*<-

While Christ is eating at Simon’s home, a woman arrives,
    Who washes His feet, wipes them, and kisses them.
Then the memory of her sins comes to her;
    She weeps for them, and Jesus comforts and calms her.

---

->A great sinner washes Christ’s feet with her tears.<-

Simon, you judge yourself; can you not bear
    That the loosened hair, which once pleased lovers,
Now serves as a cloth for His feet? Know that God hates no one
    Who practices new virtues and leaves old sins behind.
<note>This Dutch verse directly addresses Simon the Pharisee, who in the biblical account was offended that Jesus allowed a "sinful woman" to touch Him.</note>

---

->*Him that cometh unto me I will not cast forth.*<-

Mary the sinner meets Christ at His meal
    In Simon's house, and with tears wets His feet,
And wipes them with her hair, which lately were snares of lust;
    She loves so much Him who forgives her sins.
<note>The original English text uses the word "ginnes," an archaic term meaning traps or snares.</note>

---

->A great sinner washes the feet of Christ.<-

The great sinner wets the feet of the Lord Christ
    With hot tears, and dries them with her hair;
She anoints them with balsam oil in the Pharisee’s house,
    And thereby testifies to her repentance; and because she loved much,
Christ praises this deed and forgives her many sins.
    He who comes to Him with repentance, He does not cast out.

A woman (Mary Magdalene) is kneeling on the floor, washing Jesus' feet with her tears and drying them with her long hair. A small jar (an alabaster flask) sits on the floor next to her. The indoor setting features draped curtains and classical architectural elements.</image-desc>

->The Washing of the Feet by Mary Magdalene.<-
<note>In the historical context of this manuscript, the unnamed "sinful woman" from the Gospel of Luke was commonly identified as Mary Magdalene.</note>

AFTER Christ had restored the life of the dead Son at the gate of the City of Nain, he was invited to a meal by one of the Pharisees, named Simon. Christ did not refuse this invitation but went with him into his House. A sinful woman living in that same City, learning of this, went to Christ in that House and wet his feet with her tears; kissing them, drying them with her hair, and anointing them with the ointment she had brought with her in an alabaster flask <term>alabaster flask: a precious vessel made of fine-grained, translucent stone, typically used for storing expensive perfumes or oils</term>. The Pharisee, seeing this, was offended by it and concluded within himself that He could not be a Prophet. Christ, knowing his thoughts, convinced him through a parable, justified the actions of this woman, and openly declared that her sins—which were many—were forgiven. How truly blessed are all sinners who turn themselves back to God!

# The one who comes to me I will not cast out.
<note>original Latin: "Venientem ad me non ejiciam foras." This is a quote from the Gospel of John 6:37, used here as a thematic header.</note>

She dries them with the hair with which she once enticed lovers,
And because she loved much, she is cleansed of all her crimes.

After Christ had restored life to the dead son at the gate of the city of Nain, he was invited to dinner by one of the Pharisees named Simon. Christ did not refuse but went with him into his house. A sinful woman living in that same city, <note>In the early modern period, this "sinful woman" was traditionally identified as Mary Magdalene, though the biblical text does not explicitly name her as such.</note> learning of this, went to Christ in that house and wetted his feet with her tears; she kissed them, dried them with her hair, and anointed them with ointment she had brought with her in an alabaster flask. <note>An alabaster flask or "box" was a high-status vessel used to hold expensive perfumed oils.</note> Seeing this, the Pharisee was offended and concluded within himself that Jesus could not be a prophet. How blessed then are all sinners who turn themselves toward God!

# Truth begets hatred.
<note>original Latin: "Veritas odium parit." A famous aphorism from the Roman playwright Terence, often used in religious contexts to describe the fate of prophets.</note>

The Baptist condemns adultery and forbidden marriages,
And for this reason, he enters the prison caves of Machaerus.
<note>Machaerus was a hilltop fortress in modern-day Jordan where, according to the historian Josephus, John the Baptist was imprisoned and executed.</note>
By the craft of Herodias, his neck is severed;
This was the reward for justice and piety.
<note>original Latin: "Damnat adulterium & vetitos Baptista hymenæos, / Inde Machærunti carceris antra subit. / Huic Herodiadis cervix præciditur astu; / Justitiæ hæc merces & pietatis erat."</note>

<image-desc>A faint engraving to the right of the middle section depicts Salome standing and receiving the severed head of John the Baptist on a platter from an executioner or attendant within an architectural setting.</image-desc>

->*John the Baptist beheaded.*<-
<note>original French: "Jean le Baptiste decollé."</note>

*For having condemned the impudent adultery,*
*The greatest of humans born upon the earth*
*Was thrown by Herod into the dark prison:*
*Then the infamous woman, by a clever design,*
<note>original French: "l'infame putain." In the 17th century, this term was used harshly to condemn Herodias’s character and her marriage to her husband's brother.</note>
*Had the holy head of the Baptist removed in the dungeons:*
*The King too late repents of a senseless oath.*

->John the Baptist was beheaded by Herod’s command.<-

Herod allows himself to be so pleased by the wanton dance,
That she may carry away the head of the Baptist as a reward;
Though an unthinking and wicked promise creates a debt of guilt,
Yet a greater sin is committed when that promise is fulfilled.
<note>This Dutch verse reflects a common theological argument: it is better to break a sinful oath than to commit a murder to keep it.</note>

->*Truth begets hatred.*<-

*Herod’s adulterous marriage John does check;*
*Herod imprisons him, his wrath to wreak.*
*Herodias by a wile gets off his head,*
*This reward Truth and goodness ever had.*

->The head of John the Baptist is struck off.<-

The King’s conscience is stirred regarding his incest,
Because he had lured away his brother’s wife;
John was placed in the dungeon for this reason.
Herodias, with cunning, ensures that after several days
The head is struck off from the precious man of God;
Whoever speaks only the truth carries such a reward away.
<note>The German text uses the term "blutschand" (incest/disgrace of blood) to describe Herod Antipas marrying Herodias, who was the wife of his half-brother.</note>

->*John beheaded by Herod.*<-

In the preceding section, it was stated that Christ, when he had heard of the death of John, withdrew across the Sea of Galilee; this John was cast into prison and thereafter killed because he had rebuked Herod, telling him that it was not lawful for him to have his brother’s wife.
<note>The "wife" refers to Herodias, who had been married to Herod’s brother, Philip. John’s condemnation of this marriage as incestuous under Jewish law is what provoked Herod's anger.</note>
For Herod, on his birthday, preparing a great feast, had invited all the leaders and most prominent men in Galilee; and sitting with them at the feast, he took such delight in the dance of Herodias’s daughter (for she, entering, danced before him) that he said to the young girl: "Ask of me whatever you wish, and I will give it to you."
<note>Herodias's daughter is traditionally identified as Salome, though she is not named in the biblical text.</note>
She, going out and telling her mother this, at her mother’s instigation demanded the head of John; and thus this holy man was robbed of his life because he had boldly spoken the truth.

# 26

# My servants shall eat and shall be satisfied.
<note>original Latin: "Servi mei edent & saturabuntur."</note>

Five thousand men, whom long hunger tortured after their fasting, behold, he feeds with five loaves. In this way, Christ teaches that he feeds whoever tirelessly loves the words of eternal salvation.
<note>original Latin: "Millia quinque virûm, quos post jejunia torsit / Longa fames, quinis panibus, ecce, cibat. / Hac ratione docet Christus se pascere, quisquis / Impiger æternæ verba salutis amat."</note>

->*Jesus Christ gives food to five thousand men with five loaves.*<-
<note>original French: "Jesus Christ donne à manger à cinq mille hommes avec cinq pains."</note>

Five thousand men filled by five loaves in the Desert,
*Testify to the Lord's divine power;*
*Each of them had suffered greatly from hunger,*
*Each has bread in sufficiency to eat.*
<note>original French: "Cinq mille hommes repûs de cinq pains au Desert, / Témoignent du Seigneur la divine puissance; / Chacun d'eux de la faim avoit beaucoup souffert, / Chacun a pour manger, du pain à suffisance."</note>

### Christ feeds five thousand men with five loaves and two fish.
<note>original Dutch: "Christus spyzygt met vyf Broden en twee Visschen vyt duizend Mannen."</note>

The disciples share the bread, each may enjoy their desire,
And more than there was shall still be left over;
So the body is fed; whoever acts according to God's lessons,
Satisfies his hungry soul with His Word.
<note>original Dutch: "De Jong'ren deelen 't Brood, elk mag zyn lust genieten, / En meerder dan daar was, zal daar nog overschieten; / Zo word het Lyf gespyst, wie na Gods lessen doet, / Verzadigt met zyn Woord zyn hongerig gemoet."</note>

->*My servants shall eat and shall be filled.*<-

Five thousand hungry men our Lord hath fed
*With five small loaves all are satisfied.*
*Bread feeds not man alone; for Christ's own word*
*Can unto man eternal food afford.*

### Christ feeds five thousand people.
<note>original German: "Christus speist fünfftausend Menschen."</note>

Five thousand men of the people, who had listened to him for a long time,
Christ honors with a banquet of five loaves;
There was enough and in abundance, so that there was still some left over.
Whoever gladly hears God's Word and loves it from the heart,
The Lord feeds that same person and gives them everything they need,
So that they have no lack and are satisfied with all good things.
<note>original German: "Des Volks fünfftausend Mann, das ihm langh zugehöret, / Christus mit ein Bancket von fünff Brodten verehret, / Da war g'nug und vollauff, dass man noch übrig hat. / Wer Gottes Wort gern hört, dasselb von Hertzen liebet, / Denselben speist der Herr, ihm alle Nodtdurfft giebet, / Dass er kein mangel hat, wird aller Güter sat."</note>

# JOHN 6.
<note>original Latin: "IOANNIS. 6."</note>
<meta>113</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the biblical scene of the miracle of the five loaves and two fishes. In the foreground, Jesus stands with his hands raised in blessing over baskets of food. Several disciples and onlookers are gathered around him, some sitting on the ground. In the background, a vast crowd of people covers a hilly landscape under a bright, radiant sky.</image-desc>

->The Miracle of the 5 Barley Loaves.<-

Christ, after he had instructed the people through various parables of the Kingdom of Heaven, and having meanwhile heard of the beheading of John the Baptist, departs across the Sea of Gennesaret <note>Also known as the Sea of Galilee.</note>. However, he is followed by many people; he goes up the mountain and sits there with his disciples, seeing a great multitude coming toward him. Now when the sun was already setting, being moved with compassion for them, he commands them to sit down and blesses the five barley loaves and two fish which a young boy had brought to him. He distributes them to the disciples and to those who were seated; and when they were satisfied, he had the leftover fragments gathered, which were far more than the original five loaves, for the disciples filled twelve baskets with them. From this it can be learned that having much cannot help us if God does not grant His blessing upon it, and that a little is enough when He strengthens the staff of bread <term>The "staff of bread" is a biblical idiom (see Leviticus 26:26) referring to the essential supply of food that sustains human life like a walking staff supports a traveler.</term>.

<meta>Flucti-</meta> <note>This is a "catchword," a printer's tool where the first syllable of the next page is printed at the bottom of the current one to ensure the pages are bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page provides a prose account of Jesus feeding the five thousand, emphasizing that divine blessing is more vital than the quantity of one's resources.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, Miracle of the Loaves and Fish, John the Baptist, Sea of Gennesaret, providence, five thousand, twelve baskets, staff of bread</keywords>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>The Lord supports the weak.</header>
<note>original Latin: "Dominus statuminat imbecillos." In this context, "imbecillos" refers to those who are weak in faith or physical strength.</note>

Into the waves of the sea Christ enters by night,
The pressed water served the purpose of a paved floor.
Peter attempts the same, but plunged into the whirlpool of waters,
He is saved by the Lord’s intervening hand.
<note>original Latin: "Fluctibus ingreditur Christus de nocte marinis / Pressa pavimenti praestitit unda vicem..."</note>

---

## *Jesus Christ walks upon the waters, and takes Peter*
## *by the hand, who wished to do the same.*

In a dark night Christ walks upon the waters,
Peter wishes to do the same, but his faith is not certain,
He sinks, and fear attacks him,
And his master at once takes him and reassures him.

---

## *Christ comes to his Disciples, walking*
## *on the Sea.*

Distrust full of terror had troubled Peter’s heart,
Whereas Christ through his Word had made the water like a floor,
But since weak Faith needs the comfort of Heaven,
So the hand and firm help are immediately offered.

---

## *God supporteth the weak.*

Christ walks by night upon the sea's soft waves,
Peter to do the like his license craves;
Yet as he it attempts, he sinks like sand,
But Christ supports him with his holy hand.

---

## *Peter walks upon the Sea.*

Christ walks upon the sea without any difficulty,
The flood must carry him like the firmest earth,
Peter does the same, although his faith was small,
As he comes upon the water, he soon begins to sink,
Christ offers him his hand, or else he must have drowned,

## *The Lord helps those who are weak in faith.*

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>MATTHEW 14:14 <note>Though the header cites verse 14, the text describes the narrative starting from verse 22, regarding the walking on water.</note></header>

<image-desc>A rectangular copperplate engraving depicting the biblical scene of Jesus walking on water from Matthew 14. In the foreground, Christ stands serenely on the surface of the turbulent, wavy sea, his head surrounded by a radiant halo. To his left, the apostle Peter is shown sinking into the waves, reaching out toward Jesus. In the background to the left, a small boat with several figures—the other disciples—is tossed by the rough water. The sky is dark with storm clouds, though light emanates from around Christ.</image-desc>

->*Christ walks on the Sea.*<-

Christ, having fed over five thousand people in the wilderness (where he had gone after hearing of the beheading of John <note>Referring to John the Baptist</note>) with five loaves and two fish until they were satisfied, made his disciples sail over to the other side, while he dismissed the crowd. The disciples, now being in the middle of the sea, fell into great distress. Christ, having meanwhile sent the crowd away and prayed on the mountain, came to them during the fourth watch of the night <note>The Roman division of time between 3:00 AM and 6:00 AM</note>, walking on the sea, and made himself known to them. Peter, seeing this, desired to come to Christ, which Christ allowed him to do; however, becoming afraid of the wind, he began to sink, but was saved by Christ, who calmed the storm. In this same way, God allows his Church to struggle with the storms of all kinds of misfortunes in this world; yet he does not fail to come to her at the last to provide her help.

<page-num>30</page-num>

# Faith is the victory of believers. <note>original Latin: *Fides victoria credentium*</note>

->Christ<-

While the Phoenician woman <note>A reference to the woman from the region of Tyre and Sidon</note> wearies the Lord with her prayers, she obtains what she seeks through her Herculean Faith. Having come to grapple with God, she wrestles and triumphs as a victor; nourishing Faith says: whatever you wish, ask; believe, and you shall receive. <note>translated from the original Latin verse</note>

### *Faith, the Victory of the Faithful.* <note>original French: *La Foi victoire des Fideles*</note>

The most constant faith of the Canaanite woman
Wrestled so much with Christ, that her own firmness
Won out over his frequent refusals,
Three times beginning again her constant prayer.
So that she finally departs happily content,
Showing us that faith does not suffer refusal.

### **The Canaanite Woman prays to Christ, who makes her possessed daughter healthy.**

A Canaanite woman, cast down to the earth,
Persists all the more, the more she is refused.
A zealous request can overcome everything;
An Israelite's Faith is found even beyond Israel's borders. <note>Refers to the woman being a Gentile (non-Jew) yet demonstrating the kind of faith expected of God's chosen people.</note>

### *Faith the victory of believers.*

The Canaanite woman so to Christ does sue,
That what she asks of him he is forced to give,
She wrestles with him, and overcomes at last;
Believe, and ask of him: he will grant as fast. <note>This section was originally written in 17th-century English; spelling has been modernized for clarity.</note>

### **The Canaanite Woman prays to Christ, and he makes her daughter healthy.**

The Canaanite woman with much weeping and lamenting,
Runs and cries after Christ, yet she is turned away;
She pays no mind to that, but presses him hard with her plea,
Until she is heard; Faith wrestles firmly with God,
Hold on until it is granted and brings the victory;
Believe and ask what you will, you shall obtain it at last.

<summary>This page features five poetic variations in different languages regarding the biblical story of the Canaanite woman’s persistent faith. The verses emphasize that through determined prayer and "wrestling" with God, a believer can overcome any refusal.</summary>

<keywords>Canaanite woman, persistence in prayer, Matthew 15, healing of the possessed daughter, Phoenician woman, power of faith, wrestling with God</keywords>

--- Page 289 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 30, shifting from the general concept of faith to the specific biblical account of the Canaanite woman found in Matthew 15. It focuses on the persistence of prayer and the rewarding of a faith that refuses to be discouraged.</meta>

MATTHEW 15.

<page-num>31</page-num>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a biblical scene where Jesus Christ, standing with a group of disciples on a raised architectural platform, addresses a woman kneeling before him in a gesture of supplication. The setting includes classical columns and a balustrade overlooking a landscape with trees. This illustrates the story of the Canaanite woman's faith.</image-desc>

->The Canaanite woman healed by Christ<-

**C**hrist, having crossed over and coming with His disciples into the Land of Gennesaret <note>A fertile plain on the western shore of the Sea of Galilee</note>, heals many who were sick. From there, traveling to the regions of Tyre and Sidon <note>Ancient maritime cities in modern-day Lebanon, then considered Gentile (non-Jewish) territory</note>, He was approached by a Canaanite woman whose daughter was miserably possessed by a demon <note>original Dutch: *Duivel* (Devil)</note>. Pleading for relief for her daughter, she was refused by Him three times <note>In the Gospel account, Jesus first remains silent, then states He was only sent to Israel, and finally tests her with a metaphor about "dogs" and "children's bread"</note> in order to test her faith. Yet, as she persisted with equal strength, she finally received this comforting answer from Christ: "Woman, great is your faith; let it be done for you as you desire." And her daughter was made healthy from that very hour. In this miracle, Christ confirms in deed what He had taught regarding prayer: "Ask, and it shall be given to you; seek, and you shall find; knock, and it shall be opened unto you."

<page-num>32</page-num>
# Who is our neighbor?

**A** crowd of robbers robs and beats the traveler; neither the Priest <note>original Latin: *Sacrificus*, referring to the Temple priests who performed sacrifices</note> nor the Levite <note>a member of the Hebrew tribe of Levi who assisted in the Temple</note> offers him any aid. But a stranger’s hand tends and lifts the wretched man; he who is in need of help is your neighbor. <note>original Latin: *Qui caret auxilio proximus ille tibi est*</note>

->*Who is our true neighbor.*<-

**S**ee the pious duty of the Good Samaritan; he finds a wounded Jew, whom a Levite abandons. Immediately, he fulfills the duty of a neighbor; what a Priest refuses, a stranger provides.

->*The parable of a wounded Man helped by a*<-
->*Samaritan.*<-

**T**HUS on the world’s field lay the world, entirely wounded, before God’s ointment of grace had bound its wounds. Neither Priest nor Levite could be of help to us; but a Samaritan <note>In this Dutch verse, the author uses an allegory common in the 17th century: the wounded man represents fallen humanity, and the Samaritan represents Christ, who provides the "ointment of grace" that the old law (the Priest and Levite) could not provide</note> had to heal us in the end.

->*Who is our neighbor.*<-

**T**hieves wound a traveler and take off his coat; a Priest and Levite see him, yet help him not. A stranger binds him up and cares for him; he is the neighbor, while the others remain strangers.

->*A Samaritan helps the wounded man.*<-

**A** poor wanderer was found by murderers in the forest; they took what he had and wounded him mortally besides. They stripped him naked (a thing that is not new in these times <note>original German: *ein Ding das jetzt nicht new* — likely a contemporary reference to the dangers of travel or the lawlessness during the period the book was printed</note>). A Priest and Levite left him lying helpless; a stranger does him good, showing love beyond measure, and thus makes it understood that he is his neighbor.

<meta>catchword N</meta>

<summary>This page uses the Parable of the Good Samaritan to illustrate that "neighborliness" is defined by acts of mercy rather than religious office. It presents this lesson through polyglot verses in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German.</summary>
<keywords>Parable of the Good Samaritan, neighborly love, mercy, Priest, Levite, religious duty, allegory of Christ, 17th-century poetry</keywords>

# LUKE 10.

<image-desc>An etched illustration depicting the parable of the Good Samaritan. In the foreground, the Samaritan is kneeling over the injured man, tending to his wounds. A horse stands patiently beside them. The scene is set on a path through a rocky, wooded landscape with a large cliff on the left. In the middle distance, a small figure (likely the priest or Levite) walks away from the scene. In the far distance, a city or castle is perched on a hill.</image-desc>

->The Compassionate Samaritan.<-

**A**fter Christ had sent out the seventy disciples to preach the Gospel <note>original Dutch: *Euangelium*</note>, a scholar of the law <term>Scholar of the law: A man expert in the religious and civil laws of Moses</term> comes to him, and wishing to test Christ, asks what he must do to inherit eternal life; to which the Savior <term>Savior: The Dutch *Zaligmaker* literally means 'one who makes blessed'</term> answers: Love God and your neighbor <note>original: *even-naasten*, literally "equal-nearest"</note> as yourself. The scholar of the law, wishing to justify himself, asks of Christ, who was his neighbor? Christ, answering this, said: A certain man, having fallen into the hands of murderers <note>In this context, the word *Moordenaars* refers to violent robbers or bandits</note>, lay half dead upon the earth, and a certain Priest seeing him lying there, passed by, as did also a Levite; but a certain Samaritan traveling past there, and seeing him, bound his wounds and brought him to an Inn, having him cared for in all things at his own expense. Christ commands the scholar of the law to do likewise, if he wished to fulfill the Law; and teaches us that all people are our neighbors.

<meta>catchword: Collo-</meta>

<summary>This page recounts the Parable of the Good Samaritan as found in the Gospel of Luke, explaining how Jesus used the story to teach a legal scholar that neighborly love must extend to everyone. It contrasts the indifference of the religious elite (the Priest and Levite) with the active mercy of a social outsider (the Samaritan).</summary>

# This is my beloved Son:
<note>original Latin: Hic est Filius meus dilectus</note>

Christ speaks with Moses joined by Elijah,
His face is more radiant than the Sun.
The Father glorifies the Son, and testifies from on high,
This is my Son; let the World listen to him.
<note>original Latin: "Colloquitur Christo Moses conjunctus Eliæ..."</note>

---

### *Conversation of Christ with Moses & Elijah.*

On the summit of a mountain, in the midst of splendors,
Christ, Elijah, and Moses were all three together,
When a voice from heaven spoke, piercing the hearts:
"This is my Son; listen to him, and let all tremble at his Name."
<note>original French: "Entretien de Christ avec Moyse & Elie."</note>

---

### Christ is glorified in the presence of Moses and Elijah.
<note>original Dutch: "Christus word in ’t bywezen van Moses en Elias verheerlykt."</note>

Elijah and the Law, by Heaven’s good pleasure,
Surrender their rights to Christ upon the Mountain;
How his garment glistened in the bright-shining light!
Who can gaze upon the Sun with mortal sight?
<note>In this Dutch verse, "The Law" refers to Moses, who traditionally represents the Mosaic Law, while Elijah represents the Prophets.</note>

---

### *This is my beloved Son.*

With Christ Elijah <note>original: Elias</note> and great Moses talk,
Like the sun his face, his raiment white as chalk.
The Father of Christ from Heaven on high does say:
"This is my Son beloved, him you obey."

---

### Christ is transfigured on the mountain.
<note>original German: "Christus wird auff dem Berg verklärt."</note>

The Lord’s countenance shines brighter than the sun,
His garment whiter than snow; there is much joy and delight;
Moses and Elijah also converse with him.
The Father transfigures him and presents him to us all:
"This is my dear Son, in whom I am well pleased;
Hear him, follow his teaching," He says with a loud voice.
