# MATTHEW 17. 17

->Christ Transfigured on Tabor.<-

Christ, having healed many who were sick, possessed, and others of their ailments, and having also instructed his Disciples in many matters, goes after six days up a high Mountain, taking with him Peter <note>original: Petrum</note>, James <note>original: Jacobum</note>, and John <note>original: Joannem</note>. His countenance shone like the Sun, and his garments became white as Snow, and he was seen by them speaking with Moses and Elijah <note>original: Elias</note>. Peter, in a rapture of mind <note>original Dutch: "verrukking van gedagten," describing a state of spiritual ecstasy or being overwhelmed by the divine vision.</note>, said to Jesus: Lord, it is good that we are here; if you so desire, let us make here three Tabernacles <note>A 'tabernacle' in this biblical context refers to a temporary shelter or booth made of branches, echoing the dwellings used during the Feast of Tabernacles.</note>, one for You, one for Moses, and one for Elijah. While he thus spoke, a luminous cloud overshadowed them, and there was heard a voice, saying: This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased; hear him. The Disciples, struck with awe and being fearful at this, fell upon their faces, but were exhorted by Christ not to fear and to stand up. This they did, and they descended from the Mountain with Christ.

<meta>This page continues from the previous account of the Transfiguration on Mount Tabor, moving from the manifestation of Christ’s glory to a teaching moment regarding the Kingdom of Heaven through the Parable of the Laborers in the Vineyard (Matthew 20:1–16).</meta>

# The first shall be last. <note>original Latin: "Primi erunt novissimi."</note>

To cultivate the vines, at different times the Master <note>original: "Herus," referring to the Lord or householder.</note> sends laborers, promising certain rewards to the men. Though the labor here is unequal, the reward is the same for everyone, for the Lord does these things according to His own will.

->*The first shall be the last.*<-

The Master, having agreed on a price for the last worker he hires, meets other laborers at noon and orders them to go work in the vineyards until evening. Others follow after them; all receive the same wage. Then he says to the grumblers he saw were displeased: "Can I not do with my own goods as I please?"

### The Parable of the Laborers in the Vineyard.

Even as the sun sets, anyone may freely hire themselves out in Heaven’s Vineyard-court, though it be for only a few hours. A surrendered will <note>The Dutch "overgeve wil" describes a heart completely yielded to God's purpose, which the poet suggests is more valuable than the literal duration of work.</note> is weighed against the time; he who served first receives his money last.

->*The first shall be last, etc.*<-

A Husbandman <note>A traditional term for a master of a household or a farmer.</note> does hire at various times
In one day workmen to tend his vines.
Their work is unequal, yet he gives equal hire.
For with his own he may do as he desires.

### The Lord sends Workers into his Vineyard.

The Lord sends workers to labor in His vineyard for a certain wage that He wished to grant them. Some He commissions early, others late in the evening; yet He gives the same reward to the first as to the last, even though some of them grumbled and resisted. God distributes as He wills, regarding no man’s counsel.

<meta>catchword: Christ</meta>

<summary>This page presents the Parable of the Laborers in the Vineyard through poems in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German, illustrating the concept of divine grace where the reward is based on God’s sovereignty rather than the length of service.</summary>
<keywords>Parable of the Laborers, Vineyard, Matthew 20, divine grace, sovereignty of God, equality of reward, Master of the house</keywords>

--- Page 295 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Matthew 20, specifically providing a prose commentary on the Parable of the Laborers in the Vineyard which was introduced with poetic verses and imagery on the preceding page.</meta>

# MATTHEW 20 <note>original Latin: "MATHEI. 20"</note>

<image-desc>A rectangular copperplate etching illustrating the Parable of the Laborers in the Vineyard from Matthew 20. In the foreground, a householder or steward wearing a cap and long robe stands on the right, gesturing toward three laborers on the left. One laborer carries a spade over his shoulder. The background features several Dutch-style buildings, including a tall tower with a clock or sundial. To the right, behind a wooden fence, other laborers work in the vineyard. Below the illustration, there is a centered caption in italics.</image-desc>

->*The Laborers of the Vineyard.*<-

Christ, teaching that no one can inherit the Kingdom of Heaven except those who leave everything for his sake, adds to this—in order to drive out the sluggishness of the flesh <note>This "sluggishness of the flesh" refers to a lack of spiritual zeal or the human tendency toward complacency once a task has begun.</note>—that many who are the first shall be the last. By this, he makes it known that it is not enough to have started well, but that it is necessary to press on diligently. Furthermore, he teaches through a parable <term>parable: a simple story used to illustrate a moral or spiritual lesson</term> of Laborers who, though hired at various hours and sent into the Vineyard, receive equal wages in the evening. This illustrates how no reward from God is given except out of pure grace <note>original: "enkele genade." This emphasizes that divine favor is a free gift rather than a debt owed for labor performed.</note>; so that no injustice is done to anyone when God shows as much favor to the last as if they had been called from the very beginning. And to speak truly, God owes no one anything, and rightly demands all the service it is possible for us to perform.

<sig>Q 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Here (original Latin: "Hic")</meta>

<summary>This page provides a theological commentary on the Parable of the Laborers, explaining that God's rewards are based on divine grace rather than human merit or the duration of service.</summary>
<keywords>Parable of the Laborers, Kingdom of Heaven, Grace, Divine Justice, Diligence, Matthew 20, Spiritual Zeal</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the Parable of the Laborers in the Vineyard on page 37 to a series of poetic reflections on the story of the woman caught in adultery from the Gospel of John, chapter 8. # In that which you judge another, you condemn yourself. <note>original Latin: "In quo judicas alium , condemnas te ipsum." This is a citation of Romans 2:1, emphasizing that those who judge others are often guilty of the same sins.</note>

**Here** a woman caught in shameful adultery <note>original Latin: "Hic in adulterio mulier deprensa pudendo." The story refers to John 8:1–11.</note>
    Is led to Christ, her sin confessed by her blushing.
He does not say, "It is my place to condemn"; but I will make it so that as an avenger
    He who is without crime shall strike her first.

### A woman caught in adultery is presented to Jesus
### Christ. <note>original French: "Une femme surprise en adultere est presentée à Jesus Christ."</note>

***To** the Son of the living God nothing is hidden,*
    *Instead of condemning this adulterous woman;*
*He says to the Jews, he who feels himself without sin,*
*Let him strike her first; and everyone fell silent.*

### The Pharisees bring to Christ a woman 
### found in adultery. <note>original Dutch: "De Phariseen brengen tot Christum een Vrouw in Overspel bevonden."</note>

***O** judges without reason! consider yourselves well,*
    *Whether you demand such punishment for shameful adultery;*
*He whose convicted conscience must call itself guilty,*
    *Let him see that he refrains from condemning others.*

### Wherein thou judgest another, thou condemnest thyself. <note>This section was originally written in early modern English in the source manuscript.</note>

***A** young adultress unto Christ is brought,*
    *To know if stoning her be thought lawful.*
*He bids the sinless at her cast the first stone;*
*Then looking up, he finds they all were gone.*

### The Pharisees present to Christ a woman who
### had committed adultery. <note>original German: "De Phariseen stellen Christum ein Weib für die Ehebruch hatt begangen."</note>

***A** woman overtaken in the act of adultery,*
    *Is led to the Lord, who yet does not judge her,*
*Only with his finger he finely writes this upon the ground:*
*If anyone among you is found without any fault,*
*Let him throw the first stone at her as punishment for the sins;*
    *He who wishes to judge others does not remain unjudged.*

### JOHN 8

<image-desc>An engraving contained within a rectangular frame depicting the biblical scene from John 8 of Jesus and the woman taken in adultery. Jesus is shown in the lower-left foreground, crouching on a tiled floor and writing with his finger. A group of men in traditional robes (scribes and Pharisees) stand around him and a central woman who has her head bowed. The scene is set within a classical architectural hall featuring large stone columns and arches. In the top right corner of the frame is the plate number 19.</image-desc>

->*The Woman in Adultery.*<-

AFTER Christ had spent a long time in Judea and Galilee, he returned to Jerusalem for the Feast of Tabernacles <note>original: "Loof-hutten," referring to Sukkot, the Jewish autumn festival where booths are built to commemorate the wandering in the desert.</note>, where a woman who had been found in adultery was brought to him in the Temple by the scribes and Pharisees, so that he might pass judgment on her; they had done this to find an opportunity to trap him, asking Christ what he judged should be done to the adulteress. Jesus, knowing their thoughts, bowed down and wrote with his finger in the earth: and as they continued to ask him, he stood up and said, "He among you who is without sin, let him cast the first stone at her," and thus he put them to shame in their intentions, and let the woman go with an admonition. By this act, the Savior <term>Savior|Zaligmaker: literally 'one who makes blessed' or 'bringer of salvation'</term> convicts all those who are quick to judge others for their sins and failings, while they themselves are meanwhile stained and contaminated with similar or even worse sins.

<summary>This page recounts the biblical story of Jesus and the woman caught in adultery from the Gospel of John, emphasizing Jesus's clever response to the Pharisees' trap. The text concludes with a moral lesson against hypocrisy in judging others' sins.</summary>

<keywords>Jesus Christ, Adultery, Scribes and Pharisees, Feast of Tabernacles, Jerusalem, Judgment, Hypocrisy, Forgiveness</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the story of the woman caught in adultery to the Parable of the Prodigal Son (Luke 15). The catchword "Aera" from the previous page, which the previous translator speculated might refer to the "earth" (Dutch: *aarde*) where Jesus wrote, is actually the first word of the Latin poem on this page, where it likely refers to money or wealth (Latin: *aes/aera*).</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>There will be joy over a sinner who repents.</header>

Through lusts, luxury, and fatherly gluttony <note>original: "Aera libidinibus, luxu, ingluvieque paterna"</note>,
He loses great wealth in a short time.
Hence, the Prodigal fills his stomach with husks and bran;
But as a suppliant seeking pardon from his father, he received it.

---

->*The prodigal son returns to his father.*<-

This young man, consuming all his property in a short time,
And reduced to suffering the ultimate misery,
Returns to his father; and the father has nothing
But joy, which he uses to give him a grand feast.

---

### The parable of the lost Son and his conversion.

A reckless Son had, in a sensual and thoughtless manner,
Shamefully squandered his Father's inheritance <term>inheritance|erref-goed: property or wealth passed down through a family</term>;
But he returns to him again, with tears upon his cheeks;
He who turns back to God is received in grace.

---

->*There shall be joy over a sinner repenting.*<-

The lavish son his goods does waste away,
In a short space with lust, with drink, with play.
For want at last he feeds with the swine,
Yet (sorrowing) does with his father dine.

---

### Of the Lost Son and his conversion.

The disobedient son soon squandered his small estate,
Spending it on musicians, feasting, loose people, and harlots,
Until he was utterly ruined and reduced to a beggar's staff <note>original: "Bettelstab," a traditional German expression for being reduced to extreme poverty.</note>.
He must eat the husks <term>husks|Träber: the leftover skins or waste from pressed fruit or grain used as animal feed</term> from the trough with the sows.
He begs for mercy, as he repented of the shame he caused;
The Father takes him in; God scorns no penitent.

<keywords>Prodigal Son, repentance, Luke 15, forgiveness, inheritance, conversion, polyglot verses</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 40 regarding the Parable of the Prodigal Son. <header>LUKE 15:20</header>

<image-desc>An engraving illustrating the Parable of the Prodigal Son. In the foreground, a man in ragged clothing sits or kneels against a tree in a posture of prayer or despair. In the middle ground, a herd of swine is gathered around a trough near a simple wooden building. The background shows a sparse landscape with a few more trees.</image-desc>

->The Lost Son.<-

---

The Pharisees, not knowing how else they might make Christ hateful <note>In this context, "hateful" means to make him appear odious or disreputable to the public.</note>, turned every thing into an offense; when the Sinners approached Christ to hear him, and were received by Christ, they murmured, saying furthermore: "This man receives Sinners and eats with them." Christ, understanding this, justified his actions with the parable of a stray Sheep, and of a lost coin <term>lost coin|verlooren penning: a reference to the Parable of the Lost Drachma, where a woman searches for a silver coin</term>, and principally with the parable of the lost Son, who desired his inheritance from his Father, and having spent the same abroad with harlots and loose living <note>original: "hoeren en snoeren," a rhyming Dutch idiom for debauchery and riotous living</note>, fell thereby into great Famine and poverty, so that at last he even became a Shepherd to tend to the pigs; yet remembering how many Hired servants in his Father's house had abundance, he resolved with repentance and regret to go to his Father, to pray to him, that he might have mercy on him, and to take him in as his Servant instead of a Son.

<meta>This page transitions from the parables of Luke 15 (the Prodigal Son) to the Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus found in Luke 16. It follows the common early modern practice of presenting the same biblical lesson in multiple languages—Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German—to provide both moral instruction and linguistic practice for the reader.</meta>

### The end of the godly and the wicked is different.

Dives <note>original Latin: "Dives," meaning "rich man"; though used as a proper name in tradition, it is simply the Latin word for his status</note> wastes his time in delights, luxury, and pride, and once extinguished, he enters the dire pits of Tartarus <note>original: "Tartara," a term from Greek and Roman mythology for the underworld, used here to mean Hell</note>. Lazarus, full of sores, miserable, sick, and needy, seeks the stars <note>original: "Astra," a poetic reference to Heaven</note>, for God thus varies their fates.

*The dogs take care of poor Lazarus, while*
*he is despised by the rich.*

The unhappy rich man, in the flames of hell,
Receives a thousand torments for a few false pleasures;
Lazarus, on the contrary, goes to Heaven to triumph,
And for a hundred evils suffered, tastes a thousand delights.

->The Parable of the Rich Man and the poor<-
->Lazarus.<-

The Rich Man, corrupted by the world's excesses, falls into Heaven's disgrace through lust and splendor. But poor Lazarus is wealthy again with God; whoever is deprived here may expect a better fate.

->*The end of the godly and wicked unlike.*<-

Dives his days in lust and pleasure spends,
But after death his soul to hell descends.
Here Lazarus is sick, in want, and pain,
Yet after death the heavenly Joys doth gain.

->*Of the Rich Man and poor Lazarus.*<-

Full of wealth, goods, and money, overflowing with worldly pleasure, he lived every day in joy, pursuing pride, amusement, and games. At last, the rich man dies and goes to the Devil. Violent sores plague poor Lazarus, but he was carried into Paradise by the angels. See how, in death, the game turns and is reversed <note>original German: "das Spiel wendt und verkehrt," a metaphor suggesting a complete reversal of fortune or a change in the state of play</note>.

<meta>catchword: N</meta>

<summary>This page presents the Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus through a series of poems in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German, emphasizing the theme of divine justice and the reversal of fortunes in the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, Dives, afterlife, divine justice, poverty and wealth, Memento Mori, multilingual devotion</keywords>

LUKE 16:21

<image-desc>A detailed engraving illustrating the parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus. ->Lazarus and the Rich Man.<-

---

After the parable of the Prodigal Son <note>original: "verloorenZoon," literally the lost son</note>, Christ presented a parable fitting for those who, being devoted to gluttony and luxury, let their neighbors suffer miserably from hunger. A certain rich man, costly in his attire and food, lived merrily and joyfully; on the other hand, a certain beggar lived poorly, being full of sores, and he desired the crumbs from the Rich Man’s table, yet received nothing—except hereafter; for having died, his soul was carried by the angels into the bosom of Abraham <note>a biblical expression referring to a place of comfort in the biblical afterlife</note>: but the Rich Man, dying, received on the contrary—instead of constant joy—an everlasting pain in Hell, and however much he prayed, yet he could get no relief, but received as an answer: "Child, remember that you received your good things in your life"; thus becoming aware how little that small joy here in this life may avail against the eternal pain of Hell.

<note>Vocabulary from the original Dutch: Lazarus, Rich Man, Prodigal Son, Christ, parable, Beggar, Abraham, Hell, Hellish pain</note>

<summary>This page provides a Dutch prose summary and moral application of the Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, emphasizing the reversal of fortunes in the afterlife based on one's earthly conduct and charity.</summary>
<keywords>Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, Luke 16, afterlife, charity, gluttony, Abraham's bosom, divine justice</keywords>

### Scarcely one in ten gives thanks. <note>original: "Vix decimus quiſque gratias agit."</note>

Ten wretched lepers meet Christ,
Whom He cleanses with a mere nod and commands to depart.
Nine remained ungrateful; one, not forgetful,
Returns and pays his respects, giving thanks to God.

->*Christ heals ten Lepers.*<-

*To the Priest, who must judge the leprosy:*
*All feel themselves healed; but only one returns thanks.*
*You nine, where are you? Oh, too ungrateful race!*
*Why do you not imitate this prudent stranger?*

->Christ cleanses ten Lepers, of whom<-
->only one is grateful.<-

Ten men, completely leprous, Christ’s word makes healthy;
Heaven’s medicine flows from His generous mouth.
They had a measure of faith, but remain, on the other hand—
While only one is grateful—ungrateful, all nine.

### Scarcely one in ten gives thanks. <note>original Latin: "Vix decimus quisque gratias agit."</note>

Ten wretched lepers meet Christ,
Whom He cleanses with a mere nod and commands to depart.
Nine remained ungrateful; one, not forgetful,
Returns and pays his respects, giving thanks to God.

->*Christ heals ten Lepers.*<-

*Ten lepers meeting the God-Man <note>original French: "l’Homme-Dieu," a theological term for Christ's dual nature as both divine and human</note> on the road,*
*Beg for healing; He, along the way, sends them*
*To the Priest, who must judge the leprosy:*
*All feel themselves healed; but only one returns thanks.*
*You nine, where are you? Oh, too ungrateful race!*
*Why do you not imitate this prudent stranger?*

->Christ cleanses ten Lepers, of whom<-
->only one is grateful.<-

Ten men, completely leprous, Christ’s word makes healthy;
Heaven’s medicine flows from His generous mouth.
They had a measure of faith, but remain, on the other hand—
While only one is grateful—ungrateful, all nine.

->*Scarcely every tenth man grateful.*<-

*Ten leprous men upon the way meet Christ,*
*He bids them go and show themselves to the Priest.*
*Nine stay behind who had ungrateful hearts,*
*The tenth returns, thanks Christ, and so departs.*

->Christ heals ten leprous men.<-

Ten men on the street, heavily burdened with leprosy,
Cry out to the Lord; He heals their affliction.
Thereupon He bids them go to the priests as clean men.
The nine stay away, as ungrateful companions;
Only one returns to show his gratitude.
Scarcely one in ten applies himself to true thanks.

AFTER Christ had performed many miracles in Samaria and Galilee, He traveled through the midst of those regions toward Jerusalem. On the way, He was met by ten leprous men <term>leprosy: in the biblical era, this referred to various skin diseases that resulted in social exclusion and ritual impurity</term> who, calling out to Him for mercy, were commanded to show themselves to the priests <note>Jewish law in Leviticus required a priest's certification for a leper to be declared "clean" and allowed to re-enter society</note>. And behold, as they went, they were all cleansed. However, one of them, seeing this and being ignited with gratitude, turned back with a loud voice, glorifying God; falling on his face before Christ, he thanked Him—and this man was a Samaritan <note>Samaritans were often viewed as religious and ethnic outsiders by the Jews, making his singular gratitude a poignant rebuke to the others in this narrative</note>. Christ, seeing this gratitude and rebuking the other nine, told him to rise, saying: "Go your way, your faith has saved you." In this history, the detestable ingratitude of the Jews toward their Savior is clearly shown, while they themselves are put to shame by the virtue of the Samaritan.

<meta>Catchword: Mendi- (Likely the start of 'Mendicans', referring to a beggar in the next section)</meta>

**Vocabulary:** Luke 17, Christ, Samaritans, Lepers, Jews, miracles, Savior

<keywords>Luke 17, healing of ten lepers, gratitude, Samaritan, ingratitude, miracles of Jesus, faith and healing</keywords>

# The Lord restores sight to the blind. <note>original Latin: "Jehova restituit Cæcis visum."</note>

Begging, a blind man asks for the small comforts of a wretched life,
    And sits in the crossroads in perpetual night.
Christ restores his sight—wonderful to tell!—with a single word:
    And from that moment, he becomes a follower of his Physician.

---

### Christ restores sight to the blind man.

This beggar without eyes cries out with a loud voice,
    *Sensing Jesus passing by, he doubles his cries and prays,*
*“Jesus, cast upon me the eye of compassion,*
    *Let me see the light of day again.” “I will it; your strong faith*
*Saves you,” Christ tells him: the blind man is comforted,*
    *Follows Him, blesses his God, and is moved by deep affection.*

---

### Christ makes the blind Bartimaeus see near Jericho.

A poor blind man, afflicted by his infirmities,
    Receives his sight again through his persistent pleading.
He was blind in his body, but not in his soul;
Faith is the eye of the heart through which one sees Christ. <note>The author uses the physical healing of Bartimaeus as a metaphor for spiritual enlightenment, a common theme in 17th-century Dutch emblem books.</note>

---

### *The Lord restores sight to the blind.*

A blind man begging by the way in darkness <note>original: "by night," used here to represent the "night" of blindness.</note>
    Cries unto Christ with all his force and might;
Who with a word restores his sight, and he
Follows our Lord, with thanks that he can see.

---

### Christ makes Bartimaeus see.

The blind man sits by the path, forced to help himself by begging;
    As Christ passes by, he cries out that He should help him:
“O Jesus, Son of God, help me and make me see!”
The Lord, moved by such shouting to stand still,
Helps him with a word, so that he can see quite clearly;
    Bartimaeus, in gratitude, follows after his Helper.

LUKE 18:23 <note>original: "LUCÆ. 18. 23"; although the text refers to the story starting at verse 35, the header notes verse 23.</note>

An engraving illustrating the biblical scene of Jesus healing a blind man. Jesus stands in the center, gesturing toward a blind man seated on a stone step who is looking up at him. ->Christ Heals a Blind Man.<-

As Jesus approached the city of Jericho, he found nearby a certain blind man begging by the road. This man, having heard from others that Jesus the Nazarene <term>Nazarener: A title for Jesus indicating his hometown of Nazareth, but also carrying connotations of the "Branch" of Jesse mentioned in prophecy.</term> was passing by, began with great emotion to cry out thus: "Jesus, you Son of David <term>Zoon Davids: A specific Messianic title, acknowledging Jesus as the promised descendant of King David.</term>, have mercy on me!" And being rebuked for it, he cried out all the more. Christ, knowing his faith, stood still and had the blind man brought to him, saying very sweetly and kindly to him: "What do you want me to do for you?" Whereupon the blind man answered: "Lord, that I may receive my sight," and immediately he received his sight and followed Christ, glorifying God. From such an example one can learn the power of Jesus Christ, and how much a faithful and powerful prayer can accomplish with God, from whom all judgments and deliverances come.

<vocab>Jericho, Jesus, Nazarene, Son of David, Faith, Christ, God, prayer</vocab>

### Page 48

->The Savior raises Lazarus.<-

->Christ raises Lazarus from the dead.<-

When Christ’s strong voice rang in Lazarus's ears, he arose from his dismal and dark grave-cavern. Death loses its power; he who, driven by God’s Spirit, has believed in the Life, shall live after his death.

->The dead will hear the voice of the Son of Man.<-

Lazarus, parted from his woes by death, had been stinking beneath the earth for four days when he was called by Christ and bidden to rise; he stands up straight, answering his Master's cries.
<note>The original text used "The death will heare," a common archaic construction where "the death" referred to the dead as a collective.</note>

->Christ raises Lazarus from the dead.<-

When Lazarus had lain in the earth for four days and had begun to become foul and stinking, Christ came to the grave with a holy fervor. "Lazarus, come forth!" He calls, "come out this instant!" The dead man comes forward, bound with burial cloths <term>Schweißſtüchlein: Literally "little sweat cloths," referring to the sudarium or face cloth used in ancient Jewish burials.</term>; the rotting corpse hears this, for it was the voice of God.
<note>original German: "Chriſtus erwecket Lazarus von den Todten..."</note>

# JOHN 11. <note>original Latin: "IOANNIS. 11."</note>

->*Christ raises Lazarus.*<-

---

Christ, desiring for the last time, since his death was
approaching, to leave a memorable reminder of his
Divine power, demonstrates this in the raising of
Lazarus in a more than ordinary manner; for although he
had raised others from the dead, yet none who
already smelled of decay <note>original Dutch: "stonk" (stank)</note> as Lazarus did: For when Christ
had come near Bethany, not far from where Lazarus
lived, Martha met him, who was greatly mourning the death
of her brother, indeed having great confidence in Christ,
that he could have helped her if he had been there
before her brother died: But now despairing, because
he had been buried for four days; yet
Christ being moved, went with Martha and Mary to the
grave, in the presence of many Jews, and raised Lazarus
with a powerful voice: as proof that nothing is
impossible for him, and that his Father always hears him.

## Christ rides upon a she-ass into Jerusalem

# MATTHEW 21

->Christ on Palm Sunday<-

How, and with what glory Christ rode into Jerusalem, the Evangelist Mark <note>Mark 11:1-11</note> clearly lets us know, as he writes that Christ, approaching Jerusalem with his followers, sent out two Disciples for a Donkey; and when the Disciples brought it to him, he sat upon it, and so made his way toward Jerusalem, followed by his Disciples and a great multitude; so that what was prophesied before might be fulfilled: *Rejoice, O daughter of Zion; shout, O daughter of Jerusalem; behold, your King comes to you, righteous and poor, riding on a Donkey.* <note>This is a citation of Zechariah 9:9, a messianic prophecy</note> The joy of the People can be gathered from the fact that some spread their clothes, and others spread Palm-branches <note>original Dutch: "Palm-takken"</note> on the road where Christ rode, crying out together, *Blessed be the Kingdom of our Father David, which comes in the name of the Lord; Hosanna in the highest Heavens!*

# My house is a house of prayer.
<note>original Latin: "Domus mea, Domus orationis." This is a reference to Isaiah 56:7 and Matthew 21:13.</note>

He drives the money-lenders <note>original Latin: "Danistas," referring to those who lent money at interest</note> from the temple with a resounding whip,
And with the money-changers <note>original Latin: "Trapezitis," from the Greek word for the tables used by bankers</note> he puts their profane hearts to flight.
For truly, the temples of God are not dens of thieves,
But that house is sacred for the pouring out of prayers.

->*The Savior drives out of the Temple those who were selling and buying.*<-
<note>original French: "Le Sauveur chasse hors du Temple ceux qui vendo & achetoient."</note>

Moved by a just zeal, one sees the Son of God
Drive all the Money-changers and Merchants out of the Temple.
It is a place of prayer, and they make it a place
Of sordid greed and bad example.

### Christ drives the Buyers and Sellers out of the Temple.
<note>original Dutch: "Christus dryft de Kopers en Verkopers uit de Tempel."</note>

The Temple was established for God in Zion,
So that one might pay Him their due sacrificial duty;
Thus the Buyers are rightly cast out,
A zeal for God’s House had consumed Christ’s heart.
<note>This refers to the biblical sentiment "Zeal for your house consumes me" from Psalm 69:9, quoted in the Gospel of John regarding this event.</note>

->*My house is a house of prayer.*<-

Out of the temple such as sold and bought
Christ casts, and those that baser profit sought.
"My house," said he, "a prayer-house is," then
'Twas made by you of rogues and thieves a den.
<note>This section was originally in early modern English; the spelling has been slightly modernized for clarity (e.g., "bougt" to "bought").</note>

### Christ drives the traders out of the Temple.
<note>original German: "Christus treibt die Krämer zum Tempel aus."</note>

Christ, ignited in spirit with a holy zeal,
Drives out of the Temple the traders and the buyers,
Money-changers and usurers, and other godless rabble.
"Shall then my house," He says, "become a den of murderers,
Which is yet called a house of prayer for all peoples on Earth,
In which one calls upon and serves the true God?"

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Christ's actions in Jerusalem following his triumphal entry. <page-num>53</page-num>
<header>MATTHEW 21.</header>
<sig>R 3</sig>
<meta>Catchword: Passover-</meta>

<image-desc>A framed rectangular engraving depicts the biblical scene of the Cleansing of the Temple. In the center, Jesus Christ, with a radiating halo, wields a whip of cords to drive out merchants. To his right, figures recoil in alarm as a table of money-changers is overturned. To his left, several men lead oxen and sheep through the temple's classical architecture, which features massive fluted columns and high vaulted arches. A small number "26" is visible in the top right corner within the image frame.</image-desc>

->Christ drives the sellers out of the Temple.<-

After the triumphant entry into Jerusalem, Christ goes into the Temple, and seeing how the Temple was profaned by the Buyers and Sellers, who made a Market of the Church <note>The author uses the Dutch word "Kerk" (Church) here to refer to the Temple sanctuary</note>, and a den of murderers, he drives them out of the Temple, or out of the Forecourt <note>original: "Voor-zaal," referring to the Court of the Gentiles where commerce took place</note>, as it is sometimes called, overturning the Tables of the Money-changers, and the Seats of those who sold Doves: he would not permit any vessel or household goods to be carried through the Temple: the reason why Christ did this, he made known to the profaners of the Temple with these words; saying, is it not written, My House shall be called a House of prayer for all Nations, but you have made it a den of Murderers <note>original: "moordenaren." While modern English Bibles usually say "thieves" or "robbers," the Dutch text uses the stronger term for murderers</note>. Since our body is called the Temple of God <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 6:19, connecting the physical Temple to the spiritual state of the believer</note>, we must take care that we do not make of it a cavern of impurity.

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>Do this in remembrance of me. <note>original Latin: "Hoc facite in mei commemorationem."</note></header>
<meta>catchword: Christ</meta>

> The Lord had eaten the Paschal lamb according to custom,
> when He instituted the sacred signs of the New Covenant.
> Eat my flesh and drink my blood,
> and, being mindful, celebrate my death together.
> <note>original Latin: "Paschalem Dominus de more comederat agnum, / Foederis instituit cum sacra signa Novi. / Manducate meam carnem atque haurite cruorem, / Et memores mortem concelebrare meam."</note>

## *Jesus Christ institutes the Holy Supper, and John rests at the table on the breast of his Master.* <note>original French: "Jesus Christ institue le S. Cène, & Jean repose à table sur le sein de son Maître."</note>

> After the lamb was eaten, as the Law ordained,
> Jesus instituted the holy Eucharist.
> Eat, drink, he said, in memory of me,
> It is my flesh, it is my blood, I am the bread of life.
> <note>original French: "APres l'agneau mangè, comme ordonnoit la Loi, / Jesus institua la sainte Eucharistie. / Mangez, beuvez, dit-il, en memoire de moi, / C'est ma chair, c'est mon sang, je suis le pain de vie."</note>

## Christ eats the Supper with his Disciples. <note>original Dutch: "Christus eet met zyn Discipelen het Avondmaal."</note>

> When Christ had celebrated the festival according to the Law;
> He instituted another Feast and Supper <note>original: "Nagtmaal," literally "night-meal," the standard Dutch term for the Lord's Supper</note>,
> Let the Priest freely cease from pouring goat's blood,
> The true Paschal Lamb shall offer himself as a sacrifice.
> <note>original Dutch: "TOen Christus had gevierd het hoog-tyd na de Wet; / Heeft hy een ander Feest en Nagtmaal ingezet, / De Priester hou vry op met Bokke-bloed te plengen, / Het ware Paaschlam zal zig zelfs ten offer brengen."</note>

## *Do this in remembrance of me.*

> Christ eats the Paschal Lamb, a sign so near
> Of the New Covenant's most sacred tie.
> His flesh he gives us to eat and blood to drink,
> And when we do it, bids us think on him.

## Christ institutes the Supper. <note>original German: "Christus setzt das Abendmael ein."</note>

> When he had now eaten the sacrificial lamb according to the Law,
> He established a meal for us, which we shall never forget,
> His blood-shedding, suffering, and bitter death.
> He gives them bread and wine, bids them partake of such;
> This is my body and blood, which I will soon shed,
> That you may be freed from the distress of Hell.
> <note>original German: "ALs nun das Offerlam Er nach dem G'setz hat gessen / Stifftet Er uns ein Mahl, das wir nimmer vergessen, / Das Bluthvergiessen sein, Lyden und bitter'n Todt, / Reicht ihnen Brodt und Wein, heifft sie solches geniessen; / Difs ist mein Leib und Bluth, das ich bald werd vergiessen, / Das ihr befreyet seit von der Höllischen Noth."</note>

<summary>This page presents the institution of the Eucharist (the Last Supper) through a series of poems in five different languages, emphasizing the transition from the Old Testament Passover to the New Covenant sacrifice of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Last Supper, Eucharist, Paschal Lamb, New Covenant, Atonement, Polyglot, Devotional Poetry</keywords>

->Christ’s Last Supper.<-

After Christ had eaten in Bethany at the house of Simon the Leper, <note>Simon the Leper is mentioned in the Gospels as the host of a meal where Jesus was anointed; Bethany was a village near Jerusalem.</note> he sent out two of his disciples on the first day of <term>unleavened bread</term> <note>original: "onghevelde Brooden," referring to the start of the Passover festival when yeast is removed from homes.</note> into the city to a certain man, to announce to him that he wished to keep the <term>Passover</term> <note>original: "Pascha," the Jewish festival commemorating the liberation of the Israelites from Egypt.</note> there with his disciples. The disciples of Christ did as they were commanded, prepared the Passover, and when evening had come, Christ sat down with his followers to institute the <term>Lord's Supper</term>. <note>original: "Avondmaal," literally "Evening Meal," the term commonly used in Dutch for the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> Now, having eaten the Paschal lamb (according to the opinion of some), <note>This refers to a long-standing theological debate about whether the Last Supper was a formal Seder meal or took place the day before the official Passover.</note> the institution of the Supper took place with the breaking of the bread and the speaking of these words to his disciples: Take, eat, that is my body; adding to this the cup, saying: Drink all from it. When Christ had instituted the Supper with other circumstances, he foretold to his disciples their scattering, and to Peter, his fall. <note>Refers to Jesus' prediction that the disciples would desert him and that Peter would deny him three times.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the narrative of the Last Supper and the legal proceedings of the trial to the physical suffering of the Passion. <page-num>60</page-num>
<header>The punishment that brought us peace was upon him.</header>
<note>original Latin: "Castigatio pacis nostræ super ipsum," a reference to Isaiah 53:5.</note>
<meta>catchword: After</meta>

The innocent body offers its back to the whips and lashes,
The elm-wood rod cracks upon his shoulders.
The skin is saturated with deep red, blood flows from everywhere,
Lest you should suffer the infernal blows, O Man.
<note>original Latin: "Innocuum corpus scuticis sua terga flagellis / Exhibet, in scapulis ulmea virga crepat. / Tota rubore satur cutis est, cruor undique manat, / Tartareas plagas ne patiaris, Homo."</note>

### Jesus Christ is scourged.

*See how the Savior is treated for your crime,*
*How by the force of blows his tender skin is torn open:*
*Sinner, the pain he suffers is your happiness,*
*For you, through his sorrows, paradise reopens.*
<note>original French: "Jesus Christ est fouetté."</note>

### Pontius Pilate has Christ scourged.

The servants, cruel by nature, disfigure the holy body,
The disdain, insults, and mockery are the <term>Judean</term> <note>original: "Joodsche," referring to the bystanders in the biblical narrative.</note> pastime,
It is our fault alone; mankind deserves the plagues,
The guilty go free, while the innocent one is beaten.
<note>original Dutch: "Pontius Pilatus doet Christum geesselen."</note>

### The punishment that brought us peace was upon him.

*Christ is scourged so that his most precious blood*
*Flows over his body like a flooding stream:*
*And his flesh is all scarred and cut, which he*
*Endures to free mankind from hellish lashes.*
<note>This section was originally printed in 17th-century English; spelling has been slightly modernized here for accessibility.</note>

### Pilate has Christ scourged.

*The holy body is struck hard with whips,*
*Wounded and torn apart by a thousand strokes of the rod,*
*The skin dyed with blood, until nothing remains whole,*
*He offers his back to those who strike him so,*
*So that we should not have to bear the Devil's blow eternally,*
*Whipped and lashed by him without ceasing.*
<note>original German: "Pilatus thut Christus geiffelen."</note>

<summary>This page presents a series of devotional poems in five languages—Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German—meditating on the scourging of Christ. The central theme is the theological concept of substitutionary atonement: that Christ's physical wounds save the believer from the "blows" of the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>Passion of Christ, Flagellation, Scourging at the Pillar, Pontius Pilate, Atonement, Devotional Poetry, Multilingual, Isaiah 53:5</keywords>

<header>JOHN 19:30 <note>original Latin: "IOANNIS. 19. 30"</note></header>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the Flagellation of Christ. Jesus is tied to a central pillar in a stone courtyard or prison cell with barred windows. ->The Scourging of Christ.<-

AFTER the <term>soldiers</term> <note>original: "Krygs-knegten," literally war-servants or men-at-arms.</note> had brought Christ before Caiaphas, they led him from there into the <term>judgment hall</term> <note>original: "Regt-huis," referring to the Praetorium, the residence of the Roman governor.</note> early in the morning; Pilate immediately asks about the accusations they had against him, but they answered nothing, except that it should be enough for him that they condemned him as an <term>evildoer</term>; therefore Christ is questioned by Pilate himself regarding his Kingdom, which he testifies is not of this world; whereupon Pilate declares him innocent, and wishes to release him instead of Barabbas; but not being able to bring this about, he has him scourged by the soldiers, who moreover set a crown of thorns upon his head, (undoubtedly) by the command of Pilate, to press upon the Son of God a mark of this "crime"—that he had made himself a King—and thus to still the fury of the Jews; and so our Savior had to be scourged, so that we might obtain healing through his stripes. <note>This concludes with a reference to the prophecy in Isaiah 53:5: "and with his stripes we are healed."</note>

<meta>catchword: He is led <note>original Latin: "Ducitur"</note></meta>

**Vocabulary:** The Scourging of Christ, Soldiers, Caiaphas, Pilate, Judgment hall, Evildoer, Barabbas, Crown of thorns, Son of God, Savior, Healing.

<summary>This page provides a narrative summary of Christ's trial before Pontius Pilate, explaining the political motivations behind the scourging as an attempt to satisfy the crowd and the theological belief that this suffering brought spiritual healing.</summary>
<keywords>Passion of Christ, Pontius Pilate, Flagellation, Crown of Thorns, Barabbas, Praetorium, Atonement</keywords>

<page-num>64</page-num>
<header>Just as Moses lifted up the serpent in the wilderness. <note>original Latin: "Sicut Moſes in deſerto Serpentem exaltavit." A reference to John 3:14, where Jesus compares his future crucifixion to the bronze serpent Moses raised to heal the Israelites.</note></header>

<meta>catchword: Christus</meta>

The breast gapes with a wound, the trembling head hangs in death,
While the nails pierce through the hands and each foot:
The stretched arms grow stiff, blood flows from his limbs on every side.
What could be more sorrowful than this sight?
<note>Translated from the original Latin verse beginning "Vulnere pectus hiat..."</note>

### Jesus Christ on the Cross. <note>original French: "Jesus Chriſt en croix."</note>

*A marvelous spectacle of love and goodness!*
*Christ suffers here a painful death for us,*
*His blood flows from his hands, his feet, and his side,*
*It is to make our souls blessed forever.*

### The <term>soldiers</term> <note>original: "Krygs-knegten," literally war-servants.</note>, having crucified Jesus, cast lots for his garments.

Although Christ hangs on the Cross, the salvation of the human race still touches his heart in the bitterness of his pain. O man! Where are you now, you who always wish to avenge evil? He who suffers the evil prays for those who inflict that evil upon him.
<note>This Dutch verse highlights the "Imitatio Christi" or the imitation of Christ, urging the reader to choose forgiveness over vengeance.</note>

### *As Moses lifted up the serpent in the wilderness.*

*His pierced side gapes, his dying spirits fail,*
*His hands and feet upon the cross they nail,*
*His stretched arms stiffen, blood flows from each part:*
*A spectacle more sad never grieved man's heart.*
<note>This section was originally written in seventeenth-century English.</note>

### Christ is nailed to the Cross. <note>original German: "Chriſtus wird ans Creutz geſchlagen."</note>

Behold this sad image, Christ raised on the Cross,
His holy hands and feet deeply pierced with sharp nails,
His head bowed toward death, his side pierced
with a spear, his body wounded all over,
And red blood flows; his heart was so <term>inflamed</term> <note>original: "entzündet." In Baroque devotion, this refers to the "fire" of divine love for humanity.</note>,
What we have deserved, the pious Lord suffers.

<summary>This page presents a series of devotional poems in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German, all reflecting on the physical suffering and spiritual significance of the Crucifixion. It emphasizes Christ's forgiveness of his executioners and the theological link to the Old Testament story of Moses and the brazen serpent.</summary>

<image-desc>
An engraving depicting the Crucifixion of Jesus Christ. In the center, Jesus hangs from a tall wooden cross, with the title "INRI" visible at the top. To his left and right are the two thieves on shorter crosses. The scene is set on the rocky mount of Golgotha, surrounded by Roman soldiers on horseback and foot, some carrying spears and banners. ->the Crucifixion of Christ.<-

Christ having come to the place called Golgotha (which is translated as the Place of the Skull <note>original Dutch: "Hooft-scheel-plaatse," an archaic term for a skull-shaped hill.</note>), his clothes were taken from him by the soldiers <note>original: "Krygsknegten," literally war-servants or mercenaries.</note>, who (having crucified Christ between two murderers) cast lots over them, so that the prophecy of David would thus be fulfilled <note>A reference to Psalm 22:18: "They divide my clothes among them and cast lots for my garment."</note>; the inscription of Christ was written above his head with these words in three different languages, Latin, Greek, and Hebrew: **THIS IS THE KING OF THE JEWS**; thus mocking and laughing at him with it, while he still prayed for them in his extreme suffering; and not only did the Jews blaspheme Christ, but also the <term>evildoers</term> <note>original: "Quaad-doenders," referring to the two criminals executed alongside him.</note>, yet one of them, repenting, is comforted by Christ. When he had now hung for three hours, a great darkness came upon the Earth, and the <term>Veil of the Temple</term> <note>A heavy curtain that separated the most sacred part of the Jewish Temple; its tearing symbolized new access to God.</note> was torn, the Earth shook, and the graves were opened, and Christ, with great anguish, gave up the spirit.

<page-num>68</page-num>

# Raised for our justification <note>original Latin: "Resuscitatus propter justitiam nostram," a reference to Romans 4:25.</note>

The third light had come, when the earth, shaken by a tremor,
   Leaped apart, and the ground, aware of its guest, returned the Lord.
Leaping from the tomb, having trampled death,
   Christ rises and triumphs as victor over death which he crushed.
   <note>original Latin: "Tertia lux aderat, tellus cum quassa tremore..."</note>

---

### *Jesus Christ raised from the dead.*

*Christ, on the third day, emerging strong from the dark tomb,*
   *Triumphs over death, and demonstrates his glory;*
*And surprised by the sight of such a beautiful miracle,*
*The Guards are here witnesses to his victory.*

---

### Christ’s Resurrection from the Dead.

The Sun of life rises much earlier than the actual Sun,
   And creates a beautiful Day before the dawn even began;
Here he bursts from his grave where so many soldiers <note>original Dutch: "Krygs-lien," an archaic shortening of soldiers or men-of-war.</note> watched;
He who gave up his life willingly, can make himself living again.

---

### *Raised up for our justification.* <note>The original English transcription uses "against our Justice," but in the context of the Latin heading and 17th-century theology, this is a translation of "propter" meaning "on account of" or "for the sake of" our righteousness.</note>

*The third day coming, the earth—guilty of itself—*
   *Unworthy of such a treasure, gives it birth.*
*For Christ on the third day does from death arise,*
*Having subdued Hell, death, and sin in this wise.*

---

### Christ’s Resurrection from the Dead.

The third day dawned, and a great earthquake occurred;
   The grave was opened, and had to give Christ back.
He arises with power, with victory, triumph, and splendor;
The Prince of Life has broken the bonds of death,
He destroyed the kingdom of Hell, and took vengeance on Satan,
   Having flung open Heaven, and brought life back once more.

<vocab><term>Resurrection</term>: The rising of Jesus from the dead. <term>Polyglot</term>: A text written in several languages; here used to show the universal significance of the event.</vocab>

<summary>This page features a series of poems in Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German celebrating the Resurrection of Jesus Christ. Each verse emphasizes the earthquake, the defeat of death, and Christ as the "Sun of Life" who rises before the literal sun.</summary>
<keywords>Resurrection, Easter, victory over death, earthquake, guards at the tomb, polyglot poetry, Prince of Life</keywords>

<page-num>69</page-num>

# MATTHEW 28. 34 <note>While the header reads 34, Chapter 28 of Matthew contains only 20 verses; this may refer to a plate number in the original publication.</note>

<image-desc>A copperplate engraving within a rectangular border illustrating the Resurrection of Christ. In the center, a radiant Jesus Christ rises from an open stone tomb, holding a shroud. An angel sits on the displaced tomb lid, gesturing with one hand. Several Roman soldiers, the guards of the tomb, are depicted in various states of alarm and unconsciousness around the base of the tomb. The scene is set in a rocky landscape with a dark, cavernous background.</image-desc>

->The Resurrection of Christ.<-

The day after the preparation, when Christ had lain in the grave for one night, the Chief Priests <note>original Dutch: *Over-Priesters*, the high-ranking Jewish religious officials.</note>—mindful that Christ had said, "After three days I shall rise again"—had the grave guarded by a strong Watch (by order of Pilate) and sealed with a stone. They intended in this way to completely destroy the memory of Christ, yet through these very actions, they made his memory much more glorious; for the soldiers <note>original Dutch: *Krygs-knegten*, literally "war-servants" or men-at-arms.</note> on the day after the Sabbath, on the first day of the week, felt a great Earthquake while an Angel rolled away the stone and instructed the women who came to the grave regarding the resurrection of Christ. Because of this, the Guards were so affected that, coming into the City, they frankly reported to the Chief Priests all the things that had happened there; however, they later allowed themselves to be bribed with money to say that he had been stolen from the grave.

<sig>S 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Vesper (Evening)</meta>

<summary>This page describes the efforts of the Chief Priests and Pilate to secure Christ's tomb with a seal and guards, only for an earthquake and an angel to reveal the Resurrection. It concludes with the guards being bribed to spread a false report about the body being stolen.</summary>
<keywords>Resurrection, Matthew 28, Chief Priests, Pontius Pilate, Roman Guards, Earthquake, Angel at the tomb, Bribing the guards</keywords>

<page-num>70</page-num>

# Christ is the Goal of Prophetic Scripture. <note>original Latin: *Scopus Scripturæ Propheticæ Christus*. The word "scope" here refers to the ultimate aim or target toward which the scriptures point.</note>

**I**t was evening when he joined two disciples who had left the City; he blessed them with his conversation. He rebuked them and removed the veil of confusion <note>original Latin: *glaucoma stuporis*, literally the "cataract of astonishment," suggesting a spiritual blindness.</note> from their eyes as he revealed the writings of the Prophets to them.

### *Jesus Christ discusses the Scriptures with two Disciples who were going to Emmaus.*

**O**ne day, two Disciples were talking on the road about the sufferings of Christ, when Jesus met them; they were charmed at first by his divine Spirit; for he hid himself from them, but finally he revealed himself.

### Christ reveals himself to two Disciples going to Emmaus.

**T**wo followers, <note>original Dutch: *Jong’ren*, literally "youngers" or "youths," but used here in the sense of students or disciples.</note> very sad and full of contemplation, set out alone on the road to Emmaus; there, just as a traveling companion could set their hearts on fire, so the Lord speaks with us while we speak of him.

### *Christ the goal of the Prophetic Scripture.*

**C**hrist appeared to two disciples as they directed their journey from the City; he told them what the Prophets had written, and when they finally recognized him, he vanished from their sight.

### Christ reveals himself to two disciples who are going to Emmaus.

**T**wo disciples wished to walk from the city to Emmaus; Christ revealed himself and became their companion. He scolded their lack of understanding and the hardness of their hearts because they had not believed the writings of the Prophets and had not correctly understood the prophecy <note>original German: *Weissagung*.</note> concerning him. Through his teaching, he helped them out of their ignorance.

<meta>Catchword: Na</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the "Walk to Emmaus," where the risen Christ explains his role in salvation history to two disciples. It uses multilingual verses to emphasize that Christ is the central theme and fulfillment of all biblical prophecy.</summary>

<keywords>Emmaus, Jesus Christ, Disciples, Prophecy, Scripture, Revelation, Resurrection, Old Testament fulfillment</keywords>

<page-num>71</page-num>

## LUKE 24:35 <note>original Latin: *LUCÆ. 24. 35*</note>

**A**FTER Christ’s triumphant resurrection, several revelations of his rising occurred, among which not the least is the one that happened to two Disciples going to Emmaus. They were speaking among themselves with sad reflections, and as if half in doubt, concerning the suffering of Christ; for while they were questioning each other, he joined them as a Traveler, but he withheld their eyes through his Divine power so that they did not recognize him. He explained to them Moses and the Prophets, and how the Christ had to suffer all these things—namely, the death on the Cross, and so forth—and thus enter into his glory. Upon coming to the village, <note>original Dutch: *Vlek*, a term for a small town or hamlet that has some privileges but is not a city.</note> he sat at the Table with them at their request, made himself known in the breaking of bread, and vanished from their sight.

S 4

<meta>catchword: Therefore (original Latin: *Ergo*)</meta>

### God ascends with a shout, and the Lord with the sound of a trumpet. <note>original Latin: *Ascendit Deus in jubilo & Dominus in sono tubae.* This is a quotation from Psalm 47:5, traditionally associated with the Ascension.</note>

Therefore, when the time arrived to take up the heavenly kingdom,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And it was right to hold his Father’s throne by law,
Christ ascends to the palaces of ethereal Olympus; <note>original Latin: *Aetherei palatia scandi Olympi*. Renaissance writers often used "Olympus" as a poetic synonym for the Christian Heaven.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From here he shall bring judgment upon both the good and the evil.

->*Jesus Christ ascends to the heavens.*<-

At last the time has come, when the Son of God
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Must gloriously ascend back toward his Father;
The Disciples are gathered, and Jesus is in their midst,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He withdraws from the sight of such a beloved company.

->Christ blesses his Apostles and ascends to Heaven.<-

When Christ’s service on Earth had run to its end,
Then he climbed on high, and unlocked the gates of Heaven.
Thus one reaches the glory of Heaven through the cross;
Thus the reward for the work follows, once the work is finished.

Now the appointed time of Christ’s Ascension is come,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And he must go to take his place in Heaven.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From whence he shall return to judge both the living <note>original Early Modern English: *quicke*, meaning the living.</note> and the dead.

->*Christ ascends to Heaven.*<-

When Christ had finished his course upon Earth,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And now all his sufferings were to be turned to delight,
He ascends to Heaven, possessing it by right;
Where a cloud received him before their eyes,
He sits at the right hand, from whence he shall come
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To powerfully judge the entire human race.

--- Page 323 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Christ's post-Resurrection appearances, concluding the narrative of the Gospel of Luke with his Ascension into Heaven. It bridges the transition from his earthly ministry to the mission of the Apostles.</meta>

LUKE 24.

->The Ascension of Christ.<-

After Christ had shown himself alive to the two Disciples (who were traveling to Emmaus), he revealed himself alive with many certain signs to his Apostles for forty days, and commanded them how they should preach the Gospel <note>original: *Evangelium*</note> throughout the entire world (once they had received the Holy Spirit in Jerusalem). After the forty days, He was taken up in a cloud to Heaven (in the sight of his Apostles) from the Mount of Olives, a high place that was open and clear on all sides, in order to make it all the more certain and evident that he had ascended. After Christ’s Ascension, two Angels immediately appeared to the Disciples, who also testified to Christ’s Ascension and foretold to them his return in that same form: and so our Savior <note>original: *Zaligmaker*, literally "Blessed-maker"</note> ascended on high, and led captivity captive, and gave gifts to men. <note>This final phrase is a reference to Psalm 68:18 and Ephesians 4:8, signifying Christ's victory over sin and death.</note>

<sig>S 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: Christ</meta>

<vocab>Ascension, Ascension, Christ, Disciples, Emmaus, Apostles, Gospel, Jerusalem, Holy Spirit, Mount of Olives, Savior</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the period between Christ's resurrection and his ascension, detailing his final instructions to the Apostles and his physical departure from the Mount of Olives.</summary>
<keywords>Ascension, Jesus Christ, Apostles, Mount of Olives, Great Commission, Holy Spirit, Emmaus, Angels, Savior</keywords>

--- Page 324 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical departure of Christ described on the previous page to the fulfillment of his promise: the arrival of the Holy Spirit. It depicts the event of Pentecost, emphasizing the multilingual nature of the miracle, which is mirrored by the multiple languages (Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German) used in the text itself.</meta>

->I will pour out of my Spirit. <note>original: *Effundam de Spiritu meo.* This is a reference to the prophecy in Joel 2:28, later quoted by Peter in Acts 2.</note><-

> Christ, having been carried up to the summit of the ethereal Olympus <note>original: *Olympus*; the author uses this classical Greek term for the home of the gods as a poetic synonym for the Christian Heaven.</note>,
> sends heavenly gifts to his disciples.
> The Spirit, coming to them in a fiery form within a whirlwind,
> settles upon them, and instructs those who were previously unskilled in languages.

### *The Apostles are filled with the Holy Spirit.*

> The Holy Spirit descends in tongues of fire,
> And immediately alights upon each of the Apostles:
> Then, being filled with the power <note>original: *vertu*, implying both virtue and miraculous power</note> of God,
> By a divine transport they feel themselves entirely changed.

### The Apostles receive the Holy Spirit on the Day of Pentecost.

> From Sinai came the Law, at Zion in the Hall <note>original: *Zaal*; referring to the "Upper Room" or Cenacle where the disciples gathered.</note>
> Does the Heaven-glowing Spirit make all his Disciples
> (As Christ had promised) speak with new tongues;
> God’s love is like fire, and fire can ignite all things. <note>The author contrasts the Old Testament Law given on Mount Sinai with the New Testament Spirit given in Jerusalem (Zion).</note>

### *I will pour of my Spirit.*

> Christ when arriv’d within Heavens glorious coast,
> Unto th’ Apostles sends the holy Ghost.
> Who in a firie likenesse doth descend
> On them, and divers tongues to them doth lend.

### Christ gives the Holy Spirit to his Disciples.

> When Christ our Lord had taken possession of the Kingdom of Heaven,
> He forgot his promise in no way,
> For he duly sends the Holy Spirit to the Disciples:
> Who, in a storm and wind like the flames of fire,
> Sits and rests upon them, while they were together,
> Teaching and instructing them in various languages.

# THE ACTS OF THE APOSTLES. 2. 37

[A copperplate engraving depicting the Pentecost (The descent of the Holy Spirit). A group of apostles and followers are gathered in an indoor space. Radiant light and clouds descend from above, with small tongues of fire appearing over the heads of the figures. A central figure, possibly the Virgin Mary or Christ, is seated prominently with arms spread open. The floor is tiled in a checkered pattern.]

->The Feast of Pentecost.<-

THE Apostles, after Christ’s Ascension, (following Christ’s command) return to Jerusalem to receive the Holy Spirit. While they were gathered together there with one accord on the day of Pentecost in an **Upper Room** <note>original: *Opper-zaal*, referring to the Cenacle, the traditional site of the Last Supper and the coming of the Spirit</note> (as some believe), there was seen by them, after a sudden sound from Heaven like that of a rushing mighty wind, divided tongues as of fire, sitting upon each of them. By this, the various **Languages** <note>The "gift of tongues" is emphasized here as a practical tool for missionary work</note> were made known, with which the Apostles would speak in order to proclaim the **Gospel** to every Nation. After this event, they began to speak in all kinds of Languages and to proclaim the great wonders of God. Thus it was proved that Jesus Christ had truly obtained all power in Heaven and on Earth, and that his **Atoning Sacrifice** <note>original: *Verzoen-offer*; the theological point is that the Spirit's arrival confirms God the Father's acceptance of Christ's work on the cross</note> is perfect and has been accepted by the Father.

| **Pentecost** | **Apostles** | **Ascension** | **Jerusalem** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Upper Room** | **Gospel** | **Christ Jesus** | **Atoning Sacrifice** |

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>Those who lied to the Holy Spirit are punished. <note>original Latin: *Mentiti Spiritui Sancto puniuntur*</note></header>

Ananias sells a field, and with his wife
He hides the true price through fraud and evil deceit:
But the Spirit destroyed the liars with a sudden death:
It is a difficult thing to sell mere words to the most high God.
<note>original Latin: *Vendit Ananias agrum...* This verse emphasizes that while people can be fooled by appearances, God sees the intent of the heart.</note>

### Ananias & his Wife punished.

Driven by a feigned zeal, the hypocrite Ananias,
Wishes to give his field as alms; but a greedy obsession
Possessed him so, that he withheld part of the price:
His wife agrees with him: But for this trickery,
The Holy Spirit punishes them with a mortal sentence;
Because they had undertaken to lie to Him.
<note>original French: *Ananie & sa Femme punie*. The term "alms" here refers to the early Christian practice of selling property to support the poor in the community.</note>

### Ananias and Sapphira killed for their hypocrisy and lies.

When Ananias was more eager for honor
Than for godliness, he falls down before Peter
Struck by Heaven’s voice; he who deals in lies
May deceive his own mind, but God is not deceived.
<note>original Dutch: *Ananias en Saphira om haar geveinsheid en leugen gedood*. This version highlights the social pressure of the early church, where Ananias sought the status of a donor without the actual sacrifice.</note>

### *Such as lie to the Holy Ghost are punished.*

False Ananias and his wife do sell
Land, and the price in part they do conceal.
Peter rebuking them they straightway die;
A sad reward for their hypocrisy.

### Ananias and Sapphira punished with death.

Ananias sells his field, together with his wife;
They deliver a portion of the money, but out of mistrust
They keep the rest, and lie saying it was worth no more;
And because they both agreed upon the lie,
The Holy Spirit punished them, so that they both turned pale in death.
He who wants to deceive God, deceives himself greatly.
<note>original German: *Ananias und Sapphira mit den Todt gestraft*. The "mistrust" mentioned here suggests a lack of faith in the communal provision of the early church.</note>

<meta>catchword: The <note>original: *De*</note></meta>

It serves as a stern moral warning against hypocrisy and greed within the religious community.</summary>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>ACTS 5.</header>

<image-desc>An engraving illustrating the biblical story of Ananias and Sapphira from Acts 5. In a grand hall with classical columns and a set of stairs, the apostle Peter and others stand on an elevated platform. Peter gestures towards Ananias, who lies dead on the floor below. ->*Ananias and Sapphira Punished.*<-

The Jews, having heard of the healing of the man born lame <note>original: *Kreupelgeboorne*; referring to the miracle at the Beautiful Gate in Acts 3</note> and the preaching of Peter and John, took them prisoner; yet they were released again by the entire Council <note>The Sanhedrin, the high court of justice in ancient Jerusalem</note> with harsh threats that they should not preach in the name of Christ again. However, they went immediately to the converted believers, who altogether were selling their property and goods for the maintenance of the needy. Now a certain man named Ananias, with Sapphira his wife, selling a property, brought only a certain portion to the Apostles, craftily hiding the rest and denying that they had received more for it. For this, Peter sharply rebuked Ananias—who was the first to bring the gift—so that by a special punishment from God he fell dead at his feet. Likewise also Sapphira his wife <insert>suffered the same fate</insert>, because they had lied not to man, but to God; and thus God knows how to put to shame the liars and worldly-minded people.

<summary>This page recounts the biblical episode from Acts 5 where Ananias and Sapphira are struck dead after lying to the Apostles about the proceeds from a property sale. It frames the event as a divine warning against hypocrisy and deceit within the early Christian community.</summary>
<keywords>Ananias, Sapphira, Saint Peter, Saint John, Acts of the Apostles, Sanhedrin, early Church, divine judgment, hypocrisy</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the internal discipline and miracles of the early Church (described on page 77) to the first instance of capital persecution. While the previous page showed the communal life and the judgment of Ananias and Sapphira, this page illustrates the external opposition of the religious authorities in Jerusalem, culminating in the death of Stephen.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>The First Martyr of the New Testament.</header>
<sig>D</sig>

By an unjust judgment, Stephen is condemned to death, <note>original: *capitis damnatur*; literally "condemned of the head," referring to a capital sentence</note>
Upon whom the raging people rained down a shower of stones.
From his shed blood, he then bore the first palm of martyrdom,
And showed what the pious must endure.

### *Saint Stephen, the first Martyr, is stoned.*

By an unjust decree, Stephen is condemned.
With his glorious blood, he cements the Church;
This is what every faithful soul is also destined for:
Dying for Jesus Christ must be their motto.

### Stephen was stoned, and sees the Heavens open

O Stephen <note>original: *Steven*</note>, full of God! Rightly condemned by injustice,
They take your life, you who boast in eternal life.
Your name was written on high with martyr’s ink;
Virtue killed by envy <note>original: *Nyd*</note> expects a better life.

### *The first Martyr of the New Testament.*

Stephen is falsely charged with blasphemy,
And for that same reason, he is immediately stoned to death.
He is the first to gain the Martyr’s wreath:
So should all spend their life and breath for Christ.

### Stephen is innocently stoned.

Stephen is falsely reported before the court;
His life is taken from him, despite his innocence.
Thereupon the pious and holy man is stoned;
He has become the first martyr through his blood.
Many thousands follow him in this <term>Order of Knighthood</term> <note>original: *Ritter-Orden*; a metaphorical reference to the spiritual "nobility" of those who die for their faith</note>,
The crown is set aside for those who can endure such suffering.

--- Page 329 ---
<meta>This page follows the condemnation of Stephen (page 78) by providing a narrative account of his trial and execution. It transitions from the poetic reflections of the previous page to a prose summary of the events recorded in Acts 7.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>ACTS 7.</header>
<meta>catchword: He seeks <note>original: *Quærit*</note></meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the stoning of Saint Stephen. Stephen is shown on the ground in the center, looking up toward the heavens with his arms spread open. Several men surround him, holding large rocks above their heads or in the act of throwing them. In the background to the left is an arched city gate, and to the right, figures are seen observing from a raised stone platform or staircase. The entire scene is enclosed in a rectangular frame with a header and a caption.</image-desc>

->*Stephen Stoned.*<-

The first Martyr that the early Church had after Christ’s Ascension was Stephen, one of the seven newly chosen Deacons <note>officials appointed to handle the distribution of food and charity</note>, a godly and pious man, performing many miracles and wonders. This was something that the Libertines <note>members of the "Synagogue of the Freedmen"</note>, Cyrenians, and others could not endure. However, being unable to withstand his wisdom and the Spirit through which he spoke, they dragged him before the Council <note>original: *den Raad*; the Sanhedrin, the supreme religious and legal assembly in Jerusalem</note> and falsely accused him of blasphemies against the Temple and the Law. Stephen refuted this accusation at length with an excellent oration <note>original: *Oratie*; a formal speech</note> from the Old Testament. Because of this, the Jews became as if mad and raging; they led him out and stoned him. But he, seeing the Heavens open, commended his soul to Christ, prayed for his enemies, and died. Thus the first foundation of the Christian Church was laid in the blood of the martyrs, which thereafter became the seed of the Church <note>a reference to the famous saying of the church father Tertullian: "The blood of the martyrs is the seed of the Church"</note>.

**Stephen:** Stephen  
**Martelaar:** Martyr  
**Hemelvaart:** Ascension  
**Diakonen:** Deacons  
**Libertynen:** Libertines <note>In this context, Jews from the Synagogue of the Freedmen</note>  
**Cyrenen:** Cyrenians <note>People from Cyrene in North Africa</note>  
**Raad:** Council <note>The Sanhedrin</note>  
**Tempel:** Temple  
**Oratie:** Oration / Speech  
**Oude Testament:** Old Testament  
**Christelyke Kerke:** Christian Church

<summary>This page recounts the trial and death of Saint Stephen, the first Christian martyr, who was stoned by the religious authorities after his defense of the faith. It emphasizes his role as a deacon and the theological concept that the blood of martyrs serves as the foundation for the Church's growth.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Stephen, Martyrdom, Acts of the Apostles, Sanhedrin, Deacons, Christian Church history, Stoning of Stephen</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the account of the stoning of Saint Stephen (page 79), where Saul was introduced as a witness to the execution. It continues the narrative by focusing on Saul’s transition from a violent opponent of the early Church to its most famous missionary, Paul.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>Paul, from a persecutor, becomes an Apostle. <note>original: *Paulus ex persecutore Apostolus*</note></header>

Saul seeks out those called by the name of Christ for their destruction,
and rages fiercely against the wretched ones like a wolf.
Terrified by a vision <note>original: *ostento*; a miraculous sign or omen</note>, he obeys the converted Christ,
and soon Paul becomes the clear trumpet of the Apostolic choir.

---

->*Paul, from a persecutor of Christians, becomes an Apostle.*<-

From a tiger, from a lion, Paul becomes a lamb,
From an enemy of Christians, he makes himself their Apostle;
A miracle produces this change so beautiful;
In the end, it is no longer Saul; it is Paul, he is someone else entirely.

---

->*Saul, persecuting the Congregation <note>original: *Gemeente*; referring to the early community of believers</note>, is converted by Christ.*<-

Cruelty is tamed, and Saul becomes mild and tame,
He who first resembled a wolf is now a defenseless lamb,
His zeal was indeed good, but his choice <note>original: *keur*</note> had deceived him,
And while seeing he was blind, now he sees without eyes. <note>This refers to the biblical account in Acts 9, where Saul was physically blinded by a light from heaven, which led to his spiritual awakening.</note>

---

->*Paul, from a persecutor, becomes an Apostle.*<-

Saul like a lion does pursue Christians,
And many of them he does shut up <note>original: *mue*; an archaic term meaning to confine or imprison, as one would a hawk in a cage</note> in prison.
But being rebuked from Heaven, he becomes for the future
A great Apostle from a persecutor.

---

->*Paul is converted by Christ.*<-

Saul, who persecuted the Christians anew in his early years,
Dragged them forth to death, from wherever they could be found,
No differently than a wolf scatters the tender little sheep;
Through a thunderclap, Christ the Lord converted him,
So that he, whom he once persecuted, he afterwards confesses and teaches;
From a wolf a shepherd, from Saul he became Paul.

It uses the recurring metaphor of a predatory wolf or lion being transformed into a gentle lamb or shepherd to illustrate his change from a persecutor to the Apostle Paul.</summary>

# ACTS OF THE APOSTLES. 9. 40 <note>original Latin: "ACTORUM"</note>
<meta>Catchword: He calls out <note>original Latin: "Evocat"</note></meta>
T

[An engraving depicting the conversion of Saul on the road to Damascus. In the center, a horse rears up as Saul has fallen to the ground on the left, his arms outstretched. A powerful beam of divine light breaks through dark clouds in the upper right. Several startled companions and soldiers are visible in the background and foreground, some cowering or fleeing. The scene is enclosed in a rectangular frame with a title at the bottom.]

->**Paul Converted by God.**<-

Saul, fiercely persecuting the scattered disciples of Christ, travels to Damascus, the capital city of Syria, located about five miles <note>The historical distance between Jerusalem and Damascus is roughly 135 miles; the author may be using a specific regional "mile" or referring to a stage of the journey.</note> from Jerusalem. Many scattered Jews had taken refuge there, and Saul intended—according to the letters he had received from the Synagogue—to persecute the believers in that city as well. However, while he is on the journey, he is surrounded by a heavenly light, which causes him to fall to the earth. He is rebuked by Christ himself, who calls to him from Heaven, saying that it was He whom Saul was persecuting, and that it will be hard for him to <term>kick against the pricks<gloss>a proverbial expression meaning to struggle fruitlessly against a sharp goad or divine will</gloss></term>. By the glory of this vision, Saul is struck with blindness, so that he had to be led into Damascus. While Paul was there, God sends Ananias, a certain disciple, to him. Ananias, after instructing him about the vision and what had happened to him, lays his hands upon him, restores his sight, and baptizes him.

**Saul, Damascus, Jerusalem, Syria, Christ, Ananias, Conversion, Disciples**

<summary>This page recounts the miraculous conversion of Saul of Tarsus on the road to Damascus, shifting from his role as a persecutor to a baptized follower of Christ after a divine encounter.</summary>
<keywords>Paul the Apostle, Conversion of Saint Paul, Damascus Road, Ananias, religious persecution, New Testament history</keywords>
