<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Acts of the Apostles. Having just described the conversion of Saul on the previous page, the text now moves to another pivotal moment in the early Church: the inclusion of the Gentiles through the baptism of the Roman Centurion Cornelius by the Apostle Peter.</meta>

### With God there is no respect of persons. <note>original Latin: "Apud Deum non est respectus personarum."</note>

Cornelius calls Peter to Joppa, being warned by the voice of an Angel <note>original Latin: "Genii," here referring to a guardian spirit or divine messenger</note>; Peter comes without delay and drenches the believing <term>Tribune<gloss>a high-ranking Roman military officer, used here interchangeably with Centurion</gloss></term> in the sacred fount, having perceived that God does not reject the nations. <note>translated from the original Latin verse</note>

---

->*Cornelius Baptized.* <note>original French: "Corneille baptisé."</note><-

*Cornelius, entirely devout, was commanded by the Angelic voice to hear the Apostolic voice of Peter, who hastens at his petition. Then, knowing that Heaven excludes no man, but accepts the zealous vows of all pious hearts, he baptizes this Tribune full of devotion.* <note>translated from the original French verse</note>

---

->Cornelius the Captain, believing, is baptized. <note>original Dutch: "Cornelius den Hooft-man gelovende, word gedoopt."</note><-

Cornelius, a Captain and soldier, received the blessing and seal of Baptism even before the [water] Baptism occurred; now he fights for his Lord for a better fate in war: his Heathen heart was pious, and where such piety is, there is God. <note>translated from the original Dutch verse</note>

---

->*With God there is no respect of persons.*<-

*Cornelius, bidden by God, sends for Peter, who straightway comes and lends his assistance to christen him within the sacred fount; with God, there is no accounting of persons.* <note>original English verse preserved with modernized spelling for clarity</note>

---

->Cornelius is baptized by Peter. <note>original German: "Cornelius wird von Petrus getaufft."</note><-

After Cornelius received a command from God, he had Peter summoned to him with great longing from Joppa; Peter hurried quickly to Caesarea, and because he knew that God did not entirely reject the <term>Heathens<gloss>non-Jewish nations or Gentiles</gloss></term>, he preached the Word to him, which Cornelius accepted with joy; whereupon Peter administered Baptism to him as a Christian. <note>translated from the original German verse</note>

<meta>catchword: Cornelius</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the baptism of the Roman centurion Cornelius by Saint Peter, emphasizing the theological shift in the early Church toward accepting Gentiles. The polyglot verses reflect on the divine vision that brought the two men together and the principle that God does not show favoritism based on nationality.</summary>
<keywords>Cornelius the Centurion, Saint Peter, baptism, Joppa, Caesarea, Gentiles, religious equality, Acts 10</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Acts of the Apostles, moving from the general statement of God's impartiality on the previous page to the specific story of Cornelius and Peter as recorded in Acts chapter 10.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<sig>T 2</sig>

<image-desc>An etched illustration within a rectangular border. At the top of the border is the text "ACTS 10" <note>original Latin: "ACTORUM. 10."</note> and the number "41". The scene depicts an angel with large wings descending from a bank of clouds on the left, gesturing towards a man on the right. The man, Cornelius, is dressed in robes and a turban, seated at a desk with an open book behind him; he reaches out in a gesture of surprise or reception. Below the illustration is the caption: "Cornelius Visited by the Angel." <note>original Dutch: "Cornelius vande Engel Befocht."</note></image-desc>

Cornelius, a <term>Centurion<gloss>A professional officer of the Roman army; the Dutch text uses 'Hooft-man' (Head-man or Captain)</gloss></term> in Caesarea, sees an Angel in his house who commands him to summon Peter from Joppa to be instructed by him; Cornelius immediately obeys this, sending his servants to Joppa. Meanwhile, Peter is instructed on the roof of the house in Joppa by a vision of a linen sheet (lowered from Heaven with all kinds of clean and unclean animals), showing that the distinction between the Jews and the <term>Gentiles<gloss>Non-Jewish people; the Dutch 'Heidenen' refers to the nations outside the Mosaic Law</gloss></term> was now removed. After this vision, the servants of Cornelius come to Peter, who goes with them to Caesarea, where he is respectfully received by Cornelius. Peter tells Cornelius of the vision which he had seen at Joppa, and likewise Cornelius tells what had happened to him; whereupon he and his household are baptized by Peter and, as the first-fruits <note>original Dutch: "eersteling"</note> among the Gentiles, receive the Holy Spirit.

<meta>catchword: Barna-</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates and describes the pivotal biblical story of Cornelius the Centurion and the Apostle Peter from Acts 10. It details the two matching divine visions that led to the first formal baptism of Gentiles into the early Christian church.</summary>
<keywords>Cornelius, Apostle Peter, Caesarea, Joppa, Centurion, Vision of the Sheet, Baptism of Gentiles, Holy Spirit, Acts 10, clean and unclean animals</keywords>

<page-num>84</page-num>

# Sacrifice is for God, not for holy men. <note>original Latin: "Deo, non sanctis viris, sacrificandum."</note>

The <term>Lycaonians<gloss>Inhabitants of Lycaonia, an ancient region in the interior of Asia Minor</gloss></term> claim that Barnabas and Paul are 
Deities, when they see the lame man leap through the power of the cross, <note>The Latin verse uses "cruce" (cross), though the biblical account emphasizes the power of Christ's name.</note>
And they bring oxen; ready to sacrifice to them— 
A crime which the <term>Apostolic men<gloss>Referring to Paul and Barnabas as sent messengers of Christ</gloss></term> forbade.

---

->*St. Paul heals a lame man and refuses the honors that the Lycaonians wish to give him.*<-

**By** a sudden miracle the people are astonished,
And they make Paul and Barnabas their favorable Gods;
Ready to worship them, they are turned away from it,
Knowing that sacrifices are owed only to the one true God.

---

->The Priests come to Lystra to offer sacrifice to Paul.<-

**Through** Paul it was shown how, in Christ's name,
A cripple was strengthened who before could never walk;
They then attribute to him the wonders of Heaven,
Yet he accepts no sacrifice, but instead offers prayers himself.

---

->*We must sacrifice to God, not to holy men.*<-

**Gentiles** for Gods take Paul and Barnabas,
When they perceiv'd the <term>creeple<gloss>An archaic spelling of "cripple" or lame person</gloss></term> cured was.
Therefore the oxen they to them would kill
For sacrifice; but they forbid such ill.

---

->Paul makes a lame man healthy.<-

**Paul** and Barnabas spoke to those of <term>Lystra<gloss>An ancient city where Paul was later stoned after this initial attempt to worship him</gloss></term>
The Word of Christ’s Kingdom, and also performed miracles,
For they made a crooked, lame man healthy;
The superstitious people made gods of them,
Bringing oxen there to offer and slaughter,
But Paul prevents such a thing and shows them the truth.

<header>ACTS 14. 42</header>
<sig>T 3</sig>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a scene from Acts 14. In the foreground, Paul and Barnabas are surrounded by a crowd in a classical setting with large columns and statues. On the left, a priest and his attendants bring an ox and garlands to an altar with a smoking fire, intending to sacrifice to the apostles. Paul and Barnabas are shown in a state of distress, with one of them rending his clothes and gesturing to the crowd to stop. A caption at the bottom of the image frame reads "Paul and Barnabas."</image-desc>

AFTER the release <note>Referring to Peter's miraculous escape from prison in Acts 12</note> of **Peter**, **Barnabas** and **Paul** were sent by the **Holy Spirit** from among the <term>Teachers of Antioch<gloss>The leaders of the early Christian church in Antioch, a major city in Roman Syria</gloss></term> to the **Gentiles**, to preach the **Gospel** everywhere, such as at **Salamis**, **Paphos**, **Antioch** located in **Pisidia**, **Iconium** and elsewhere; they finally also come to **Lystra** in the province of **Lycaonia**; where **Paul** makes a man **born crippled** healthy; the **crowd** seeing this call **Barnabas** **Jupiter**, and **Paul** **Mercury**; the **Priest of Jupiter** brings forth **oxen and garlands** in their honor, to sacrifice to them as **Gods**; which **Paul** and **Barnabas**, intervening there, could hardly prevent—even with great earnestness—directing them instead toward the **living true God**, who made the **Heaven**, and the **Earth**, and the **Sea**, and all that is within them, and who now invited the **Gentiles** to his **Kingdom** through the **Gospel**.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Apostle Paul’s travels, moving from his ministry in Asia Minor (described on the previous page) to his perilous journey toward Rome as recorded in Acts 27 and 28.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>God’s care for his servants. <note>original: "Cura Dei pro servis suis."</note></header>

He sails toward Italy, but a whirlwind tosses the swimming man,
And the shipwrecked vessel is driven onto Malta.
Here a dreadful snake clings to Paul's hand
But does no harm: for he flings it into the flames.
<note>original Latin: "Navigat Italiam versus, sed turbo natantem / Jactat, & in Maltam naufraga puppis agit. / Hic manui coluber Paulinae dirus inhaeret / Nec nocet: in flammas nam jaculatur eum."</note>

*St. Paul having escaped the shipwreck, a serpent leaps onto his hand as he was warming himself, but he shakes it off without being harmed.*
<note>original French: "S. Paul étant échapé du naufrage, un serpent saute sur sa main comme il se chaufoit, mais il le secouë, sans en être endommagé."</note>

Cast upon the Maltese shore by the storm,
Paul sees a serpent attach itself to his hand;
At first, this accident is misinterpreted; <note>The local people initially believed Paul must be a murderer whom divine justice would not allow to live, even after surviving the sea.</note>
But without the slightest harm, the serpent is cast off.
<note>original French: "Au rivage Maltois par l'orage jetté, / Paul void dessus sa main un serpent qui s'attache; / D'abord cet accident est mal interpreté; / Mais sans le moindre mal le serpent se detache."</note>

### The Shipwreck of the Apostle Paul.

The Sea and the abyss are released to take their prey,
But Paul’s presence saves the lives of the soldiers.
The storm brings them safely to land along with him,
Who through miracles plants the Faith in Malta.
<note>original Dutch: "De Zee en afgrond tot een roof zyn uitgelaten, / Maar Paulus by-zyn red het leven der Soldaten. / De storm brengt haar met hem behouden aan het Land, / Die door mirakelen 't Geloove in Malta plant."</note>

### *God's care for his servants.*

*The ship which carries Paul by storms is tossed*
*And on the shore of Malta is wrecked and lost.*
*Yet he and all escaping come to land;*
*A viper, without harming, bites Paul's hand.*

### Paul suffers Shipwreck.

Paul sails for Rome, a great storm arises,
The ship strikes Malta, breaks, and goes to wreck;
Paul reaches the land, having saved himself by swimming.
A poisonous and evil snake hangs itself upon his hand;
As he tends the fire, Paul shakes into the fire
The harmful beast without injury; God holds His pious servants dear.
<note>original German: "Paulus schiffet nach Rom, ein Wetter gross entstehet, / Das Schiff an Malta stösst, bricht und zu scheitern gehet, / Paulus kommt an das Land, mit schwimmen sich erwehrt, / Ein Slang vergift und böss an seine Hand sich hencket, / Als er das Feuer schütt, Paulus ins Feuer schlencket / Das schädlich Thier ohn schad, Godt hat fromm' Diener werth."</note>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Apostle Paul’s travels, transitioning from the poetic summaries of his shipwreck and the miracle of the viper on Malta to a detailed prose account of the events recorded in Acts 27 and 28.</meta>

### ACTS 27:43

->**The Shipwreck of Paul**<-

After Paul had traveled through many places, he finally arrives in Jerusalem, where he is seized by the Jews, handed over to the Gentiles <note>The original Dutch uses "Heidenen" (heathens), referring here to the Roman authorities.</note>, and taken to Caesarea. Having been placed on a ship at Caesarea (since he had appealed to the Emperor <note>original: "Keizer." Paul, as a Roman citizen, exercised his right to have his case heard by Nero in Rome.</note>), he was to be taken to Rome. Passing by Sidon, Myra, and Crete, he was compelled by a great, tempestuous wind—which caused the ship to sink—to swim to land. Others did the same, and they arrived safely at Melite, now called Malta, where he and the others were well received by the inhabitants. While sitting together by the fire (because of the cold), Paul was bitten by a viper <note>original: "Adder."</note> (which came out of the fire). He shook it off without injury, which amazed the islanders, who then took him for a god.

<meta>This page transitions from the historical accounts of the Acts of the Apostles to the visionary Book of Revelation. **The Author of the Apocalypse: Christ in Glory.** <note>original Latin: "Auctor Apocalypseos Christus in gloria."</note>

Christ reveals himself in divine majesty to John
And commands him to write wondrous things for the Church.
His eyes glow with fire, his face flashes with brilliant light,
From his mouth flashes a sword, and his hand holds seven stars.
<note>original Latin: "Divinâ Christus se Majestate Johanni / Monstrat & Ecclesiæ scribere mira jubet. / Igne micant oculi, facies candore coruscat, / Ore vibrat gladium, septem habet astra manus."</note>

->*Jesus Christ appears to Saint John, with seven stars in*<-
->*his hand, and as many candlesticks around*<-
->*him, with a sword coming out of his mouth.*<-
<note>original French: "Jesus Christ se montre à S. Jean, avec 7. étoiles en sa main, & autant de chandeliers autour de soi, avec une épée qui sort de sa bouche."</note>

Christ appears to Saint John with eyes of fire,
A sword in his mouth, seven stars in his hand,
As many candlesticks arranged in the same place
And many truths hide beneath these veils.
<note>This French verse emphasizes the "veils" or allegories of the vision.</note>

### The first vision of the seven Candlesticks, and
### the Person of Christ.
<note>original Dutch: "Het eerste gezigte van de zeven Kandelaren, en den Persoon Christi."</note>

John sees the Lord there where everything glows and burns,
Beside seven Candlesticks with Stars in his hand,
In the trailing garment he covers all his limbs,
His word is like a sword with a sharp edge on both sides.
<note>The "trailing garment" refers to the priestly robe of Christ described in Revelation 1:13.</note>

### The Author of the Apocalypse: Christ in glory.

Christ unto John in Majesty appears,
*And to his Churches what to write inspires.*
His eyes like fire, his face shines like the Sun.
In his hand seven stars, from his mouth a sword does run.

### John sees the Glory of the Lord.
<note>original German: "Johannes sihet die Herrlichkeit des Herren."</note>

John sees in the Spirit the glory of the Lord,
In His majesty and His divine honors,
Who bids him write down hidden wonders;
Brightly shines His countenance, His eyes like two flames,
His hand holds seven stars, on His hip His name, <note>original German: "auff seiner Hüfft fein Namen." This refers to Revelation 19:16, where "King of Kings" is written on His thigh.</note>
A sharp two-edged sword went out from His mouth.

# APOCALYPSE 1. 44

<image-desc>An etched illustration within a rectangular border depicting the vision from Revelation 1. On the left, Christ (the Son of Man) stands on clouds with a radiant aura. He holds seven stars in his right hand, and a sword protrudes from his mouth. He is surrounded by seven tall candlesticks. On the ground below, John the Apostle is shown prostrate on a rocky landscape next to the water. In the background, a hilly landscape features trees and a distant castle or city. At the bottom of the illustration frame is a title banner.</image-desc>

->7 Stars and the 7 Candlesticks.<-

John the Apostle of Christ, banished by the Emperor Domitian <note>Domitian was a Roman Emperor who reigned from 81 to 96 AD; tradition holds he persecuted Christians and exiled John.</note> to the Island of Patmos, saw there many and various visions of the things that would happen in and concerning the Christian Church. In the first vision, one appeared to him resembling the **Son of Man** <term>Son of Man: A messianic title used in the Book of Daniel and by Jesus himself to denote his divine and human nature</term>, whose head and hair were white as snow, eyes like a flame of fire, feet shining like brass, and a voice like the rushing of many waters; having seven Stars in his right hand, and a two-edged sword coming out of his mouth, and a face like the Sun, shining in its full strength; standing in a long garment down to the feet, and girded around the chest with a golden belt, in the midst of seven Candlesticks. He gave John the command to write, saying that the seven golden Candlesticks are the seven Congregations <term>Congregations: Often translated as "Churches," referring to the seven specific faith communities in Asia Minor</term>, and the seven Stars are the Angels of the seven Congregations. <note>In this context, "Angels" is often interpreted by scholars as the messengers or leaders (bishops) of the seven churches.</note>

<sig>T 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: John</meta>

<summary>This page describes John the Apostle's exile on Patmos and his first vision of the glorified Christ, who appears with a sword and stars among seven candlesticks representing the early churches.</summary>
<keywords>John the Apostle, Patmos, Domitian, Son of Man, Seven Stars, Seven Candlesticks, Revelation, Seven Churches</keywords>

### The twenty-four elders fell down. <note>original Latin: "Procidebant viginti quatuor ſeniores."</note>

The Lord reveals great mysteries to John,
And the evils that the flock of Christ-worshippers must endure.
The twenty-four fathers, having set aside their crowns,
Extol and celebrate God with praises.

### *Twenty-four Elders prostrate themselves before the Throne of God.* <note>original French: "Vingt quatre Vieillards ſe proſternent devant le Trôn de Dieu."</note>

Saint John *sees in spirit what those must suffer,*
*Who will always keep the name of Christ in their memory;*
Twenty-four elders *then come to offer*
*Praises to God, whose glory they preach.*

### The second vision of the Glory of God and the Lamb. <note>original Dutch: "Het tweede gezigte van de Heerlykheid God- en des Lams."</note>

God sits upon an Emerald <note>In Revelation 4:3, the throne is surrounded by a rainbow that looks like an emerald (Smaragd).</note> where twice-twelve Souls
Kneel down reverently on their faces without Crowns.
How His glory shines in the everlasting Court!
Heaven always gives praise to the Lord of Heaven.

### *The four and twenty Elders fell down.*

Christ unto *John* great mysteries reveals,
*And what his flock should bear, he to him tells;*
The twice-twelve Elders putting off their Crowns
Fall down, extolling him with pleasant sounds.

### The twenty-four men fall down before the Throne. <note>original German: "Die Vier-und-zwantzig Männer fallen nieder für den Throon."</note>

The Lord reveals many secrets to John,
How He preserves His Church in the midst of the Cross <note>original: "Creutz," used here to mean the suffering or trials of the faithful.</note>,
Even when everything goes to ruin through various plagues;
Here twenty-four men, magnificent and well along in years,
Lay down their crowns, giving thanks to God with humility,
Singing a song of praise that resounds in Heaven.

# REVELATION 4. 45

<image-desc>An engraving depicting the vision of the heavenly throne room from the Book of Revelation, chapter 4. In the center, a divine figure sits on a throne surrounded by a circular radiance and seven lamps. A lamb stands on the clouds before the throne. Surrounding the central scene are the twenty-four elders, some with harps and crowns, seated on their own thrones amidst celestial columns and angels. In the foreground, a man (John the Apostle) kneels in a posture of adoration, looking up at the vision. Below the illustration is the caption: "The Glory of the Lamb."</image-desc>

->The Glory of the Lamb<-

AFTER the first vision in which Christ depicted himself in his **Royal** and **Priestly** state, walking among the **seven Candlesticks** or Churches, follows the second in which God shows himself, sitting on a **Royal Throne**, which was like the stone **Jasper** and **Sardius**, and around the Throne a **rainbow** is shown, and furthermore **twenty-four Thrones**, on which sit **twenty-four Elders** with **Crowns** on their heads, who—when the **four Living Creatures** <note>original: "vier Dieren," often translated as "four beasts" in older Bibles.</note> that are around the Throne give glory, honor, and gratitude to God—throwing down their Crowns, sing all praise and glory to God: thereafter appears a **Lamb**, standing as if slain, which points to **Christ**, who alone receives and opens the **sealed Book** that God had in his hand, since no **Creature** in Heaven or on Earth could open the same.

## The twenty-four elders fell down.

SAINT John sees in spirit what those who will always keep the name of Christ in memory must suffer. Twenty-four elders then come to offer praises to God, whose glory they proclaim.

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>REVELATION 4. 5.</header>

<image-desc>An engraving illustrating a vision from the fourth chapter of the Book of Revelation (Apocalypse). In the upper center, God is seated on a throne within a brilliant sunburst radiance, surrounded by angels and clouds. Seven flaming lamps are positioned below the throne. A Lamb stands on a small cloud in the center. Encircling the central figures are the four living creatures (the lion, the ox, the man, and the eagle) and the twenty-four elders wearing crowns. In the bottom foreground, the apostle John is depicted from behind, kneeling in awe. The entire illustration is contained within a rectangular border, with a caption at the bottom.</image-desc>

->The Glory of the Lamb.<-

AFTER the first vision in which Christ portrayed himself in his royal and priestly state, walking among the seven candlesticks or congregations <note>In the Book of Revelation, the seven candlesticks represent the seven churches of Asia Minor.</note>, there follows the second in which God shows himself, sitting upon a royal throne, which was like the stones jasper and sardius <note>These gemstones were used to describe the brilliant, multi-colored light radiating from the Divine presence.</note>, and around the throne was shown a rainbow, and also twenty-four thrones, upon which sit twenty-four elders with crowns on their heads, who—when the four living creatures <note>original Dutch: "Dieren." These are the four winged beings (the lion, ox, man, and eagle) that serve as guardians of the throne.</note> who are around the throne give glory, honor, and gratitude to God—casting down their crowns, sing to God all praise and glory: afterward, there appears a Lamb, standing as if slain, which represents Christ, who alone receives and opens from God's hand the sealed book that He held in His hand, because no creature in heaven or on earth was able to open it.

<meta>catchword: Albus (Latin: White; likely referring to the "White Horse" in the subsequent chapter).</meta>

## The twenty-four elders fell down.
<note>original Latin: "Procidebant viginti quatuor seniores." This title is taken directly from the Latin Vulgate Bible, Revelation 4:10.</note>

The Lord reveals great mysteries to John,
And the evils which the flock of Christ’s followers must suffer.
The twenty-four fathers, having laid aside their crowns,
Extol and celebrate God with praises.
<note>original: "Johanni Dominus mysteria magna revelat..."</note>

### *Twenty-four Elders prostrate themselves before the Throne of God.*

*Saint John sees in the spirit what must be suffered*
*By those who will always keep the name of Christ in memory;*
*Twenty-four elders then come to offer*
*Praises to God, whose glory they proclaim.*

### The second vision of the Glory of God and of the Lamb.

God sits upon an emerald throne where twice-twelve souls
Kneel down reverently on their faces without their crowns;
How His glory shines in the everlasting Court!
Heaven always gives praise to the Lord of Heaven.

### *The four and twenty Elders fell down.*

*Christ unto John great mysteries reveals,*
*And what his flock should bear, he to him tells;*
*The twice-twelve Elders putting off their Crowns*
*Fall down, extolling him with pleasant sounds.*

### The Twenty-four Men fall down before the Throne.

The Lord reveals many secrets to John,
How He preserves His Church in the midst of the Cross, <note>In this context, "the Cross" refers to the suffering and trials faced by the Christian community.</note>
When everything is destroyed through manifold plagues.
Here twenty-four men, glorious and well-advanced in years,
Lay down their crowns, giving thanks to God with humility,
Singing a song of praise that resounds in Heaven.

<note>technical terms: Apocalypse (Revelation), Twenty-four Elders (representing the twelve tribes of Israel and the twelve apostles), Revelation (the unveiling of divine secrets), John the Apostle (the traditional author of the book), Lamb of God (Jesus Christ), Heavenly Court (the assembly of divine beings), Diadem/Crowns (symbols of status surrendered to God).</note>

->The Glory of the Lamb.<-

Following the first vision in which Christ depicted himself in his Royal and Priestly state, walking among the seven Lampstands or Congregations <note>original Dutch: "Kandelaren ofte Gemeinten"; referring to the seven churches of Asia Minor addressed in the opening of Revelation</note>, the second follows in which God reveals Himself sitting upon a Royal Throne, which was like the stones Jasper and Sardius; and around the Throne a rainbow was shown, and also twenty-four Thrones upon which sit twenty-four Elders with Crowns upon their heads, who—whenever the four Living Creatures <note>original Dutch: "vier Dieren", traditionally translated as "beasts" or "living creatures" in the King James and other English bibles</note> who are around the Throne give glory, honor, and gratitude to God—throwing down their Crowns, sing all praise and glory to God: thereafter a Lamb appears, standing as if slaughtered <note>original Dutch: "geflagtet"; this is the "Lamb of God" representing the crucified and risen Christ</note>, which signifies Christ, who alone receives and opens the sealed Book that God had in His hand, because no other Creature in Heaven or on Earth was able to open it.

## The twenty-four elders fell down.
<note>original Latin: "Procidebant viginti quatuor seniores."</note>

The Lord reveals great mysteries to John,
And the evils that the flock of Christ-followers <term>Christicolûm</term> <note>Latin: "Christicolûm," meaning those who worship or follow Christ</note> must suffer.
The twenty-four fathers, having set aside their crowns,
Extol and celebrate God with praises.

---

### Twenty-four Elders prostrate themselves before the Throne of God.

Saint John sees in spirit what those who always keep
The name of Christ in memory must suffer;
Twenty-four elders then come to offer
Praises to God, whose glory they proclaim.

---

### The second vision of the Glory of God and the Lamb.

God sits upon an emerald where twice-twelve souls <note>A poetic way of describing the twenty-four elders</note>
Kneel down reverently on their faces without their crowns;
How His glory shines in the everlasting Court!
Heaven always gives praise to the Lord of Heaven.

---

### *The twenty-four Elders fell down.*

Christ reveals great mysteries to John,
And tells him what his flock should bear;
The twice-twelve Elders, putting off their crowns,
Fall down, extolling him with pleasant sounds.

---

### The Twenty-four Men fall down before the Throne.

The Lord reveals many secrets to John,
How He preserves His Church in the midst of the Cross <term>Creutz</term> <note>German: "Creutz" (Cross), here used metaphorically to mean suffering or tribulation</note>,
When everything goes to ruin through various plagues.
Here, twenty-four men, glorious and advanced in years,
Lay down their crowns and thank God with humility,
Singing a song of praise <term>Lobgesang</term> that echoes in Heaven.

APOCAL. 4.
<meta>Handwritten "91" in upper right margin. Catchword "Albus" (White) at bottom right.</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving illustrating the vision of the heavenly throne room from Revelation 4. At the center, God sits on a throne surrounded by a radiant halo and an arched rainbow. Seven lamps burn before the throne. A Lamb stands directly in front of the seated figure. Surrounding the throne are the four living creatures (lion, ox, man, and eagle) and twenty-four elders seated on smaller thrones. In the foreground, the apostle John kneels in awe, looking up at the vision. Above, numerous angels and cherubim are visible in the clouds.</image-desc>

->The Glory of the Lamb.<-

AFTER the first vision in which Christ depicted Himself in His Royal and Priestly state, walking among the seven Lampstands or Congregations <note>original Dutch: "Gemeinten," referring to the seven churches of Asia Minor</note>, there follows the second vision. In this, God reveals Himself sitting upon a Royal Throne, which appeared like the stones Jasper and Sardius <note>Jasper and Sardius (carnelian) are used in the biblical text to describe the fiery and brilliant appearance of God’s glory</note>. Around the Throne a rainbow was shown, along with twenty-four other Thrones. Upon these sit twenty-four Elders with Crowns on their heads. When the four Living Creatures <note>original Dutch: "vier Dieren," often translated as "four beasts," representing all of creation (lion, ox, man, and eagle)</note> that surround the Throne give glory, honor, and gratitude to God, the Elders cast down their Crowns and sing to God all praise and glory. Thereafter appears a Lamb, standing as if it had been slain, which signifies Christ. He alone receives from God’s hand and opens the sealed Book, for no other creature in Heaven or on Earth was found worthy to open it.

->Image of the Four Calamities.<-

A white horse, a red, a black, and one shameful for its leanness,
Four signs denote a fourfold punishment:
War, death, grim famine, pestilence, and the Underworld <note>original Latin: "Erebumque," referring to Erebus, a personification of darkness and the underworld</note>.
These same things are signaled by the star falling from the Sky.

### *Four Horsemen represent the four calamities.*

Here four horses appear at once,
One white, one red, one black, and one of lean and sad appearance;
The four Horsemen threaten all at once
With war and death, with pestilence and famine.

### The wonders occur after the opening of the seven Seals.

Power and might are given to the Lion from the Tribe of Judah,
So that he may break all seven seals of the Book;
Four plagues are on horseback; on the third is Famine,
This one carries a bow, that one a sword; on the fourth sits Death.

### *A Figure of the four Calamities.*

The horses white, red, black, and pale presage
With their four riders gainst poore man to rage,
With warr, death, famine, sickness and with hell
Which the Sun, Moon, and Starres likewise foretell.

### The wonders happen after the opening of the seals.

A white horse and a chestnut <note>original German: "Fuchs," literally "fox," referring to a reddish-brown horse</note>; along with those who ride them,
A black horse and a lean pale one <note>original German: "Falck," likely a variation of "Falb" meaning a pale or dun-colored horse</note> signify four harsh punishments:
Foreign yokes and tyranny, war and murder in the world,
Hunger and times of scarcity <note>original German: "theure Zeit," referring to inflation or high prices during famine</note>; everything comes together now,
Also pestilence and death, along with the flames of hell,
This is witnessed by many a star that falls from the sky.

## REVELATION 6 and 7 <margin>46</margin>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting scenes from the Book of Revelation, chapters 6 and 7. At the top left, God or the Lamb sits on a heavenly throne surrounded by a host of angels in the clouds. Below this, the Four Horsemen of the Apocalypse emerge from the clouds: the first is a skeleton representing Death with a scythe on a pale horse, followed by a rider with a sword, one with a bow, and one holding a pair of scales. In the background to the right, stars are seen falling from the sky toward the earth, while a darkened sun and crescent moon appear above. In the foreground at the bottom right, people are shown cowering in fear and seeking shelter among rocky cliffs and caves. The entire scene is enclosed in a rectangular border.</image-desc>

->The Opening of the 7 Seals.<-

AFTER the Lamb has received the Book sealed with seven seals from the right hand of Him who sits upon the Throne, He opens the seven seals; and after the opening of the first seal, there appears a white Horse, upon which sits one who conquers, which according to most interpretations <note>The author refers to the common Protestant commentaries of the 17th century</note> signifies the pure Preaching of the holy Gospel of Christ; after the opening of the second seal, there comes forth a red Horse, upon which sits one who takes away peace; the third seal being opened, a black horse is shown, upon which sits one with a pair of scales in his hand; this signifies false teachings: the fourth seal being opened, there comes forth a pale horse upon which Death sits; after the opening of the fifth, the cry of the holy Martyrs to God is heard: after the sixth follow great signs in Heaven and on Earth: after the seventh follows a silence in Heaven. These things occurred after Christ's death until the time of Constantine <note>Constantine the Great (r. 306–337 AD), the first Roman emperor to convert to Christianity, often seen by historians of this period as the end of the era of martyrdom</note>, as some believe.

### The third vision of the seven Angels.

The seals are open, the Book is spread wide,
Where the angels everywhere trumpet the world’s plagues,

### The third vision of the seven angels.

When the Lamb opened the book with the seals <term>seals: the original German "Pitſchieren" refers specifically to wax signet seals used to secure official documents</term>,
The angels at that hour present themselves before God,
Seven trumpets are handed to them there,
With which to sound the terrible plagues,
And endless misery, so much to be lamented,
Which shall befall the godless world.

<meta>Catchword: Star</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving within a framed border illustrating scenes from the Book of Revelation, Chapter 8. In the upper portion, a group of angels is depicted among the clouds, seven of whom are blowing long trumpets. In the center, an angel stands behind a cloth-covered altar, holding a smoking censer. ->the 7 Trumpeting Angels.<-

AFTER the vision of the seven seals, seven Angels appear, standing before God, to whom seven Trumpets were given; and after that another Angel, having a Censer filled with the prayers of the Saints, which he fills with fire from the Altar and throws upon the Earth: this having been done, the first Angel sounds his trumpet, from which hail and fire come forth, which some interpret as the turmoil <note>original: "beroerte"; in this context, it refers to political or civil upheaval and uprising</note> that occurred between the Romans and the Jews, by which peace was taken away from the Earth; and the second, after which something like a great mountain of burning fire was thrown into the Sea: after the sounding of the third Angel, a Star named Wormwood <term>Wormwood: "Alsem" in the original; a plant known for its extreme bitterness, used here to symbolize a poisonous or bitter spiritual influence</term> falls from Heaven into the waters; this was interpreted as the Heresy of Arius <note>Arius (c. 250–336 AD) taught that Jesus Christ was a created being, a view that sparked centuries of theological conflict in the early Church</note>: the fourth Angel having also sounded his trumpet, a third part of the Sun, the Moon, and the Stars were darkened; many interpret this as the apostasy <note>a falling away from the faith</note> in the Western Church, <margin>a</margin> after the fourth Angel follows another calling out woe, woe, woe, over Mankind.

<header>God arms his creatures against the ungodly. <note>original Latin: "Deus armat creaturas in impios."</note></header>

## The plagues of the next two Angels out of the seven.

The plagues increase; a Star descended from above

The well of the Abyss is unlocked by a star,

The Kingdom of the Antichrist <note>The author here adopts a common Reformation-era interpretation, identifying the destructive locusts with the perceived corruption or forces of the Antichrist.</note> bears this image within itself.

# REVELATION 9
V
<meta>catchword: Nubibus (Latin: Clouds)</meta>

<image-desc>A detailed engraving illustrating scenes from the ninth chapter of the Book of Revelation. The image is enclosed in a decorative rectangular border. In the sky, two angels blow trumpets. On the left side, thick smoke rises from a pit (the bottomless pit); within the smoke, a star is falling, and winged locusts with human-like features emerge. In the center and right background, a massive army of horsemen advances, and many figures are shown lying dead or dying on the ground. At the top right, a figure sits on a throne amidst clouds. The number 48 is printed in the top right corner of the image area. A caption at the bottom of the engraving reads: "The Plagues of the two following Angels."</image-desc>

->The Plagues of the two following Angels.<-

After the previous Angels had sounded their trumpets, the fifth Angel follows and sounds his, upon which a Star falls from Heaven, which has opened the key to the abyss <note>The "abyss" or "bottomless pit" in biblical literature represents the realm of chaos or the demonic.</note>, from which smoke emerges just as from a Furnace; and from the smoke come forth Locusts, which sting the Men who do not have the seal of God; this Star is interpreted by some as the Antichrist. After this, the sixth Angel sounds, whereupon four Angels are released at the river Euphrates, and a great multitude of Horsemen appear, who kill a third part of mankind, and yet not all were converted who remained from these Plagues. By these four Angels who appeared, most Interpreters understand the Mohammedans <note>original Dutch: "Mahometanen"; a historical term used by the author to refer to Muslims.</note>, who were divided into four groups: Arabs, Saracens, Tartars, and Turks; and their destruction and slaughter were carried out against the Greeks and other Christians. Others [interpret these forces as] the power of the Spiritual Babylon <note>A common Protestant rhetorical term in the 17th and 18th centuries used to refer to the Roman Catholic Church.</note> which takes the field against the Church that has departed from her.

# The knowledge of God’s works is sweet and bitter.
<note>original Latin: "Cognitio operum Dei est dulcis & amara."</note>

Clad in clouds as if in a garment, the Spirit
Gives the book to John, and bids him devour it with a greedy tooth.
He obeys, and discovers a sweet taste in his mouth,
But thereafter it creates agonizing pains in his swollen belly.
<note>This Latin verse describes Revelation 10:9-10, where the Apostle John is commanded to eat a small scroll which tastes like honey but turns his stomach bitter.</note>

->*An Angel clothed in a cloud, and having for legs co-*<-
->*lumns, resting one of his feet on the earth, and*<-
->*the other on the sea, offers a book to John.*<-

*The Angel seen here, clothed in a cloud,*
*Presents a book to John, who devours it at once;*
*The taste pleases the palate, but his belly is struck*
*With terrible pains, the cause of which is unknown.*

->A vision of an Angel standing on the Sea and<-
->Earth.<-

John receives a Book from the Angel from on high,
As if with a garment about the body, covered with Clouds;
He eats it up with relish and feels the pain thereafter,
That which is sweet in the mouth, is bitter for the heart.
<note>This Dutch verse emphasizes the internal emotional shift from the "sweetness" of receiving God's word to the "bitterness" of the judgment it contains.</note>

->*An Angel standing upon the sea and the earth.*<-

*An Angel clad in clouds whose face like sun*
*For to be eaten gives a book to John:*
*Which in his mouth for sweetness passeth all,*
*But in his belly bitterer than Gall.*
<note>The original text included this English version alongside the other languages, demonstrating the international nature of 17th-century religious emblems.</note>

->An Angel stands upon the Sea and Earth.<-

An Angel, whose head is like unto the Sun,
Clad with Clouds, reaches a book to John;
Which he shall devour, which he did without delay,
At first in his mouth it tastes sweet like Honey,
Thereafter it awakens painful griping in his belly.
Such is the nature and character of human ordinances.
<note>The German commentary adds a moralizing twist, comparing the experience to "human ordinances" or laws, which may seem attractive at first but become burdensome.</note>

# APOCALYPSE 10.
V 2

<meta>Illustration frame contains number 49 in the top right corner; catchword: And</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting a scene from Revelation 10. A massive angel, clothed in a cloud with a rainbow above his head and a face like the sun, stands with his right foot on the sea and his left foot on the land. His legs are like pillars of fire. He holds an open book up towards the sky. On the left, a smaller figure representing John the Apostle is kneeling on the ground. The background shows a sea with ships and a coastal town with several towers and buildings.</image-desc>

->The Angel Standing on the Sea and the Earth.<-

After the sixth Angel had finished his trumpets and poured out his plagues, an Angel comes clothed with a Cloud (having a face like the Sun, and a Rainbow above his head) down from Heaven with the Little Book—namely <term>providence<note>The original Dutch uses 'voorzienigheid', referring to God's divine foreknowledge and management of the world.</note></term>—which is open in his right hand. He sets his right foot on the Sea and his left on the Earth, calling out with a great voice, upon which seven Thunders (which are certain mysteries and predictions of future things) follow. He swears by Him who lives for eternity that there shall be no more time after the seven trumpets have sounded. Thereafter, he gives the Little Book to the Apostle to eat; that is, to carefully consider, understand, and <term>ruminate<note>The Dutch 'herkauwen' literally means 'to chew the cud,' a common theological metaphor for deep, repetitive meditation on scripture.</note></term> upon. It was indeed sweet in his mouth, but bitter in his body. By this Angel, some understand the Angel Gabriel; others believe it to be the Lord Jesus Christ himself, descending from Heaven in regard to his works and the taking up of his Kingdom on Earth.

# The Struggle of God’s Martyrs with the Antichrist.
<note>original: "Lucta Martyrum Dei cum Antichrifto."</note>

See the twin Witnesses, both olive trees and lamps;
    They piously oppose your enemy, O Christ:
The Beast slays them; but soon, both restored to life,
    Rebuke the wicked men with zealous speech.

->*Two Prophets clothed in sackcloth, and announcing God, are killed by the Dragon, and subsequently resurrected.*<-
<note>original: "Deux Prophetes vêtes de ſacs, & annonçant Dieu font tuez par le Dragon, & reſuſcitez en ſuite."</note>

Two Prophets of God are dressed in sackcloth,
    *And the beast of hell declares war upon them;*
While they preach the virtues of the Lord,
They are killed, and soon they return to the earth.

->The two Witnesses of Christ, killed by the Beast, are made living again and taken up.<-
<note>original: "De twee Getuigen Chriſti van 't Beeſt gedood, worden weder levendig, en opgenomen."</note>

The Hellish Beast has two Witnesses, great in power,
    Fought against and at last murderously struck down,
Though they lie murdered, God shall not forsake them,
They fear no Death, they died for the sake of Life.

->*The wrestling of Martyrs with the Antichrist.*<-

*See the two witnesses, the Candlesticks*
    *And the Olive trees which do oppose the tricks*
*Of the Antichrist; whom the Beast kills in hate,*
*Yet they revive, and blame the reprobate.*

->The two witnesses of Christ killed by the Beast.<-
<note>original: "Die zwey Zeugen Chriſti von dem Thier getödt."</note>

The Lord sends two witnesses, two olive trees, two lamps
    Which shine in the world like two shimmering stars,
    Rebuking the Antichrist for his idolatry,
The Beast kills them both, yet after several days
They appear alive again and prophesy,
    The people wonder how such a thing could have happened.

REVELATION 11. 50
V 3
<meta>Catchword: Beast (original Latin: Bestia)</meta>

->The Two Witnesses of God<-

After the angel had given the little book <term>little book<note>original: "Boeksken"; referring to the scroll in Revelation 10 that the prophet John is told to eat, symbolizing the internalization of God's word.</note></term> to the Apostle to eat, he declared to him that he must prophesy once more; also, a measuring rod <note>original: "Ried-stok"</note> was given to him to measure the Temple, except for the outer court which lies outside the Temple. Christ grants power to two Witnesses to prophesy for one thousand two hundred and sixty days; they were clothed in sackcloth, mighty in word and deed, whom the Beast, arising from the Abyss <term>Abyss<note>original: "Afgrond"; refers to the bottomless pit or the realm of the dead.</note></term>, overcame and killed. But after three and a half days they became living once again through the Spirit of life that had entered into them from God, and were taken up into Heaven, after which the City collapsed in many places due to an earthquake. By these two Witnesses, some understand Enoch and Elijah <note>Two figures from the Hebrew Bible who were said to have been taken to heaven without dying, and thus were traditionally expected to return as precursors to the end of the world.</note>, who would contend against the Antichrist in the final days; others understand them to be the Books of the Old and New Testaments. After this, the seventh Angel sounds his trumpet, portraying the coming of Christ in the fullness of His Kingdom.

# False miracles are the cause of Idolatry.

The terrible Beast, joined by unholy vows to the Dragon,
Deceiving the nations, performs astonishing signs.
Hence the people and foolish Kings adore the Evil Spirit <note>original Latin: "Cacodæmona"; a term derived from Greek meaning an evil spirit or demon.</note>,
Nothing can be more foolish than idolaters.

---

### *The Beast with seven heads.*

By a new wonder, here one sees emerging
From the depths of the sea a beast with seven heads,
She deceives the people, and strives to render them mindless,
Her goal is to stir up terrible storms.

---

### The entire Earth worships the Beast with seven heads.

The seven-headed Beast, through false wonders,
Is celebrated and worshipped by the foolish People;
While God’s praise should be spread, the World stops its ear
And listens to the Dragon; slander finds an audience.

---

### *False miracles the cause of Idolatry.*

A Beast seven-headed of a monstrous kind
With his false wonders cheats ten nations blind.
Kings and all men adore his Deity;
There is nothing so foolish as Idolatry.

---

### The People worship the Beast.

The Beast of blasphemy performs wonders before all,
(The Dragon gave him power) he makes fire fall from Heaven,
By which the blind people were wretchedly led astray,
Worshipping the Dragon and the Beast alike;
Whoever lets himself be fooled by false miraculous signs,
Gives the creature the honor that is due only to the Creator.

# REVELATION 13.

V 4
<meta>handwritten page number: 103, catchword: Divine</meta>

<image-desc>An engraving depicting scenes from the biblical book of Revelation, chapter 13. On the left, a large seven-headed beast with dragon-like features emerges from the waves of the sea. On the right, a second beast with two horns like a lamb stands on a rocky elevation. In the foreground, a large crowd of people, including figures wearing crowns (kings), are kneeling and bowing in worship toward the beasts. The sky shows smoke or clouds above the horizon.</image-desc>

->The Kings Worship the Beast.<-

---

IN the fourth vision (after the appearance of a Woman in the pains of childbirth <note>Referring to the "Woman clothed with the Sun" from Revelation 12, who symbolizes the Church.</note>) there is shown to John a Beast, coming out of the Sea, that is, with a roar arising from the gathering of many nations <note>In biblical prophecy, "waters" and "seas" often represent large multitudes of people or unstable political states.</note>, which Beast had seven heads and ten horns, was like a horse <note>The Dutch text uses the word *Paard* (horse), though the biblical source in Revelation 13:2 describes the beast as a leopard. This may be a transcription error or a specific local interpretation.</note>, and was adorned upon its heads with ten Royal Crowns, speaking blasphemy, and waged war against the Saints, which Beast was nevertheless worshipped by all the nations. After this, another Beast appeared, coming out of the Earth, having two horns like a Lamb's horns, exercising all the power of the first Beast, and performing great signs alongside this, deceiving the People and forcing them to make an Image of the Beast and worship it. By the "Image," some understand that person who sets himself up as the head of the visible Church. It is very remarkable that the Name **LUDOVICUS** <note>The Latinized form of "Louis." 17th-century Protestant scholars often calculated the Roman numeral values of names to identify the Antichrist; L(50)+V(5)+D(500)+V(5)+I(1)+C(100)+V(5) equals 666. This was likely a contemporary reference to King Louis XIV of France.</note> so precisely makes up the number of the Beast, being 666, as can be read in the Holy Revelation of John, Chapter 13, verse 18.

<vocab>Revelation 13, John, Beast, 666, Ludovicus, Revelation, Birth-pains</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the two beasts of Revelation 13—one from the sea and one from the earth—and provides a 17th-century Protestant interpretation linking the name "Ludovicus" to the biblical number of the beast.</summary>
<keywords>Beast from the sea, Beast from the earth, Ludovicus, 666, Antichrist, Image of the Beast, Louis XIV, Gematria</keywords>

<page-num>104</page-num>
# The wrath of God is revealed against all ungodliness.
<meta>catchword: Now</meta>

The Angels pour out the Vials of Divine Vengeance <note>original Latin: "Divinæ Phiolas... Vindictæ"</note> upon the World, while the spirits’ trumpet sounds a mournful tone. From this arise various disasters, diseases, death <note>original Latin: "Libitina", the Roman goddess of funerals, used here as a poetic term for death or the grave</note>, and famine, bringing destruction and ruin to the wicked.

---

->*Seven Angels pour seven vials upon the earth.*<-

Into the earth, into the sea, into the fiery Euphrates, seven Angels at once pour out their seven vials; a bad omen for Mankind of great and numerous evils, and these predictions shall not be in vain.

---

->A vision of seven Angels pouring out their Vials.<-

The seven Angels pour down God's wrath, and cause his flood of anger to strike down upon the Earth, which bring forth disaster, and woe, and plagues beyond measure. Here one sees then how God hates the godless nature.

---

->*The wrath of God manifested against all impiety.*<-

>*Seven vials full of wrath God’s Angels pour*
>*Upon the World <note>original: "Word", likely a printer's error for "World"</note> there the wicked to devour.*
>*From whence come such murders, sickness, war, and famine <note>original: "dearth"</note>,*
>*Thunders and earthquakes to the solid earth.*

---

->The seven Angels pour out their bowls.<-

The bowls of God's wrath, so full that they overflow, seven angels pour out upon the godless world; whereupon many punishments, heavy and hard, arise. Great misery, fear, and distress stir in every corner; hunger and pestilence, and illnesses that cannot be turned away—thus the company of the Antichrist was plagued.

<term>Angeli</term> (Angels), <term>Phiolas</term> (Vials or shallow bowls used in ritual), <term>Euphrate</term> (The River Euphrates), <term>Engelen</term> (Angels), <term>Vials</term> (Vessels containing the plagues), <term>Schalen</term> (Bowls), <term>Antichristisch</term> (Relating to the Antichrist), <term>Apocalypse</term> (The Book of Revelation).

<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 104, transitioning from the description of the two Beasts and the 144,000 redeemed on Mount Zion to the specific sequence of the seven last plagues in Revelation 15 and 16. It serves as a summary of the divine judgments poured out from the vials.</meta>

# REVELATION 15 and 16 .. 52

<image-desc>An engraving illustrating the pouring of the seven vials of God's wrath from Revelation 15 and 16. In the upper half, multiple angels emerge from the clouds, each holding and pouring a large phial onto the earth, sea, and sun. A sun with a human face is visible in the center sky. Below, on the left, a multi-headed beast (the dragon) stands on the shore. On the right, kings and various figures are depicted in distress or observation. The background shows a landscape with mountains and a city. The scene is enclosed in a rectangular border.</image-desc>

->Seven Angels Pour Out their Plagues.<-

After the vision of the two Beasts, who persecuted the Saints, and the Lamb standing on Mount Zion with his 144,000 marked ones <note>original: "getekende", referring to the 144,000 sealed or marked by God in Revelation 14</note>, seven Angels are shown in a vision, pouring out seven Vials <term>vials: from the Greek "phiale," these were actually shallow, broad bowls used in temple rituals, here containing God's final judgments</term> or Plagues over the Kingdom of the Beast. The first is poured upon the Earth—which, according to some interpretations, refers to the earthly people dwelling in the Kingdom of the Antichrist. The second is poured into the Sea, the third into the Rivers, and the fourth into the Sun. The fifth is poured upon the Throne of the Beast, by which is understood the downfall or expulsion of the Beast. The sixth is poured upon the great River Euphrates; after its drying up, Kings from the East arise to bring about the destruction of the Beast. Then, out of the mouth of the Dragon go forth three unclean spirits, which are like frogs <note>original: "Vorſchen", an archaic Dutch term for frogs</note>, to gather the Kings of the Earth for the War of the great day. Finally, the seventh vial is poured out into the Air, with which all things come to an end.

V 5
<meta>catchword: Casting down (original Latin: "Dejiciens")</meta>

**Vocabulary:** Revelation, 7 Angels, Beasts, Saints, Lamb, Mount Zion, 144,000, Vials, Plagues, Antichrists, Euphrates, Dragons, Spirits, Frogs, War.

<summary>This page summarizes the seven bowls of God's wrath described in Revelation 15 and 16, detailing the specific targets of each plague and the eventual gathering for the final battle.</summary>
<keywords>Seven Vials, Book of Revelation, Antichrist, River Euphrates, Seven Plagues, Seven Angels, Apocalypse, 144,000</keywords>

--- Page 368 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the description of the seven vials of wrath on the previous page to a specific, dramatic scene from Revelation 18: the symbolic destruction of Babylon. It serves as a multilingual reflection—featuring Latin, French, Dutch, German, and 17th-century English—on the angel casting the millstone into the sea.</meta>

# The spiritual destruction of Babylon. <note>original Latin: "Interitus spiritualis Babylonis"</note>

*A Spirit casting a millstone down from a cloud* <note>The Latin text uses "Genius," referring here to the angel described in Revelation 18:21.</note>
*Exclaims: "Thus Babylon, doomed to perish, shall fall!"*
*Lament, O Nobles and greedy Merchants,*
*By the judgment of the Lord, your shop collapses.* <note>original Latin: "taberna," literally a shop or place of trade, emphasizing the city's commercial nature.</note>

*An Angel lets a millstone fall from heaven, and predicts the spiritual ruin of Babylon.*

*The Angel predicts evils, from which humans tremble,*
*And throws a heavy millstone into the sea;*
*But this Angel, above all by certain signs,*
*Predicts the resounding ruin of Babylon.*

### The Angel announces the fall of the great city of Babylon.

An Angel throws a stone down with great force,
And cries out that Babylon shall thus be overturned;
Whoever loves Virtue, let them depart freely from her walls,
So that the disaster of vice shall not overtake them. <note>This reflects the biblical call for God's people to "Come out of her, my people" to avoid sharing in her punishment.</note>

### The spiritual destruction of Babylon.

*An Angel lets a millstone fall, and cries;*
*Babylon, this shall crush thy braveries.* <note>This is the 17th-century English verse provided in the original manuscript.</note>
*Bewail O Nobles, Merchants, peasants all,*
*Your warehouse by God's Judgment's like to fall.*

### On the destruction of Babylon.

Here an Angel throws a millstone upon the earth,
"Thus must Babylon," he says, "be cast away,"
Because of her idolatry and heaped-up sins.
You merchant lords all, you have great need to lament;
The city is overturned, which carried so much for you,
Where so many goods were sold with great profit.

<summary>This page illustrates the fall of Babylon as described in Revelation 18, using the metaphor of an angel casting a heavy millstone into the sea to represent the city's irreversible destruction. The text warns of the end of worldly greed and calls for the virtuous to distance themselves from the city's corruption.</summary>
<keywords>Revelation 18, Babylon, Angel, Millstone, Divine Judgment, Merchants, Idolatry, Fall of the City</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exposition of Revelation 18, following the multilingual verses on the previous page with a prose summary in Dutch of the vision shown to the Apostle John. It provides a narrative overview of the symbolic actions of the angels and the resulting judgment on the city of Babylon.</meta>

# REVELATION 18. <note>original: "APOCAL. 18."</note>
<meta>catchword: Terribili (Dreadful); illustration-num: 53</meta>

->The Fall of Babylon.<-

AFTER the vision of the seven Bowls <note>The Dutch text uses "Phiolen," referring to the bowls or vials of God's wrath described in Revelation 15–16.</note>, the great Whore of Babylon, sitting on a scarlet Beast, is shown to John by one of the preceding seven Angels, who explains to him all the characteristics of the Babylonian Whore: after this Angel, he sees another Angel coming down with great power (which is interpreted by many as referring to Christ and his Last Judgment) proclaiming anew the fall of Babylon, whereupon a voice from Heaven was heard, exhorting the People of God to depart from Babylon <note>Refers to the command "Come out of her, my people," to avoid sharing in the city's sins and plagues.</note>, and crying out the coming Woe upon her and her inhabitants; a mighty Angel, lifting up a great stone <note>Specifically a large millstone, symbolizing something that sinks and never rises again.</note>, throws it into the Sea, thus depicting and confirming the final destruction and the terrible Judgment upon the Kingdom of the Antichrist, to the sorrow of her and all those who have committed fornication or conducted trade with her.

<summary>This page provides a narrative summary of Revelation 18, describing an angel casting a millstone into the sea to symbolize the permanent and violent fall of Babylon and the Antichrist's kingdom.</summary>
<keywords>Revelation 18, Fall of Babylon, Whore of Babylon, Last Judgment, Antichrist, millstone, seven bowls of wrath, Apostle John</keywords>

--- Page 370 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of visions from the Book of Revelation. Having previously described the fall of Babylon (Revelation 18), the text now moves to the events of Revelation 20: the binding of Satan for a thousand years and the final judgment before the Ancient of Days.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
# The Prince of this world is judged. <note>original Latin: "Princeps hujus mundi judicatus est."</note>

Sitting upon his terrifying throne, the Ancient of Days <note>A title for God from the Book of Daniel (7:9), representing His eternal and sovereign nature as Judge.</note> calls all to account and judgment. Here the Beast and the deceptions of the Dragon are laid bare; a dreadful abyss of sulfur and pitch seizes them.

*The Angel binds the Dragon and shuts him in the abyss, from which he must emerge after a thousand years to do evil.*

At last, the Angel chains the infernal Dragon and holds him for a thousand years locked in the abyss; afterwards, he will emerge to do evil once more, and woe to all those who live in sin.

### An Angel binds Satan for a thousand years.

Here the cunning Dragon is chained and secured in the sulfurous pool of the Abyss, by which he is prevented from misleading the nations or winning them to his side. The false language of lies must fall silent before the Truth.

### The Prince of this world judged.

Sitting on his throne, the Ancient of Days calls all before him to judge their ways; He exposes the Dragon's deceptions <note>original: "cosenage," an archaic word for fraud or trickery.</note> and confines him for a time to the dark, sulfurous mines of Hell.

### An Angel throws Satan into the Abyss.

The books are opened and judgment is held; God sits upon His throne in the form of an Ancient One. Everyone appears before His judgment seat. The Dragon and the ancient Serpent, who deceived the heathens—along with the Beast and the lying mouth <note>Referring to the False Prophet or the deceptive speech of the Antichrist.</note> that told such terrible lies—are both thrown into the fiery pool.

<meta>catchword: Na</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates and describes the binding of the Dragon (Satan) by an angel for a thousand years and the final judgment of the Beast before the throne of the Ancient of Days.</summary>
<keywords>Revelation 20, Satan, Ancient of Days, Final Judgment, Dragon, the Abyss, the Beast, thousand-year reign</keywords>

--- Page 371 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of visions from the Book of Revelation. Following the destruction of the Great Whore (representing worldly corruption) described on the previous page, the narrative now shifts to the binding of Satan and the "Millennium" or thousand-year reign as described in Revelation 20.</meta>

# REVELATION 20. 54 <note>original: "APOCAL. 20. 54"</note>

*The Binding of Satan for a Thousand Years.*

Following the vision of the fall of the **Great Whore** <note>A symbolic figure from Revelation 17–18 representing worldly corruption or a fallen city, often identified by Protestant reformers with Rome.</note> and the countless miseries that followed, John is shown in a vision an **Angel** descending from Heaven, holding the key to the **Abyss**, with which he binds **Satan** for a thousand years. This does not necessarily mean that this occurs only after the downfall of the **Babylonian Whore**; rather, as most interpreters <note>Referring to contemporary biblical scholars or theologians.</note> believe, it is a continuation of the struggle between the **Dragon** and **Christ** (who is introduced here in the form of an **Angel** <note>Some early modern theologians interpreted the "mighty angel" in Revelation as a representation of Christ Himself acting in His divine authority.</note>) previously touched upon by the **Apostle John** in his 12th chapter. 

While the **Devil** was thus bound fast, the **holy Martyrs** triumph with **Christ** in Heaven. After the completion of the thousand years, **Satan** is released again and misleads **Gog and Magog** <note>Names used in the Bible to represent the nations or forces of the world gathering for a final battle against the people of God.</note> to battle against the true Christians. However, they and their followers are devoured by fire from Heaven, and the **Devil** is cast into the **Pit of the Abyss**, whereupon the **Judgment** follows.

**Key Terms:** Revelation, Revelation, John, Satan, The Millennium, Whore of Babylon, Gog and Magog, Judgment, Martyrs.

Here <note>original Latin: "Hic"</note>

<summary>This page describes the vision of an angel binding Satan for a thousand years, the triumph of the martyrs, and the eventual final battle involving Gog and Magog before the Last Judgment.</summary>
<keywords>Revelation 20, Binding of Satan, Millennium, Whore of Babylon, Gog and Magog, Final Judgment, Martyrs, Archangel Michael</keywords>

Following the destruction of the old world, the author presents the "Heavenly City" as the eternal reward for the faithful.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>
-># Image of the Heavenly City. <note>original Latin: "Imago civitatis cælestis."</note><-

Here a new vision of the Holy City presents itself to the eyes,
Built entirely of gold, it shines with precious gems:
The glory of heavenly life is depicted in this form,
Which, O pious Christ, grant to your servants upon your return.
<note>original Latin: "Hic nova se facies oculis Sanctæ ingerit Urbis..."</note>

---

### *The Image of the celestial Jerusalem.* <note>original French: "L'Image de la celeste Jerusalem."</note>

*Here it is nothing but colored gold and jasper,* <note>Jasper is a variety of silica often mentioned in the Bible as a precious stone representing the glory of God.</note>
*It is the marvelous plan of the celestial City;*
*Grant that to us, O Great God! in your gilded Palaces,*
*Your glory may one day be fully manifested.*

---

### John sees the new Jerusalem descending from Heaven. <note>original Dutch: "Johannes ziet het nieuwe Jerusalem nederdalen uit den Hemel."</note>

Hier I see in the Spirit the city of God newly rebuilt,
The New Jerusalem where everything glitters with gold.
Why should one toil here on Earth for the world's treasures!
In Zion’s City of Peace <note>original Dutch: "Sions Vrede-stad." Jerusalem literally translates as "foundation of peace."</note> one treads upon gold with one's feet.

---

### *The Image of the celestial City.*

*Here you the holy City may behold,*
*Built all with precious stones made fast with gold;*
*Under this likeness I Heaven's Joys express,*
*With which, Dear Christ, at your return us bless.*
<note>This section was originally written in early modern English; the spelling has been modernized for clarity.</note>

---

### John sees the new Jerusalem! <note>original German: "Johannes siehet das neue Jerusalem!"</note>

The new Jerusalem, built up by God Himself,
With gold and precious stones most costly to behold,
Is a magnificent prototype of the Heavenly Kingdom.
Through this are depicted joy, bliss, and eternal life,
Which the true Lamb of God, our Lord Jesus Christ,
Will graciously share and give to us.

<meta>catchword: After</meta>

<summary>This page provides a multilingual meditation (Latin, French, Dutch, English, and German) on the New Jerusalem as described in Revelation 21. It emphasizes the city's splendor—gold and precious stones—as a symbolic representation of the eternal joy and peace awaiting believers in the presence of Christ.</summary>

--- Page 373 ---
<meta>This page follows the poetic and visual descriptions of the New Jerusalem from page 110. While the previous page provided verses in Latin, French, and English to evoke the beauty of the city, this page (numbered 55 in this section) provides a theological summary and a cautionary note on how to interpret the vision of Saint John. It marks the conclusion of the commentary on the Book of Revelation.</meta>

REVELATION 21. <note>original Latin: "APOCAL. 21."</note>
<margin>III</margin>

<image-desc>A detailed engraving showing Saint John the Apostle kneeling on a hillside in the foreground, gesturing toward a vision. Behind him stands a winged angel pointing toward the valley. In the background, the "Heavenly Jerusalem" is depicted as a vast, rectangular walled city with architectural structures and gardens arranged in a grid. The city is set in a mountainous landscape under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

->*The Heavenly Jerusalem.*<-

FOLLOWING the holding of the Great Judgment, John beholds a new Heaven and a new Earth, as well as the new Jerusalem descending from God out of Heaven. The form and characteristics of this city were more clearly shown to John by an Angel; namely, its glory and brilliance, consisting of the bright shining light, and the elegance of its structure, gleaming with twelve precious stones <note>The "twelve stones" refer to the foundations of the city wall, traditionally identified as jasper, sapphire, chalcedony, emerald, sardonyx, carnelian, chrysolite, beryl, topaz, chrysoprase, jacinth, and amethyst.</note> and the purest gold. There, the Elect <term>Elect: A theological term referring to those chosen by God for eternal life.</term> walk in all glory and joy with the Almighty God and the Lamb <term>the Lamb: A symbolic title for Jesus Christ, representing his sacrifice and subsequent triumph.</term>, who there, instead of a Temple, manifest their brilliance and majesty. That which is said here regarding the spiritual Jerusalem—that it descended out of Heaven—must not be understood as something that will happen according to the Letter <note>i.e., literally or physically</note>, but is only said thus in regards to the example or vision that John saw of it; otherwise, it would conflict with other passages of the Holy Scripture.

->T H E  E N D.<-

<summary>This concluding page describes the New Jerusalem as a city of light and gold where the saved dwell with God, while offering a scholarly warning that the vision is a spiritual metaphor rather than a literal physical event.</summary>
<keywords>New Jerusalem, Saint John, Revelation 21, the Elect, the Lamb of God, biblical interpretation, spiritual vs literal, heavenly city</keywords>
